### root.text.Part 1..73.9.sub_1 (Main Text)
**English:** Thus, for example, one would say: This copper is no more apt to receive the form of an ewer than to receive that of a candlestick; if, therefore, we find it a candlestick or an ewer, we know thereby of necessity that a <i>determining</i> (artificer) <i>acting with intention</i> hath had in view one of these two <i>admissible</i> forms. Now, it is evident that the copper <i>existed</i>, and that before the action of him who gave preference to one of the two <i>admissible</i> forms attributed to it, there was naught non-existent there save these very forms[[fn:1166]]. But, as for that being concerning which there is no agreement whether its existence hath always been and shall always be such as it is, or whether it began to exist after (absolute) non-being, it can in no wise give rise to this thought[[fn:1167]]; and one could not ask: “Who is he that hath preferred its existence to its non-existence?” save after having recognized[[fn:1168]] that it came into existence after not having existed, which is precisely the matter whereon there is no agreement. For if we took its existence and its non-existence for something ideal[[fn:1169]], we should simply return to the <sup>Xth</sup> proposition, which saith that regard must be had only to imaginations and ideas, and not to real and <i>intelligible</i> things[[fn:1170]]; for the adversary, who admitteth the eternity of the world, thinketh that the imaginary supposition of the non-existence of the world is like unto the supposition of any other impossible thing that might come to our imagination. — But it is not the purpose here to refute their opinions[[fn:1171]]; I have desired only to set forth unto thee that it is not true, as hath been pretended, that this method[[fn:1172]] is different from the preceding one, and that on the contrary it is with this as with the former, in that it supposeth[[fn:1173]] that <i>admissibility</i> which is known[[fn:1174]].
**Correction:** Thus, for example, one would say: This copper is no more apt to receive the form of an ewer than to receive that of a candlestick; if, therefore, we find it a candlestick or an ewer, we know thereby of necessity that a <i>determining</i> (artificer) <i>acting with intention</i> hath had in view one of these two <i>admissible</i> forms. Now, it is evident that the copper <i>existed</i>, and that before the action of him who gave preference to one of the two <i>admissible</i> forms attributed to it, there was naught non-existent there save these very forms[[fn:1166]]. But, as for that being concerning which there is no agreement whether its existence hath always been and shall always be such as it is, or whether it began to exist after (absolute) non-being, it can in no wise give rise to this thought[[fn:1167]]; and one could not ask: “Who is he that hath preferred its existence to its non-existence?” save after having recognized[[fn:1168]] that it came into existence after not having existed, which is precisely the matter whereon there is no agreement. For if we took its existence and its non-existence for something ideal[[fn:1169]], we should simply return to the <sup>Xth</sup> proposition, which saith that regard must be had only to imaginations and ideas, and not to real and <i>intelligible</i> things[[fn:1170]]; for the adversary, who admitteth the eternity of the world, thinketh that the imaginary supposition of the non-existence of the world is like unto the supposition of any other impossible thing that might come to our imagination. — But it is not the purpose here to refute their opinions[[fn:1171]]; I have desired only to set forth unto thee that it is not true, as hath been pretended, that this method[[fn:1172]] is different from the preceding one, and that on the contrary it is with this as with the former, in that it supposeth[[fn:1173]] that <i>admissibility</i> which is known[[fn:1174]].

**French (Source):** Ainsi, par exemple, on dirait: Ce cuivre n’est pas plutôt apte à recevoir la forme d’une aiguière qu’à recevoir celle d’un chandelier; donc, si nous le trouvons chandelier ou aiguière, nous savons par là nécessairement qu’un (ouvrier) [[t:36]]déterminant[[t:37]] et [[t:38]]agissant avec intention[[t:39]] a eu en vue l’une de ces deux formes [[t:40]]admissibles[[t:41]]. Or, il est évident que le cuivre [[t:42]]existait[[t:43]], et qu’avant l’action de celui qui a donné la préférence à l’une des deux formes [[t:44]]admissibles[[t:45]] qui lui sont attribuées, il n’y avait là de non existant que celles-ci[[fn:1166]]. Mais, pour ce qui est de cet être au sujet duquel on n’est pas d’accord si son existence a toujours été et sera toujours telle qu’elle est, ou s’il a commencé à exister après le non-être (absolu), il ne peut nullement donner lieu à cette pensée[[fn:1167]]; et on ne saurait demander: «Qui est celui qui en a préféré l’existence à la non-existence?» si ce n’est après avoir reconnu[[fn:1168]] qu’il est arrivé à l’existence après ne pas avoir existé, ce qui est précisément la chose sur laquelle on n’est pas d’accord. Que si nous prenions son existence et sa non-existence pour quelque chose d’idéal[[fn:1169]], nous en reviendrions tout simplement à la X[[t:46]]e[[t:47]] proposition, qui dit qu’il faut avoir égard seulement aux imaginations et aux idées, et non pas aux choses réelles et [[t:48]]intelligibles[[t:49]][[fn:1170]]; car l’adversaire, qui admet l’éternité du monde, pense qu’il en est de la supposition imaginaire de la non-existence du monde comme de la supposition de toute autre chose impossible qui nous viendrait à l’imagination. — Mais on n’a pas ici pour but de réfuter leurs opinions[[fn:1171]]; j’ai voulu seulement t’exposer qu’il n’est pas vrai, comme on l’a prétendu, que cette méthode[[fn:1172]] soit différente de celle qui précède, et qu’au contraire il en est de celle-ci comme de la précédente, en ce qu’elle suppose[[fn:1173]] cette [[t:50]]admissibilité[[t:51]] qu’on connaît[[fn:1174]].

**Hebrew (Makbili):** [Not Found]

### root.text.Part 1..75.3 (Main Text)
**English:** This proof may be refuted[[fn:1246]] in two manners. First, one might object: «I do not grant the <i>non-similitude</i>, for how shall you demonstrate that God can in no wise resemble any of His creatures? Unless, by God[[fn:1247]]! you rely in this regard [I mean, concerning the negation of similitude] upon some text of a prophetic book; but then the incorporeality of God would be a thing received by tradition, and not an object of the intellect.» And if it were said that God, if He resembled aught among His creatures, would have created His like, the adversary might reply: «But it is not His like in every aspect; for I deny not that the divinity containeth many ideas and presenteth itself under several aspects.» And indeed, he who believeth in the corporeality of God contesteth not this.
**Correction:** This proof may be refuted[[fn:1246]] in two manners. First, one might object: «I do not grant the <i>non-similitude</i>, for how shall you demonstrate that God can in no wise resemble any of His creatures? Unless, by God[[fn:1247]]! you rely in this regard [I mean, concerning the negation of similitude] upon some text of a prophetic book; but then the incorporeality of God would be a thing received by tradition, and not an object of the intellect.» And if it were said that God, if He resembled aught among His creatures, would have created His like, the adversary might reply: «But it is not His like in every aspect; for I deny not that the divinity containeth many ideas and presenteth itself under several aspects.» And indeed, he who believeth in the corporeality of God contesteth not this.

**French (Source):** Cette preuve peut se réfuter[[fn:1246]] de deux manières. Premièrement, quelqu’un pourrait objecter: «Je n’accorde pas la [[t:0]]non-similitude[[t:1]], car comment démontrerez-vous que Dieu ne saurait ressembler en rien à aucune de ses créatures? A moins, par Dieu[[fn:1247]]! que vous ne vous en rapportiez à cet égard [je veux dire, pour ce qui concerne la négation de la similitude] à quelque texte d’un livre prophétique; mais alors l’incorporalité de Dieu serait une chose reçue par tradition, et non pas un objet de l’intelligence.» Et si l’on disait que Dieu, s’il ressemblait à quelque chose d’entre ses créatures, aurait créé son semblable, l’adversaire pourrait répondre: «Mais ce n’est pas son semblable sous toutes les faces; car je ne nie pas que la divinité renferme des idées nombreuses et qu’elle se présente sous plusieurs faces.» Et en effet, celui qui croit à la corporéité de Dieu ne conteste pas cela. 

**Hebrew (Makbili):** <sup>5</sup> את הראיה הזו יש להפריך בשני אופנים. <br><sup>א)</sup> האחד מהם הוא שיאמר האומר: "איני מקבל את העדר הדומוּת, ואיזו הוכחה יש לך שלא ייתכן שהאלוה ידמה לדבר מברואיו בדבר כלשהו? אלא אם כן אתה נתלה בכך בלשון ספר נבואה, כוונתי לשלילת כל דומוּת שהיא, ואם כן שלילת הגשמות מקובלת (מן הדת) ולא מושכלת". ואם תאמר: "אילו היה דומה לדבר מברואיו, הרי ברא משהו כמוהו" – יאמר החולק: "אין הוא כמוהו בכל הבחינות כולן, ואיני מכחיש שיש באלוה עניינים רבים ושיש לו אופנים"; כי מאמין הגשמות אינו נרתע מכך.

---

### root.text.Part 1..75.5.sub_0 (Main Text)
**English:** <small>THE THIRD METHOD</small> is this: If God, say they, were a body, He would be <i>finite</i> [which is true], and if He were finite, He would have a certain measure and a certain determinate figure [which is likewise a true consequence]. Now, say they, whatever be the measure and the figure (one supposeth), God, inasmuch as He is a <i>body</i>, could be greater or smaller than that measure and have a figure different from that one; if therefore He be determined by a certain measure and a certain figure, there was required for this a <i>determining being</i>[[fn:1253]]. — This demonstration also I have heard extolled by them, although it be weaker than all that precedeth, being based upon the X<sup>th</sup> proposition, of which we have already exposed all the difficulties that it presenteth even with regard to other beings [since it is supposed they might have a nature different from that which they have[[fn:1254]]], and how much more so with regard to God. There is no difference between this reasoning and what they say concerning the <i>preference</i> accorded to the existence of the world over its non-existence, and which (according to them) would prove that there is an agent who hath <i>preferred</i> its existence over its non-existence, both being possible[[fn:1255]]. Now, if one were to ask them: why doth this not apply to God, and why say we not that, since He existeth, there must be a being who hath preferred His existence to His non-existence? they would doubtless answer: it is because that would lead to an (infinite) chain, and that it would be necessary to stop at a <i>necessary</i> being, in whom there is no <i>possibility</i> and who hath no need of another being to bring Him into existence. But this same answer may be applied to figure and to measure. In effect, that figures and measures should be of but <i>possible</i> existence, they must apply to a being who existeth after not having existed, and of such a being only can it be said[[fn:1256]]: it was possible that he were greater or smaller than he really is and that he had a figure different from that which he hath, so that he had absolute need of a <i>determining</i> being.
**Correction:** <small>THE THIRD METHOD</small> is this: If God, say they, were a body, He would be <i>finite</i> [which is true], and if He were finite, He would have a certain measure and a certain determinate figure [which is likewise a true consequence]. Now, say they, whatever be the measure and the figure (one supposeth), God, inasmuch as He is a <i>body</i>, could be greater or smaller than that measure and have a figure different from that one; if therefore He be determined by a certain measure and a certain figure, there was required for this a <i>determining being</i>[[fn:1253]]. — This demonstration also I have heard extolled by them, although it be weaker than all that precedeth, being based upon the X<sup>th</sup> proposition, of which we have already exposed all the difficulties that it presenteth even with regard to other beings [since it is supposed they might have a nature different from that which they have[[fn:1254]]], and how much more so with regard to God. There is no difference between this reasoning and what they say concerning the <i>preference</i> accorded to the existence of the world over its non-existence, and which (according to them) would prove that there is an agent who hath <i>preferred</i> its existence over its non-existence, both being possible[[fn:1255]]. Now, if one were to ask them: why doth this not apply to God, and why say we not that, since He existeth, there must be a being who hath preferred His existence to His non-existence? they would doubtless answer: it is because that would lead to an (infinite) chain, and that it would be necessary to stop at a <i>necessary</i> being, in whom there is no <i>possibility</i> and who hath no need of another being to bring Him into existence. But this same answer may be applied to figure and to measure. In effect, that figures and measures should be of but <i>possible</i> existence, they must apply to a being who existeth after not having existed, and of such a being only can it be said[[fn:1256]]: it was possible that he were greater or smaller than he really is and that he had a figure different from that which he hath, so that he had absolute need of a <i>determining</i> being.

**French (Source):** L[[t:0]]A TROISIÈME MÉTHODE[[t:1]] est celle-ci: Si Dieu, disent-ils, était un corps, il serait [[t:2]]fini[[t:3]] [ce qui est vrai], et s’il était fini, il aurait une certaine mesure et une certaine figure déterminée [ce qui est également une conséquence vraie]. Or, disent-ils, quelles que soient la mesure et la figure (qu’on suppose), Dieu, en tant que [[t:4]]corps[[t:5]], pourrait être plus grand ou plus petit que cette mesure et avoir une figure différente de celle-là; si donc il est déterminé par une certaine mesure et une certaine figure, il a fallu pour cela un [[t:6]]être déterminant[[t:7]][[fn:1253]]. — Cette démonstration aussi, je l’ai entendu vanter par eux, bien qu’elle soit plus faible que tout ce qui précède, étant basée sur la X[[t:8]]e[[t:9]] proposition, dont nous avons déjà exposé toutes les difficultés qu’elle présente à l’égard même des autres êtres [puisqu’on suppose qu’ils pourraient avoir une nature différente de celle qu’ils ont[[fn:1254]]], et à plus forte raison à l’égard de Dieu. Il n’y a pas de différence entre ce raisonnement-ci et ce qu’ils disent au sujet de la [[t:10]]préférence[[t:11]] accordée à l’existence du monde sur sa non-existence, et qui (selon eux) prouverait qu’il y a un agent qui en a [[t:12]]préféré[[t:13]] l’existence à la non-existence, l’une et l’autre étant possibles[[fn:1255]]. Or, si on leur demandait: pourquoi cela ne s’applique-t-il pas à Dieu et pourquoi ne dit-on pas que, puisqu’il existe, il doive y avoir un être qui en ait préféré l’existence à la non-existence? ils répondraient sans doute: c’est parce que cela conduirait à un enchaînement (infini) et qu’il faudrait nécessairement s’arrêter à un être [[t:14]]nécessaire[[t:15]], dans lequel il n’y ait point de [[t:16]]possibilité[[t:17]] et qui n’ait pas besoin d’un autre être qui le fasse exister. Mais cette même réponse peut s’appliquer à la figure et à la mesure. En effet, pour que les figures et les mesures ne soient que d’une existence [[t:18]]possible[[t:19]], il faut qu’elles s’appliquent à un être qui existe après ne pas avoir existé, et d’un tel être seulement on pourra dire[[fn:1256]]: il était possible qu’il fût plus grand ou plus petit qu’il n’est réellement et qu’il eût une figure différente de celle qu’il a, de sorte qu’il avait absolument besoin d’un être [[t:20]]déterminant[[t:21]].

**Hebrew (Makbili):** [Not Found]

### root.text.Part 1..75.5.sub_1 (Main Text)
**English:** But as touching the figure and the measure of God [far from Him be all imperfection and assimilation!], the partisan of corporeality will say: «It was not after not having existed that He arrived at existence, so that He should have had need of a <i>determining</i> being. On the contrary, His essence with the measure and the figure which it hath is thus of a <i>necessary</i> existence; it hath had no need of a being who should <i>determine</i> it nor who should <i>prefer</i>[[fn:1257]] its existence over its non-existence, for there is in it no possibility of non-existence; and likewise it hath had no need of a being who should give it a determinate figure and measure, for its existence was necessary in this manner[[fn:1258]].»
**Correction:** But as touching the figure and the measure of God [far from Him be all imperfection and assimilation!], the partisan of corporeality will say: «It was not after not having existed that He arrived at existence, so that He should have had need of a <i>determining</i> being. On the contrary, His essence with the measure and the figure which it hath is thus of a <i>necessary</i> existence; it hath had no need of a being who should <i>determine</i> it nor who should <i>prefer</i>[[fn:1257]] its existence over its non-existence, for there is in it no possibility of non-existence; and likewise it hath had no need of a being who should give it a determinate figure and measure, for its existence was necessary in this manner[[fn:1258]].»

**French (Source):** Mais, pour ce qui est de la figure et de la mesure de Dieu [loin de lui toute imperfection et assimilation!], le partisan de la corporéité dira: «Ce n’est point après ne pas avoir existé qu’il est arrivé à l’existence, de sorte qu’il ait eu besoin d’un être [[t:22]]déterminant[[t:23]]. Au contraire, son essence avec la mesure et la figure qu’elle a est ainsi d’une existence [[t:24]]nécessaire;[[t:25]] elle n’a point eu besoin d’un être qui la [[t:26]]déterminât[[t:27]] ni qui en [[t:28]]préférât[[t:29]][[fn:1257]] l’existence à la non-existence, car il n’y a en elle aucune possibilité de non-existence; et de même elle n’a point eu besoin d’un être qui lui donnât une figure et une mesure déterminées, car son existence était nécessaire de cette manière[[fn:1258]].»

**Hebrew (Makbili):** [Not Found]

### root.text.Part 2..1.0 (Main Text)
**English:** This fifth body, which is the celestial sphere, must perforce be either something which is generated and perisheth [and it shall be the same with motion], or else something which is neither generated nor perisheth, as the adversary saith[[fn:1397]]. Now, if the celestial sphere be a thing which is generated and perisheth, that which hath caused it to exist after non-existence is God [glorified be His name!]; this is a primary notion, for whatsoever existeth after not having existed necessarily presupposeth something that hath called it into existence, and it is inadmissible that it should have brought itself into existence. If, on the contrary, this sphere hath never ceased and shall never cease to move thus by a perpetual and eternal motion, it must needs be, by virtue of the preceding propositions, that that which imparteth unto it eternal motion be neither a body nor a force within a body; and this shall still be God [glorified be His name!]. It is therefore manifest that the existence of God [a Necessary Being, without cause, and whose existence is in itself exempt from all <i>possibility</i>] is demonstrated by decisive and certain proofs[[fn:1398]], no matter whether the world be a creation <i>ex nihilo</i>, or whether it be not. Likewise, it is established by demonstrations that He (God) is <i>one</i> and incorporeal, as we have previously stated: for the demonstration of His unity and His incorporeity remaineth established, whether the world be, or be not, a creation <i>ex nihilo</i>, as we have set forth in the third of the philosophical methods[[fn:1399]], and as we have (afterwards) separately set forth the incorporeity and the unity by philosophical methods[[fn:1400]].
**Correction:** This fifth body, which is the celestial sphere, must perforce be either something which is generated and perisheth [and it shall be the same with motion], or else something which is neither generated nor perisheth, as the adversary saith[[fn:1397]]. Now, if the celestial sphere be a thing which is generated and perisheth, that which hath caused it to exist after non-existence is God [glorified be His name!]; this is a primary notion, for whatsoever existeth after not having existed necessarily presupposeth something that hath called it into existence, and it is inadmissible that it should have brought itself into existence. If, on the contrary, this sphere hath never ceased and shall never cease to move thus by a perpetual and eternal motion, it must needs be, by virtue of the preceding propositions, that that which imparteth unto it eternal motion be neither a body nor a force within a body; and this shall still be God [glorified be His name!]. It is therefore manifest that the existence of God [a Necessary Being, without cause, and whose existence is in itself exempt from all <i>possibility</i>] is demonstrated by decisive and certain proofs[[fn:1398]], no matter whether the world be a creation <i>ex nihilo</i>, or whether it be not. Likewise, it is established by demonstrations that He (God) is <i>one</i> and incorporeal, as we have previously stated: for the demonstration of His unity and His incorporeity remaineth established, whether the world be, or be not, a creation <i>ex nihilo</i>, as we have set forth in the third of the philosophical methods[[fn:1399]], and as we have (afterwards) separately set forth the incorporeity and the unity by philosophical methods[[fn:1400]].

**French (Source):** Ce cinquième corps, qui est la sphère céleste, doit nécessairement être, ou bien quelque chose qui naît et périt [et il en sera de même du mouvement], ou bien quelque chose qui ne naît ni ne périt, comme le dit l’adversaire[[fn:1397]]. Or, si la sphère céleste est une chose qui naît et périt, ce qui l’a fait exister après le non-être, c’est Dieu [que son nom soit glorifié!]; c’est là une notion première, car tout ce qui existe après ne pas avoir existé suppose nécessairement quelque chose qui l’ait appelé à l’existence, et il est inadmissible qu’il se soit fait exister lui-même. Si, au contraire, cette sphère n’a jamais cessé et ne cessera jamais de se mouvoir ainsi par un mouvement perpétuel et éternel, il faut, en vertu des propositions qui précèdent, que ce qui lui imprime le mouvement éternel ne soit ni un corps, ni une force dans un corps; et ce sera encore Dieu [que son nom soit glorifié!]. Il est donc clair que l’existence de Dieu [être nécessaire, sans cause, et dont l’existence est en elle-même exempte de toute [[t:0]]possibilité[[t:1]]] est démontrée par des preuves décisives et certaines[[fn:1398]], n’importe que le monde soit une création [[t:2]]ex nihilo[[t:3]], ou qu’il ne le soit pas. De même, il est établi par des démonstrations qu’il (Dieu) est [[t:4]]un[[t:5]] et incorporel, comme nous l’avons dit précédemment: car la démonstration de son unité et de son incorporalité reste établie, n’importe que le monde soit, ou non, une création [[t:6]]ex nihilo[[t:7]], comme nous l’avons exposé dans la troisième des méthodes philosophiques[[fn:1399]], et comme (ensuite) nous avons exposé (à part) l’incorporalité et l’unité par des méthodes philosophiques[[fn:1400]].

**Hebrew (Makbili):** <b>סיכום ההוכחות ומטרת החיבור</b><br><span class="mediumGrey">סיכום ההוכחות: האל קיים</span><br><sup>1</sup> הגוף החמישי הזה, כלומר הגלגל – הכרח שיהיה <br><sup>חידוש)</sup> או מתהווה וכלה, וכן התנועה מתהווה וכלה, <br><sup>קדמות)</sup> או לא מתהווה ולא כלה, כמו שטוען היריב (=אריסטו). <br><sup>חידוש)</sup> אם הגלגל מתהווה וכלה – הרי המביאו למציאות אחר ההעדר הוא האלוה יתהדר שמו. וזה מושכל ראשון, כי כל מה שנמצא אחר ההעדר יש לו בהכרח מביא למציאות, ובלתי אפשרי שיברא את עצמו. <br><sup>קדמות)</sup> ואם הגלגל הזה נע כך תנועה תמידית ונצחית מעולם ועד עולם – נובע מן ההנחות שהקדמתי שהמניע אותו בתנועה הנצחית הזאת אינו גוף ולא כוח בגוף, והוא האלוה יתהדר שמו. <br><sup>מסקנה)</sup> הובהר לך אם כן שעל מציאות האלוה יתעלה, שהוא מחויב המציאות שאין לו סיבה ושאין "אפשרות" להימצאותו בבחינת עצמו, מורות הוכחות חותכות ווודאיות, בין אם העולם מחודש לאחר העדר ובין אם אינו מחודש לאחר העדר. וכן מורות ההוכחות על היותו אחד ושאינו גוף, כמו שאמרנו לעיל, כי ההוכחה שהוא אחד ושאינו גוף תקפה בין אם העולם מחודש לאחר העדר ובין אם אינו כך, כמו שביארנו (ב,א) בדרך השלישית מן הדרכים הפילוסופיות, וכמו שביארנו בביאור שלילת הגשמות וקביעת הייחוד בדרכים הפילוסופיות.

---

### root.text.Part 2..12.1 (Main Text)
**English:** I. The <i>first opinion</i>, embraced by all those who admit the Law of Moses our Master, is this: That the universe in its totality, I mean every being except God, was produced by God from pure and absolute non-existence; that there had existed at first only God alone, and naught beside Him, neither angel, nor sphere, nor that which is within the celestial sphere; that subsequently He produced all these beings, such as they are, by His free will and not <i>out of</i> any thing; finally, that time itself is likewise part of the created things, since it accompanies motion, which is an accident of the thing moved, and that this thing itself, whose motion time accompanies, was created and hath <i>come into being</i> after having not existed. For if it be said, “God was before He created the world,”—where the word <i>was</i> denoteth a time,—and likewise if it followeth thence for the mind[[fn:1585]] that His existence before the creation of the world was <i>prolonged</i> unto infinity, there is in all this naught but a supposition or imagination of time, and not the reality of time; for time is indubitably an accident, and it belongeth, in our view, to the <i>created</i> accidents as much as blackness and whiteness. Although it be not of the species of <i>quality</i>[[fn:1586]], it is yet, in short, an accident inherent in motion, as is clear unto him who hath understood what Aristotle saith to explain time and its true being[[fn:1587]].
**Correction:** I. The <i>first opinion</i>, embraced by all those who admit the Law of Moses our Master, is this: That the universe in its totality, I mean every being except God, was produced by God from pure and absolute non-existence; that there had existed at first only God alone, and naught beside Him, neither angel, nor sphere, nor that which is within the celestial sphere; that subsequently He produced all these beings, such as they are, by His free will and not <i>out of</i> any thing; finally, that time itself is likewise part of the created things, since it accompanies motion, which is an accident of the thing moved, and that this thing itself, whose motion time accompanies, was created and hath <i>come into being</i> after having not existed. For if it be said, “God was before He created the world,”—where the word <i>was</i> denoteth a time,—and likewise if it followeth thence for the mind[[fn:1585]] that His existence before the creation of the world was <i>prolonged</i> unto infinity, there is in all this naught but a supposition or imagination of time, and not the reality of time; for time is indubitably an accident, and it belongeth, in our view, to the <i>created</i> accidents as much as blackness and whiteness. Although it be not of the species of <i>quality</i>[[fn:1586]], it is yet, in short, an accident inherent in motion, as is clear unto him who hath understood what Aristotle saith to explain time and its true being[[fn:1587]].

**French (Source):** I. La [[t:0]]première opinion[[t:1]], embrassée par tous ceux qui admettent la Loi de Moïse, notre maître, est (celle-ci): Que l’univers, dans sa totalité, je veux dire tout être hormis Dieu, c’est Dieu qui l’a produit du néant pur et absolu; qu’il n’avait existé (d’abord) que Dieu seul et rien en dehors de lui, ni ange, ni sphère, ni ce qui est à l’intérieur de la sphère céleste; qu’ensuite il a produit tous ces êtres, tels qu’ils sont, par sa libre volonté et non pas [[t:2]]de[[t:3]] quelque chose; enfin, que le temps lui-même aussi fait partie des choses créées, puisqu’il accompagne le mouvement, lequel est un accident de la chose mue, et que cette chose ellemême dont le temps accompagne le mouvement a été créée et est [[t:4]]née[[t:5]] après ne pas avoir existé. Que si l’on dit: «Dieu fut avant de créer le monde,» — où le mot [[t:6]]fut[[t:7]] indique un temps,— et de même s’il s’ensuit de là pour la pensée[[fn:1585]] que son existence avant la création du monde s’est [[t:8]]prolongée[[t:9]] à l’infini, il n’y a dans tout cela que supposition ou imagination de temps et non pas réalité de temps; car le temps est indubitablement un accident, et il fait partie, selon nous, des accidents [[t:10]]créés[[t:11]] aussi bien que la noirceur et la blancheur. Bien qu’il ne soit pas de l’espèce de la [[t:12]]qualité[[t:13]][[fn:1586]], il est pourtant, en somme, un accident inhérent au mouvement, comme il est clair pour celui qui a compris ce que dit Aristote pour expliquer le temps et son véritable être[[fn:1587]].

**Hebrew (Makbili):** <span class="mediumGrey">דעת התורה: חידוש העולם</span><br><sup>2</sup> הדעה הראשונה והיא דעת כל המאמין בתורת משה, היא שהעולם בכללותו, כוונתי לכל מצוי חוץ מהאל יתעלה – האל הביאו למציאות לאחר ההעדר המוחלט והגמור; ושהאל יתעלה לבדו היה מצוי ולא דבר מלבדו: לא מלאך ולא גלגל ולא מה שבתוך הגלגל, ולאחר מכן הביא למציאות את כל המצויים האלה כפי שהם ברצונו ובחפצו, לא מדָבָר; ושהזמן עצמו גם הוא מכלל הנבראים, כי הזמן הולך אחר התנועה, והתנועה היא מקרה בנע, והנע הזה עצמו, שהזמן הולך אחר תנועתו, מחודש, והתהווה לאחר שלא היה.<br><span class="mediumGrey">ניתוח המושג זמן</span><br><sup>3</sup> ומה שאומרים "האל היה לפני שנברא העולם", כשהמילה "היה" מורה על זמן, וכן כל מה שעולה על הדעת כאילו שמציאותו לפני בריאת העולם ממשיכה עד אינסוף – כל זה זמן משוער או זמן מדומיין, ולא זמן לאמיתו. כי הזמן הוא בלי ספק מקרה, והוא לדעתנו מכלל המקרים הנבראים כמו השחרות והלובן, אף על פי שאין הוא ממין האיכות, אלא הוא בכללותו מקרה הנלווה לתנועה, כמבואר למי שמבין את דברי אריסטו בנוגע לביאור הזמן ולמהותו האמיתית של קיומו.

---

### root.text.Part 2..12.6 (Main Text)
**English:** II. The <i>second opinion</i> is that of all the philosophers of whom we have heard tell, or whose words we have seen. It is inadmissible, say they, that God should produce aught from nothing; nor is it possible, according to their view, that a thing be reduced to (absolute) non-existence; I mean to say, that it is not possible that any being whatsoever, having matter and form, should be born without matter having ever existed, nor that it should perish in such wise that matter itself be reduced to absolute non-existence[[fn:1592]]. To attribute unto God the power of (doing) such a thing would be, in their sight, as though one attributed unto Him the power of joining together at the same instant[[fn:1593]] two contraries, or of creating His like, or of becoming corporeal, or of creating a square whose diagonal should be equal to its side, or similar impossible things. What is implied in their words is that they mean to say that, even as He cannot be charged with impotence for not producing impossible things—for the impossible hath a stable nature, which is not the work of an agent, and which, because of this, is invariable[[fn:1594]]—likewise one cannot attribute impotence unto Him because He should not be capable of producing aught from (absolute) non-existence; for that belongeth to the category of all impossible things. They believe, therefore, that there existeth a matter which is eternal even as God is eternal; that He existeth not without it, nor it without Him. Nevertheless, they do not believe for all that that it occupieth in being the same rank as God; but, on the contrary, God is (according to them) the <i>cause</i> wherefore it existeth, and it is unto Him as the clay is unto the potter, or as the iron is unto the smith. He createth therein what He willeth: now He formeth of it heaven and earth, and now He formeth of it somewhat else.
**Correction:** II. The <i>second opinion</i> is that of all the philosophers of whom we have heard tell, or whose words we have seen. It is inadmissible, say they, that God should produce aught from nothing; nor is it possible, according to their view, that a thing be reduced to (absolute) non-existence; I mean to say, that it is not possible that any being whatsoever, having matter and form, should be born without matter having ever existed, nor that it should perish in such wise that matter itself be reduced to absolute non-existence[[fn:1592]]. To attribute unto God the power of (doing) such a thing would be, in their sight, as though one attributed unto Him the power of joining together at the same instant[[fn:1593]] two contraries, or of creating His like, or of becoming corporeal, or of creating a square whose diagonal should be equal to its side, or similar impossible things. What is implied in their words is that they mean to say that, even as He cannot be charged with impotence for not producing impossible things—for the impossible hath a stable nature, which is not the work of an agent, and which, because of this, is invariable[[fn:1594]]—likewise one cannot attribute impotence unto Him because He should not be capable of producing aught from (absolute) non-existence; for that belongeth to the category of all impossible things. They believe, therefore, that there existeth a matter which is eternal even as God is eternal; that He existeth not without it, nor it without Him. Nevertheless, they do not believe for all that that it occupieth in being the same rank as God; but, on the contrary, God is (according to them) the <i>cause</i> wherefore it existeth, and it is unto Him as the clay is unto the potter, or as the iron is unto the smith. He createth therein what He willeth: now He formeth of it heaven and earth, and now He formeth of it somewhat else.

**French (Source):** II. La [[t:0]]deuxième opinion[[t:1]] est celle de tous les philosophes dont nous avons entendu parler, ou dont nous avons vu les paroles. Il est inadmissible, disent-ils, que Dieu produise quelque chose du néant, et il n’est pas non plus possible, selon eux, qu’une chose soit réduite au néant (absolu); je veux dire, qu’il n’est pas possible qu’un être quelconque, ayant matière et forme, soit né sans que la matière ait jamais existé, ni qu’il périsse de manière que la matière elle-même soit réduite au néant absolu[[fn:1592]]. Attribuer à Dieu la faculté de (faire) pareille chose, ce serait, selon eux, comme si on lui attribuait la faculté de réunir au même instant[[fn:1593]] les deux contraires, ou de créer son semblable, ou de se corporifier, ou de créer un carré dont la diagonale soit égale au côté, ou de semblables choses impossibles. Ce qui est sousentendu dans leurs paroles, c’est qu’ils veulent dire que, de même qu’il ne peut être taxé d’impuissance pour ne pas produire les choses impossibles, — car l’impossible a une nature stable, qui n’est pas l’œuvre d’un agent, et qui, à cause de cela, est invariable[[fn:1594]], — de même on ne saurait lui attribuer l’impuissance, parce qu’il ne serait pas capable de produire quelque chose du néant (absolu); car cela est de la catégorie de toutes les choses impossibles. Ils croient donc qu’il existe une matière qui est éternelle comme Dieu; que lui, il n’existe pas sans elle, ni elle sans lui. Cependant ils ne croient pas pour cela qu’elle occupe dans l’être le même rang que Dieu; mais, au contraire, Dieu est (selon eux) la [[t:2]]cause[[t:3]] par laquelle elle existe, et elle est pour lui ce que l’argile est pour le potier, ou ce que le fer est pour le forgeron. Il crée dans elle ce qu’il veut: tantôt il en forme ciel et terre, tantôt il en forme autre chose. 

**Hebrew (Makbili):** <span class="mediumGrey">דעת אפלטון: בריאה מחומר קדמון</span><br><sup>7</sup> הדעה השנייה היא דעתם של כל הפילוסופים ששמענו את שמעם וראינו את דבריהם. והיא, שהם אומרים שבלתי אפשרי שהאל יביא למציאות דבר מן האין, וכן לא ייתכן לדעתם שדבר יכלה אל האין. כלומר לא ייתכן שיתהווה מצוי כלשהו בעל חומר וצורה מתוך העדר מוחלט של החומר הזה, ולא יכלה אל העדר מוחלט של החומר הזה. לדעתם לתאר את האל כבעל יכולת לגבי זה הוא כמו לתאר אותו כבעל יכולת לקבץ שני הפכים בזמן אחד, או לברוא (אלוה) כמותו יתעלה, או להפוך לגוף, או לברוא מרובע שאלכסונו שווה לצלעו, ונמנעות כיוצא באלה.<br><span class="mediumGrey">ניתוח קטגוריית הנמנעוּת</span><br><sup>8</sup> מה שמובן מדבריהם הוא, שלשיטתם כמו שאין לייחס לו אין־אונים משום שאין הוא מביא למציאות את הנמנעות – כי לנמנע יש טבע יציב שאינו מפעולת פועֵל ולכן לא ייתכן לשנותו – כך אין לייחס לו אין־אונים כשאין הוא יכול להביא למציאות דבר מן האין, כי זה מסוג כל הנמנעות.<br><sup>9</sup> לכן הם מאמינים שיש חומר מסוים מצוי קדום כקדמות האלוה, שאין האלוה נמצא בלעדיו ולא הוא נמצא מבלעדי האלוה. אין הם מאמינים שהחומר הוא בדרגתו של האלוה יתעלה במציאות, אלא האלוה הוא סיבת מציאותו, והיחס של החומר אל האלוה הוא כמו יחס החומר ליוצר או הברזל לנפח, למשל, והוא אשר בורא בו מה שהוא רוצה: לעתים הוא יוצר ממנו שמים וארץ, ולעתים הוא יוצר ממנו דברים אחרים. המחזיקים בדעה זו מאמינים שהשמים גם הם מתהווים וכלים, אבל אין הם מתהווים מן האין ולא כלים אל האין. אלא כמו שכל פרט מבעלי החיים מתהווה וכלה מחומר מצוי ואל חומר מצוי, כך השמים מתהווים וכלים, והתהוותם וכיליונם הם כשאר המצויים תחתיהם.

---

### root.text.Part 2..12.7 (Main Text)
**English:** The partisans of this opinion believe that the heaven also was born and is perishable, but that it was not born of nothing, nor is it to perish (in such wise as to return) to nothing. On the contrary, even as the individuals among animals are born and perish (issuing) from a matter which existeth and (returning) to a matter which existeth, likewise the heaven was born and must perish, and it is with its birth and its corruption even as with those of the other beings which are beneath it. Those who belong to this sect are divided into several classes, the divisions and opinions of which it is useless to mention in this treatise; but[[fn:1595]] the universal principle of this sect is that which I have told thee. Plato also professeth this opinion; thou shalt find that Aristotle relateth of him in the <i>Acroasis</i> (or the Physics) that he believed—that is to say, Plato—that the heaven was born and is perishable[[fn:1596]], and likewise thou shalt find his opinion clearly expressed in his book to Timaeus[[fn:1597]]. Nevertheless, he believeth not that which we believe, as is thought by those who scrutinize not opinions and study not with care, and who imagine that our opinion and his are similar. It is not so at all; for we believe that the heaven was born, not of something, but of absolute non-existence, whilst he believeth that it existed (virtually) and was formed of something[[fn:1598]]. Such is the second opinion.
**Correction:** The partisans of this opinion believe that the heaven also was born and is perishable, but that it was not born of nothing, nor is it to perish (in such wise as to return) to nothing. On the contrary, even as the individuals among animals are born and perish (issuing) from a matter which existeth and (returning) to a matter which existeth, likewise the heaven was born and must perish, and it is with its birth and its corruption even as with those of the other beings which are beneath it. Those who belong to this sect are divided into several classes, the divisions and opinions of which it is useless to mention in this treatise; but[[fn:1595]] the universal principle of this sect is that which I have told thee. Plato also professeth this opinion; thou shalt find that Aristotle relateth of him in the <i>Acroasis</i> (or the Physics) that he believed—that is to say, Plato—that the heaven was born and is perishable[[fn:1596]], and likewise thou shalt find his opinion clearly expressed in his book to Timaeus[[fn:1597]]. Nevertheless, he believeth not that which we believe, as is thought by those who scrutinize not opinions and study not with care, and who imagine that our opinion and his are similar. It is not so at all; for we believe that the heaven was born, not of something, but of absolute non-existence, whilst he believeth that it existed (virtually) and was formed of something[[fn:1598]]. Such is the second opinion.

**French (Source):** Les partisans de cette opinion croient que le ciel aussi est né et (qu’il est) périssable, mais qu’il n’est pas né du néant, ni ne doit périr (de manière à retourner) au néant. Au contraire, de même que les individus des animaux naissent et périssent (en sortant) d’une matière qui existe et (en retournant) à une matière qui existe, de même le ciel est né et doit périr, et il en est de sa naissance et de sa corruption comme de celles des autres êtres qui sont au-dessous de lui. Ceux qui appartiennent à cette secte se divisent en plusieurs classes, dont il est inutile de mentionner dans ce traité les divisions et les opinions; mais[[fn:1595]] le principe universel de cette secte est celui que je t’ai dit. Platon aussi professe cette opinion; tu trouveras qu’Aristote rapporte de lui dans l’[[t:0]]Acroasis[[t:1]] (ou la Physique) qu’il croyait, — c’est-à-dire Platon, — que le ciel est né et (qu’il est) périssable[[fn:1596]], et de même tu trouveras son opinion clairement exprimée dans son livre à Timée[[fn:1597]]. Cependant il ne croit pas ce que nous croyons, comme le pensent ceux qui n’examinent pas les opinions et n’étudient pas avec soin, et qui s’imaginent que notre opinion et la sienne sont semblables. Il n’en est point ainsi; car nous, nous croyons que le ciel est né, non pas de quelque chose, mais du néant absolu, tandis que lui, il croit qu’il existait (virtuellement) et qu’il a été formé de quelque chose[[fn:1598]]. Telle est la deuxième opinion.

**Hebrew (Makbili):** <sup>10</sup> אנשי קבוצה זו מתחלקים לקבוצות, שאותן ואת דעותיהן אין תועלת להזכיר בחיבור זה, אבל העיקר המשותף של הקבוצה הזאת הוא מה שציינתי לך. כך מאמין גם אפלטון: תמצא שאריסטו מספר עליו ב"פיזיקה" (ח,1) שהוא, כלומר אפלטון, מאמין שהשמים מתהווים וכלים. וכך תמצא את שיטתו מפורשת בספרו לטימאוס (28; 38). אך אין הוא מאמין במה שאנחנו מאמינים, כמו שחושב מי שאינו בוחן את הדעות ואינו מדקדק בעיון ומדמיין שדעתנו ודעתו שוות. אין הדבר כן, כי אנחנו מאמינים שהשמים התהוו לא מדָבר אלא אחַר העדר מוחלט, והוא מאמין שהם הובאו למציאות והתהוו מדָבר. זוהי אם כן הדעה השנייה.

---

### root.text.Part 2..12.10 (Main Text)
**English:** Such is the summary of these opinions and their true sense; and these are the opinions of those for whom it is a thing demonstrated that there existeth a God for this universe. As for those who have not acknowledged the existence of God, but who have thought that things are born and perish[[fn:1601]] by <i>aggregation</i> and <i>separation</i>[[fn:1602]], according to chance, and that there is no being who governeth and ordereth the universe—and these are Epicurus, his sect, and those like unto him, as Alexander relateth—it is of no utility for us to speak of these sects; for the existence of God hath been demonstrated, and it would be useless to mention the opinions of people who have built their system upon a foundation which hath already been overthrown by demonstration[[fn:1603]]. It would be equally useless for us to make efforts to establish the truth of what is said by the partisans of the second opinion, namely, that the heaven is <i>born</i> and that it is perishable; for those men admit the eternity (of matter), and there is no difference, in our view, between those who believe that the heaven was necessarily born of something and that it shall return thither in perishing, and the opinion of Aristotle, who believeth that it was not <i>born</i> and that it shall not perish. In truth, all who follow the Law of Moses and of our father Abraham, or who walk in their footsteps, tend to naught else but this belief: that there existeth absolutely no eternal thing beside God, and that to produce being from (absolute) non-existence is not for God of the category of the impossible; nay, in the opinion of certain thinkers, it is even a necessary thing[[fn:1604]].
**Correction:** Such is the summary of these opinions and their true sense; and these are the opinions of those for whom it is a thing demonstrated that there existeth a God for this universe. As for those who have not acknowledged the existence of God, but who have thought that things are born and perish[[fn:1601]] by <i>aggregation</i> and <i>separation</i>[[fn:1602]], according to chance, and that there is no being who governeth and ordereth the universe—and these are Epicurus, his sect, and those like unto him, as Alexander relateth—it is of no utility for us to speak of these sects; for the existence of God hath been demonstrated, and it would be useless to mention the opinions of people who have built their system upon a foundation which hath already been overthrown by demonstration[[fn:1603]]. It would be equally useless for us to make efforts to establish the truth of what is said by the partisans of the second opinion, namely, that the heaven is <i>born</i> and that it is perishable; for those men admit the eternity (of matter), and there is no difference, in our view, between those who believe that the heaven was necessarily born of something and that it shall return thither in perishing, and the opinion of Aristotle, who believeth that it was not <i>born</i> and that it shall not perish. In truth, all who follow the Law of Moses and of our father Abraham, or who walk in their footsteps, tend to naught else but this belief: that there existeth absolutely no eternal thing beside God, and that to produce being from (absolute) non-existence is not for God of the category of the impossible; nay, in the opinion of certain thinkers, it is even a necessary thing[[fn:1604]].

**French (Source):** Tel est le résumé de ces opinions et leur véritable sens; et ce sont les opinions de ceux pour lesquels c’est une chose démontrée qu’il existe un Dieu pour cet univers. Quant à ceux qui n’ont pas reconnu l’existence de Dieu, mais qui ont pensé que les choses naissent et périssent[[fn:1601]] par l’[[t:0]]agrégation[[t:1]] et la [[t:2]]séparation[[t:3]][[fn:1602]], selon le hasard, et qu’il n’y a pas d’être qui gouverne et ordonne l’univers, — et ce sont Épicure, sa secte et ses semblables, comme le rapporte Alexandre, — il n’est d’aucune utilité pour nous de parler de ces sectes; car l’existence de Dieu a été démontrée, et il serait inutile de mentionner les opinions de gens qui ont construit leur système sur une base qui déjà a été renversée par la démonstration[[fn:1603]]. Il serait également inutile pour nous de faire des efforts pour établir la vérité de ce que disent les partisans de la deuxième opinion, à savoir que le ciel est [[t:4]]né[[t:5]] et qu’il est périssable; car ceux-là admettent l’éternité (de la matière), et il n’y a pas de différence, selon nous, entre ceux qui croient que le ciel est nécessairement né de quelque chose et qu’il y retournera en périssant, et l’opinion d’Aristote, qui croit qu’il n’est pas [[t:6]]né[[t:7]] et qu’il ne périra pas. En effet, tous ceux qui suivent la Loi de Moïse et de notre père Abraham, ou qui marchent sur leurs traces, ne tendent à autre chose qu’à cette croyance: qu’il n’existe absolument aucune chose éternelle à côté de Dieu, et que produire l’être du néant (absolu) n’est point pour Dieu de la catégorie de l’impossible; bien plus, dans l’opinion de certains penseurs, c’est même une chose nécessaire[[fn:1604]].

**Hebrew (Makbili):** <span class="mediumGrey">סיכום הצגת הדעות והבחנה ביניהן</span><br><sup>12</sup> זוהי אם כן תמצית הדעות הללו ואמיתתן, והן דעותיו של מי שהוכח לו קיום האלוה לעולם הזה. אך מי שלא ידע את קיומו של האלוה יתהדר ויתרומם, אלא חשב שהדברים מתהווים וכלים בהתקבצות והתפרדות באקראי, ושאין מנהיג ולא מסדר מציאות, והוא אֶפִּיקוּרוֹס וסיעתו ודומיו, כדברי אלכסנדר (מאפרודיסיאס) – אין לנו תועלת להזכיר את הקבוצות האלה, כי קיומו של האלוה כבר הוכח, ואין תועלת בהזכרת דעותיהם של אלה שבנו את דבריהם על יסוד שהופרך.<br><sup>13</sup> כמו כן אין לנו תועלת לנסות לאמת את דברי בעלי הדעה השנייה, דהיינו שהשמים מתהווים וכלים. כי הם מאמינים בקדמות, ואין הבדל לדעתנו בין מי שמאמין שהשמים מתהווים בהכרח מדָבָר וכלים אל דבר לבין מה שמאמין אריסטו, שאין הם מתהווים ולא כלים. כי מטרת כל ההולך אחר תורת משה ואברהם אבינו או כיוצא בהם היא רק ההאמנה שאין בשום אופן דבר קדום עם האל, ושלגבי האל הבאה למציאות של המצוי מהעדר אינה מכלל הנמנע, ואף הכרחית לטענת חלק מאנשי העיון.

---

### root.text.Part 2..13.12 (Main Text)
**English:** They say further, as if to reduce the matter to an absurdity: How could it be that God had been idle, doing absolutely naught and producing naught throughout all past eternity[[fn:1625]], and that after having done naught during the whole duration of His eternal existence which is without end, He should since yesterday have commenced (to create) the universe? For, even shouldst thou say, for example, that before this world God created many others, as numerous as the grains of mustard-seed which the globe of the utmost sphere might contain, and that each of these worlds hath existed for as many years as that same content of grains of mustard-seed, all this would still be, in comparison with the infinite existence of God, as though thou saidst that it was but yesterday that God created the world. Verily, so soon as we affirm that the universe <i>commenced</i> after absolute non-existence, it mattereth little whether thou admittest that this took place hundreds of thousands of years ago[[fn:1626]] or from a time most nigh; for they who admit the eternity (of the world) find this equally absurd.
**Correction:** They say further, as if to reduce the matter to an absurdity: How could it be that God had been idle, doing absolutely naught and producing naught throughout all past eternity[[fn:1625]], and that after having done naught during the whole duration of His eternal existence which is without end, He should since yesterday have commenced (to create) the universe? For, even shouldst thou say, for example, that before this world God created many others, as numerous as the grains of mustard-seed which the globe of the utmost sphere might contain, and that each of these worlds hath existed for as many years as that same content of grains of mustard-seed, all this would still be, in comparison with the infinite existence of God, as though thou saidst that it was but yesterday that God created the world. Verily, so soon as we affirm that the universe <i>commenced</i> after absolute non-existence, it mattereth little whether thou admittest that this took place hundreds of thousands of years ago[[fn:1626]] or from a time most nigh; for they who admit the eternity (of the world) find this equally absurd.

**French (Source):** Ils disent encore, comme pour réduire à l’absurde: Comment se pourrait-il que Dieu eût été oisif, ne faisant absolument rien et ne produisant rien dans toute l’éternité passée[[fn:1625]], et qu’après n’avoir rien fait pendant toute la durée de son existence éternelle qui est sans fin, il eût depuis hier commencé (à créer) l’univers? Car, lors même que tu dirais, par exemple, qu’avant ce monde Dieu en a créé beaucoup d’autres, aussi nombreux que les grains de sénevé que pourrait contenir le globe de la sphère dernière, et que chacun de ces mondes a existé pendant des années aussi nombreuses que ce même contenu de grains de sénevé, tout cela serait encore, par rapport à l’existence infinie de Dieu, comme si tu disais que c’est d’hier que Dieu a créé le monde. En effet, dès que nous affirmons que l’univers a [[t:0]]commencé[[t:1]] après le néant absolu, il importe peu que tu admettes que cela a eu lieu depuis des centaines de mille ans[[fn:1626]] ou depuis un temps très rapproché; car ceux qui admettent l’éternité (du monde) trouvent cela également absurde.

**Hebrew (Makbili):** <span class="mediumGrey">גינוי: האל ישב בטל?!</span><br><sup>11</sup> הם משתמשים גם בדרך הגינוי ואומרים: "כיצד יכול היה האלוה יתהדר ויתרומם להיות בטל ולא לעשות שום דבר, ולא לחדש חידוש מאז ומקדם עד אין קץ, ובכל משך קיומו הקדום עד אינסוף לא עשה דבר, וכשאך הגיע יום אתמול התחיל להביא דברים למציאות? כי אפילו תאמר למשל שהאל ברא עולמות רבים לפני כן, כמספר גרגרי חרדל היכולים למלא את הגלגל הקיצון, ושכל עולם מהם התקיים מספר שנים כמלוא הגלגל בחרדל – כל זה היה ביחס למציאותו יתעלה שאין לה סוף כאילו אמרת שהאל ברא את העולם אתמול. כי משעה שקבענו שהמציאות התחילה אחרי העדר, אין הבדל בין אם תאמר שהדבר היה מלפני מאתיים אלף שנה או מלפני זמן קרוב מאוד". זהו גם כן גינוי שמשתמש בו מאמין הקדמות.

---

### root.text.Part 2..14.4 (Main Text)
**English:** And now, O all ye who are thinkers! is there yet, after this preliminary declaration, aught for which to blame this man? Shall it still be believed, after such words, that he possessed a demonstration upon this question? Can any man whatsoever, and much more so Aristotle, imagine that a thing which hath been <i>demonstrated</i> could be feebly received if one hath not heard the arguments of those who gainsay it? — Furthermore, Aristotle declaring that this is an opinion[[fn:1637]] of his own and that his proofs thereupon are but dialectical argumentations, — is it an Aristotle who could be ignorant of the difference between argumentations and demonstrations, between opinions which appear to the mind strong or weak and demonstrative things? Finally, this oratorical expression of <i>impartiality toward the adversary</i>, which he addeth as if to strengthen his opinion! is there need of all this in a demonstration? Certes, no; but all that he hath in view is to show that his opinion is truer than that of his adversaries, or of those who claim that philosophical speculation leadeth to the admission that the heavens are subject to birth and corruption, but that nevertheless they were never absolutely non-existent, — or that they were <i>formed</i> from something, — and that they shall not absolutely perish[[fn:1638]]; and other similar things which he reporteth of these opinions. And this is indubitably true; for indeed his opinion is nearer to the truth than theirs, when one seeketh to argue from the nature of being. But we do not think thus[[fn:1639]], as I shall set forth. Nevertheless, all the sects, and even the philosophers, have suffered themselves to be led away by passions, so that they have wished to establish that Aristotle hath demonstrated this question. Perchance, according to their opinion, Aristotle made a demonstration upon this question without perceiving it himself[[fn:1640]], so that it was only after him that the remark was made! — As for me, it seemeth to me beyond doubt that all the opinions which Aristotle expresseth upon these subjects, — I mean the eternity of the world, the cause of the varied motions of the spheres, and the order of the Intelligences, — that all this, I say, is not susceptible of a demonstration. Thus Aristotle never had the thought that these reasonings could be considered a demonstration; on the contrary, as he himself saith, we have no means of approaching these things by demonstrative methods[[fn:1641]], and they possess for us no principle from which we might argue[[fn:1642]].
**Correction:** And now, O all ye who are thinkers! is there yet, after this preliminary declaration, aught for which to blame this man? Shall it still be believed, after such words, that he possessed a demonstration upon this question? Can any man whatsoever, and much more so Aristotle, imagine that a thing which hath been <i>demonstrated</i> could be feebly received if one hath not heard the arguments of those who gainsay it? — Furthermore, Aristotle declaring that this is an opinion[[fn:1637]] of his own and that his proofs thereupon are but dialectical argumentations, — is it an Aristotle who could be ignorant of the difference between argumentations and demonstrations, between opinions which appear to the mind strong or weak and demonstrative things? Finally, this oratorical expression of <i>impartiality toward the adversary</i>, which he addeth as if to strengthen his opinion! is there need of all this in a demonstration? Certes, no; but all that he hath in view is to show that his opinion is truer than that of his adversaries, or of those who claim that philosophical speculation leadeth to the admission that the heavens are subject to birth and corruption, but that nevertheless they were never absolutely non-existent, — or that they were <i>formed</i> from something, — and that they shall not absolutely perish[[fn:1638]]; and other similar things which he reporteth of these opinions. And this is indubitably true; for indeed his opinion is nearer to the truth than theirs, when one seeketh to argue from the nature of being. But we do not think thus[[fn:1639]], as I shall set forth. Nevertheless, all the sects, and even the philosophers, have suffered themselves to be led away by passions, so that they have wished to establish that Aristotle hath demonstrated this question. Perchance, according to their opinion, Aristotle made a demonstration upon this question without perceiving it himself[[fn:1640]], so that it was only after him that the remark was made! — As for me, it seemeth to me beyond doubt that all the opinions which Aristotle expresseth upon these subjects, — I mean the eternity of the world, the cause of the varied motions of the spheres, and the order of the Intelligences, — that all this, I say, is not susceptible of a demonstration. Thus Aristotle never had the thought that these reasonings could be considered a demonstration; on the contrary, as he himself saith, we have no means of approaching these things by demonstrative methods[[fn:1641]], and they possess for us no principle from which we might argue[[fn:1642]].

**French (Source):** Et maintenant, ô vous tous qui êtes penseurs! y a t-il encore, après cette déclaclaration préliminaire, de quoi blâmer cet homme? croira-t-on encore, après de telles paroles, qu’il ait eu une démonstration sur cette question? Un homme quelconque, et à plus forte raison Aristote, peut-il s’imaginer qu’une chose qui a été [[t:0]]démontrée[[t:1]] puisse être faiblement accueillie si on n’a pas entendu les argumentations de ceux qui la contredisent? — Ensuite, Aristote déclarant que c’est là une opinion[[fn:1637]] à lui et que ses preuves làdessus ne sont que des argumentations (dialectiques), — est-ce un Aristote qui pourrait ignorer la différence entre les argumentations et les démonstrations, entre les opinions qui paraissent à la pensée fortes ou faibles et les choses démonstratives? Enfin, cette expression oratoire [[t:2]]d’impartialité envers l’adversaire[[t:3]], qu’il ajoute comme pour fortifier son opinion! a-t-on besoin de tout cela dans la démonstration? Non, certes; mais tout ce qu’il a pour but, c’est de montrer que son opinion est plus vraie que celle de ses adversaires, ou de ceux qui prétendent que la spéculation philosophique conduit à (admettre) que le ciel est sujet à la naissance et à la corruption, mais que cependant il n’a jamais été (absolument) non existant, — ou qu’il a été [[t:4]]formé[[t:5]] (de quelque chose), — et qu’il ne périra pas (absolument)[[fn:1638]]; et autres choses semblables qu’il rapporte de ces opinions. Et cela est indubitablement vrai; car (en effet) son opinion est plus près de la vérité que la leur, quand on cherche à argumenter de la nature de l’être. Mais nous ne pensons pas ainsi[[fn:1639]], comme je l’exposerai. Cependant toutes les sectes, et même les philosophes, se sont laissés entraîner par les passions, de sorte qu’ils ont voulu établir qu’Aristote a démontré cette question. Peut-être, selon leur opinion, Aristote a-t-il fait une démonstration sur cette question, sans s’en apercevoir lui-même[[fn:1640]], de sorte que ce ne serait qu’après lui qu’on en aurait fait la remarque! — Quant à moi, il me semble hors de doute que toutes les opinions qu’Aristote exprime sur ces sujets, — je veux parler de l’éternité du monde, de la cause des mouvements variés des sphères et de l’ordre des Intelligences, — que tout cela, dis-je, n’est pas susceptible d’une démonstration. Aussi Aristote n’a-t-il jamais eu la pensée que ces raisonnements pussent être (considérés comme) une démonstration; au contraire, comme il le dit lui-même, nous n’avons aucun moyen d’aborder ces choses par des méthodes démonstratives[[fn:1641]], et elles n’ont pour nous aucun principe dont nous puissions argumenter[[fn:1642]].

**Hebrew (Makbili):** <span class="mediumGrey">סיכומו של הרמב"ם ביחס לתוקף הראיות</span><br><sup>5</sup> קהל אנשי העיון! <br><sup>א)</sup> האם נותר מקום לגנות את האיש הזה אחרי ההקדמה הזאת? <br><sup>ב)</sup> האם אפשר לחשוב אחרי האמירה הזאת שעלתה בידו הוכחה בשאלה זו? <br><sup>ג)</sup> האם מישהו, לא כל שכן אריסטו, מדמיין שדבר שהוכח יהיה פחות נוח להתקבל אם לא יישמעו טיעוני החולקים עליו? <br><sup>ד)</sup> ועוד, שהוא הגדיר זאת כ"דעה" שלו, ואת ראיותיו עליה כ"טיעונים"; האם אריסטו אינו יודע את ההבדל בין טיעונים לבין הוכחות, ובין דעות שהנטייה לקבלן מתחזקת או נחלשת לבין דברים מוכחים? <br><sup>ה)</sup> ועוד: כל הדברים הרטוריים שהקדים באשר להגינות כלפי בר הפלוגתא כדי לחזק את דעתו הוא – האם יש צורך לכל זה בהוכחה? לא, אלא כל מטרתו היא להבהיר שדעתו נכונה יותר מדעות החולקים עליו, הטוענים שהעיון הפילוסופי מוביל לכך שהשמים הם בני התהוות וכיליון אלא שהם לא נעדרו מעולם, או שהם התהוו אך לא יכלו, ודעות אחרות כאלה שהוא מזכיר. וזה נכון בלי ספק, כי דעתו קרובה יותר להיות נכונה מדעותיהם לפי הראיות מטבע המציאות. אך אנחנו איננו טוענים כך, כמו שאבאר (ב,יט4). <br><span class="mediumGrey">ביקורת כלפי הסוברים שיש לאריסטו הוכחה</span><br><sup>6</sup> אבל על כל הכתות התגברו היצרים, ואפילו על הפילוסופים, והם רוצים לקבוע שאריסטו הוכיח את הנושא הזה. ושמא לדעתם אריסטו הוכיח את הנושא הזה בלי שחש שהוכיח, עד שהבאים אחריו התעוררו לכך.<br><sup>7</sup> ואילו לדידי אין ספק שהדעות האלה שאריסטו הזכיר בעניינים האלה – כוונתי לקדמות העולם, וסיבת השוני בתנועות הגלגלים וסדרם של השכלים – בכל זה אין הוכחה. ואריסטו לא דמיין מעולם שהאמירות האלה הן הוכחה, אלא כמו שציין, ששעריהן של דרכי הבאת הראיות על הדברים האלה נעולים בפנינו, ואין לנו עיקרון בשבילם, שבעזרתו נביא ראיה.

---

### root.text.Part 2..14.6 (Main Text)
**English:** In sum, it is not by any of the manners which we have related in this chapter that an opinion may be confirmed, or destroyed, or called into doubt.[[fn:1646]]
**Correction:** In sum, it is not by any of the manners which we have related in this chapter that an opinion may be confirmed, or destroyed, or called into doubt.[[fn:1646]]

**French (Source):** En somme, ce n’est pas de l’une des manières que nous avons rapportées dans ce chapitre qu’une opinion peut être confirmée, ou détruite, ou mise en doute[[fn:1646]]. 

**Hebrew (Makbili):** <span class="mediumGrey">סיכום: אין במדע הוכחה לקדמות העולם</span><br><sup>8</sup> כללו של דבר: אף לא אחת מן הדרכים שהזכרנו בפרק הזה אינה מאמתת דעה או מבטלת אותה או מטילה בה ספק.

---

### root.text.Part 2..16.2 (Main Text)
**English:** Examine well this example and reflect thereon, O thou who seekest to think! and thou shalt find that such is likewise our condition in respect of Aristotle. Forsooth, all we, the followers of Moses our Master and of Abraham our Father, believe that the world was formed in such and such a manner, that it developed in such a manner[[fn:1662]], and that one thing was created after another; but Aristotle setteth himself to contradict us, arguing against us from the nature of being as it hath attained its final state, perfect and existing in act, whilst we, on our part, affirm unto him that after having attained its final state and become perfect, it resembleth in no wise that which it was at the moment of its birth, and that it was produced out of absolute non-existence. What argument, therefore, can be drawn against us from all that he saith? for these arguments strike only him who pretendeth that it is the nature of this being, having reached its final state, which proveth of itself that it was <i>created,</i> whereas I have already made known unto thee that, for my part, I do not maintain this.
**Correction:** Examine well this example and reflect thereon, O thou who seekest to think! and thou shalt find that such is likewise our condition in respect of Aristotle. Forsooth, all we, the followers of Moses our Master and of Abraham our Father, believe that the world was formed in such and such a manner, that it developed in such a manner[[fn:1662]], and that one thing was created after another; but Aristotle setteth himself to contradict us, arguing against us from the nature of being as it hath attained its final state, perfect and existing in act, whilst we, on our part, affirm unto him that after having attained its final state and become perfect, it resembleth in no wise that which it was at the moment of its birth, and that it was produced out of absolute non-existence. What argument, therefore, can be drawn against us from all that he saith? for these arguments strike only him who pretendeth that it is the nature of this being, having reached its final state, which proveth of itself that it was <i>created,</i> whereas I have already made known unto thee that, for my part, I do not maintain this.

**French (Source):** Examine bien cet exemple et réfléchis-y, ô penseur! et tu trouveras que c’est là également la condition dans laquelle nous sommes vis-à-vis d’Aristote. En effet, nous tous, les sectateurs de Moïse, notre maître, et d’Abraham, notre père, nous croyons que le monde a été formé de telle et telle manière, qu’il s’est développé de telle manière[[fn:1662]], et que telle chose a été créée après telle autre; mais Aristote se prend à nous contredire, en argumentant contre nous de la nature de l’être arrivé à son état définitif, parfait et existant en acte, tandis que nous, nous lui affirmons qu’après être arrivé à son état définitif et être devenu parfait, il ne ressemble à rien de ce qu’il était au moment de naître, et qu’il a été produit du néant absolu. Quel argument donc peut-on tirer contre nous de tout ce qu’il dit? car ces arguments ne frappent que celui qui prétend que c’est la nature de cet être, arrivée à son état définitif, qui prouve (elle-même) qu’il a été [[t:0]]créé,[[t:1]] tandis que je t’ai déjà fait savoir que, quant à moi, je ne soutiens pas cela.

**Hebrew (Makbili):** <span class="mediumGrey">הנמשל: אריסטו כנער היתום</span><br><sup>5</sup> התבונן במשל הזה ובחן אותו, אתה המעיין, ותמצא שכך הוא בדיוק מצבנו ביחס לאריסטו. כי אנחנו, קהל ההולכים אחר משה רבינו ואברהם אבינו עליהם השלום, מאמינים שהעולם התהווה בצורה כזו וכזו, והתהווה דבר זה מדבר זה, ונברא דבר זה אחר דבר זה. ואריסטו מתחיל לסתור את דברינו ולהביא ראיות נגדנו מטבע המציאות היציב השלם שהגיע לפועל, שאנו מודים לו שלאחר שהתייצב והושלם אינו דומה כלל למצבו בעת ההתהוות, ושהוא נברא אחר העדר מוחלט. איזה טיעון תקף נגדנו מכל מה שהוא אמר? הטיעונים האלה מחייבים רק את מי שטוען שטבע המציאות היציב הנוכחי מורה על כך שהיא מחודשת, וכבר הודעתיך (ב,טז4) שאיני טוען זאת.

---

### root.text.Part 2..16.4 (Main Text)
**English:** The First Matter, saith he, hath neither been born nor shall it perish; and, arguing from things born and perishable, he showeth that it is impossible it should have been born[[fn:1664]]. And this is true[[fn:1665]]. For we do not maintain that the first matter was formed as man is formed from the semen, nor that it must perish as man perisheth by becoming dust; but we maintain, on the contrary, that God produced it out of nothing, and that after its production it is such as it is[[fn:1666]],—I mean to say, that all things are formed therefrom, and that all which is formed therefrom returneth thereto in perishing. It existeth not divested of form[[fn:1667]], and it is the term of generation and of corruption. As for itself, it was not born (<i>of</i> anything), as everything is born which is formed from it, and it shall not perish (<i>into</i> anything), as that perisheth which perisheth within it; but on the contrary, it is a thing <i>created,</i> and whensoever its Creator shall will it, He shall reduce it to pure and absolute non-existence.
**Correction:** The First Matter, saith he, hath neither been born nor shall it perish; and, arguing from things born and perishable, he showeth that it is impossible it should have been born[[fn:1664]]. And this is true[[fn:1665]]. For we do not maintain that the first matter was formed as man is formed from the semen, nor that it must perish as man perisheth by becoming dust; but we maintain, on the contrary, that God produced it out of nothing, and that after its production it is such as it is[[fn:1666]],—I mean to say, that all things are formed therefrom, and that all which is formed therefrom returneth thereto in perishing. It existeth not divested of form[[fn:1667]], and it is the term of generation and of corruption. As for itself, it was not born (<i>of</i> anything), as everything is born which is formed from it, and it shall not perish (<i>into</i> anything), as that perisheth which perisheth within it; but on the contrary, it is a thing <i>created,</i> and whensoever its Creator shall will it, He shall reduce it to pure and absolute non-existence.

**French (Source):** La matière première, dit-il, n’est pas née ni ne périra; et, argumentant des choses nées et périssables, il montre qu’il est impossible qu’elle soit née[[fn:1664]]. Et cela est vrai[[fn:1665]]. Car nous ne soutenons pas que la matière première se soit formée, comme l’homme se forme du sperme, ni qu’elle doive périr, comme périt l’homme en devenant poussière; mais nous soutenons au contraire que Dieu l’a produite du néant, et qu’après sa production elle est telle qu’elle est[[fn:1666]], je veux dire que toute chose se forme d’elle et que tout ce qui s’est formé d’elle retourne à elle en périssant. Elle n’existe point dénuée de forme[[fn:1667]], et elle est le terme de la naissance et de la corruption. Quant à elle, elle n’est pas née ([[t:0]]de[[t:1]] quelque chose), comme naît tout ce qui se forme d’elle, et elle ne périra pas ([[t:2]]en[[t:3]] quelque chose), comme périt ce qui périt en elle; mais au contraire, elle est une chose [[t:4]]créée,[[t:5]] et quand son créateur le voudra, il la réduira au néant pur et absolu.

**Hebrew (Makbili):** הוא אמר שהחומר הראשון אינו מתהווה ולא כלה. והחל להביא לכך ראיות מן הדברים המתהווים והכלים, ולהבהיר שבלתי אפשרי שהוא התהווה. וזה נכון, כי אנחנו איננו טוענים שהחומר הראשון נוצר כמו היווצרות האדם מן הזרע, או כלה ככיליון האדם אל העפר. אלא אנו טוענים שהאל הביא אותו למציאות מן האין כשהוא במצבו לאחר שהובא למציאות, כוונתי לכך שממנו נוצר כל דבר וכל דבר שנוצר ממנו כלה אליו, ואין הוא קיים מופשט מצורה, וההתהוות והכיליון מגיעים בסופו של דבר אליו. התהוותו אינה כהיווצרות מה שנוצר ממנו, וכיליונו אינו ככיליון של מה שכלה אליו, אלא הוא מחודש, ואם המחדש שלו יחפוץ, הוא יגרום לו להעדר מוחלט וגמור.<br><span class="mediumGrey">דחיית הראיה הראשונה של אריסטו: נצחיות התנועה</span><br><sup>7</sup> וכך בדיוק אנו אומרים לגבי התנועה. כי הוא הביא ראיה מטבע התנועה, שאינה מתהווה ולא כלה; והדבר נכון, כי אנו טוענים שלאחר הימצאות התנועה בטבעה היציב הזה, אין לדמיין שהתהווּתה הכוללת וכיליונה הכולל הם כהתהוות התנועות החלקיות המתהוות וככיליון התנועות החלקיות, והוא הדין לכל מה שמחייב טבע התנועה.

---

### root.text.Part 2..18.16 (Main Text)
**English:** In sum, all that is known is that all this occurreth for a reason which we know not, yet it is not a useless work, nor due to chance. Verily, thou knowest that the veins and nerves of an individual dog or ass are not the work of chance, nor have they fortuitously such a measure; neither is it by mere chance that one vein is thick and another slender, or that one nerve brancheth out into many ramifications whilst another doth not, or that one descendeth straightly whilst another is wound upon itself; for naught of all this occurreth save for certain advantages the necessity whereof is known. How then could an intelligent man imagine that the positions of these stars, their measures, their number, and the movements of their diverse spheres should be without reason, or the work of chance? There is no doubt that each of these things is necessary in relation to the design of Him who acted with intention, and it is most difficult to conceive that this order of things proceedeth from a (blind) necessity, and not from a <i>design</i>.
**Correction:** In sum, all that is known is that all this occurreth for a reason which we know not, yet it is not a useless work, nor due to chance. Verily, thou knowest that the veins and nerves of an individual dog or ass are not the work of chance, nor have they fortuitously such a measure; neither is it by mere chance that one vein is thick and another slender, or that one nerve brancheth out into many ramifications whilst another doth not, or that one descendeth straightly whilst another is wound upon itself; for naught of all this occurreth save for certain advantages the necessity whereof is known. How then could an intelligent man imagine that the positions of these stars, their measures, their number, and the movements of their diverse spheres should be without reason, or the work of chance? There is no doubt that each of these things is necessary in relation to the design of Him who acted with intention, and it is most difficult to conceive that this order of things proceedeth from a (blind) necessity, and not from a <i>design</i>.

**French (Source):** Tout ce qu’on sait, en somme, c’est que tout cela a lieu pour une raison que nous ne connaissons pas, mais que ce n’est pas cependant une œuvre inutile, ni due au hasard. En effet, tu sais que les veines et les nerfs de l’individu chien ou âne ne sont pas l’œuvre du hasard, ni n’ont fortuitement telle mesure, et que ce n’est pas non plus par le simple hasard que telle veine est grosse et telle autre mince, que tel nerf se déploie en beaucoup de branches tandis que tel autre ne se déploie pas ainsi, que l’un descend tout droit tandis qu’un autre se replie sur lui-même; car rien de tout cela n’a lieu que pour certains avantages dont on connaît la nécessité. Et comment donc un homme intelligent pourrait-il s’imaginer que les positions de ces astres, leurs mesures, leur nombre et les mouvements de leurs sphères diverses soient sans raison, ou l’œuvre du hasard ? Il n’y a pas de doute que chacune de ces choses ne soit nécessaire par rapport au dessein de celui qui a agi avec intention, et il est très difficile de concevoir que cet ordre des choses vienne d’une (aveugle) nécessité, et non pas d’un [[t:0]]dessein[[t:1]].

**Hebrew (Makbili):**  אך אם מאמינים שכל זה בכוונת מכוון שעשה כך, שום פלא ושום אי־סבירות אינם נלווים לדעה הזאת.

---

### root.text.Part 2..20.0 (Main Text)
**English:** Know thou that, among the modern philosophers who profess the eternity of the world, there be some who say that God is the efficient cause of the world, whose existence He hath preferred (to non-existence), that He hath made it with design and hath determined it even as it is, but that it is inadmissible that this should have taken place in one time rather than in another, and that, on the contrary, it hath always been and shall always be thus. That which causeeth, say they, that we could not figure to ourselves that an agent hath made something without this agent having preceded his action in time, is that in what we ourselves do, it is necessarily thus; for, in every agent of this sort[[fn:1769]], there is a certain <i>privation:</i> he is (first) an agent in potentiality, and, after having acted, he hath passed into actuality. But God, in whom there is no privation, nor absolutely anything that is in potentiality, doth not precede His action; on the contrary, He hath never ceased to act, and even as there is an immense difference between His essence and ours, so also the relation which existeth between His action and Himself differeth from that which existeth between our action and ourselves. They follow the same reasoning concerning <i>determination</i> and <i>will;</i> for, it mattereth little whether thou sayest <i>agent</i>, or <i>willing</i>, or <i>acting with design</i>, or <i>preferring</i>, or <i>determining</i>, (all these words) having the same sense[[fn:1770]]. It is inadmissible, say they further, that His action or His will should be subject to change, as we have already set forth[[fn:1771]].
**Correction:** Know thou that, among the modern philosophers who profess the eternity of the world, there be some who say that God is the efficient cause of the world, whose existence He hath preferred (to non-existence), that He hath made it with design and hath determined it even as it is, but that it is inadmissible that this should have taken place in one time rather than in another, and that, on the contrary, it hath always been and shall always be thus. That which causeeth, say they, that we could not figure to ourselves that an agent hath made something without this agent having preceded his action in time, is that in what we ourselves do, it is necessarily thus; for, in every agent of this sort[[fn:1769]], there is a certain <i>privation:</i> he is (first) an agent in potentiality, and, after having acted, he hath passed into actuality. But God, in whom there is no privation, nor absolutely anything that is in potentiality, doth not precede His action; on the contrary, He hath never ceased to act, and even as there is an immense difference between His essence and ours, so also the relation which existeth between His action and Himself differeth from that which existeth between our action and ourselves. They follow the same reasoning concerning <i>determination</i> and <i>will;</i> for, it mattereth little whether thou sayest <i>agent</i>, or <i>willing</i>, or <i>acting with design</i>, or <i>preferring</i>, or <i>determining</i>, (all these words) having the same sense[[fn:1770]]. It is inadmissible, say they further, that His action or His will should be subject to change, as we have already set forth[[fn:1771]].

**French (Source):** Sache que, parmi les philosophes modernes qui professent l’éternité du monde, il y en a qui disent que Dieu est l’efficient du monde, dont il a préféré l’existence (à la non-existence), qu’il l’a fait avec dessein et l’a déterminé tel qu’il est, mais qu’il est inadmissible que cela ait eu lieu dans un temps plutôt que dans un autre, et qu’au contraire, cela a toujours été et sera toujours ainsi. Ce qui fait, disent-ils, que nous ne saurions nous figurer qu’un agent ait fait quelque chose sans que cet agent ait précédé son action dans le temps, c’est que dans ce que nous faisons, nous autres, il en est nécessairement ainsi; car, dans tout agent de celte sorte[[fn:1769]], il y a une certaine [[t:0]]privation:[[t:1]] il est (d’abord) agent en puissance, et, après avoir agi, il a passé à l’acte. Mais Dieu, dans lequel il n’y a point de privation, ni absolument rien qui soit en puissance, ne précède point son action; au contraire, il n’a jamais cessé d’agir, et de même qu’il y a une immense différence entre son essence et la nôtre, de même aussi le rapport qui existe entre son action et lui diffère de celui qui existe entre notre action et nous. Ils font le même raisonnement sur la [[t:2]]détermination[[t:3]] et la [[t:4]]volonté;[[t:5]] car, peu importe que tu dises [[t:6]]agent[[t:7]], ou [[t:8]]voulant[[t:9]], ou [[t:10]]agissant avec dessein[[t:11]], ou [[t:12]]préférant[[t:13]], ou [[t:14]]déterminant[[t:15]], (tous ces mots) ayant le même sens[[fn:1770]]. Il est inadmissible, disent-ils encore, que son action ou sa volonté soit sujette au changement, ainsi que nous l’avons exposé[[fn:1771]].
