### root.text.Part 3..14.0.sub_0 (Main Text)
**English:** The impossible hath a stable and constant nature which is not the work of an agent and which is variable under no condition; wherefore one cannot attribute to God any power in this regard[[fn:2787]]. This is what none of the thinkers[[fn:2788]] in any wise contest, and it is ignored only by those who understand not intelligible notions. If there be dissent among thinkers, it is only with respect to certain <i>imaginable</i> things which, according to some thinkers, are in the category of the impossible which God Himself hath not the power to change[[fn:2789]], and which, according to others, are in the domain of the possible, which it dependeth upon the Divine omnipotence to bring into existence at will. Thus, for example, the union of contraries at the same instant and in the same subject, the transformation of <i>essentials</i>[[fn:2790]], I mean the change of substance into accident and of accident into substance, or the existence of a corporeal substance without accident, all this is, for every thinker, of the category of the impossible. Likewise, it is impossible that God should call into existence His like, or that He should render Himself non-existent, or that He should become corporeal, or that He should change, and one cannot attribute to Him the power to do aught of all this. As for the question of knowing whether He can produce an accident alone which be not in a substance, a sect of thinkers, namely the Mutazilites, have imagined this and believed it to be in the domain of the possible[[fn:2791]], whilst others have judged it impossible. It is true that those who have professed the existence of an <i>accident without a substratum</i> were not led thereto by simple speculation, but by their regard for certain religious doctrines which speculation violently rejecteth, so that they had recourse to this hypothesis[[fn:2792]]. Likewise, to produce a corporeal thing without making use for that purpose of any pre-existing matter, is, according to us, in the category of the possible; but, according to the philosophers, it is impossible[[fn:2793]]. Likewise, the philosophers will say that it is in the domain of the impossible to produce a square whose diagonal be equal to the side, or a solid angle which be surrounded by four plane right angles[[fn:2794]], or other like things. But many a man who is ignorant of mathematics and who knoweth of these things only the simple words, without conceiving the idea thereof, will believe them possible.
**Correction:** The impossible hath a stable and constant nature which is not the work of an agent and which is variable under no condition; wherefore one cannot attribute to God any power in this regard[[fn:2787]]. This is what none of the thinkers[[fn:2788]] in any wise contest, and it is ignored only by those who understand not intelligible notions. If there be dissent among thinkers, it is only with respect to certain <i>imaginable</i> things which, according to some thinkers, are in the category of the impossible which God Himself hath not the power to change[[fn:2789]], and which, according to others, are in the domain of the possible, which it dependeth upon the Divine omnipotence to bring into existence at will. Thus, for example, the union of contraries at the same instant and in the same subject, the transformation of <i>essentials</i>[[fn:2790]], I mean the change of substance into accident and of accident into substance, or the existence of a corporeal substance without accident, all this is, for every thinker, of the category of the impossible. Likewise, it is impossible that God should call into existence His like, or that He should render Himself non-existent, or that He should become corporeal, or that He should change, and one cannot attribute to Him the power to do aught of all this. As for the question of knowing whether He can produce an accident alone which be not in a substance, a sect of thinkers, namely the Mutazilites, have imagined this and believed it to be in the domain of the possible[[fn:2791]], whilst others have judged it impossible. It is true that those who have professed the existence of an <i>accident without a substratum</i> were not led thereto by simple speculation, but by their regard for certain religious doctrines which speculation violently rejecteth, so that they had recourse to this hypothesis[[fn:2792]]. Likewise, to produce a corporeal thing without making use for that purpose of any pre-existing matter, is, according to us, in the category of the possible; but, according to the philosophers, it is impossible[[fn:2793]]. Likewise, the philosophers will say that it is in the domain of the impossible to produce a square whose diagonal be equal to the side, or a solid angle which be surrounded by four plane right angles[[fn:2794]], or other like things. But many a man who is ignorant of mathematics and who knoweth of these things only the simple words, without conceiving the idea thereof, will believe them possible.

**French (Source):** L’impossible a une nature stable et constante qui n’est pas l’œuvre d’un agent et qui n’est variable à aucune condition; c’est pourquoi on ne saurait attribuer à Dieu aucun pouvoir à cet égard[[fn:2787]]. C’est ce qu’aucun des penseurs[[fn:2788]] ne conteste nullement, et cela n’est ignoré que par ceux qui ne comprennent pas les notions intelligibles. S’il y a dissentiment entre les penseurs, ce n’est que par rapport à certaines choses [[t:0]]imaginables[[t:1]] qui, selon certains penseurs, sont dans la catégorie de l’impossible que Dieu lui-même n’a pas le pouvoir de changer[[fn:2789]], et qui, selon d’autres, sont dans le domaine du possible, qu’il dépend de la toute-puissance divine de faire exister à volonté. Ainsi, par exemple, la réunion des contraires au même instant et dans le même sujet, la transformation des [[t:2]]principaux[[t:3]][[fn:2790]], je veux dire le changement de la substance en accident et de l’accident en substance, ou l’existence d’une substance corporelle sans accident, tout cela est, pour chaque penseur, de la catégorie de l’impossible. De même, il est impossible que Dieu appelle à l’existence son semblable, ou qu’il se rende lui-même non existant, ou qu’il se corporifie, ou qu’il se change, et on ne saurait lui attribuer le pouvoir de faire rien de tout cela. Quant à la question de savoir s’il peut produire un accident seul qui ne soit pas dans une substance, une secte de penseurs, à savoir les Motazales, ont imaginé cela et l’ont cru du domaine du possible[[fn:2791]], tandis que d’autres l’ont jugé impossible. Il est vrai que ceux qui ont professé l’existence d’un [[t:4]]accident sans substratum[[t:5]] n’y ont pas été amenés par la simple spéculation, mais par leurs égards pour certaines doctrines religieuses que la spéculation repousse violemment, de sorte qu’ils ont eu recours à cette hypothèse[[fn:2792]]. De même, produire une chose corporelle sans se servir pour cela d’aucune matière préexistante, est, selon nous, dans la catégorie du possible; mais, selon les philosophes, c’est impossible[[fn:2793]]. De même, les philosophes diront qu’il est du domaine de l’impossible de produire un carré dont la diagonale soit égale au côté, ou un angle solide qui soit environné de quatre angles droits plans[[fn:2794]], ou d’autres choses semblables. Mais, maint homme qui ignore les mathématiques et qui ne connaît de ces choses que les simples mots, sans en concevoir l’idée, les croira possibles.

**Hebrew (Makbili):** [Not Found]

### root.text.Part 3..15.2 (Main Text)
**English:** Thou shalt be astonished to see how they have fallen into something worse than that which they sought to avoid, and how they themselves were ignorant of a thing unto which they constantly called our attention and which they incessantly pretended to explain to us. If I say that they have fallen into something worse than that which they sought to avoid, it is because, in wishing to avoid attributing unto God <i>heedlessness</i> (of human affairs), they have declared that He <i>is ignorant</i> (of these things) and that all which passeth in this world is for Him a mystery which He perceiveth not. If, furthermore, I say that they were themselves ignorant of the thing unto which they were constantly calling our attention, it is because they considered being from the point of view of the conditions of human individuals, who themselves cause the evils wherewith they are afflicted, or receive them from the fatal nature of matter, as (these philosophers) cease not to say and to develop[[fn:2808]]. We have already set forth in this regard that which was necessary[[fn:2809]]. Having established themselves upon a foundation which destroyeth all goodly principles and which disfigureth the beauty of every true opinion[[fn:2810]], they have endeavoured to put away the absurdity which it presenteth[[fn:2811]], by pretending that it is impossible, for several reasons, to attribute unto God the knowledge of these individual things. First (say they), particular things are perceived only by the senses and not by the intellect; but God perceiveth not by means of a sense. Next, particular things are infinite, whilst science consisteth in <i>encompassing;</i> but that which is infinite cannot be encompassed by science. Finally, the knowledge of things which happen, and which without contradiction are particular, would cause God to undergo a species of change; for it would be a successive renewal of knowledge. As for that which we maintain, we who are believers, that God knoweth these things before they are born, they say that we profess therein two absurdities: first, that science can have for its object pure non-being; secondly, that the knowledge of that which is in potentiality and the knowledge of that which is in act are one and the same thing[[fn:2812]]. There hath been among them a conflict of opinions[[fn:2813]]: some have said that God knoweth only the species and not the individuals, whilst others have maintained that He knoweth absolutely naught outside of His essence, so that, according to this latter opinion, there would not be in Him a multiplicity of knowledges[[fn:2814]].
**Correction:** Thou shalt be astonished to see how they have fallen into something worse than that which they sought to avoid, and how they themselves were ignorant of a thing unto which they constantly called our attention and which they incessantly pretended to explain to us. If I say that they have fallen into something worse than that which they sought to avoid, it is because, in wishing to avoid attributing unto God <i>heedlessness</i> (of human affairs), they have declared that He <i>is ignorant</i> (of these things) and that all which passeth in this world is for Him a mystery which He perceiveth not. If, furthermore, I say that they were themselves ignorant of the thing unto which they were constantly calling our attention, it is because they considered being from the point of view of the conditions of human individuals, who themselves cause the evils wherewith they are afflicted, or receive them from the fatal nature of matter, as (these philosophers) cease not to say and to develop[[fn:2808]]. We have already set forth in this regard that which was necessary[[fn:2809]]. Having established themselves upon a foundation which destroyeth all goodly principles and which disfigureth the beauty of every true opinion[[fn:2810]], they have endeavoured to put away the absurdity which it presenteth[[fn:2811]], by pretending that it is impossible, for several reasons, to attribute unto God the knowledge of these individual things. First (say they), particular things are perceived only by the senses and not by the intellect; but God perceiveth not by means of a sense. Next, particular things are infinite, whilst science consisteth in <i>encompassing;</i> but that which is infinite cannot be encompassed by science. Finally, the knowledge of things which happen, and which without contradiction are particular, would cause God to undergo a species of change; for it would be a successive renewal of knowledge. As for that which we maintain, we who are believers, that God knoweth these things before they are born, they say that we profess therein two absurdities: first, that science can have for its object pure non-being; secondly, that the knowledge of that which is in potentiality and the knowledge of that which is in act are one and the same thing[[fn:2812]]. There hath been among them a conflict of opinions[[fn:2813]]: some have said that God knoweth only the species and not the individuals, whilst others have maintained that He knoweth absolutely naught outside of His essence, so that, according to this latter opinion, there would not be in Him a multiplicity of knowledges[[fn:2814]].

**French (Source):** Tu seras étonné de voir comment ils sont tombés dans quelque chose de pire que ce qu’ils ont cherché à éviter, et comment ils ignoraient eux-mêmes une chose sur laquelle ils appelaient constamment notre attention et qu’ils prétendaient sans cesse nous expliquer. Si je dis qu’ils sont tombés dans quelque chose de pire que ce qu’ils ont cherché à éviter, c’est qu’en voulant éviter d’attribuer à Dieu l’[[t:0]]insouciance[[t:1]] (des choses humaines), ils ont déclaré qu’il [[t:2]]ignore[[t:3]] (ces choses) et que tout ce qui se passe dans ce monde est pour lui un mystère qu’il ne perçoit pas. Si ensuite je dis qu’ils ignoraient eux-mêmes la chose sur laquelle ils appelaient constamment notre attention, c’est qu’ils ont considéré l’être au point de vue des conditions des individus humains, qui causent eux-mêmes les maux dont ils sont affligés ou les reçoivent de la nature fatale de la matière, comme (ces philosophes) ne cessent de le dire et de le développer[[fn:2808]]. Nous avons déjà exposé à cet égard ce qui était nécessaire[[fn:2809]]. Après s’être fondés sur une base qui détruit tous les bons principes et qui défigure la beauté de toute opinion vraie[[fn:2810]], ils ont essayé d’écarter ce qu’elle présente d’absurde[[fn:2811]], en prétendant qu’il est impossible, par plusieurs raisons, d’attribuer à Dieu la connaissance de ces choses individuelles. D’abord (disent-ils), les choses partielles sont perçues seulement par les sens et non par l’intelligence; mais Dieu ne perçoit pas au moyen d’un sens. Ensuite, les choses partielles sont infinies, tandis que la science consiste à [[t:4]]embrasser;[[t:5]] mais ce qui est infini ne saurait être embrassé par la science. Enfin la connaissance des choses qui surviennent, et qui sans contredit sont partielles, ferait subir à Dieu une espèce de changement; car ce serait un renouvellement successif de connaissances. Quant à ce que nous soutenons, nous autres croyants, que Dieu connaît ces choses avant qu’elles naissent, ils disent que nous professons là deux absurdités: d’abord, que la science peut avoir pour objet le pur non-être; ensuite, que la connaissance de ce qui est en puissance et la connaissance de ce qui est en acte sont une seule et même chose[[fn:2812]]. Il y a eu entre eux un conflit d’opinions[[fn:2813]]: les uns ont dit que Dieu connaît seulement les espèces et non les individus, tandis que les autres ont soutenu qu’il ne connaît absolument rien en dehors de son essence, de sorte que, selon cette dernière opinion, il n’y aurait point en lui une multiplicité de connaissances[[fn:2814]]. 

**Hebrew (Makbili):** <span class="mediumGrey">הביקורת על מסקנת הפילוסופים בייחוס אי ידיעה לאל</span><br><sup>8</sup> ראה והתפלא <br><sup>א)</sup> כיצד נפלו לרוע חמור יותר ממה שברחו ממנו, <br><sup>ב)</sup> וכיצד גילו בורות בדבר שהם לא חדלו מלהסב אליו את תשומת לבנו, ופירשוהו לנו תמיד. <br><sup>הסבר א)</sup> נפילתם לרוע חמור יותר ממה שברחו ממנו, הוא שהם נמנעו מלייחס לאל הזנחה – ופסקו לו אי ידיעה, ושכל מה שבעולם השפל הזה נסתר ממנו ואין הוא משיגו. <br><sup>הסבר ב)</sup> הבורות שהם גילו במה שהם לא חדלו מלהסב אליו את תשומת לבנו, היא שהם התבוננו במציאות לפי מצבי בני האדם, שרעותיהם באות מהם או מהכרח טבע החומר, כמו שהם אומרים ומבהירים תמיד. וכבר ביארנו מכך מה שראוי (ג,יב).<br><span class="mediumGrey">ניסיון הפילוסופים להצדקת דעתם בטיעונים חלשים</span><br><sup>9</sup> כיוון שהם קבעו כעיקרון את היסוד הזה, המחריב כל יסוד טוב ומכער את יופיה של כל דעה נכונה, החלו לאחר מכן לסלק את זרותו, וטענו שידיעת הדברים האלה בלתי אפשרית לגבי האל מכמה בחינות: <br><sup>א)</sup> מהן, שהפרטים מושגים רק בחושים ולא בשכל, והאל אינו משיג בחוש. <br><sup>ב)</sup> ומהן, שאין סוף לפרטים, ואילו הידיעה מקיפה, וידיעה אינה יכולה להקיף אינסוף. <br><sup>ג)</sup> ומהן, שידיעת הדברים המתחדשים, שהם בלי ספק פרטיים, תחייב בו שינוי מסוים, כי זו התחדשות ידיעה אחר ידיעה. לגבי טענתנו שלנו, קהל הדתיים, שהוא ידע אותם לפני שהיו, הם מאשימים אותנו בשתי זרויות: <br><sup>א)</sup> האחת, שידיעה תיקשר בהעדר מוחלט, <br><sup>ב)</sup> והשנייה, שידיעה של היותו של דבר בכוח וידיעת היותו בפועל, יהיו דבר אחד.

---

### root.text.Part 3..15.4 (Main Text)
**English:** In sum, it is manifest that all the philosophers, had they found the circumstances of human individuals so ordered that even the vulgar might discern therein a proper harmony, would have refrained from embarking upon all this speculation[[fn:2815]], and would not have refuted one another. But that which gave the first occasion for this speculation was the consideration of the respective conditions of virtuous and wicked men, which conditions, in their opinion, were not rightly ordered, as the ignorant among us were wont to say: <i>The way of the Lord is not equal</i> (Ezek. xxxiii. 17).
**Correction:** In sum, it is manifest that all the philosophers, had they found the circumstances of human individuals so ordered that even the vulgar might discern therein a proper harmony, would have refrained from embarking upon all this speculation[[fn:2815]], and would not have refuted one another. But that which gave the first occasion for this speculation was the consideration of the respective conditions of virtuous and wicked men, which conditions, in their opinion, were not rightly ordered, as the ignorant among us were wont to say: <i>The way of the Lord is not equal</i> (Ezek. xxxiii. 17).

**French (Source):** En somme, il est clair que tous (les philosophes), s’ils avaient trouvé les conditions des individus humains tellement ordonnées que le vulgaire même y reconnût le bon ordre, se seraient gardés de se lancer dans toute cette spéculation[[fn:2815]], et ne se seraient pas réfutés les uns les autres. Mais ce qui a donné la première occasion à cette spéculation, c’était la considération des conditions respectives des hommes vertueux et des méchants, conditions qui dans leur opinion n’étaient pas bien réglées, comme disaient les ignorants d’entre nous: [[t:0]]La voie de l’Éternel n’est pas bien réglée[[t:1]] (Ézéch., 33, 17).

**Hebrew (Makbili):** <span class="mediumGrey">סיכום מקור הכשל</span><br><sup>11</sup> כללו של דבר, התברר לך שאילו הם כולם היו מוצאים את מצבי בני האדם סדורים לפי מה שנראה לנבער מדעת כסדירות – לא היו נופלים כלל לכל העיון הזה ולא היו מתפרצים. אלא הגורם הראשוני לעיון זה הוא ההתבוננות במצבי האדם, הטובים והרעים שבהם, ושדבר זה אינו סדור לדעתם, כמו שאמרו הבורים מבינינו: "לֹא יִתָּכֵן דֶּרֶךְ ה'" (יחזקאל לג,יז).

---

### root.text.Part 3..16.2.sub_1 (Main Text)
**English:** As touching the other motions which betide all individuals[[fn:2828]] of a species, they are, according to Aristotle, the result of chance, and not the work of a Being who governeth and ordaineth. Thus, for example, if there bloweth a wind more or less violent, it shall indubitably cause leaves to fall from a certain tree, shall break the branches of another, shall hurl down stones from a certain wall[[fn:2829]], shall cover with dust a certain plant so as to destroy it, and shall agitate certain waters[[fn:2830]] in such wise that a vessel found therein shall perish and all the crew, or a part thereof, shall be drowned. According unto him (Aristotle), there is no distinction between the falling of a leaf or a stone and the submersion of those virtuous and distinguished men who were within the ship; likewise, he maketh no distinction between an ox which causeth the death of a host of ants by depositing its excrements thereon, and a building whose foundations give way and which, in its collapse, causeth the death of all those who are praying therein. Neither is there any distinction, according to him, between a cat that meeteth a mouse and teareth it, a spider that devoureth a fly, and a hungry lion that meeteth a prophet and teareth him[[fn:2831]]. In sum, here is the substance of his opinion: All that which he beheld continuing with sequence, without interruption and without its course undergoing any change, such as the conditions of the celestial spheres, or that which followeth a certain rule and faileth therein only by exception[[fn:2832]], as do physical things, he attributed to a governance, that is to say (he believed) that Divine Providence accompanied it; but that which he beheld not following any rules and not being subject to a certain law, such as the conditions of the individuals of each species, whether plant, or animal, or man[[fn:2833]], he said was the effect of chance and not of a governance, that is to say, that Divine Providence did not accompany it. He even deemed it impossible that these conditions should depend upon Providence, which connecteth with his opinion concerning the eternity of the world, and according to which it is impossible that all that which is should be otherwise than it is. Amongst us also, there were heretics who admitted this opinion, and they are those who said: <i>The Lord hath forsaken the earth</i> (Ezek. ix. 9).
**Correction:** As touching the other motions which betide all individuals[[fn:2828]] of a species, they are, according to Aristotle, the result of chance, and not the work of a Being who governeth and ordaineth. Thus, for example, if there bloweth a wind more or less violent, it shall indubitably cause leaves to fall from a certain tree, shall break the branches of another, shall hurl down stones from a certain wall[[fn:2829]], shall cover with dust a certain plant so as to destroy it, and shall agitate certain waters[[fn:2830]] in such wise that a vessel found therein shall perish and all the crew, or a part thereof, shall be drowned. According unto him (Aristotle), there is no distinction between the falling of a leaf or a stone and the submersion of those virtuous and distinguished men who were within the ship; likewise, he maketh no distinction between an ox which causeth the death of a host of ants by depositing its excrements thereon, and a building whose foundations give way and which, in its collapse, causeth the death of all those who are praying therein. Neither is there any distinction, according to him, between a cat that meeteth a mouse and teareth it, a spider that devoureth a fly, and a hungry lion that meeteth a prophet and teareth him[[fn:2831]]. In sum, here is the substance of his opinion: All that which he beheld continuing with sequence, without interruption and without its course undergoing any change, such as the conditions of the celestial spheres, or that which followeth a certain rule and faileth therein only by exception[[fn:2832]], as do physical things, he attributed to a governance, that is to say (he believed) that Divine Providence accompanied it; but that which he beheld not following any rules and not being subject to a certain law, such as the conditions of the individuals of each species, whether plant, or animal, or man[[fn:2833]], he said was the effect of chance and not of a governance, that is to say, that Divine Providence did not accompany it. He even deemed it impossible that these conditions should depend upon Providence, which connecteth with his opinion concerning the eternity of the world, and according to which it is impossible that all that which is should be otherwise than it is. Amongst us also, there were heretics who admitted this opinion, and they are those who said: <i>The Lord hath forsaken the earth</i> (Ezek. ix. 9).

**French (Source):** Quant aux autres mouvements qui surviennent à tous les individus[[fn:2828]] d’une espèce, ils sont, selon Aristote, l’effet du hasard, et non pas l’œuvre d’un être qui gouverne et ordonne. Ainsi, par exemple, s’il souffle un vent plus ou moins violent, il fera indubitablement tomber des feuilles de tel arbre, brisera des branches de tel autre arbre, précipitera des pierres de tel mur[[fn:2829]], couvrira de poussière telle plante de manière à la détruire, et agitera telle eau[[fn:2830]] de sorte qu’un vaisseau qui se trouvera là périra et que tout l’équipage, ou une partie, se noiera. Selon lui (Aristote), il n’y a point de différence entre la chute de la feuille ou de la pierre et la submersion de ces hommes vertueux et distingués qui étaient dans le vaisseau; de même, il ne fait pas de différence entre un bœuf qui cause la mort d’une troupe de fourmis en y déposant ses excréments, et un édifice dont les fondements se disjoignent et qui, en s’écroulant, cause la mort de tous ceux qui y prient. Il n’y a pas non plus de différence, selon lui, entre un chat qui rencontre une souris et la déchire, une araignée qui dévore une mouche et un lion affamé qui rencontre un prophète et le déchire[[fn:2831]]. En somme, voici le fond de son opinion: Tout ce qu’il voyait se continuer avec suite, sans interruption et sans que sa marche subît aucun changement, comme les conditions des sphères célestes, ou ce qui suit une certaine règle et n’y fait défaut que par exception[[fn:2832]], comme les choses physiques, il l’attribuait à un régime, c’est-à-dire (il croyait) que la Providence divine l’accompagnait; mais, ce qu’il voyait ne pas suivre de règles et ne pas être soumis à une certaine loi, comme les conditions des individus de chaque espèce, soit plante, soit animal, soit homme[[fn:2833]], il disait que c’était l’effet du hasard et non d’un régime, c’est-à dire que la Providence divine ne l’accompagnait pas. Il croyait même impossible que ces conditions dépendissent de la Providence, ce qui se rattache à son opinion concernant l’éternité du monde, et selon laquelle il est impossible que tout ce qui est soit autrement qu’il n’est. Parmi nous aussi, il y avait des hérétiques qui admettaient cette opinion, et ce sont ceux qui disaient: [[t:12]]L’Éternel a abandonné la terre[[t:13]] (Ézéch., 9, 9).

**Hebrew (Makbili):** [Not Found]

### root.text.Part 3..19.1 (Main Text)
**English:** But, from this belief (it may be objected), it would follow that (Divine) knowledge hath for its very object things which exist not, and that it encompasseth the infinite[[fn:2901]]. And this is indeed what we believe. We maintain that it is in no wise impossible that the knowledge of God should have for its object things which exist not yet, but whose future existence He knoweth beforehand and which He is able to bring into being; only that which never existeth, that is what is, in regard to the knowledge of God, absolute non-being, which this knowledge cannot have for its object, even as our own knowledge cannot have for its object that which for us hath no existence[[fn:2902]]. But that which presenteth a (real) difficulty, is to admit that it (Divine knowledge) encompasseth the infinite. Certain thinkers have had recourse to this assertion: that, in a certain sense, Divine knowledge attacheth to the <i>species</i> and extendeth thereby over all the individuals of the species[[fn:2903]]; such is the opinion to which all theologians have perforce been led by speculation[[fn:2904]]. Howbeit, the philosophers have maintained in an absolute manner that Divine knowledge cannot have non-being for its object, and that no knowledge can encompass the infinite; now (said they), as no new knowledge can accrue to God, it is inadmissible that He should learn any of the things newly occurred, and, consequently, He knoweth only things stable and invariable[[fn:2905]]. Unto some among them, another doubt arose: even though, said they, He should know only stable things, His knowledge would be manifold; for the multitude of things known implieth the multiplicity of knowledges, each thing known supposing a special knowledge. Consequently (they concluded), He knoweth only His own essence[[fn:2906]].
**Correction:** But, from this belief (it may be objected), it would follow that (Divine) knowledge hath for its very object things which exist not, and that it encompasseth the infinite[[fn:2901]]. And this is indeed what we believe. We maintain that it is in no wise impossible that the knowledge of God should have for its object things which exist not yet, but whose future existence He knoweth beforehand and which He is able to bring into being; only that which never existeth, that is what is, in regard to the knowledge of God, absolute non-being, which this knowledge cannot have for its object, even as our own knowledge cannot have for its object that which for us hath no existence[[fn:2902]]. But that which presenteth a (real) difficulty, is to admit that it (Divine knowledge) encompasseth the infinite. Certain thinkers have had recourse to this assertion: that, in a certain sense, Divine knowledge attacheth to the <i>species</i> and extendeth thereby over all the individuals of the species[[fn:2903]]; such is the opinion to which all theologians have perforce been led by speculation[[fn:2904]]. Howbeit, the philosophers have maintained in an absolute manner that Divine knowledge cannot have non-being for its object, and that no knowledge can encompass the infinite; now (said they), as no new knowledge can accrue to God, it is inadmissible that He should learn any of the things newly occurred, and, consequently, He knoweth only things stable and invariable[[fn:2905]]. Unto some among them, another doubt arose: even though, said they, He should know only stable things, His knowledge would be manifold; for the multitude of things known implieth the multiplicity of knowledges, each thing known supposing a special knowledge. Consequently (they concluded), He knoweth only His own essence[[fn:2906]].

**French (Source):** Mais, de cette croyance (peut-on objecter), il s’ensuivrait que la science (divine) a pour objet même les choses qui n’existent pas, et qu’elle embrasse l’infini[[fn:2901]]. Et c’est là ce que nous croyons en effet. Nous soutenons qu’il n’est point impossible que la science de Dieu ait pour objet les choses qui n’existent pas encore, mais dont il sait d’avance la future existence et qu’il est capable de faire naître; seulement ce qui n’existe jamais, c’est là ce qui est à l’égard de la science de Dieu le non-être absolu que cette science ne peut avoir pour objet, de même que notre science à nous ne peut avoir pour objet ce qui pour nous n’a pas d’existence[[fn:2902]]. Mais ce qui est une difficulté (réelle), c’est d’admettre qu’elle (la science divine) embrasse l’infini. Certains penseurs ont eu recours à cette assertion: que, dans un certain sens, la science divine s’attache à l’[[t:0]]espèce[[t:1]] et s’étend par là sur tous les individus de l’espèce[[fn:2903]]; telle est l’opinion à laquelle tous les théologiens ont été forcément amenés par la spéculation[[fn:2904]]. Cependant, les philosophes ont soutenu d’une manière absolue que la science divine ne peut avoir pour objet le non-être, et qu’aucune science ne peut embrasser l’infini; or (disaient-ils), comme il ne peut survenir à Dieu aucune science nouvelle, il est inadmissible qu’il apprenne aucune des choses nouvellement survenues, et, par conséquent, il ne sait que les choses stables et invariables[[fn:2905]]. A quelques-uns d’entre eux, il a surgi un autre doute: lors même, disaient-ils, qu’il ne connaîtrait que les choses stables, sa science serait multiple; car la multitude des choses sues implique la multiplicité des sciences, chaque chose sue supposant une science spéciale. Par conséquent (concluaient-ils), il ne connaît que sa propre essence[[fn:2906]].

**Hebrew (Makbili):** <span class="mediumGrey">מחלוקת בדבר היקף הידיעה האלוהית</span><br><sup>2</sup> מהאמנה זו מתחייב שהידיעה יכולה לחול על מקרי העדר, ולכלול את האינסופי. <br><sup>העדר)</sup> אימצנו האמנה זו ואמרנו שאין זה מן הנמנע שידיעתו תחול על מקרי ההעדר שהבאתם למציאות קדמה בידיעתו, ושהוא יכול להביאם למציאות. ואילו מה שלא יימצא כלל – זהו ההעדר המוחלט מצד ידיעתו, שידיעתו אינה חלה עליו, כמו שידיעתנו שלנו אינה חלה על מה שהוא נעדר מבחינתנו.<br><sup>3</sup> <sup>קושי בידיעת האינסוף)</sup> אשר ל(ידיעה) הכוללת את האינסופי – יש בדבר קושי. <br><sup>דעת כל דתי)</sup> חלק מאנשי העיון סברו שהידיעה חלה על המין, ומתפשטת במובן מסוים על כל פרטי המין. זוהי דעת כל דתי בהתאם למה שהכרח העיון מוביל אליו. <br><sup>דעת הפילוסופים)</sup> ואילו הפילוסופים פסקו ואמרו שידיעה אינה חלה על העדר ואינה כוללת את האינסופי. ומכיוון שאין מתחדשת לו ידיעה – בלתי אפשרי שידע משהו מן הדברים המתחדשים. נמצא שאין הוא יודע אלא את הדברים הקבועים שאינם משתנים. <br><sup>דעה נוספת)</sup> לאחד מהם עלה ספק אחר, ואמר: אפילו הדברים הקבועים – אם הוא יודע אותם נעשו לו ידיעות רבות, כי ריבוי ידועים גורר ריבוי ידיעות, כי לכל ידוע יש ידיעה מיוחדת לו. נמצא שאין הוא יודע אלא את עצמותו.

---

### root.text.Part 3..19.2 (Main Text)
**English:** For my part, I deem the cause of all these perplexities to be that men have established a relation between our knowledge and that of God, so that each party, considering all that is impossible for our own knowledge, hath imagined that it is necessarily the same[[fn:2907]] for Divine knowledge, or at least hath found therein difficulties. In sum, the philosophers are in this matter to be blamed far more than any other party; for it is they who have demonstrated that, in the essence of God, there is no multiplicity, that God hath no attribute outside His essence, and that, on the contrary, His knowledge and His essence are one and the same thing. It is they also who have demonstrated that our intellects are incapable of grasping His essence in all its reality, as we have set forth; how then can they have the pretension to comprehend His knowledge, since this latter is not a thing outside His essence? When we say that our intellects are incapable of comprehending His essence, do we not say thereby that they are incapable of comprehending how He hath knowledge of things?[[fn:2908]] Verily, this knowledge is not of the same species as ours, that we might judge thereof by analogy. It is, on the contrary, a thing totally different; and even as there is there an essence of a necessary existence, an essence wherefrom, according to the opinion of the philosophers, all beings have emanated by necessity, or which, according to our opinion, hath produced from nothingness all that is outside itself, in like manner do we say that this essence perceiveth all that is outside itself, and that naught of that which existeth[[fn:2909]] is unknown to it, but that there is nothing in common between our knowledge and His, even as there is naught in common between our essence and His. It is only the homonymy of the word <i>knowledge</i> which hath given rise to the error; for there is therein but community of names, whilst for the real sense there is complete divergence. This, then, is what hath led to the absurd, because it was imagined that all that pertaineth to our knowledge, pertaineth also to that of God[[fn:2910]].
**Correction:** For my part, I deem the cause of all these perplexities to be that men have established a relation between our knowledge and that of God, so that each party, considering all that is impossible for our own knowledge, hath imagined that it is necessarily the same[[fn:2907]] for Divine knowledge, or at least hath found therein difficulties. In sum, the philosophers are in this matter to be blamed far more than any other party; for it is they who have demonstrated that, in the essence of God, there is no multiplicity, that God hath no attribute outside His essence, and that, on the contrary, His knowledge and His essence are one and the same thing. It is they also who have demonstrated that our intellects are incapable of grasping His essence in all its reality, as we have set forth; how then can they have the pretension to comprehend His knowledge, since this latter is not a thing outside His essence? When we say that our intellects are incapable of comprehending His essence, do we not say thereby that they are incapable of comprehending how He hath knowledge of things?[[fn:2908]] Verily, this knowledge is not of the same species as ours, that we might judge thereof by analogy. It is, on the contrary, a thing totally different; and even as there is there an essence of a necessary existence, an essence wherefrom, according to the opinion of the philosophers, all beings have emanated by necessity, or which, according to our opinion, hath produced from nothingness all that is outside itself, in like manner do we say that this essence perceiveth all that is outside itself, and that naught of that which existeth[[fn:2909]] is unknown to it, but that there is nothing in common between our knowledge and His, even as there is naught in common between our essence and His. It is only the homonymy of the word <i>knowledge</i> which hath given rise to the error; for there is therein but community of names, whilst for the real sense there is complete divergence. This, then, is what hath led to the absurd, because it was imagined that all that pertaineth to our knowledge, pertaineth also to that of God[[fn:2910]].

**French (Source):** Pour ma part, je pense que la cause de tous ces embarras, c’est qu’on a établi un rapport entre notre science et celle de Dieu, de sorte que chaque parti, considérant tout ce qui est impossible pour notre science à nous, s’est imaginé qu’il en est nécessairement de même[[fn:2907]] pour la science divine, ou du moins a trouvé là des difficultés. En somme, il faut sur ce point blâmer les philosophes bien plus encore que tout autre parti; car ce sont eux qui ont démontré que, dans l’essence de Dieu, il n’y a point de multiplicité, que Dieu n’a pas d’attribut en dehors de son essence, et qu’au contraire sa science et son essence sont une seule et même chose. Ce sont eux aussi qui ont démontré que nos intelligences sont incapables de saisir son essence dans toute sa réalité, comme nous l’avons exposé; comment donc alors peuvent-ils avoir la prétention de comprendre sa science, puisque celle-ci n’est point une chose en dehors de son essence? Quand nous disons que nos intelligences sont incapables de comprendre son essence, ne disons-nous pas par là même qu’elles sont incapables de comprendre comment il a connaissance des choses[[fn:2908]] En effet, cette connaissance n’est pas de la même espèce que la nôtre, pour que nous puissions en juger par analogie. C’est au contraire une chose totalement différente; et de même qu’il y a là une essence, d’une existence nécessaire, essence dont, selon l’opinion des philosophes, tous les êtres sont émanés par nécessité, ou qui, selon notre opinion, a produit du néant tout ce qui est en dehors d’elle, de même, nous disons que cette essence perçoit tout ce qui est en dehors d’elle, et que rien de ce qui existe[[fn:2909]] ne lui est inconnu, mais qu’il n’y a rien de commun entre notre science et la sienne, comme il n’y a non plus rien de commun entre notre essence et la sienne. Ce n’est que l’homonymie du mot [[t:0]]science[[t:1]] qui a donné lieu à l’erreur; car il n’y a là que communauté de noms, tandis que pour le sens réel il y a complète divergence. C’est donc là ce qui a conduit à l’absurde, parce qu’on s’est imaginé que tout ce qui compète à notre science, compète aussi à celle de Dieu[[fn:2910]].

**Hebrew (Makbili):** <span class="mediumGrey">דעת הרמב"ם: ביקורת חריפה</span><br><sup>4</sup> מה שאני אומר הוא שהסיבה לכל מה שנכשלו בו היא שהם הניחו יחס בין ידיעתנו לידיעתו יתעלה. וכל קבוצה מסתכלת על הדברים שאינם אפשריים לגבי ידיעתנו, וחושבת שדבר זה מחויב לגבי ידיעתו, או שהיא מסופקת אם הדבר כך הוא. וראוי לנזוף בעיקר בפילוסופים בשאלה זו, יותר מבכל אחד אחר, כי <br><sup>א)</sup> הם אלה שהוכיחו שאין ריבוי בעצמותו יתעלה ושאין לו תואר חיצוני לעצמותו, אלא ידיעתו היא עצמותו ועצמותו היא ידיעתו. <br><sup>ב)</sup> והם שהוכיחו ששכלינו אינם מסוגלים להשיג את אמיתת עצמותו כפי שהיא, כמו שביארנו (א:נד1;נח6), ואם כן, כיצד הם טוענים שהם משיגים את ידיעתו, והרי ידיעתו אינה דבר מחוץ לעצמותו? אלא עצם חוסר היכולת הזה מצד שכלנו להשיג את עצמותו, הוא אותו חוסר יכולת להשיג כיצד היא ידיעתו את הדברים. ואין זו ידיעה ממינה של ידיעתנו כך שאנו יכולים להקיש מזו אל זו, אלא זהו דבר שונה לחלוטין.<br><span class="mediumGrey">היקף אינסופי לידיעה האלוהית</span><br><sup>5</sup> וכמו שיש עצמוּת מחויבת המציאות שממנה מתחייב כל מצוי, לפי דעתם, או שהיא שעשתה את כל מה שזולתה לאחר ההעדר, לפי דעתנו – כך אנו אומרים שאותה עצמות משיגה את כל מה שזולתה, ולא נעלם ממנה שום דבר מכל מה שבא למציאות, ואין מכנה משותף בין ידיעתנו וידיעתו כמו שאין מכנה משותף בין עצמותנו ועצמותו. מה שגרם לטעות כאן הוא הרב־משמעיות של המונח "ידיעה", כי המכנה המשותף הוא המינוח בלבד והשונות היא במהות. בשל דבר זה מתחייבים אבסורדים, משום שאנחנו מדמיינים שהדברים המחויבים ביחס לידיעתנו מחויבים ביחס לידיעתו.

---

### root.text.Part 3..19.5 (Main Text)
**English:** In sum, thus do I resume my thought: Even as, without comprehending the true essence of God, we nevertheless know that His being is the most perfect being, that He is affected, in no wise, by imperfection, nor change, nor passion; even so, without comprehending what His science is in reality, since it is His essence, we nevertheless know that He cannot now know and now be ignorant; I mean to say that no new science can come unto Him, that His science can have neither multiplicity nor end, that none of the things which exist can be unknown to Him, and that the knowledge He hath of these things leaveth their nature intact, the possible preserving the nature of <i>possibility.</i> If in the whole of these propositions there be some which seem to involve contradiction, it is because we judge of them by our own science, which hath nothing in common with the science of God, save the name. Likewise, the word <i>intention</i> is applied, by simple homonymy, to that which we ourselves have in view, and to that which God is said to have in view. Finally, in like manner, the word <i>providence</i>[[fn:2917]] is used by homonymy of that wherewith we ourselves are preoccupied, and of that wherewith God is said to be preoccupied. The truth is, consequently, that <i>science</i>, <i>intention</i>, and <i>providence</i>, attributed to us, have not the same sense as when they are attributed to God. It is therefore when one taketh in one and the same sense the two <i>providences,</i> the two <i>sciences</i> or the two <i>intentions</i>, that the difficulties arise and the doubts of which we have spoken are born[[fn:2918]]; but when it is known that all which is attributed to us differeth from that which is attributed to God, the truth becometh manifest. The difference that existeth between these things attributed to God and the same things attributed to us hath been clearly enunciated by these words: <i>Your ways are not my ways</i>, as we have previously stated.
**Correction:** In sum, thus do I resume my thought: Even as, without comprehending the true essence of God, we nevertheless know that His being is the most perfect being, that He is affected, in no wise, by imperfection, nor change, nor passion; even so, without comprehending what His science is in reality, since it is His essence, we nevertheless know that He cannot now know and now be ignorant; I mean to say that no new science can come unto Him, that His science can have neither multiplicity nor end, that none of the things which exist can be unknown to Him, and that the knowledge He hath of these things leaveth their nature intact, the possible preserving the nature of <i>possibility.</i> If in the whole of these propositions there be some which seem to involve contradiction, it is because we judge of them by our own science, which hath nothing in common with the science of God, save the name. Likewise, the word <i>intention</i> is applied, by simple homonymy, to that which we ourselves have in view, and to that which God is said to have in view. Finally, in like manner, the word <i>providence</i>[[fn:2917]] is used by homonymy of that wherewith we ourselves are preoccupied, and of that wherewith God is said to be preoccupied. The truth is, consequently, that <i>science</i>, <i>intention</i>, and <i>providence</i>, attributed to us, have not the same sense as when they are attributed to God. It is therefore when one taketh in one and the same sense the two <i>providences,</i> the two <i>sciences</i> or the two <i>intentions</i>, that the difficulties arise and the doubts of which we have spoken are born[[fn:2918]]; but when it is known that all which is attributed to us differeth from that which is attributed to God, the truth becometh manifest. The difference that existeth between these things attributed to God and the same things attributed to us hath been clearly enunciated by these words: <i>Your ways are not my ways</i>, as we have previously stated.

**French (Source):** En somme, voici comment je résume ma pensée: De même que, sans comprendre la véritable essence de Dieu, nous savons pourtant que son être est l’être le plus parfait, qu’il n’est affecté, en aucune façon, d’imperfection, ni de changement, ni de passion, de même, sans comprendre ce que sa science est en réalité, puisqu’elle est son essence, nous savons pourtant qu’il ne peut pas tantôt savoir et tantôt ignorer; je veux dire qu’il ne peut lui survenir aucune science nouvelle, que sa science ne peut avoir ni multiplicité ni fin, qu’aucune des choses qui existent ne peut lui être inconnue, et que la connaissance qu’il a de ces choses laisse intacte leur nature, le possible conservant la nature de [[t:0]]possibilité.[[t:1]] Si dans l’ensemble de ces propositions il y en a qui paraissent impliquer contradiction, c’est parce que nous en jugeons par notre science à nous, qui n’a rien de commun avec la science de Dieu, si ce n’est le nom. De même, le mot [[t:2]]intention[[t:3]] s’applique, par simple homonymie, à ce que nous avons en vue, nous autres, et à ce que Dieu est dit avoir en vue. De même, enfin, le mot [[t:4]]providence[[t:5]][[fn:2917]] se dit par homonymie de ce dont nous nous préoccupons, nous autres, et de ce dont Dieu est dit se préoccuper. La vérité est, par conséquent, que la [[t:6]]science[[t:7]], l[[t:8]]’intention[[t:9]] et la [[t:10]]providence[[t:11]], attribuées à nous, n’ont pas le même sens que lorsqu’elles sont attribuées à Dieu. C’est donc lorsqu’on prend dans un seul et même sens les deux [[t:12]]providences,[[t:13]] les deux [[t:14]]sciences[[t:15]] ou les deux [[t:16]]intentions[[t:17]], qu’arrivent les difficultés et que naissent les doutes dont nous avons parlé[[fn:2918]]; mais lorsqu’on sait que tout ce qui est attribué à nous diffère de ce qui est attribué à Dieu, la vérité devient manifeste. La différence qu’il y a entre ces choses attribuées à Dieu et les mêmes choses attribuées à nous a été clairement énoncée par ces paroles: [[t:18]]Vos voies ne sont pas les miennes[[t:19]], comme nous l’avons dit précédemment.

**Hebrew (Makbili):** <span class="mediumGrey">סיכום: פער בלתי ניתן לגישור בין האדם לבין האל</span><br><sup>9</sup> כללו של דבר שאני אומר ומסכם: <br><sup>אחדות מציאותו וידיעתו)</sup> כמו שאיננו משיגים את אמיתת עצמותו, אבל אף על פי כן אנו יודעים שמציאותו היא המציאות השלמה ביותר ואין בה חסרון ולא שינוי ולא היפעלות בשום אופן – כך אף על פי שאיננו יודעים את אמיתת ידיעתו, שהרי היא עצמותו, אנו יודעים שאין הוא יודע בזמן מסוים ואינו יודע בזמן אחר, כלומר שאין מתחדשת לו ידיעה בשום אופן; ושידיעתו אינה מתרבה, ושהיא אינסופית, ושאין נעלם ממנו שום דבר מכל המצויים, וידיעתו אותם אינה מבטלת את טבעיהם, אלא האפשרי נותר בטבע האפשריוּת. וכל סתירה לכאורה באמירות האלה היא לפי ההתבוננות בידיעתנו, שאינה משותפת לידיעתו אלא בשם בלבד. <br><sup>כוונה)</sup> וכן המונח "כוונה" נאמר באופן רב־משמעי לגבי מה שאנחנו מתכוונים אליו ולגבי מה שאומרים שהוא מתכוון אליו. <br><sup>השגחה)</sup> וכן המונח "השגחה" נאמר באופן רב־משמעי לגבי מה שאנחנו משגיחים עליו ולגבי מה שאומרים שהוא יתעלה משגיח עליו.<br><sup>10</sup> והנכון, אם כן, שמשמעות ה"ידיעה" ומשמעות ה"כוונה" ומשמעות ה"השגחה" המיוחסות אלינו אינן המשמעויות של אלה המיוחסות אליו. כשמבינים את שתי ההשגחות או את שתי הידיעות או את שתי הכוונות כאילו יש להן משמעות אחת – נוצרים הקשיים ומתעוררים הספקות שנזכרו לעיל. אך כשיודעים שכל מה שמיוחס אלינו שונה מכל מה שמיוחס אליו – מתבררת האמת. השוני בין אלה המיוחסות אליו והמיוחסות אלינו כבר נאמר במפורש בדבריו: "וְלֹא דַרְכֵיכֶם דְּרָכָי", כמו שכבר ציינו לעיל (פסקה 8).

---

### root.text.Part 3..21.0 (Main Text)
**English:** The history of Job, so strange and so astonishing[[fn:2930]], relateth to the subject wherewith we are occupied; I mean that it is a parable which hath for its aim to set forth the opinions of men concerning Providence. Thou knowest that certain doctors say expressly: “Job never existed, and this is but a parable[[fn:2931]].” Even those who believe that he existed and that it is a history which (really) happened, know not how to assign to him[[fn:2932]] either time or place. Some doctors say that he existed in the time of the patriarchs; others say, in the time of Moses; others again, in the time of David; others finally say that he was of those who returned from Babylon. But all this doth but confirm the opinion of those who say that he never existed. In sum, whether he existed or not, the fact remaineth that all readers have been cast into perplexity by his history such as it is related to us[[fn:2933]]; so that there have been objected against the science and the Providence of God what I have already mentioned[[fn:2934]], to wit, that the virtuous and perfect man, full of probity in his actions, and who hath the greatest care to avoid sins, is yet smitten, stroke upon stroke, with great misfortunes, in his fortune, in his children, and in his person, without having deserved it by any sin whatsoever. According to both opinions again, whether Job existed or not, the prologue of the book, I mean the discourse of Satan, the words which God addresseth to Satan, Job delivered into the power of the latter, all this (say I), for every intelligent man, is indubitably a parable. Nevertheless, this is not a parable such as there are many, but a parable to which are attached profound thoughts, <i>things which form the mystery of the universe</i>[[fn:2935]], and which serveth to clear up great obscurities and to manifest the highest truths[[fn:2936]]. I shall tell thee of it all that can be told[[fn:2937]], and I shall relate the words of the doctors who have awakened my attention to all that I have been able to understand of this important parable.
**Correction:** The history of Job, so strange and so astonishing[[fn:2930]], relateth to the subject wherewith we are occupied; I mean that it is a parable which hath for its aim to set forth the opinions of men concerning Providence. Thou knowest that certain doctors say expressly: “Job never existed, and this is but a parable[[fn:2931]].” Even those who believe that he existed and that it is a history which (really) happened, know not how to assign to him[[fn:2932]] either time or place. Some doctors say that he existed in the time of the patriarchs; others say, in the time of Moses; others again, in the time of David; others finally say that he was of those who returned from Babylon. But all this doth but confirm the opinion of those who say that he never existed. In sum, whether he existed or not, the fact remaineth that all readers have been cast into perplexity by his history such as it is related to us[[fn:2933]]; so that there have been objected against the science and the Providence of God what I have already mentioned[[fn:2934]], to wit, that the virtuous and perfect man, full of probity in his actions, and who hath the greatest care to avoid sins, is yet smitten, stroke upon stroke, with great misfortunes, in his fortune, in his children, and in his person, without having deserved it by any sin whatsoever. According to both opinions again, whether Job existed or not, the prologue of the book, I mean the discourse of Satan, the words which God addresseth to Satan, Job delivered into the power of the latter, all this (say I), for every intelligent man, is indubitably a parable. Nevertheless, this is not a parable such as there are many, but a parable to which are attached profound thoughts, <i>things which form the mystery of the universe</i>[[fn:2935]], and which serveth to clear up great obscurities and to manifest the highest truths[[fn:2936]]. I shall tell thee of it all that can be told[[fn:2937]], and I shall relate the words of the doctors who have awakened my attention to all that I have been able to understand of this important parable.

**French (Source):** L’histoire de Job, si étrange et si étonnante[[fn:2930]], se rapporte au sujet dont nous nous occupons; je veux dire qu’elle est une parabole qui a pour but d’exposer les opinions des hommes sur la Providence. Tu sais que certains docteurs disent expressément: «Job n’a jamais existé, et ce n’est là qu’une parabole[[fn:2931]].» Ceux-là même qui croient qu’il a existé et que c’est une histoire qui est (réellement) arrivée, ne savent lui[[fn:2932]] assigner ni temps ni lieu. Quelques docteurs disent qu’il exista du temps des patriarches; d’autres disent, du temps de Moïse; d’autres encore, du temps de David; d’autres enfin disent qu’il fut de ceux qui revinrent de Babylone. Mais tout cela ne fait que confirmer l’opinion de ceux qui disent qu’il n’a jamais existé. En somme, qu’il ait existé ou non, toujours est-il que tous les lecteurs ont été jetés dans la perplexité par son histoire telle qu’elle nous est racontée[[fn:2933]]; de sorte qu’on a objecté contre la science et la Providence de Dieu ce que j’ai déjà mentionné[[fn:2934]], à savoir, que l’homme vertueux et parfait, plein de probité dans ses actions, et qui a le plus grand soin d’éviter les péchés, est pourtant frappé, coup sur coup, de grands malheurs, dans sa fortune, dans ses enfants et dans sa personne, sans l’avoir mérité par un péché quelconque. Selon les deux opinions encore, que Job ait existé ou non, le prologue du livre, je veux dire le discours de Satan, les paroles que Dieu adresse à Satan, Job livré au pouvoir de ce dernier, tout cela (dis-je), pour tout homme intelligent, est indubitablement une parabole. Cependant ce n’est pas là une parabole comme il y en a tant, mais une parabole à laquelle se rattachent des pensées profondes, [[t:0]]des choses qui forment le mystère de l’univers[[t:1]][[fn:2935]], et qui sert à éclaircir de grandes obscurités et à manifester les plus hautes vérités[[fn:2936]]. Je vais t’en dire tout ce qui peut se dire[[fn:2937]], et je rapporterai les paroles des docteurs qui ont éveillé mon attention sur tout ce que j’ai pu comprendre de cette importante parabole.

**Hebrew (Makbili):** <b>איוב: סיפור הפתיחה וזהות השטן</b><br><span class="mediumGrey">פתיחה</span><br><sup>1</sup> פרשת איוב הנפלאה והמופלאה הוא מסוג הדברים שאנו עוסקים בו. כוונתי שהוא משל שנועד להבהרת דעות בני האדם על ההשגחה.<br><span class="mediumGrey">המשל וחשיבותו</span><br><sup>2</sup> ידועים לך דבריהם המפורשים (של חז"ל), ואמירת אחד מהם: <br><sup>א: משל)</sup> "איוב לא היה ולא נברא אלא משל היה" (בבלי בבא בתרא טו,א). <br><sup>ב: מציאות מסופקת)</sup> ומי שטען שהוא היה ונברא ושהוא מעשה שהיה – לא ידע לא מתי התרחש ולא היכן. אלא יש מן החכמים מי שאמר שהוא היה בימי האבות, ויש שאמר שהוא היה בימי משה, ויש שאמר שהוא היה בימי דוד, ויש שאמר שהוא מעולי בבל (שם טו,א-ב). דבר זה מחזק את דברי האומר שהוא לא היה ולא נברא.<br><sup>3</sup> כללו של דבר, בין היה בין לא היה, בדברים מעין בעייתו זו, המצויים תמיד, נבוך כל מעיין מבני האדם, עד שנאמר על ידיעת האל ועל השגחתו מה שציינתי לך (ג:טז,יז,יט). כוונתי לכך שעל אדם צדיק, שלם, שמעשיו ישרים, ירא חטא ביותר – נוחתות צרות קשות בזו אחר זו על נכסיו ובניו וגופו, ולא בשל חטא שחייב זאת.<br><sup>4</sup> לכל הדעות, בין היה בין לא היה, אותם דברים שנאמרו כהקדמה, כלומר דברי השטן ודברי ה' לשטן ומסירתו בידו (איוב א,א-ב,ז) – כל אלה משל בלי ספק לכל בר דעת. אבל הוא משל שאינו ככל המשלים, אלא משל שנקשרו בו נפלאות ודברים שהם כבשונו שלעולם, והתבררו על ידו קשיים חמורים, והתחוורו ממנו אמיתות שאין נעלות מהן. אציין לך מה שאפשר לציין, ואביא את דברי החכמים שהסבו את תשומת לבי לכל מה שהבנתי מאותו משל גדול.

---

### root.text.Part 3..23.8 (Main Text)
**English:** Thou shalt know that this idea hath been confirmed and set forth in the Law, where it is said that the sum of the whole Law, all that it containeth in the way of commands, prohibitions, promises, and narrations, hath but one single object, which is the fear of God. These are the terms: <i>If thou wilt not observe to do all the words of this law that are written in this book, that thou mayest fear this glorious and fearful name, etc.</i> (Deut. xxviii. 58). — Such is one of the two ideas which were intended in the (narrative of the) sacrifice of Isaac.
**Correction:** Thou shalt know that this idea hath been confirmed and set forth in the Law, where it is said that the sum of the whole Law, all that it containeth in the way of commands, prohibitions, promises, and narrations, hath but one single object, which is the fear of God. These are the terms: <i>If thou wilt not observe to do all the words of this law that are written in this book, that thou mayest fear this glorious and fearful name, etc.</i> (Deut. xxviii. 58). — Such is one of the two ideas which were intended in the (narrative of the) sacrifice of Isaac.

**French (Source):** Tu sauras que cette idée a été confirmée et exposée dans la Loi, où l’on dit que l’ensemble de toute la Loi, tout ce qu’elle renferme en faits d’ordres, de défenses, de promesses et de narrations, n’a pour but qu’une seule chose, qui est la crainte de Dieu. Voici les termes: [[t:0]]Si tu ne prends garde d’observer toutes les paroles de cette loi qui sont écrites dans ce livre, en craignant ce nom glorieux et redoutable etc.[[t:1]] (Deutér., 28, 58). — Telle est l’une des deux idées qu’on a eues en vue dans le (récit du) sacrifice d’Isaac.

**Hebrew (Makbili):** <sup>10</sup> <sup>מטרת התורה: יראה)</sup> דע שעניין זה הודגש והובהר בתורה, ונאמר שתכלית מכלול התורה כולה, על הציוויים והאיסורים וההבטחות והסיפורים הכלולים בה – אינה אלא דבר אחד, והוא היראה ממנו יתעלה, כמו שנאמר: "אִם לֹא תִשְׁמֹר לַעֲשׂוֹת אֶת כָּל דִּבְרֵי הַתּוֹרָה הַזֹּאת הַכְּתֻבִים בַּסֵּפֶר הַזֶּה, לְיִרְאָה אֶת הַשֵּׁם הַנִּכְבָּד וְהַנּוֹרָא הַזֶּה [אֵת ה' אֱלֹהֶיךָ]" (דברים כח,נח). זהו אחד משני העניינים המכוונים בעקידה.

### root.text.Part 3..24.4.sub_0 (Main Text)
**English:** As for those who have said that God, in all that He hath wrought, had in view no end, they were necessarily led thereto by considering the whole of being from the standpoint of their opinion[[fn:3066]]; for having asked themselves what might be the end of the existence of the world in its entirety, they necessarily answered, as do all those who maintain the novelty of the world: “Even so hath He <i>willed</i> it, without having any other reason.” Thereafter they continued this reasoning with respect to all the details of the universe, so that, for example, far from agreeing that the perforation of the uvea and the transparency of the cornea had for their end to give passage to the <i>visual spirit</i>[[fn:3067]] in order to produce perception, they denied on the contrary that such was the cause of vision. It is not, said they, in view of vision that this membrane was perforated and that which is above was rendered transparent, but it is thus that God willed it, although vision were possible had it been otherwise. We have certain biblical passages whose literal sense, at first sight, might give rise to such a notion. It is said, for instance: <i>The Lord hath done whatsoever He pleased</i> (Ps. 135, 6); <i>and what His soul desireth, even that He doeth</i> (Job 23, 13); and elsewhere: <i>and who shall say unto Him, What doest Thou?</i> (Eccles. VIII, 4.) But the sense of these passages and other like ones is this: That which God <i>willeth</i> is necessarily done, and there is naught that can hinder the accomplishment of His will. [Howbeit, God willeth only that which is possible; not all that is possible, but only that which is required by His wisdom[[fn:3068]]]. Likewise, the absolutely good work which God willeth to perform cannot be stayed by any obstacle, and naught can prevent it[[fn:3069]]. Such is the opinion of every theologian and that of the philosophers, and such also is our own; for, although we believe that the world was <i>created</i>, all our doctors and all our sages admit that this event took place not by the sole will of God; but they say that divine wisdom, which we are unable to comprehend, necessitated the existence of this whole universe at the moment when it came into being, and that this same invariable wisdom had necessitated non-existence before the world existed.
**Correction:** As for those who have said that God, in all that He hath wrought, had in view no end, they were necessarily led thereto by considering the whole of being from the standpoint of their opinion[[fn:3066]]; for having asked themselves what might be the end of the existence of the world in its entirety, they necessarily answered, as do all those who maintain the novelty of the world: “Even so hath He <i>willed</i> it, without having any other reason.” Thereafter they continued this reasoning with respect to all the details of the universe, so that, for example, far from agreeing that the perforation of the uvea and the transparency of the cornea had for their end to give passage to the <i>visual spirit</i>[[fn:3067]] in order to produce perception, they denied on the contrary that such was the cause of vision. It is not, said they, in view of vision that this membrane was perforated and that which is above was rendered transparent, but it is thus that God willed it, although vision were possible had it been otherwise. We have certain biblical passages whose literal sense, at first sight, might give rise to such a notion. It is said, for instance: <i>The Lord hath done whatsoever He pleased</i> (Ps. 135, 6); <i>and what His soul desireth, even that He doeth</i> (Job 23, 13); and elsewhere: <i>and who shall say unto Him, What doest Thou?</i> (Eccles. VIII, 4.) But the sense of these passages and other like ones is this: That which God <i>willeth</i> is necessarily done, and there is naught that can hinder the accomplishment of His will. [Howbeit, God willeth only that which is possible; not all that is possible, but only that which is required by His wisdom[[fn:3068]]]. Likewise, the absolutely good work which God willeth to perform cannot be stayed by any obstacle, and naught can prevent it[[fn:3069]]. Such is the opinion of every theologian and that of the philosophers, and such also is our own; for, although we believe that the world was <i>created</i>, all our doctors and all our sages admit that this event took place not by the sole will of God; but they say that divine wisdom, which we are unable to comprehend, necessitated the existence of this whole universe at the moment when it came into being, and that this same invariable wisdom had necessitated non-existence before the world existed.

**French (Source):** Quant à ceux qui ont dit que Dieu, dans tout ce qu’il a fait, n’a eu en vue aucun but, ils y ont été nécessairement amenés en considérant l’ensemble de l’être au point de vue de leur opinion[[fn:3066]]; car s’étant demandé quel serait le but de l’existence du monde dans son ensemble, ils ont nécessairement répondu, comme le font tous ceux qui soutiennent la nouveauté du monde: «C’est ainsi qu’il l’a [[t:0]]voulu[[t:1]], sans avoir d’autre raison.» Ensuite ils ont continué ce raisonnement à l’égard de tous les détails de l’univers, de sorte que, par exemple, loin de convenir que la perforation de l’uvée et la transparence de la cornée avaient pour but de donner passage à l’[[t:2]]esprit visuel[[t:3]][[fn:3067]] afin de produire la perception, ils niaient au contraire que ce fût là la cause de la vision. Ce n’est pas, disaient-ils, en vue de la vision que cette membrane a été perforée et que celle qui est au-dessus a été rendue transparente, mais c’est ainsi que Dieu l’a voulu, quoique la vision fût possible s’il en eût été autrement. Nous avons certains passages bibliques dont le sens littéral, de prime abord, pourrait donner lieu à une pareille idée. Il est dit par exemple: [[t:4]]L’Éternel a fait tout ce qu’il a voulu[[t:5]] (Ps., 135, 6); [[t:6]]et ce que son âme a désiré, il l’a fait[[t:7]] (Job, 23, 13); et ailleurs: [[t:8]]et qui lui dira que fais-tu?[[t:9]] (Eccles., VIII, 4.) Mais le sens de ces passages et d’autres semblables est celui-ci: Ce que Dieu [[t:10]]veut[[t:11]] se fait nécessairement, et il n’y a rien qui puisse empêcher l’accomplissement de sa volonté. [Cependant, Dieu ne veut que ce qui est possible; non pas tout ce qui est possible, mais seulement ce qui est demandé par sa sagesse[[fn:3068]]]. De même, l’œuvre absolument bonne que Dieu veut faire ne peut être arrêtée par aucun obstacle, et rien ne peut l’empêcher[[fn:3069]]. Telle est l’opinion de tout théologien et celle des philosophes, et telle est aussi la nôtre; car, bien que nous croyions que le monde a été [[t:12]]créé[[t:13]], tous nos docteurs et tous nos savants admettent que ce fait n’a pas eu lieu par la seule volonté de Dieu; mais ils disent que la sagesse divine, que nous sommes incapables de comprendre, a nécessité l’existence de cet univers entier, au moment où il arriva à l’existence, et que cette même sagesse invariable avait nécessité le néant avant que le monde existât.

**Hebrew (Makbili):** [Not Found]

### root.text.Part 3..24.5 (Main Text)
**English:** It must be known that the greater part of the false opinions which have brought such perplexity into the researches concerning the final cause, whether of the whole universe or of each of its parts, have no other source[[fn:3072]] than, on the one hand, the error wherein man was concerning himself, imagining that the whole universe existeth only for him, and, on the other hand, his ignorance both as touching the nature of this inferior matter and as touching the primary end of the Creator, which was to bring into existence all that whose existence was possible, existence being indubitably the good[[fn:3073]]. It is from this error and from the ignorance of these two things that doubts and perplexity arise, so that one imagineth that, among the actions of God, there be some that are frivolous, or idle, or vain. Know that those who have resigned themselves to this absurdity, so that for them the divine actions resemble idle actions which have absolutely no end, have sought thereby only to avoid making them depend upon a <i>wisdom</i>, fearing lest that should lead to professing <i>the eternity of the world</i>[[fn:3074]]; they have therefore closed the door to this opinion. But I have already informed thee what is in this regard the opinion of our Law, and that it is this opinion which must be admitted; for we say naught absurd in maintaining that the being and non-being of all these acts depend upon the divine wisdom, but that we are often ignorant how this wisdom manifesteth itself in the works of God. It is upon this opinion that the whole Law of Moses, our master, is based. It saith at the beginning: <i>And God saw every thing that He had made, and, behold, it was very good</i> (Genesis 1, 31), and it saith toward the end: <i>The Rock</i> (the Creator), <i>His work is perfect</i> (Deut. 32, 4.) Thou must well penetrate thyself with this. If thou examinest this opinion, as well as the philosophical opinion, by studying all the preceding chapters of this treatise which relate to this subject, thou shalt find that the two opinions differ in respect of none of the smallest details of the universe[[fn:3075]]. Thou shalt find only the single difference which we have already set forth[[fn:3076]], to wit, that, according to them (the philosophers), the world is eternal, whereas, according to us, it is created. Understand this well.
**Correction:** It must be known that the greater part of the false opinions which have brought such perplexity into the researches concerning the final cause, whether of the whole universe or of each of its parts, have no other source[[fn:3072]] than, on the one hand, the error wherein man was concerning himself, imagining that the whole universe existeth only for him, and, on the other hand, his ignorance both as touching the nature of this inferior matter and as touching the primary end of the Creator, which was to bring into existence all that whose existence was possible, existence being indubitably the good[[fn:3073]]. It is from this error and from the ignorance of these two things that doubts and perplexity arise, so that one imagineth that, among the actions of God, there be some that are frivolous, or idle, or vain. Know that those who have resigned themselves to this absurdity, so that for them the divine actions resemble idle actions which have absolutely no end, have sought thereby only to avoid making them depend upon a <i>wisdom</i>, fearing lest that should lead to professing <i>the eternity of the world</i>[[fn:3074]]; they have therefore closed the door to this opinion. But I have already informed thee what is in this regard the opinion of our Law, and that it is this opinion which must be admitted; for we say naught absurd in maintaining that the being and non-being of all these acts depend upon the divine wisdom, but that we are often ignorant how this wisdom manifesteth itself in the works of God. It is upon this opinion that the whole Law of Moses, our master, is based. It saith at the beginning: <i>And God saw every thing that He had made, and, behold, it was very good</i> (Genesis 1, 31), and it saith toward the end: <i>The Rock</i> (the Creator), <i>His work is perfect</i> (Deut. 32, 4.) Thou must well penetrate thyself with this. If thou examinest this opinion, as well as the philosophical opinion, by studying all the preceding chapters of this treatise which relate to this subject, thou shalt find that the two opinions differ in respect of none of the smallest details of the universe[[fn:3075]]. Thou shalt find only the single difference which we have already set forth[[fn:3076]], to wit, that, according to them (the philosophers), the world is eternal, whereas, according to us, it is created. Understand this well.

**French (Source):** Il faut savoir que la plupart des fausses opinions qui ont apporté tant de perplexité dans les recherches sur la cause finale, soit de l’ensemble de l’univers, soit de chacune de ses parties, n’ont d’autre source[[fn:3072]] que, d’une part, l’erreur dans laquelle était l’homme à l’égard de lui-même, s’imaginant que l’univers entier n’existe que pour lui, et, d’autre part, son ignorance tant à l’égard de la nature de cette matière inférieure qu’à l’égard du premier but du Créateur, qui était de faire exister tout ce dont l’existence était possible, l’existence étant indubitablement le bien[[fn:3073]]. C’est de cette erreur et de l’ignorance de ces deux choses que naissent les doutes et la perplexité, de sorte qu’on s’imagine que, parmi les actions de Dieu, il y en a de frivoles, ou d’oiseuses, ou de vaines. Sache que ceux qui se sont résignés à cette absurdité, de sorte que pour eux les actions divines ressemblent à des actions oiseuses qui n’ont absolument aucun but, ont voulu par là éviter seulement de les faire dépendre d’une [[t:0]]sagesse[[t:1]], craignant que cela ne conduisît à professer [[t:2]]l’éternité du monde[[t:3]][[fn:3074]]; ils ont donc fermé la porte à cette opinion. Mais je t’ai déjà fait savoir quelle est à cet égard l’opinion de notre loi, et que c’est cette opinion qu’il faut admettre; car nous ne disons rien d’absurde en soutenant que l’être et le non-être de tous ces actes dépendent de la sagesse divine, mais que nous ignorons souvent comment cette sagesse se manifeste dans les œuvres de Dieu. C’est sur cette opinion qu’est basée toute la Loi de Moïse, notre maître. Elle dit au commencement: [[t:4]]Et Dieu vit tout ce qu’il avait fait et c’était très-bien[[t:5]] (Genèse, 1, 31), et elle dit vers la fin: [[t:6]]Le rocher[[t:7]] (le Créateur), [[t:8]]son œuvre est parfaite[[t:9]] (Deutér., 32, 4.) Il faut te bien pénétrer de cela. Si tu examines cette opinion, ainsi que l’opinion philosophique, en étudiant tous les chapitres précédents de ce traité qui se rattachent à ce sujet, tu trouveras que les deux opinions ne diffèrent à l’égard d’aucun des moindres détails de l’univers[[fn:3075]]. Tu ne trouveras que la seule différence que nous avons déjà exposée[[fn:3076]], à savoir que, selon eux (les philosophes), le monde est éternel, tandis que, selon nous, il est créé. Comprends bien cela.

**Hebrew (Makbili):** <span class="mediumGrey">הכשלים בחיפוש תכלית העולם</span><br><sup>16</sup> דע שהשורש של רוב דמיונות השווא המביאים למבוכה בבקשת תכלית מציאות העולם בכללותו, או תכלית כל חלק מחלקיו, הוא רק <br><sup>טעות)</sup> טעות האדם בעצמו, ודמיונו שהמציאות כולה היא עבורו בלבד, <br><sup>אי ידיעה: א)</sup> ואי ידיעת טבע החומר השפל של העולם הזה, <br><sup>ב)</sup> ואי ידיעת המטרה הראשונה, שהיא ההבאה למציאות של כל מה שמציאותו אפשרית, כי המציאות טובה בלי ספק. בשל הטעות הזאת והאי ידיעה של שני העניינים האלה, נוצרים הספקות והמבוכה, עד שמדמיינים שחלק מפעולות האל הן משחק, וחלקן הבל, וחלקן שווא.<br><sup>17</sup> דע שאלה שהעמיסו על עצמם את האבסורד הזה, עד שלשיטתם נעשו פעולותיו יתעלה דומות לפעולות ההבל, שאין מכוונים בהן לשום תכלית שהיא – ברחו מלומר שהן נובעות מחכמה רק כדי שהדבר לא יוביל לטענה שהעולם קדום, ולפיכך הם חסמו את השער מלהגיע לכך.<br><span class="mediumGrey">שלמות המציאות וגבולות ידיעת האדם</span><br><sup>18</sup> וכבר הודעתיך (ב,יח11-10) את דעת תורתנו על כך, ושהיא מה שראוי להאמין בו. כי אין אבסורד בכך שאנו אומרים שמציאותן והעדרן של כל הפעולות האלה נובעים מחכמתו יתעלה, ואנחנו איננו יודעים רבים מאופני החכמה במעשיו. על דעה זו מבוססת כל תורת משה רבינו; בה נפתחה: "וַיַּרְא אֱלֹהִים אֶת כָּל אֲשֶׁר עָשָׂה וְהִנֵּה טוֹב מְאֹד" (בראשית א,לא), ובה נחתמה: "הַצּוּר תָּמִים פָּעֳלוֹ [כִּי כָל דְּרָכָיו מִשְׁפָּט, אֵל אֱמוּנָה וְאֵין עָוֶל צַדִּיק וְיָשָׁר הוּא]" (דברים לב,ד). דע זאת.<br><span class="mediumGrey">ההסכמה עם הפילוסופים</span><br><sup>19</sup> אם תתחקה אחר דעה זו והדעה הפילוסופית, תוך התבוננות בכלל הפרקים הקודמים בחיבור זה הקשורים בעניין זה, לא תמצא בין שתיהן מחלוקת בשום אופן באף דבר מכל פרטי המציאות, פרט למה שביארנו לגבי קדמות העולם לשיטתם וחידושו לשיטתנו (ב,יג, ועוד). הבן זאת.
