### root.text.Part 3..45.11 (Main Text)
**English:** The ordinances which particularly concern the paschal lamb, to wit, that it must be eaten only <i>roasted with fire</i>, in <i>one</i> house, and <i>without breaking a single bone thereof</i> (Exodus, xii, 8 and 46), have all an evident reason. Indeed, even as the unleavened bread is motivated by haste, so also the roasting had for its motive precipitancy[[fn:3664]]; for they had not leisure to make diverse dishes nor to prepare dainties. They would even have feared to tarry for the breaking of the bones and the taking out of the contents; for, to sum up all this[[fn:3665]], it is said: <i>and ye shall eat it in haste</i> (<i>ibid., v</i>. 11). Now, in haste, one cannot stop to break the bones, nor to send portions from one house to another and await the return of the messenger; for all such actions denote negligence and delay, whilst the object was to secure themselves by speed and precipitancy, lest any person should be late and miss the opportunity of departing with the multitude, so that harm might have befallen him by being taken unawares. These customs were thereafter perpetuated in commemoration of the event, as it is said: <i>Thou shalt therefore keep this ordinance in his season from year to year</i> (<i>ibid.</i>, xiii, 10). If it was said that "the paschal lamb could only be eaten by those who had been numbered to partake of it[[fn:3666]]," it was to inculcate the duty of purchasing it, and so that no one should rely upon a kinsman, a friend, or the first comer who might have offered it to him, lest he should have failed to provide for it beforehand. As for the prohibition against giving it to be eaten by the uncircumcised[[fn:3667]], the doctors have already explained it[[fn:3668]], saying that the Hebrews, during their long sojourn in Egypt, had neglected the commandment of circumcision, in order to assimilate themselves unto the Egyptians[[fn:3669]]. When, therefore, the paschal lamb was ordained and it was made a condition that no one should slaughter it save after having practiced circumcision upon himself, upon his children, and upon the men of his house, and that only then <i>he might come near and keep it</i> (<i>ibid.</i>, xii, 48), they all underwent circumcision. The multitude of the circumcised, say they, caused the blood of circumcision to be mingled with the blood of the paschal lamb, and it is to this that the prophet alludeth when he saith: <i>weltering in thy blood</i> (Ezekiel, xvi, 6), to wit, the blood of the paschal lamb and that of the circumcision[[fn:3670]].
**Correction:** The ordinances which particularly concern the paschal lamb, to wit, that it must be eaten only <i>roasted with fire</i>, in <i>one</i> house, and <i>without breaking a single bone thereof</i> (Exodus, xii, 8 and 46), have all an evident reason. Indeed, even as the unleavened bread is motivated by haste, so also the roasting had for its motive precipitancy[[fn:3664]]; for they had not leisure to make diverse dishes nor to prepare dainties. They would even have feared to tarry for the breaking of the bones and the taking out of the contents; for, to sum up all this[[fn:3665]], it is said: <i>and ye shall eat it in haste</i> (<i>ibid., v</i>. 11). Now, in haste, one cannot stop to break the bones, nor to send portions from one house to another and await the return of the messenger; for all such actions denote negligence and delay, whilst the object was to secure themselves by speed and precipitancy, lest any person should be late and miss the opportunity of departing with the multitude, so that harm might have befallen him by being taken unawares. These customs were thereafter perpetuated in commemoration of the event, as it is said: <i>Thou shalt therefore keep this ordinance in his season from year to year</i> (<i>ibid.</i>, xiii, 10). If it was said that "the paschal lamb could only be eaten by those who had been numbered to partake of it[[fn:3666]]," it was to inculcate the duty of purchasing it, and so that no one should rely upon a kinsman, a friend, or the first comer who might have offered it to him, lest he should have failed to provide for it beforehand. As for the prohibition against giving it to be eaten by the uncircumcised[[fn:3667]], the doctors have already explained it[[fn:3668]], saying that the Hebrews, during their long sojourn in Egypt, had neglected the commandment of circumcision, in order to assimilate themselves unto the Egyptians[[fn:3669]]. When, therefore, the paschal lamb was ordained and it was made a condition that no one should slaughter it save after having practiced circumcision upon himself, upon his children, and upon the men of his house, and that only then <i>he might come near and keep it</i> (<i>ibid.</i>, xii, 48), they all underwent circumcision. The multitude of the circumcised, say they, caused the blood of circumcision to be mingled with the blood of the paschal lamb, and it is to this that the prophet alludeth when he saith: <i>weltering in thy blood</i> (Ezekiel, xvi, 6), to wit, the blood of the paschal lamb and that of the circumcision[[fn:3670]].

**French (Source):** Les dispositions qui concernent particulièrement l’agneau pascal, à savoir qu’on ne doit le manger que [[t:0]]rôti au feu[[t:1]], dans une [[t:2]]même[[t:3]] maison et [[t:4]]sans en rompre un seul os[[t:5]] (Exode, 12, 8 et 46), ont toutes une raison évidente. En effet, de même que le pain azyme est motivé par la précipitation, de même le rôti avait pour motif la précipitation[[fn:3664]]; car on n’avait pas le temps de faire différents plats et d’apprêter des mets. On aurait même craint de s’arrêter à rompre les os et à en prendre le contenu; car, pour résumer tout cela[[fn:3665]], il est dit: [[t:6]]et vous le mangerez avec précipitation[[t:7]] ([[t:8]]ibid., v[[t:9]]. 12). Or, dans la précipitation, on ne saurait s’arrêter à en rompre les os, ni à en envoyer d’une maison à une autre et à attendre le retour du messager; car toutes ces actions dénotent la négligence et le retard, tandis qu’on avait pour but de se garantir par la hâte et la précipitation, afin que personne ne fût en retard et ne manquât l’occasion de partir avec la foule, de sorte qu’on aurait pu lui faire du mal en le surprenant. Ces usages se sont ensuite perpétués en commémoration de l’événement, comme il est dit: [[t:10]]Tu observeras cette institution au temps fixé, d’année en année[[t:11]] ([[t:12]]ibid.[[t:13]], XIII, 10). S’il a été dit que «l’agneau pascal ne pourrait être mangé que par ceux qui auraient été comptés pour y participer[[fn:3666]]», c’était pour inculquer le devoir de l’acheter, et afin que personne ne comptât sur un parent, sur un ami, ou sur le premier venu qui aurait pu le lui offrir, de sorte qu’il n’en eût pas pris soin d’avance. Quant à la défense d’en donner à manger aux incirconcis[[fn:3667]], les docteurs déjà l’ont expliquée[[fn:3668]], en disant que les Hébreux, pendant leur long séjour en Égypte, avaient négligé le commandement de la circoncision, afin de s’assimiler aux Égyptiens[[fn:3669]]. Lors donc que l’agneau pascal fut ordonné et qu’on y mit pour condition que personne ne l’immolerait qu’après avoir pratiqué la circoncision sur lui, sur ses enfants et sur les gens de sa maison, et qu’alors seulement [[t:14]]il pourrait s’approcher pour le faire[[t:15]] ([[t:16]]ibid.[[t:17]], XII, 48), ils se firent tous circoncire. La multitude des circoncis, disent-ils, fit que le sang de la circoncision se mêla au sang de l’agneau pascal, et c’est à cela que le prophète fait allusion en disant: [[t:18]]trempée dans ton sang[[t:19]] (Ézéchiel, XVI, 6), à savoir le sang de l’agneau pascal et celui de la circoncision[[fn:3670]].

**Hebrew (Makbili):** <span class="mediumGrey">דיני קרבן פסח: זיכרון החיפזון, הכנה מראש והדגשת הברית</span><br><sup>12</sup> <sup>זיכרון החיפזון)</sup> אשר לדינים המיוחדים לפסח, והם <br><sup>א)</sup> שעליו להיאכל "צְלִי אֵשׁ" (שמות יב,ח-ט) דווקא, <br><sup>ב)</sup> "בְּבַיִת אֶחָד [...] <br><sup>ג)</sup> וְעֶצֶם לֹא תִשְׁבְּרוּ בוֹ" (שם,מו) – לכולם טעם ברור. כי כמו שהמצה היא בשל החיפזון, כך <br><sup>א)</sup> הצלייה היא בשל החיפזון, ואין שהות לעשות מיני מאכלים ולתקן מזונות, <br><sup>ג)</sup> ואסור אפילו להשתהות כדי לשבור את עצמותיו ולהוציא את מה שבתוכו, כי כבר צוין עיקר העניין בכל זה, והוא שנאמר: "וַאֲכַלְתֶּם אֹתוֹ בְּחִפָּזוֹן" (שם,יא). בשל החיפזון אין פנאי להתמהמה כדי לשבור עצמות <br><sup>ב)</sup> ולא להוציא ממנו מבית לבית ולחכות לשליח עד שישוב, וכל אלה מעשי עצלות והתמהמהות. המטרה היתה להכריח חיפזון ומהירות, כדי שאף אחד לא יתעכב ויחמיץ את היציאה עם כלל האנשים כך שניתן יהיה להזיק לו ולהורגו. נסיבות אלה הונצחו לעד, לזיכרון כיצד היה הדבר, כמו שנאמר: "וְשָׁמַרְתָּ אֶת הַחֻקָּה הַזֹּאת לְמוֹעֲדָהּ [מִיָּמִים יָמִימָה]" (שם יג,י).<br><sup>13</sup> <sup>בני חבורה)</sup> אשר לכך שהוא אינו נאכל אלא למנוייו – זאת כדי לזרז לרוכשו, ושלא יסמוך אדם על אחד מקרוביו או חבר או מי שיזדמן לו לפגוש, ובשל כך לא ידאג לו מלכתחילה. <br><sup>ערל)</sup> אשר לאיסורו על הערלים – דבר זה ביארו החכמים ז"ל, כי הם אמרו שמשֶאָרכה שהותם במצרים הם ביטלו את מצות מילה, כדי להידמות למצרִים (שמות רבה א,ח). וכאשר ציווה על הפסח והתנה בו שלא יישחט אלא לאחר מילת עצמו ובניו ובני ביתו ואז יִקְרַב לַעֲשֹׂתוֹ (שמות יב,מח) – נימולו כולם, והיה דם מילה מתערב בדם הפסח בשל רוב הנימולים כמו שאמרו (חז"ל), ולכך רמז באומרו: "מִתְבּוֹסֶסֶת בְּדָמָיִךְ" (יחזקאל טז,ו) – דם פסח ודם מילה (פסיקתא דרב כהנא, ויהי בחצי הלילה, ד; פרקי דרבי אליעזר, כט ).

---

### root.text.Part 3..48.9 (Main Text)
**English:** I must here point out to thee the great equity of these <i>righteous statutes and judgments</i> (Deut. iv. 8), in the judgment pronounced against the slanderer of his wife[[fn:3822]]. Without doubt, this wicked man loved not his wife whom he hath slandered, and found her unpleasing: if, therefore, he had desired to divorce her according to the manner of all those who repudiate their wives, naught would have prevented him; but, had he divorced her[[fn:3823]], he would have been obliged to pay her what was her rightful due. He therefore brought an <i>evil name</i> upon her, in order to rid himself of her without payment; he calumniated and slandered her with lies, in order to withhold from her the sum which she had the right to demand of him and which is fifty shekels of silver[[fn:3824]], for that is the <i>mohar</i> of virgins, fixed in the Law. Wherefore God hath condemned him to pay <i>an hundred shekels of silver</i> (<i>ibid.</i>, xxii. 19)[[fn:3825]], according to this principle: <i>Whom the judges shall condemn, he shall pay double unto his neighbour</i> (Exod. xxii. 8), and in conformity with the judgment of false witnesses, as we have already set forth[[fn:3826]]. It is the same with this slanderer, who, having desired to make her lose the fifty shekels which are due to her from him[[fn:3827]], is condemned to pay an hundred. Such is his punishment for having wished to withhold the indemnity imposed upon him and having sought to seize it; but his punishment for having assailed the honour of the woman and having accused her of fornication consisted in being himself dishonoured by scourging: <i>and they shall chastise him</i> (Deut. xxii. 18). Finally, his punishment for having obeyed his concupiscence and having sought only pleasure alone consisted in remaining perpetually bound to her: <i>He may not put her away all his days</i> (<i>ibid.</i>, <i>v.</i> 19); for the cause of all that occurred was that he found her unpleasing.
**Correction:** I must here point out to thee the great equity of these <i>righteous statutes and judgments</i> (Deut. iv. 8), in the judgment pronounced against the slanderer of his wife[[fn:3822]]. Without doubt, this wicked man loved not his wife whom he hath slandered, and found her unpleasing: if, therefore, he had desired to divorce her according to the manner of all those who repudiate their wives, naught would have prevented him; but, had he divorced her[[fn:3823]], he would have been obliged to pay her what was her rightful due. He therefore brought an <i>evil name</i> upon her, in order to rid himself of her without payment; he calumniated and slandered her with lies, in order to withhold from her the sum which she had the right to demand of him and which is fifty shekels of silver[[fn:3824]], for that is the <i>mohar</i> of virgins, fixed in the Law. Wherefore God hath condemned him to pay <i>an hundred shekels of silver</i> (<i>ibid.</i>, xxii. 19)[[fn:3825]], according to this principle: <i>Whom the judges shall condemn, he shall pay double unto his neighbour</i> (Exod. xxii. 8), and in conformity with the judgment of false witnesses, as we have already set forth[[fn:3826]]. It is the same with this slanderer, who, having desired to make her lose the fifty shekels which are due to her from him[[fn:3827]], is condemned to pay an hundred. Such is his punishment for having wished to withhold the indemnity imposed upon him and having sought to seize it; but his punishment for having assailed the honour of the woman and having accused her of fornication consisted in being himself dishonoured by scourging: <i>and they shall chastise him</i> (Deut. xxii. 18). Finally, his punishment for having obeyed his concupiscence and having sought only pleasure alone consisted in remaining perpetually bound to her: <i>He may not put her away all his days</i> (<i>ibid.</i>, <i>v.</i> 19); for the cause of all that occurred was that he found her unpleasing.

**French (Source):** Je dois te faire remarquer ici la grande équité de ces [[t:0]]statuts et ordonnances justes[[t:1]] (Deut., IV, 8), dans le jugement prononcé contre le diffamateur de sa femme[[fn:3822]]. Sans doute, cet homme méchant n’aimait pas sa femme qu’il a diffamée, et la trouvait laide: si donc il avait voulu divorcer avec elle selon la manière de tous ceux qui répudient leur femme, rien ne l’en aurait empêché; mais, s’il avait divorcé[[fn:3823]], il aurait été obligé de lui payer ce qui lui revenait de droit. Il tenait donc [[t:2]]de mauvais propos[[t:3]] sur son compte, afin de s’en débarrasser sans payement; il la calomniait et la diffamait par des mensonges, afin de lui retenir la somme qu’elle avait le droit d’exiger de lui et qui est de cinquante sicles d’argent[[fn:3824]], car c’est là le [[t:4]]mohar[[t:5]] des vierges, fixé dans la Loi. C’est pourquoi Dieu l’a condamné à payer [[t:6]]cent sicles d’argent[[t:7]] ([[t:8]]ibid.[[t:9]], XXII, 19)[[fn:3825]], suivant ce principe: [[t:10]]Celui que les juges condamneront payera le double à son prochain[[t:11]] (Exode, 22, 8), et conformément au jugement des faux témoins, comme nous l’avons déjà exposé[[fn:3826]]. Il en est de même de ce diffamateur, lequel, ayant voulu lui faire perdre les cinquante sicles qui lui sont dus de sa part[[fn:3827]], est condamné à en payer cent. Telle est sa punition pour avoir voulu retenir l’indemnité qui lui est imposée et avoir cherché à s’en emparer; mais son châtiment pour avoir porté atteinte à l’honneur de la femme et l’avoir accusée de fornication consistait à se voir lui-même déshonoré par des coups de fouet: [[t:12]]et ils le châtieront[[t:13]] (Deut., XXII, 18). Enfin, son châtiment pour avoir obéi à sa concupiscence et n’avoir cherché que la seule volupté consistait à rester perpétuellement enchaîné à elle: [[t:14]]Il ne pourra la renvoyer tant qu’il vivra[[t:15]] ([[t:16]]ibid.[[t:17]], [[t:18]]v.[[t:19]] 19); car la cause de tout ce qui est arrivé, c’est qu’il la trouvait laide. 

**Hebrew (Makbili):** <span class="mediumGrey">מוציא שם רע: חוקי צדק של מידה כנגד מידה</span><br><sup>12</sup> התבונן נא בגודל הצדק של חֻקִּים וּמִשְׁפָּטִים צַדִּיקִם (דברים ד,ח) אלה, כיצד דנו במוציא שם רע. <br><sup>העבירה והמניע)</sup> כי אין ספק שאשתו של אותו רשע שהוציא עליה שם רע לא מצאה חן בעיניו והוא מאס בה, ואילו רצה לגרשה כדין כל המגרש את אשתו לא היתה לו מניעה מכך, אבל אילו גירשה – היה חייב לפרוע לה את המגיע לה. לכן שם לה עֲלִילֹת דְּבָרִים (שם כב,יד;יז) כדי להיפטר ממנה בלי תשלומין, והוציא עליה דיבה ולשון הרע שקרי כדי להחזיק אצלו את מה שמוטל עליו לתת לה, והוא חֲמִשִּׁים כָּסֶף (שם,כט), כי זהו מֹהַר הַבְּתוּלֹת (שמות כב,טז) הקבוע בתורה. <br><sup>תגובת התורה א)</sup> לכן גזר עליו יתעלה לשלם מֵאָה כֶסֶף (דברים כב,יט), בהתאם לעיקרון של "אֲשֶׁר יַרְשִׁיעֻן אֱלֹהִים יְשַׁלֵּם שְׁנַיִם לְרֵעֵהוּ" (שמות כב,ח), וכדין עדים זוממין, כמו שביארנו (ג:לה6;מא6). כך מוציא שם רע זה זמם לגרום לה להפסד של החמישים המוטלים עליו לתת לה, וישלם מאה. זהו העונש על כך שהחזיק אצלו את החוב המוטל עליו וזמם לקחתו. <br><sup>ב)</sup> והעונש על כך שפגע בשמה הטוב והשמיץ אותה שניאפה – שייפגע שמו הטוב במלקות בשוט: "וְיִסְּרוּ אֹתוֹ" (דברים כב,יח). <br><sup>ג)</sup> והעונש על כך שביכר את התאווה וביקש הנאה גרידא – שיהיה מחויב לה תמיד: "לֹא יוּכַל לְשַׁלְּחָהּ כָּל יָמָיו" (שם,יט), כי מה שהביא אותו לכל מה שעשה אינו אלא שהוא מאס בה.

---

### root.text.Part 3..49.0 (Main Text)
**English:** There are yet other matters which belong to the <i>mysteries of the Law</i>, and which, having perplexed many persons, stand in need of explanation. These are certain narratives related in the Pentateuch, wherein no utility is apparent, such as, for instance, when there are enumerated the peoples descended from Noah, their names and the places of their habitations (Genesis, ch. X), <i>the Horites, descendants of Seir</i>, as also <i>the kings that reigned in the land of Edom</i> (<i>Ibid.</i>, 36, 20, 31 et seq.), and other like accounts. The Doctors say, as thou knowest, that the impious Manasseh did naught but continually occupy[[fn:3884]] his ignoble council with the criticism of these passages: “He sat there,” say they, “interpreting the <i>Haggadôth</i>[[fn:3885]] in a mocking spirit; he would say, for example: Had Moses need to write, ‘<i>And Lotan's sister was Timna</i>’ (<i>Ibid., v.</i> 22)?” I shall first make known unto thee a general principle, and thereafter I shall return to the details, even as I did in assigning motives for the commandments.
**Correction:** There are yet other matters which belong to the <i>mysteries of the Law</i>, and which, having perplexed many persons, stand in need of explanation. These are certain narratives related in the Pentateuch, wherein no utility is apparent, such as, for instance, when there are enumerated the peoples descended from Noah, their names and the places of their habitations (Genesis, ch. X), <i>the Horites, descendants of Seir</i>, as also <i>the kings that reigned in the land of Edom</i> (<i>Ibid.</i>, 36, 20, 31 et seq.), and other like accounts. The Doctors say, as thou knowest, that the impious Manasseh did naught but continually occupy[[fn:3884]] his ignoble council with the criticism of these passages: “He sat there,” say they, “interpreting the <i>Haggadôth</i>[[fn:3885]] in a mocking spirit; he would say, for example: Had Moses need to write, ‘<i>And Lotan's sister was Timna</i>’ (<i>Ibid., v.</i> 22)?” I shall first make known unto thee a general principle, and thereafter I shall return to the details, even as I did in assigning motives for the commandments.

**French (Source):** Il y a encore d’autres choses qui font partie des [[t:0]]mystères de la Loi[[t:1]], et qui, ayant embarrassé beaucoup de personnes, ont besoin d’explication. Ce sont certains récits rapportés dans le Pentateuque, et dans lesquels on ne voit aucune utilité, comme, par exemple, lorsqu’on énumère les peuples descendus de Noé, leurs noms et les lieux de leurs habitations (Genèse, chap. X), [[t:2]]les ’Horéens, descendants de Séir[[t:3]], ainsi que [[t:4]]les rois qui régnèrent dans le pays d’Édom[[t:5]] ([[t:6]]Ibid.[[t:7]], 36, 20, 31 et suiv.), et d’autres récits semblables. Les docteurs disent, comme tu le sais, que l’impie Manassé ne faisait qu’occuper sans cesse[[fn:3884]] son ignoble conseil de la critique de ces passages: «Il y était assis, disent-ils, faisant l’exégète en parodiant les [[t:8]]Haggadôth[[t:9]][[fn:3885]]; il disait, par exemple: Moïse avait-il besoin d’écrire: [[t:10]]la sœur de Lotan fut Timna[[t:11]]’ ([[t:12]]Ibid., v.[[t:13]] 22)?» Je vais d’abord te faire connaître un principe général, ensuite je reviendrai aux détails, comme je l’ai fait en motivant les commandements. 

**Hebrew (Makbili):** <b>הטעמים לסיפורי התורה הנראים חסרי תועלת</b><br><span class="mediumGrey">סיפורי התורה הנראים חסרי תועלת</span><br><sup>1</sup> יש גם דברים שהם מסתרי תורה שרבים נכשלו בהם וראוי לבארם. והם אותם סיפורים המסופרים בתורה שחושבים שאין תועלת בהזכרתם, <br><sup>דוגמאות)</sup> כגון תיאור הסתעפות העממים מנח ושמותם ומקומותיהם (בראשית י), וכן בְּנֵי שֵׂעִיר הַחֹרִי (שם לו,כ-ל), ותיאור הַמְּלָכִים אֲשֶׁר מָלְכוּ בְּאֶרֶץ אֱדוֹם (שם,לא-לט) וכיוצא בזה. <br><sup>הביקורת)</sup> ידועים לך דבריהם (של חז"ל) שמנשה הרשע היה ממלא את מושבו השפל בביקורת על המקומות האלה דווקא. הם אמרו: "היה יושב ודורש בהגדות שלדופי. היה אומר: לא היה לו למשה לכתוב אלא 'וַאֲחוֹת לוֹטָן תִּמְנָע' (בראשית לו,כב)" וכו' (בבלי סנהדרין צט,ב).<br><span class="mediumGrey">הכלל: הסיפורים בתורה באים לאמת דעות או לתקן מעשים</span><br><sup>2</sup> אני אודיעך כלל, ולאחר מכן אחזור לפרט אותו, כמו שעשיתי בהטעמת המצוות.

---

### root.text.Part 3..49.6 (Main Text)
**English:** In sum, that which I have told thee concerning the interval which now separateth us from the religious practices of the Sabeans[[fn:3899]] may also be applied to the history of those times which is now unknown to us; for, if we knew it, and if the events which then occurred were manifest to us, we should understand, in respect of many details, the ground which caused them to be mentioned in the Pentateuch. 
**Correction:** In sum, that which I have told thee concerning the interval which now separateth us from the religious practices of the Sabeans[[fn:3899]] may also be applied to the history of those times which is now unknown to us; for, if we knew it, and if the events which then occurred were manifest to us, we should understand, in respect of many details, the ground which caused them to be mentioned in the Pentateuch.

**French (Source):** En somme, ce que je t’ai dit de l’intervalle qui nous sépare aujourd’hui des usages religieux des Sabiens[[fn:3899]] peut aussi s’appliquer à l’histoire de ces temps qui nous est aujourd’hui inconnue; car, si nous la connaissions et si les événements arrivés dans ces temps-là nous étaient connus, nous comprendrions, à l’égard de nombreux détails, le motif qui les a fait mentionner dans le Pentateuque. 

**Hebrew (Makbili):** <span class="mediumGrey">הקושי בטעמי סיפורי המקרא – אי הכרת הרקע</span><br><sup>8</sup> כללו של דבר: כמו שציינתי לך (ג,מט32) את ריחוקן של דרכי הצאבים מאיתנו היום, כך תולדות הימים ההם נסתרות מאיתנו היום; ואילו ידענו אותן, וידענו את המאורעות שאירעו באותם ימים – היינו מבינים בפירוט את הטעם של דברים רבים שנזכרו.

---

### root.text.Part 3..49.7 (Main Text)
**English:** It must further be well understood that written narratives are not regarded in the same light as the events of which one is a witness; for in the latter, there are certain details having necessary consequences which cannot be related without prolixity[[fn:3900]]. Wherefore, in reading these narratives, the reader findeth therein prolixity or repetitions; but had he been a witness of that which is related, he would understand that what hath been said is necessary. Whensoever, therefore, thou seest narratives in the non-legislative portion of the Pentateuch, it seemeth to thee at times that such or such a narrative had no need to be written, or that it containeth either tedious lengths or repetitions[[fn:3901]]; the reason thereof is that thou hast not been a witness of the details which led the author to frame his narrative as he hath done.
**Correction:** It must further be well understood that written narratives are not regarded in the same light as the events of which one is a witness; for in the latter, there are certain details having necessary consequences which cannot be related without prolixity[[fn:3900]]. Wherefore, in reading these narratives, the reader findeth therein prolixity or repetitions; but had he been a witness of that which is related, he would understand that what hath been said is necessary. Whensoever, therefore, thou seest narratives in the non-legislative portion of the Pentateuch, it seemeth to thee at times that such or such a narrative had no need to be written, or that it containeth either tedious lengths or repetitions[[fn:3901]]; the reason thereof is that thou hast not been a witness of the details which led the author to frame his narrative as he hath done.

**French (Source):** Ce qu’il faut encore bien comprendre, c’est qu’on ne considère pas les relations écrites, au même point de vue que les événements dont on est témoin; car, dans ces derniers, il y a certains détails ayant des suites nécessaires qu’on ne peut rapporter sans prolixité[[fn:3900]]. C’est pourquoi, en lisant ces relations, le lecteur y trouve de la prolixité ou des répétitions; mais s’il avait été témoin de ce qui est raconté, il comprendrait que ce qui a été dit est nécessaire. Quand donc tu vois des relations dans la partie non législative du Pentateuque, il te semble quelquefois que telle ou telle relation n’avait pas besoin d’être écrite, ou qu’elle renferme soit des longueurs, soit des répétitions[[fn:3901]]; la raison en est que tu n’as pas été témoin des détails qui ont amené (l’auteur) à faire sa relation telle qu’il l’a faite.

**Hebrew (Makbili):** <sup>הסבר)</sup> ראוי שתבין שבחינת הדברים הכתובים אינה כבחינת המצבים הנראים לעין. כי במצבים הנראים לעין יש פרטים, שנובעות מהם תוצאות הכרחיות גדולות, שאין אפשרות להזכירם או שהדבר יארך. כשמתבוננים באותם סיפורים, חושב האדם המתבונן שיש בסיפורים האלה אריכות או כפילויות; ואילו היה רואה את מה שסופר היה מבין את ההכרח במה שנאמר. לכן כשאתה רואה בתורה סיפור שאינו במסגרת המצוות, אתה חושב שזהו סיפור שלא היה הכרח לתארו, או שיש בו אריכות או כפילויות, בעוד הטעם לזה הוא שלא ראית את הפרטים שהביאו להזכיר מה שנזכר כפי שנזכר.

---

### root.text.Part 3..50.9.sub_0 (Main Text)
**English:** we return to the purpose of this chapter, the object whereof is to encourage man to occupy his thought with God alone, after he hath learned to know Him, as we have set forth. This is the true worship which befitteth those who have perceived the transcendent truths; the more they meditate upon God and abide with Him, the more doth He become the object of their worship. As for those who meditate upon God and who speak much of Him without possessing science, but rather attaching themselves to a mere being of their imagination, or to a belief which they have received by tradition, these, I say, finding themselves[[fn:3918]] without the palace and far removed from it, do not really think of God nor meditate upon Him. Verily, that being who existeth only in their imagination and of whom their mouth speaketh respondeth to naught that is real and is but an invention of their fancy, as we have set forth when speaking of the <i>attributes</i>[[fn:3919]]. One must not give oneself unto this species of worship save after having conceived (the idea of <i>God</i>) by means of the intellect; it is only after having understood God and His works, as far as the intelligence requireth[[fn:3920]], that thou canst entirely consecrate thyself unto Him, seek to draw nigh unto Him and strengthen the bond which existeth between thee and Him, to wit, the intellect[[fn:3921]], even as it is said: <i>Unto thee it was shewn to know that the Lord, etc.</i> (Deuter., 4, 35); <i>know therefore this day and consider it in thine heart, etc.</i> (<i>ibid., v.</i> 39); <i>know ye that the Lord He is God</i> (Ps. 100, 3). Already the Pentateuch setteth forth that this supreme worship, whereon we call attention in this chapter, can only take place following upon perception: <i>To love the Lord your God</i>, it is said, <i>and to serve Him with all your heart and with all your soul</i> (Deuter., 11, 13). We have already set forth several times[[fn:3922]] that <i>love</i> (of God) is in proportion to perception; it is only following upon love that there can come this worship whereon the Doctors also have called attention, saying: «c’est le culte du cœur[[fn:3923]].» This consisteth, in my view, in applying the thought to the principal object of the intellect[[fn:3924]] and in devoting oneself unto Him as much as one is able.
**Correction:** we return to the purpose of this chapter, the object whereof is to encourage man to occupy his thought with God alone, after he hath learned to know Him, as we have set forth. This is the true worship which befitteth those who have perceived the transcendent truths; the more they meditate upon God and abide with Him, the more doth He become the object of their worship. As for those who meditate upon God and who speak much of Him without possessing science, but rather attaching themselves to a mere being of their imagination, or to a belief which they have received by tradition, these, I say, finding themselves[[fn:3918]] without the palace and far removed from it, do not really think of God nor meditate upon Him. Verily, that being who existeth only in their imagination and of whom their mouth speaketh respondeth to naught that is real and is but an invention of their fancy, as we have set forth when speaking of the <i>attributes</i>[[fn:3919]]. One must not give oneself unto this species of worship save after having conceived (the idea of <i>God</i>) by means of the intellect; it is only after having understood God and His works, as far as the intelligence requireth[[fn:3920]], that thou canst entirely consecrate thyself unto Him, seek to draw nigh unto Him and strengthen the bond which existeth between thee and Him, to wit, the intellect[[fn:3921]], even as it is said: <i>Unto thee it was shewn to know that the Lord, etc.</i> (Deuter., 4, 35); <i>know therefore this day and consider it in thine heart, etc.</i> (<i>ibid., v.</i> 39); <i>know ye that the Lord He is God</i> (Ps. 100, 3). Already the Pentateuch setteth forth that this supreme worship, whereon we call attention in this chapter, can only take place following upon perception: <i>To love the Lord your God</i>, it is said, <i>and to serve Him with all your heart and with all your soul</i> (Deuter., 11, 13). We have already set forth several times[[fn:3922]] that <i>love</i> (of God) is in proportion to perception; it is only following upon love that there can come this worship whereon the Doctors also have called attention, saying: «c’est le culte du cœur[[fn:3923]].» This consisteth, in my view, in applying the thought to the principal object of the intellect[[fn:3924]] and in devoting oneself unto Him as much as one is able.

**French (Source):** nous revenons au but de ce chapitre, qui a pour objet d’encourager l’homme à n’occuper sa pensée que de Dieu seul, après avoir appris à le connaître, comme nous l’avons exposé. C’est là le vrai culte qui convient à ceux qui ont perçu les vérités transcendantes; plus ils méditent sur Dieu et s’arrêtent auprès de lui, et plus il devient l’objet de leur culte. Quant à ceux qui méditent sur Dieu et qui en parlent beaucoup sans posséder la science, ne s’attachant au contraire qu’à un simple être de leur imagination, ou à une croyance qu’ils ont reçue par tradition, ceux-là, dis-je, se trouvant[[fn:3918]] en dehors du palais et éloignés de lui, ne pensent pas réellement à Dieu et ne méditent pas sur lui. En effet, cet être qui n’existe que dans leur imagination et dont parle leur bouche ne répond absolument à rien de réel et n’est qu’une invention de leur imagination, comme nous l’avons exposé en parlant des [[t:0]]attributs[[t:1]][[fn:3919]]. Il ne faut se livrer à cette espèce de culte qu’après avoir conçu (l’idée de [[t:2]]Dieu[[t:3]]) au moyen de l’intellect; ce n’est qu’après avoir compris Dieu et ses œuvres, autant que l’exige[[fn:3920]] l’intelligence, que tu peux entièrement te consacrer à lui, chercher à te rapprocher de lui et affermir le lien qui existe entre toi et lui, à savoir l’intellect[[fn:3921]], comme il est dit: [[t:4]]On t’a montré à connaître que l’Éternel, etc.[[t:5]] (Deutér., 4, 35); [[t:6]]tu sauras aujourd’hui et tu rappelleras à ton cœur, etc.[[t:7]] ([[t:8]]ibid., v.[[t:9]] 39); [[t:10]]sachez que l’Éternel seul est Dieu[[t:11]] (Ps. 100, 3). Déjà le Pentateuque expose que ce culte suprême, sur lequel nous appelons l’attention dans ce chapitre, ne peut avoir lieu qu’à la suite de la perception: [[t:12]]Pour aimer l’Éternel votre Dieu[[t:13]], est-il dit, [[t:14]]et pour le servir de tout votre cœur et de toute votre âme[[t:15]] (Deutér., 11, 13). Nous avons déjà exposé plusieurs fois[[fn:3922]] que l’[[t:16]]amour[[t:17]] (de Dieu) est en raison de la perception; ce n’est qu’à la suite de l’amour que peut venir ce culte sur lequel les docteurs aussi ont appelé l’attention, en disant: «c’est le culte du cœur[[fn:3923]].» Celui-ci consiste, selon moi, à appliquer la pensée à l’objet principal de l’intellect[[fn:3924]] et à se consacrer à lui autant qu’on le peut.

**Hebrew (Makbili):** [Not Found]

### fn.4 (Footnote)
**English:** The <i>Makâmât</i> among the Arabs are tales or narratives written in rhymed prose mingled with verse; one is acquainted with the celebrated <i>Makâmât</i> or <i>Sessions</i> of Harizi. This species of composition was imitated in Hebrew by the Jews as early as the first half of the XII<sup>th</sup> century, and they were given the name of <span dir="rtl">מחברות</span>; it is indeed this word which R. Yehuda al-Harizi employeth here in his translation, and which rendereth the sense of the Arabic word far better than the word <span dir="rtl">חרוזיך</span> used by Ibn Tibbon. Al-Harizi, in his collection of Makâmât known under the title of <i>Taḥkemoni</i> (<span dir="rtl">תחכמוני</span>), himself citeth from our Joseph a <i>Makâma</i> which had become celebrated. Vid. my aforementioned Notice, p. 48 et seq.
**Correction:** The <i>Makâmât</i> among the Arabs are tales or narratives written in rhymed prose mingled with verse; one is acquainted with the celebrated <i>Makâmât</i> or <i>Sessions</i> of Harizi. This species of composition was imitated in Hebrew by the Jews as early as the first half of the XII<sup>th</sup> century, and they were given the name of <span dir="rtl">מחברות</span>; it is indeed this word which R. Yehuda al-Harizi employeth here in his translation, and which rendereth the sense of the Arabic word far better than the word <span dir="rtl">חרוזיך</span> used by Ibn Tibbon. Al-Harizi, in his collection of Makâmât known under the title of <i>Taḥkemoni</i> (<span dir="rtl">תחכמוני</span>), himself citeth from our Joseph a <i>Makâma</i> which had become celebrated. Vid. my aforementioned Notice, p. 48 et seq.

**French (Source):** Les [[t:0]]Makâmât[[t:1]] chez les Arabes sont des nouvelles ou récits écrits en prose rimée mêlée de vers; on connaît les célèbres [[t:2]]Makâmât[[t:3]] ou [[t:4]]Séances[[t:5]] de’Harizi. Ce genre de compositions fut imité en hébreu, par les juifs, dès la première moitié du XII[[t:6]]e[[t:7]] siècle, et on leur donnait le nom de [[t:8]]מחברות[[t:9]]; c’est en effet ce mot que R. Iehouda al-’Harizi emploie ici dans sa traduction, et qui rend beaucoup mieux le sens du mot arabe que le mot [[t:10]]חרוזיך[[t:11]], employé par Ibn-Tibbon. Al-’Harizi, dans son recueil de Makâmât connu sous le titre de [[t:12]]Ta’hkemôni[[t:13]] ([[t:14]]תחכמוני[[t:15]]), cite luimême de notre Joseph une [[t:16]]Makâma[[t:17]] qui était devenue célèbre. Voy. ma Notice précitée, p. 48 et suiv.

### fn.6 (Footnote)
**English:** This name designateth the religious philosophers or the scholastics of the Arabs. Touching its origin, vid. my Notice upon Rabbi Saadia Gaon, p. 16 et seq. (Bible of M. Cahen, vol. IX, p. 88 et seq.). The author himself giveth numerous details upon the <i>Motekallemin</i> in several places of this work, and notably in the I<sup>st</sup> part, ch. 71, 73 et seq. I have given a succinct overview of their doctrines in the <i>Dictionnaire des sciences philosophiques</i>, art. <i>Arabes</i>.

IX, p. 88 et seq.). The author himself giveth numerous details upon the <i>Motekallemin</i> in several places of this work, and notably in the I<sup>st</sup> part, ch. 71, 73 et seq. I have given a succinct overview of their doctrines in the <i>Dictionnaire des sciences philosophiques</i>, art. <i>Arabes</i>.

**French (Source):** Ce nom désigne les philosophes religieux ou les scolastiques des Arabes. Sur son origine voyez ma Notice sur Rabbi Saadia Gaon, p. 16 et suiv. (Bible de M. Cahen, t. IX, p. 88 et suiv.). L’auteur donne luimême de nombreux détails sur les [[t:0]]Motécallemîn[[t:1]] dans plusieurs endroits de cet ouvrage, et notamment I[[t:2]]re[[t:3]] partie, ch. 71, 73 et suiv. J’ai donné un aperçu succinct de leurs doctrines dans le [[t:4]]Dictionnaire des sciences philosophiques[[t:5]], art. [[t:6]]Arabes[[t:7]].

### fn.33 (Footnote)
**English:** That is to say, in other words, that, on the one hand, one doth not consider as an allegory that which is one, and that, on the other hand, one considereth as an allegory that which ought to be taken in its literal sense. The commentary of <i>Ephodi</i> citeth for example, on the one hand, the image of the adulterous woman (Prov., ch. VII), which, according to our author, designateth matter, and which many commentators take in the literal sense, and, on the other hand, the narrative relating to Bathsheba (II. Sam., ch. XI, <small>v</small>. 2 et seq.), which evidently must be taken in its literal sense, and wherein certain commentators have seen an allegory.
**Correction:** That is to say, in other words, that, on the one hand, one doth not consider as an allegory that which is one, and that, on the other hand, one considereth as an allegory that which ought to be taken in its literal sense. The commentary of <i>Ephodi</i> citeth for example, on the one hand, the image of the adulterous woman (Prov., ch. VII), which, according to our author, designateth matter, and which many commentators take in the literal sense, and, on the other hand, the narrative relating to Bathsheba (II. Sam., ch. XI, <small>v</small>. 2 et seq.), which evidently must be taken in its literal sense, and wherein certain commentators have seen an allegory.

**French (Source):** C’est-à-dire, en d’autres termes, que, d’une part, on ne considère pas comme une allégorie ce qui en est une, et que, d’autre part, on considère comme une allégorie ce qui doit être pris dans son sens littéral. Le commentaire [[t:0]]Éphodi[[t:1]] cite pour exemple, d’une part, l’image de la femme adultère (Prov., ch. VII), qui, selon notre auteur, désigne la matière, et que beaucoup de commentateurs prennent dans le sens littéral, et, d’autre part, le récit relatif à Bathséba (II. Sam., ch. XI, [[t:2]]V[[t:3]]. 2 et suiv.), qui évidemment doit être pris dans son sens littéral et dans lequel certains commentateurs ont vu une allégorie.

### fn.69 (Footnote)
**English:** Allusion is here likely made to some Oriental fable; I suppose reference is intended to Nimrod, who, according to Oriental traditions, rebelled against God and caused the <i>Tower of Babel</i> to be built, and who is said to have been placed in the heavens by identifying him with the constellation of the <i>Giant</i> <span dir="rtl">(الجبّار)</span> or <i>Orion.</i> Vid. the <i>Chronicon Paschale</i>, p. 36; concerning the <i>Djebbâr</i> or <i>Orion</i> vid. the commentary on the treatise of Ulug-Beigh, by Hyde, in the <i>Syntagma dissertationum</i>, vol. I, pages 42 et seq., and p. 57.
**Correction:** Allusion is here likely made to some Oriental fable; I suppose reference is intended to Nimrod, who, according to Oriental traditions, rebelled against God and caused the <i>Tower of Babel</i> to be built, and who is said to have been placed in the heavens by identifying him with the constellation of the <i>Giant</i> <span dir="rtl">(الجبّار)</span> or <i>Orion.</i> Vid. the <i>Chronicon Paschale</i>, p. 36; concerning the <i>Djebbâr</i> or <i>Orion</i> vid. the commentary on the treatise of Ulug-Beigh, by Hyde, in the <i>Syntagma dissertationum</i>, vol. I, pages 42 et seq., and p. 57.

**French (Source):** On fait ici allusion probablement à quelque fable orientale; je suppose qu’on veut parler de Nimrod, qui, selon les traditions orientales, se révolta contre Dieu et fit construire la [[t:0]]Tour de Babel[[t:1]], et qu’on dit avoir été placé au ciel en l’identifiant avec la constellation du [[t:2]]Géant[[t:3]] [[t:4]](الجبّار)[[t:5]]  ou de l’[[t:6]]Orion.[[t:7]] Voy. le [[t:8]]Chronicon Paschale[[t:9]], p. 36; sur le [[t:10]]Djebbâr[[t:11]] ou l’[[t:12]]Orion[[t:13]] voy. le commentaire sur le traité d’Ulug-Beigh, par Hyde, dans le [[t:14]]Syntagma dissertationum[[t:15]], tome I, pages 42 et suiv., et p. 57.

### fn.107 (Footnote)
**English:** Use is here made, saith the author, of the expression <i>to descend</i> to indicate that the punishment proceedeth from the supreme divine will; for it cannot be admitted that God decreeth punishment solely in consequence of the evil actions of men, which would suppose that God may be influenced and change His will. Concerning the important problem of the foreknowledge of God and the free will of man, one may consult Part III<sup>rd</sup> of this work, chap. XVII et seq.
**Correction:** Use is here made, saith the author, of the expression <i>to descend</i> to indicate that the punishment proceedeth from the supreme divine will; for it cannot be admitted that God decreeth punishment solely in consequence of the evil actions of men, which would suppose that God may be influenced and change His will. Concerning the important problem of the foreknowledge of God and the free will of man, one may consult Part III<sup>rd</sup> of this work, chap. XVII et seq.

**French (Source):** On se sert ici, dit l’auteur, de l’expression de [[t:0]]descendre[[t:1]] pour indiquer que le châtiment vient de la suprême volonté divine; car on ne saurait admettre que Dieu décrète le châtiment uniquement par suite des mauvaises actions des hommes, ce qui supposerait que Dieu peut être influencé et changer de volonté. Sur le problème important de la prescience de Dieu et du libre arbitre de l’homme, on peut voir la III[[t:2]]e[[t:3]] partie de cet ouvrage, chap. XVII et suiv.

---

### fn.142 (Footnote)
**English:** The expressions <i>male</i> and <i>female</i> which are encountered here and there amongst the Neoplatonists and the Gnostics are not precisely those whereof Plato maketh use. The latter employeth, inter alia, to designate matter, the words <i>nurse</i> (<i>τιθ̇ηνη</i>), <i>mother</i>, etc., whilst form, that is to say the intelligent principle or <i>reason</i> (<i>λόγος</i>), is presented by him as the <i>father</i> of the universe. Vide the <i>Timaeus</i>, p. 49 <i>a</i>, 28 <i>a</i>, et <i>passim</i>. — Ibn-Roshd likewise attributeth to Plato the denomination of matter and form by the words <i>female</i> and <i>male</i>. In his Epitome of the Organon (towards the end of the book on Demonstration, corresponding to the Posterior Analytics), in speaking of the inconvenience presented by figures and images employed in philosophical reasoning, he citeth for example Plato, <i>who saith of matter that it is the female, and of form that it is the male, an assertion which is far from making understood the essence of matter</i>. <span dir="rtl">مثال ذلك قول افلاطون في المادة انها الانثى 
**Correction:** The expressions <i>male</i> and <i>female</i> which are encountered here and there amongst the Neoplatonists and the Gnostics are not precisely those whereof Plato maketh use. The latter employeth, inter alia, to designate matter, the words <i>nurse</i> (<i>τιθ̇ηνη</i>), <i>mother</i>, etc., whilst form, that is to say the intelligent principle or <i>reason</i> (<i>λόγος</i>), is presented by him as the <i>father</i> of the universe. Vide the <i>Timaeus</i>, p. 49 <i>a</i>, 28 <i>a</i>, et <i>passim</i>. — Ibn-Roshd likewise attributeth to Plato the denomination of matter and form by the words <i>female</i> and <i>male</i>. In his Epitome of the Organon (towards the end of the book on Demonstration, corresponding to the Posterior Analytics), in speaking of the inconvenience presented by figures and images employed in philosophical reasoning, he citeth for example Plato, <i>who saith of matter that it is the female, and of form that it is the male, an assertion which is far from making understood the essence of matter</i>. <span dir="rtl">مثال ذلك قول افلاطون في المادة انها الانثى وفي الصورة انها الذكر فان هذا القول بعيد من ان يفهم جوهر المادة</span> The passage which followeth (from the words <span dir="rtl">ואנת תעלם</span> to the words <span dir="rtl">פי אלעלם אלטביעי</span>) appeareth to be a parenthesis, having no other purpose than to justify the image employed by Plato.
وفي الصورة انها الذكر فان هذا القول بعيد من ان يفهم جوهر المادة</span> The passage which followeth (from the words <span dir="rtl">ואנת תעלם</span> to the words <span dir="rtl">פי אלעלם אלטביעי</span>) appeareth to be a parenthesis, having no other purpose than to justify the image employed by Plato.

**French (Source):** Les expressions de [[t:0]]mâle[[t:1]] et de [[t:2]]femelle[[t:3]] qu’on rencontre ça et là chez les Néoplatoniciens et les Gnostiques ne sont pas précisément celles dont se sert Platon. Celui-ci emploie entre autres, pour désigner la matière, les mots [[t:4]]nourrice[[t:5]] ([[t:6]]τιθ̇ηνη[[t:7]]), [[t:8]]mère[[t:9]], etc., tandis que la forme, c’est-à-dire le principe intelligent ou la [[t:10]]raison[[t:11]] ([[t:12]]λόγος[[t:13]]), est présentée par lui comme le [[t:14]]père[[t:15]] de l’univers. Voy. le [[t:16]]Timée[[t:17]], p. 49 [[t:18]]a[[t:19]], 28 [[t:20]]a[[t:21]] et [[t:22]]passim[[t:23]]. — Ibn-Rosch attribue également à Platon la dénomination de la matière et de la forme par les mots [[t:24]]femelle[[t:25]] et [[t:26]]mâle[[t:27]]. Dans son Abrégé de l’Organon (vers la fin du livre de la Démonstration, correspondant aux Derniers Analytiques), en parlant de l’inconvénient que présentent les figures et les images employées dans le raisonnement philosophique, il cite pour exemple Platon, [[t:28]]qui dit de la matière qu’elle est la femelle, et de la forme qu’elle est le mâle, assertion qui est loin de faire comprendre l’essence de la matière[[t:29]]. [[t:30]]مثال ذلك قول افلاطون في المادة انها الانثى 
وفي الصورة انها الذكر فان هذا القول بعيد من ان يفهم جوهر المادة[[t:31]]  Le passage qui suit (depuis les mots [[t:32]]ואנת תעלם[[t:33]] jusqu’aux mots [[t:34]]פי אלעלם אלטביעי[[t:35]]) paraît être une parenthèse, n’ayant d’autre but que de justifier l’image employée par Platon.

### fn.173 (Footnote)
**English:** The word <span dir="rtl">عدم</span> (pl. <span dir="rtl">أحلام</span>), as was seen above, ch. XVII, correspondeth to the Aristotelian term <i>στερησις</i>, <i>privation</i>, which designateth <i>non-being</i>, or the negation of that which is positive. Thus, in the Biblical passage cited by the author, <i>evil</i> and <i>darkness</i> are naught else but the <i>privation</i> or the <i>negation</i> of good and of light.
**Correction:** The word <span dir="rtl">عدم</span> (pl. <span dir="rtl">أحلام</span>), as was seen above, ch. XVII, correspondeth to the Aristotelian term <i>στερησις</i>, <i>privation</i>, which designateth <i>non-being</i>, or the negation of that which is positive. Thus, in the Biblical passage cited by the author, <i>evil</i> and <i>darkness</i> are naught else but the <i>privation</i> or the <i>negation</i> of good and of light.

**French (Source):** Le mot [[t:0]]عدم[[t:1]]  (pl. [[t:2]]أحلام[[t:3]] ), ainsi qu’on l’a vu plus haut, ch. XVII, correspond au terme aristotélique [[t:4]]στερησις[[t:5]], [[t:6]]privation[[t:7]], qui désigne le [[t:8]]non être[[t:9]], ou la négation de ce qui est positivement. Ainsi, dans le passage biblique cité par l’auteur, le [[t:10]]mal[[t:11]] et l’[[t:12]]obscurité[[t:13]] ne sont autre chose que la [[t:14]]privation[[t:15]] ou la [[t:16]]négation[[t:17]] du bien et de la lumière.

### fn.227 (Footnote)
**English:** The version of Ibn-Tibbon beareth <span dir="rtl">להיותו</span>; but it must be translated <span dir="rtl">והיותו</span>, for the Ar. mss. generally have <span dir="rtl">וכונהא</span>, with the exception of one of the two Leyden mss., which beareth <span dir="rtl">לכונהא</span>. The word <span dir="rtl">וכונהא</span> (<i>et esse eam</i>) indicateth, like that which precedeth, the object of the perception of the <i>Elect</i>.

### fn.249 (Footnote)
**English:** This famous commentator on Aristotle, who flourished at the close of the II<sup>nd</sup> and at the commencement of the III<sup>rd</sup> century, enjoyed amongst the Arabs, as amongst the Greeks, a very great authority. Maimonides, in a letter addressed to the Hebrew translator of his <i>Guide for the Perplexed</i>, R. Samuel Ibn-Tibbon, recommendeth especially to the latter the study of the commentaries of Alexander. Upon the Arabic versions of the numerous works of Alexander, vid. Casiri, <i>Biblioth. arab. hisp.</i>, t. I, p. 243 et seq.; Wenrich, <i>De Auctorum Grœcorum versionibus etc.</i>, p. 273 et seq.
**Correction:** This famous commentator on Aristotle, who flourished at the close of the II<sup>nd</sup> and at the commencement of the III<sup>rd</sup> century, enjoyed amongst the Arabs, as amongst the Greeks, a very great authority. Maimonides, in a letter addressed to the Hebrew translator of his <i>Guide for the Perplexed</i>, R. Samuel Ibn-Tibbon, recommendeth especially to the latter the study of the commentaries of Alexander. Upon the Arabic versions of the numerous works of Alexander, vid. Casiri, <i>Biblioth. arab. hisp.</i>, t. I, p. 243 et seq.; Wenrich, <i>De Auctorum Grœcorum versionibus etc.</i>, p. 273 et seq.

**French (Source):** Ce célèbre commentateur d’Aristote, qui florissait à la fin du II[[t:0]]e[[t:1]] et au commencement du III[[t:2]]e[[t:3]] siècle, jouissait chez les Arabes, comme chez les Grecs, d’une très grande autorité. Maïmonide, dans une lettre adressée au traducteur hébreu de son [[t:4]]Guide des égarés[[t:5]], R. Samuel Ibn-Tibbon, recommande tout particulièrement à celui-ci l’étude des commentaires d’Alexandre. Sur les versions arabes des nombreux ouvrages d’Alexandre, voy. Casiri, [[t:6]]Biblioth. arab. hisp.[[t:7]], t. I, p. 243 et suiv.; Wenrich. [[t:8]]De Auctorum Grœcorum versionibus etc.[[t:9]], p. 273 et suiv.

### fn.290 (Footnote)
**English:** Concerning the division <i>of the virtues</i> (<i>ἀρεταί</i>) into <i>moral</i> (<i>ἠθιϰαί</i>) and <i>rational</i> or <i>intellectual</i> (<i>διανοητιϰαί</i>) and their mutual relations, vide Aristotle, <i>Nicomachean Ethics</i>, Bk. I, chap. 13; Bk. II, chap. 1 et seq.: Bk. VI, chap. 2. Compare also that which Maimonides himself saith in this regard in the <i>Eight Chapters</i>, placed at the head of his commentary on the treatise <i>Aboth;</i> in chap. II we read: "The virtues are of two kinds, <i>moral virtues</i> (<span dir="rtl">פצׄאיל כׄלקיה̈</span>) and <i>rational virtues</i> (<span dir="rtl">פצׄאיל נטקיה̈</span>), and there are opposed to them two kinds of vices, etc." See the <i>Porta Mosis</i> of Pococke, p. 191.
**Correction:** Concerning the division <i>of the virtues</i> (<i>ἀρεταί</i>) into <i>moral</i> (<i>ἠθιϰαί</i>) and <i>rational</i> or <i>intellectual</i> (<i>διανοητιϰαί</i>) and their mutual relations, vide Aristotle, <i>Nicomachean Ethics</i>, Bk. I, chap. 13; Bk. II, chap. 1 et seq.: Bk. VI, chap. 2. Compare also that which Maimonides himself saith in this regard in the <i>Eight Chapters</i>, placed at the head of his commentary on the treatise <i>Aboth;</i> in chap. II we read: "The virtues are of two kinds, <i>moral virtues</i> (<span dir="rtl">פצׄאיל כׄלקיה̈</span>) and <i>rational virtues</i> (<span dir="rtl">פצׄאיל נטקיה̈</span>), and there are opposed to them two kinds of vices, etc." See the <i>Porta Mosis</i> of Pococke, p. 191.

**French (Source):** Sur la division [[t:0]]des vertus[[t:1]] ([[t:2]]ἀρεταί[[t:3]]) en [[t:4]]morales[[t:5]] ([[t:6]]ἠθιϰαί[[t:7]]) et [[t:8]]rationnelles[[t:9]] ou [[t:10]]intellectuelles[[t:11]] ([[t:12]]διανοητιϰαί[[t:13]]) et sur leurs rapports mutuels, voy. Aristote, [[t:14]]Morale à Nicomaque[[t:15]], l. I, chap. 13; 1. II, chap. 1 et suiv.: l. VI, chap. 2. Comparez aussi ce que Maïmonide lui-même dit à cet égard dans les [[t:16]]Huit chapitres[[t:17]], placés en tête de son commentaire sur le traité [[t:18]]Abôth;[[t:19]] au chap. II on lit: «Les vertus sont de deux espèces, [[t:20]]vertus morales[[t:21]] ([[t:22]]פצׄאיל כׄלקיה̈[[t:23]]) et [[t:24]]vertus rationnelles[[t:25]] ([[t:26]]פצׄאיל נטקיה̈[[t:27]]), et il leur est opposé deux espèces de vices, etc.» Voir la [[t:28]]Porta Mosis[[t:29]] de Pococke, p. 191.

### fn.327 (Footnote)
**English:** That is to say, the <i>Intelligences of the spheres</i>, or the superior and <i>abstract</i> spirits, which, according to the Arabian philosophers, preside over the different celestial <i>spheres</i>; the Arabians, as well as the Schoolmen, believed that Aristotle had designated these <i>Intelligences</i> by the words <i>τὰ ϰεχωρισμένα, separated things</i> (treatise <i>on the Soul</i>, book III, chap. 7). Albertus Magnus, in speaking of <i>separated substances</i>, expresseth himself thus: «Et ideo quæ (substantia) nec dividitur divisione corporis, nec movetur motu corporis, nec operatur instrumentis corporis, illa <i>separata</i> est, non per locum, sed a corporalis materiæ quantumcumque simplicis obligatione. Hæc autem omnia competunt <i>substantiis coelorum</i>, etc.» (Parva naturalia, <i>de Motibus animalium</i>, lib. I, tr. I, c. 4). Vid. also Maimonides, in the present work, part II<sup>nd</sup>, chap. IV, and mine article <i>Ibn-Roschd</i>, in the <i>Dictionnaire des sciences philosophiques</i>, t. III, p. 168.
**Correction:** That is to say, the <i>Intelligences of the spheres</i>, or the superior and <i>abstract</i> spirits, which, according to the Arabian philosophers, preside over the different celestial <i>spheres</i>; the Arabians, as well as the Schoolmen, believed that Aristotle had designated these <i>Intelligences</i> by the words <i>τὰ ϰεχωρισμένα, separated things</i> (treatise <i>on the Soul</i>, book III, chap. 7). Albertus Magnus, in speaking of <i>separated substances</i>, expresseth himself thus: «Et ideo quæ (substantia) nec dividitur divisione corporis, nec movetur motu corporis, nec operatur instrumentis corporis, illa <i>separata</i> est, non per locum, sed a corporalis materiæ quantumcumque simplicis obligatione. Hæc autem omnia competunt <i>substantiis coelorum</i>, etc.» (Parva naturalia, <i>de Motibus animalium</i>, lib. I, tr. I, c. 4). Vid. also Maimonides, in the present work, part II<sup>nd</sup>, chap. IV, and mine article <i>Ibn-Roschd</i>, in the <i>Dictionnaire des sciences philosophiques</i>, t. III, p. 168.

**French (Source):** C’est-à-dire, les [[t:0]]Intelligences des sphères[[t:1]], ou les esprits supérieurs et [[t:2]]abstraits[[t:3]], qui, selon les philosophes arabes, président aux différentes [[t:4]]sphères[[t:5]] célestes; les Arabes, ainsi que les Scolastiques, croyaient qu’Aristote avait désigné ces [[t:6]]Intelligences[[t:7]] par les mots [[t:8]]τἀ ϰεχωρισμένα, les choses séparées[[t:9]] (traité [[t:10]]de l’Ame[[t:11]], liv. III, chap. 7). Albert le Grand, en parlant des [[t:12]]substances séparées[[t:13]], s’exprime ainsi: «Et ideo quæ (substantia)  nec dividitur divisione corporis, nec movetur motu corporis, nec  operatur instrumentis corporis, illa [[t:14]]separata[[t:15]] est, non per locum, sed  a corporalis materiæ quantumcumque simplicis obligatione. Hæc  autem omnia competunt [[t:16]]substantiis coelorum[[t:17]], etc.» (Parva naturalia, [[t:18]]de Motibus animalium[[t:19]], lib. I, tr. I, c. 4). Voy. aussi Maïmonide, dans le présent ouvrage, II[[t:20]]e[[t:21]] partie, chap. IV, et mon article [[t:22]]Ibn-Roschd[[t:23]], dans le [[t:24]]Dictionnaire des sciences philosophiques[[t:25]], t. III, pag. 168.

### fn.345 (Footnote)
**English:** If the author distinguish here from the <i>rational soul</i> that which remaineth of man after death, it must be remembered that he beholdeth in the rational soul naught but a physical <i>disposition</i>; it is the intellect in potentiality which, through meditation and the influence of the universal active intellect, becometh the <i>acquired intellect</i> and the <i>intellect in actu</i>, and, as such, is imperishable. Vid. infra, chap. LXX and chap. LXXII, toward the end. Concerning this doctrine and its divers nuances among the Arabian philosophers, vid. my articles <i>Ibn-Bâdja</i> and <i>Ibn-Roschd</i> in the <i>Dictionnaire des sciences philosophiques</i>, volume III, p. 157, 166 et seq. Regarding Maimonides in particular, vid. Scheyer, <i>Das psychologische System des Maimonides</i>, p. 30 et seq. We shall have occasion to return thereto in other places.
**Correction:** If the author distinguish here from the <i>rational soul</i> that which remaineth of man after death, it must be remembered that he beholdeth in the rational soul naught but a physical <i>disposition</i>; it is the intellect in potentiality which, through meditation and the influence of the universal active intellect, becometh the <i>acquired intellect</i> and the <i>intellect in actu</i>, and, as such, is imperishable. Vid. infra, chap. LXX and chap. LXXII, toward the end. Concerning this doctrine and its divers nuances among the Arabian philosophers, vid. my articles <i>Ibn-Bâdja</i> and <i>Ibn-Roschd</i> in the <i>Dictionnaire des sciences philosophiques</i>, volume III, p. 157, 166 et seq. Regarding Maimonides in particular, vid. Scheyer, <i>Das psychologische System des Maimonides</i>, p. 30 et seq. We shall have occasion to return thereto in other places.

**French (Source):** Si l’auteur distingue ici de [[t:0]]l’âme rationnelle[[t:1]] ce qui reste de l’homme après la mort, il faut se rappeler qu’il ne voit dans l’âme rationnelle qu’une [[t:2]]disposition[[t:3]] physique; c’est l’intellect en puissance qui, par la méditation et l’influence de l’intellect actif universel, devient [[t:4]]intellect acquis[[t:5]] et [[t:6]]intellect en acte[[t:7]], et, comme tel, est impérissable. Voy. ci-après, chap. LXX et chap. LXXII, vers la fin. Sur cette doctrine et ses diverses nuances chez les philosophes arabes, voy. mes articles [[t:8]]Ibn-Bâdja[[t:9]] et [[t:10]]Ibn-Roschd[[t:11]] dans le [[t:12]]Dictionnaire des sciences philosophiques[[t:13]], tome III, pag. 157, 166 et suiv. Sur Maïmonide en particulier, voy. Seheyer, [[t:14]]Das psychologische System des Maimonides[[t:15]], pag. 30 et suiv. Nous aurons l’occasion d’y revenir dans d’autres endroits.

---

### fn.434 (Footnote)
**English:** That is to say, that the corporeal figures under which they appear exist only in the mind of him who beholdeth them, and possess no real existence. One frequently encountereth the expression <span dir="rtl">כֿארג׳ אלדֿהן</span>, <i>outside the mind</i>, to indicate <i>objective</i> reality, even as it is said that a thing is <span dir="rtl">פי אלדֿהן</span>, <i>in the mind</i>, to indicate the <i>subjective</i> conception, whether it correspondeth or no to aught real. The word <span dir="rtl">דֿהן</span> <span dir="rtl">(ذهن)</span> embraseth the whole of all spiritual faculties and dispositions, even the imagination, whilst <span dir="rtl">עקל</span> <span dir="rtl">(عقل)</span> designateth the intellect or the intelligence; one may therefore say that a thing is <span dir="rtl">פי אלדֿהן</span> <i>in the mind</i>, even when it is only in the imagination. Vide, e.g., supra, chap. III (pp. 43 and 44), where the perception of the senses, <i>independently</i> or <i>outside the mind</i> (<span dir="rtl">כֿארג׳ אלדֿהן</span>), is opposed at once to the imagination and to the perception of the intelligence (<span dir="rtl">עקל</span>). The Hebrew translators, having found no word to render the Arabic word <span dir="rtl">דֿהן</span>, have almost always rendered it, as well as <span dir="rtl">עקל</span> (intellect), by the word <span dir="rtl">שׂכל</span>, which may cause confusion. It is necessary to be thoroughly imbued with what we have just stated in order to understand aright, in the Hebrew versions, the expressions <span dir="rtl">בשׂכל</span> and <span dir="rtl">חוץ לשׂכל</span>. See the excellent observations made upon this subject by M. Scheyer, in his work entitled: <i>Das Psychologische System des Maimonides</i>, pp. 60 et seq.
**Correction:** That is to say, that the corporeal figures under which they appear exist only in the mind of him who beholdeth them, and possess no real existence. One frequently encountereth the expression <span dir="rtl">כֿארג׳ אלדֿהן</span>, <i>outside the mind</i>, to indicate <i>objective</i> reality, even as it is said that a thing is <span dir="rtl">פי אלדֿהן</span>, <i>in the mind</i>, to indicate the <i>subjective</i> conception, whether it correspondeth or no to aught real. The word <span dir="rtl">דֿהן</span> <span dir="rtl">(ذهن)</span> embraseth the whole of all spiritual faculties and dispositions, even the imagination, whilst <span dir="rtl">עקל</span> <span dir="rtl">(عقل)</span> designateth the intellect or the intelligence; one may therefore say that a thing is <span dir="rtl">פי אלדֿהן</span> <i>in the mind</i>, even when it is only in the imagination. Vide, e.g., supra, chap. III (pp. 43 and 44), where the perception of the senses, <i>independently</i> or <i>outside the mind</i> (<span dir="rtl">כֿארג׳ אלדֿהן</span>), is opposed at once to the imagination and to the perception of the intelligence (<span dir="rtl">עקל</span>). The Hebrew translators, having found no word to render the Arabic word <span dir="rtl">דֿהן</span>, have almost always rendered it, as well as <span dir="rtl">עקל</span> (intellect), by the word <span dir="rtl">שׂכל</span>, which may cause confusion. It is necessary to be thoroughly imbued with what we have just stated in order to understand aright, in the Hebrew versions, the expressions <span dir="rtl">בשׂכל</span> and <span dir="rtl">חוץ לשׂכל</span>. See the excellent observations made upon this subject by M. Scheyer, in his work entitled: <i>Das Psychologische System des Maimonides</i>, pp. 60 et seq.

**French (Source):** C’est-à-dire, que les figures corporelles sous lesquelles ils apparaissent n’existent que dans l’esprit de celui qui les voit, et n’ont point d’existence réelle. On retrouve souvent l’expression [[t:0]]כׄארגׄ אלדׄהן[[t:1]], [[t:2]]en dehors de l’esprit[[t:3]], pour indiquer la réalité [[t:4]]objective[[t:5]], de même qu’on dit que quelque chose est [[t:6]]פי אלדׄהן[[t:7]], [[t:8]]dans l’esprit[[t:9]], pour indiquer la conception [[t:10]]subjective[[t:11]], qu’elle corresponde ou non à quelque chose de réel. Le mot [[t:12]]דׄהן[[t:13]] [[t:14]](ذهن)[[t:15]]  embrasse l’ensemble de toutes les facultés et dispositions spirituelles, même l’imagination, tandis que [[t:16]]עקל[[t:17]] [[t:18]]عقل[[t:19]]  désigne l’intellect ou l’intelligence; on peut donc dire qu’une chose est [[t:20]]פי אלדׄהן[[t:21]] [[t:22]]dans l’esprit[[t:23]], lors même qu’elle n’est que dans l’imagination. Voy., p. ex., ci-dessus, chap. III (pag. 43 et 44), où la perception des sens, [[t:24]]indépendamment[[t:25]] ou [[t:26]]en dehors de l’esprit[[t:27]] ([[t:28]]כׄארגׄ אלדׄהן[[t:29]]), est opposée à la fois à l’imagination et à la perception de l’intelligence ([[t:30]]עקל[[t:31]]). Les traducteurs hébreux, n’ayant pas trouvé de mot pour rendre le mot arabe [[t:32]]דׄהן[[t:33]], l’ont presque toujours rendu, ainsi que [[t:34]]עקל[[t:35]] (intellect), par le mot [[t:36]]שׂכל[[t:37]], ce qui peut causer de la confusion. Il faut se pénétrer de ce que nous venons de dire pour bien comprendre, dans les versions hébraïques, les expressions [[t:38]]בשכל[[t:39]] et [[t:40]]חוץ לשכל[[t:41]]. Voy. les excellentes observations faites à ce sujet par M. Scheyer, dans son écrit intitulé: [[t:42]]Das Psychologische System des Maimonides[[t:43]], pag. 60 et suiv.

### fn.448 (Footnote)
**English:** The question of <i>attributes</i> is one of those which most occupied the Muslim and Jewish doctors of the Middle Ages. Certain doctors, whilst professing the absolute unity of God, yet believed they might admit a certain number of eternal and <i>essential</i> attributes, that is to say, inherent in the divine essence, such as life, science, power, etc.; the sect of the <i>Mo’tazales</i>, in general, denied the <i>attributes</i> as incompatible with absolute unity; there were however, amongst them, some who implicitly admitted <i>essential</i> attributes by saying that God is living by His essence, and not by the <i>attribute</i> of life, that He knows by His essence, and not by the <i>attribute</i> of science, and so forth. (Cf. Pococke, Spec. hist. Arab., pag. 214 et seq., and <i>ibidem</i>, the text of Abu’l-Faraj, pag. 19). These latter, according to our author, are no more in the truth than those who openly profess the attributes of God. Faithful to the principles of the philosophers, he rejecteth <i>essential</i> attributes in an absolute manner. See the details further on, in chap. LIII.
**Correction:** The question of <i>attributes</i> is one of those which most occupied the Muslim and Jewish doctors of the Middle Ages. Certain doctors, whilst professing the absolute unity of God, yet believed they might admit a certain number of eternal and <i>essential</i> attributes, that is to say, inherent in the divine essence, such as life, science, power, etc.; the sect of the <i>Mo’tazales</i>, in general, denied the <i>attributes</i> as incompatible with absolute unity; there were however, amongst them, some who implicitly admitted <i>essential</i> attributes by saying that God is living by His essence, and not by the <i>attribute</i> of life, that He knows by His essence, and not by the <i>attribute</i> of science, and so forth. (Cf. Pococke, Spec. hist. Arab., pag. 214 et seq., and <i>ibidem</i>, the text of Abu’l-Faraj, pag. 19). These latter, according to our author, are no more in the truth than those who openly profess the attributes of God. Faithful to the principles of the philosophers, he rejecteth <i>essential</i> attributes in an absolute manner. See the details further on, in chap. LIII.

**French (Source):** La question des [[t:0]]attributs[[t:1]] est une de celles qui a le plus occupé les docteurs musulmans et juifs du moyen âge. Certains docteurs, tout en professant l’unité absolue de Dieu, croyaient pourtant pouvoir admettre un certain nombre d’attributs éternels et [[t:2]]essentiels[[t:3]], c’est-à-dire inhérents à l’essence divine, tels que la vie, la science, la puissance, etc.; la secte des [[t:4]]Mo’tazales[[t:5]], en général, niait les [[t:6]]attributs[[t:7]] comme incompatibles avec l’unité absolue; il y en avait cependant, parmi eux, qui admettaient implicitement des attributs [[t:8]]essentiels[[t:9]] en disant que Dieu est vivant par son essence, et non par l’[[t:10]]attribut[[t:11]] de la vie, qu’il sait par son essence, et non par l’[[t:12]]attribut[[t:13]] de la science, et ainsi de suite. (Cf. Pococke, Spec. hist. Arab., pag. 214 et suiv., et [[t:14]]ibidem[[t:15]], le texte d’Abou’l-Faradj, pag. 19). Ces derniers, selon notre auteur, ne sont pas plus dans le vrai que ceux qui professent ouvertement les attributs de Dieu. Fidèle aux principes des philosophes, il rejette les attributs [[t:16]]essentiels[[t:17]] d’une manière absolue. Voir les détails plus loin, au chap. LIII.

### fn.449 (Footnote)
**English:** Those, saith the author, who bestow upon God numerous attributes whilst proclaiming His unity, His incorporeality, and His absolute simplicity, are in contradiction with themselves, and it would seem that, according to them, it is rather a matter of expressing themselves in a certain manner than of penetrating the true sense of beliefs. The <i>Mo’tazales</i>, in arguing against the partisans of eternal and essential attributes, reproach them, like our author, with falling into an error similar to that of the Christians, who admit three persons in God. Vid. Pococke, <i>loco citato</i>, pag. 216: <i>Infidelitatis arguuntur christiani quod tria statuunt æterna; quid ergo de iis pronuntiandum qui septem aut plura statuunt ?</i>
**Correction:** Those, saith the author, who bestow upon God numerous attributes whilst proclaiming His unity, His incorporeality, and His absolute simplicity, are in contradiction with themselves, and it would seem that, according to them, it is rather a matter of expressing themselves in a certain manner than of penetrating the true sense of beliefs. The <i>Mo’tazales</i>, in arguing against the partisans of eternal and essential attributes, reproach them, like our author, with falling into an error similar to that of the Christians, who admit three persons in God. Vid. Pococke, <i>loco citato</i>, pag. 216: <i>Infidelitatis arguuntur christiani quod tria statuunt æterna; quid ergo de iis pronuntiandum qui septem aut plura statuunt ?</i>

**French (Source):** Ceux-là, dit l’auteur, qui donnent à Dieu de nombreux attributs tout en proclamant son unité, son incorporalité et sa simplicité absolue, sont en contradiction avec eux-mêmes, et on dirait que, selon eux, il s’agit plutôt de s’exprimer d’une certaine manière que de se pénétrer du vrai sens des croyances. Les [[t:0]]Mo’tazales[[t:1]], en argumentant contre les partisans des attributs éternels et essentiels, leur reprochent, comme notre auteur, de tomber dans une erreur semblable à celle des chrétiens, qui admettent dans Dieu trois personnes.Voy. Pococke, [[t:2]]loco citato[[t:3]], pag. 216: [[t:4]]Infidelitatis arguuntur christiani quod tria statuunt œterna; quid ergo de iis pronuntiandum qui septem aut plura statuunt ?[[t:5]]

**Correction:** Vide <i>Ibid.</i>, book 6, chap. 1 et seq.

### fn.464.sub_0 (Footnote)
**English:** This proposition pertaineth unto the atomistic <i>Motécallemîn</i>, whose doctrine shall be expounded hereafter (chap. LXXIII). By <span dir="rtl">מכאן</span> <span dir="rtl">(سكان)</span>, they seem to understand space in relation to its extension or its dimensions; by <span dir="rtl">חיז (حيّز)</span>, space circumscribed within limits. Albeit the atom, they say, hath no extension, it is nevertheless circumscribed and separated, by limits, from that which is adjacent to it; it is, as it were, the body without dimensions, the <i>point</i> considered as the first element of extension, yet which possesseth none itself. Lo, thus doth Al-Djordjâni, in his <i>Kitâb al-Ta’rifât</i> or Book of Definitions, explain the words <span dir="rtl">مكان</span> and <span dir="rtl">حيز</span>, as they are employed by the <i>Motécallemîn:</i> <span dir="rtl">المكان عند</span> <span dir="rtl">المتكلمين هو الفراغ المتوهم الذي يشغله الجسم وينفذ فيه ابعاده</span> «<i>Macân</i>, among the <i>Motécallemîn</i>, is the ideal void which the body occupieth and into which it causeth its dimensions to penetrate.» <span dir="rtl">الحيز عند المتكلمين هو الفراغ المتوهم</span> <span dir="rtl">الذي يشغله شيء ممتد كالجسم أو غير ممتد كالجوهر الفرد</span> «’<i>Hayyiz</i>, among the <i>Motécallemîn</i>, is the ideal void which is occupied by a thing whether extended like the body, or non-extended like the simple substance (or the <i>atome</i>).» It is perceived that <i>macân</i> is attributed only to the body having dimensions, whilst <i>’hayyiz</i> is attributed simultaneously to the extended body and to the atom. The words <span dir="rtl">الجوهر الفرد</span> signify, according to M. Silv. de Sacy, <i>substance isolated from form</i> (see Notices et extraits des mss., vol. X, p. 65); but this explanation is incorrect. These words, which, in truth, signify literally <i>isolated substance</i>, were employed by the <i>Motécallemîn</i> to designate the <i>atome</i> which they also call <span dir="rtl">الجزء الذي</span> <span dir="rtl">لا يتجزأ</span> or simply <span dir="rtl">الجزء</span>, as might have been seen somewhat further back.
**Correction:** This proposition pertaineth unto the atomistic <i>Motécallemîn</i>, whose doctrine shall be expounded hereafter (chap. LXXIII). By <span dir="rtl">מכאן</span> <span dir="rtl">(سكان)</span>, they seem to understand space in relation to its extension or its dimensions; by <span dir="rtl">חיז (حيّز)</span>, space circumscribed within limits. Albeit the atom, they say, hath no extension, it is nevertheless circumscribed and separated, by limits, from that which is adjacent to it; it is, as it were, the body without dimensions, the <i>point</i> considered as the first element of extension, yet which possesseth none itself. Lo, thus doth Al-Djordjâni, in his <i>Kitâb al-Ta’rifât</i> or Book of Definitions, explain the words <span dir="rtl">مكان</span> and <span dir="rtl">حيز</span>, as they are employed by the <i>Motécallemîn:</i> <span dir="rtl">المكان عند</span> <span dir="rtl">المتكلمين هو الفراغ المتوهم الذي يشغله الجسم وينفذ فيه ابعاده</span> «<i>Macân</i>, among the <i>Motécallemîn</i>, is the ideal void which the body occupieth and into which it causeth its dimensions to penetrate.» <span dir="rtl">الحيز عند المتكلمين هو الفراغ المتوهم</span> <span dir="rtl">الذي يشغله شيء ممتد كالجسم أو غير ممتد كالجوهر الفرد</span> «’<i>Hayyiz</i>, among the <i>Motécallemîn</i>, is the ideal void which is occupied by a thing whether extended like the body, or non-extended like the simple substance (or the <i>atome</i>).» It is perceived that <i>macân</i> is attributed only to the body having dimensions, whilst <i>’hayyiz</i> is attributed simultaneously to the extended body and to the atom. The words <span dir="rtl">الجوهر الفرد</span> signify, according to M. Silv. de Sacy, <i>substance isolated from form</i> (see Notices et extraits des mss., vol. X, p. 65); but this explanation is incorrect. These words, which, in truth, signify literally <i>isolated substance</i>, were employed by the <i>Motécallemîn</i> to designate the <i>atome</i> which they also call <span dir="rtl">الجزء الذي</span> <span dir="rtl">لا يتجزأ</span> or simply <span dir="rtl">الجزء</span>, as might have been seen somewhat further back.

**French (Source):** Cette proposition appartient aux [[t:0]]Motécallemîn[[t:1]] atomistes, dont la doctrine sera exposée plus loin (chap. LXXIII). Par [[t:2]]מכאן[[t:3]] [[t:4]](سكان)[[t:5]] , ils paraissent entendre l’espace par rapport à son étendue ou à ses dimensions; par [[t:6]]חיז (حيّز)[[t:7]] , l’espace circonscrit dans des limites. Bien que l’atome, disent-ils, n’ait pas d’étendue, il est pourtant circonscrit et séparé, par des limites, de ce qui l’avoisine; c’est, pour ainsi dire, le corps sans dimensions, le [[t:8]]point[[t:9]] considéré comme premier élément de l’étendue, mais qui n’en a pas lui-même. Voici comment Al-Djordjâni, dans son [[t:10]]Kitâb al-Ta’rifât[[t:11]] ou Livre des Définitions, explique les mots [[t:12]]مكان[[t:13]]  et [[t:14]]حيز[[t:15]] , tels qu’ils sont employés par les [[t:16]]Motécallemîn:[[t:17]] [[t:18]]المكان عند[[t:19]]  [[t:20]]المتكلمين هو الفراغ المتوهم الذي يشغله الجسم وينفذ فيه ابعاده[[t:21]]  «[[t:22]]Macân[[t:23]], chez les [[t:24]]Motécallemîn[[t:25]], est le vide idéal qu’occupe le corps et dans lequel il fait pénétrer ses dimensions.» [[t:26]]الحيز عند المتكلمين هو الفراغ المتوهم[[t:27]]  [[t:28]]الذي يشغله شيء ممتد كالجسم أو غير ممتد كالجوهر الفرد[[t:29]]  «’[[t:30]]Hayyiz[[t:31]], chez les [[t:32]]Motécallemîn[[t:33]], est le vide idéal qu’occupe une chose soit étendue comme le corps, soit non étendue comme la substance simple (ou l’[[t:34]]atome[[t:35]]).» On voit que le [[t:36]]macân[[t:37]] n’est attribué qu’au corps ayant des dimensions, tandis que le [[t:38]]’hayyiz[[t:39]] est attribué en même temps au corps étendu et à l’atome. Les mots [[t:40]]الجوهر الفرد[[t:41]]  signifieraient, selon M. Silv. de Sacy, [[t:42]]substance isolée de la forme[[t:43]] (voir Notices et extraits des mss., t. X, pag. 65); mais cette explication est inexacte. Ces mots, qui, en effet, signifient littéralement [[t:44]]substance isolée[[t:45]], ont été employés par les [[t:46]]Motécallemîn[[t:47]] pour désigner l’[[t:48]]atome[[t:49]] qu’ils appellent aussi [[t:50]]الجزء الذي[[t:51]]  [[t:52]]لا يتجزأ[[t:53]]  ou simplement [[t:54]]الجزء[[t:55]] , comme on a pu le voir un peu plus haut.
