### fn.464.sub_1 (Footnote)
**English:** Thus expresseth himself Ibn-Roschd, in his Epitome of the <i>Metaphysics</i>, Book I, when speaking of the <i>substance</i> <span dir="rtl">الجوهر</span>; we cite the Hebrew version: <span dir="rtl">וכן מי שראה כי העצמות הרמוז אליו יתחבר מחלקים אשד לא יתחלקו קרא אותם עצמים כמו שנשמע המדברים מאנשי זמננו יקראו החלק אשר לא יתחלק העצם הפדדי</span> «In like manner, those who think that the indicated substance is composed of <i>atoms</i> give to these latter the name of <i>substances</i>, even as we hear the <i>Motécallemîn</i> of our day call the atom the <i>isolated</i> or <i>simple</i> substance <span dir="rtl">(الجوهر الفرد)</span>.» The term <span dir="rtl">الجوهر الفرد</span> resembleth that of the <i>monad</i>, used by Leibnitz, albeit it doth not designate exactly the same thing; the proposition of the <i>Motécallemîn</i>, quoted here by Maïmonides, offereth a striking analogy with what Leibnitz saith, when speaking of monads: <i>Substantia nempe simplex, etsi non habeat in se extensionem, habet tamen positionem, quæ est fundamentum extensionis</i>. See the Works of Leibnitz, Dutens edition, vol. II, part I (<i>Epistolæ ad P. des Bosses</i>), p. 280.
**Correction:** Thus expresseth himself Ibn-Roschd, in his Epitome of the <i>Metaphysics</i>, Book I, when speaking of the <i>substance</i> <span dir="rtl">الجوهر</span>; we cite the Hebrew version: <span dir="rtl">וכן מי שראה כי העצמות הרמוז אליו יתחבר מחלקים אשד לא יתחלקו קרא אותם עצמים כמו שנשמע המדברים מאנשי זמננו יקראו החלק אשר לא יתחלק העצם הפדדי</span> «In like manner, those who think that the indicated substance is composed of <i>atoms</i> give to these latter the name of <i>substances</i>, even as we hear the <i>Motécallemîn</i> of our day call the atom the <i>isolated</i> or <i>simple</i> substance <span dir="rtl">(الجوهر الفرد)</span>.» The term <span dir="rtl">الجوهر الفرد</span> resembleth that of the <i>monad</i>, used by Leibnitz, albeit it doth not designate exactly the same thing; the proposition of the <i>Motécallemîn</i>, quoted here by Maïmonides, offereth a striking analogy with what Leibnitz saith, when speaking of monads: <i>Substantia nempe simplex, etsi non habeat in se extensionem, habet tamen positionem, quæ est fundamentum extensionis</i>. See the Works of Leibnitz, Dutens edition, vol. II, part I (<i>Epistolæ ad P. des Bosses</i>), p. 280.

**French (Source):** Voici comment s’exprime Ibn-Roschd, dans son Abrégé de la [[t:56]]Métaphysique[[t:57]], livre I, en parlant de la [[t:58]]substance[[t:59]] [[t:60]]الجوهر[[t:61]] ; nous citons la version hébraïque: [[t:62]]וכן מי שראה כי העצמות הרמוז אליו יתחבר מחלקים אשד לא יתחלקו קרא אותם עצמים כמו שנשמע המדברים מאנשי זמננו יקראו החלק אשר לא יתחלק העצם הפדדי[[t:63]] «De même, ceux qui pensent que la substance indiquée est composée d’[[t:64]]atomes[[t:65]] donnent à ces derniers le nom de [[t:66]]substances[[t:67]], comme nous entendons les [[t:68]]Motécallemîn[[t:69]] de nos jours appeler l’atome la [[t:70]]substance isolée[[t:71]] ou [[t:72]]simple[[t:73]] [[t:74]](الجوهر الفرد)[[t:75]] .» Le terme de [[t:76]]الجوهر الفرد[[t:77]]  ressemble à celui de [[t:78]]monade[[t:79]], employé par Leibnitz, quoiqu’il ne désigne pas exactement la même chose; la proposition des [[t:80]]Motécallemîn[[t:81]], citée ici par Maïmonide, offre une analogie frappante avec ce que dit Leibnitz, en parlant des monades: [[t:82]]Substantia nempe simplex, etsi non habeat in se extensionem, habet tamen positionem, quœ est fundamentum extensionis[[t:83]]. Voy. les OEuvres de Leibnitz, édition de Dutens, t. II, part. I ([[t:84]]Epistolœ ad P. des Bosses[[t:85]]), p. 280.

### fn.465 (Footnote)
**English:** Several doctors of the sect of the <i>Asharites</i> (who profess an absolute fatalism), whilst admitting that the actions of men emanate only from the will and the omnipotence of God (see Part III<sup></sup> of this work, chap. XVII, 3<sup>rd</sup> opinion), sought nevertheless to attribute unto man, in the causality of actions, a small part whereby he acquireth merit or demerit; this concurrence of man in the action created by God, an ungraspable hypothesis and void of sense, is designated by the word <i>acquisition</i> <span dir="rtl">اكتساب كسب</span>. See Pococke, <i>Specimen hist. Arab.</i>, pp. 239, 240, 248 et seq.), and mine article <i>Arabes in</i> the <i>Dictionnaire des sciences philosophiques</i>, vol. I, p. 176. Cf. hereafter, chap. LXXIII, towards the end of the 6<sup>th</sup> proposition, and Aaron ben-Elijah, <span dir="rtl">עץ חיים</span>, chaps. IV and LXXXVI (pages 17 and 115 of the Leipzig edition), where <i>acquisition</i> is designated, in Hebrew, by the word <span dir="rtl">ריוח</span>.
**Correction:** Several doctors of the sect of the <i>Asharites</i> (who profess an absolute fatalism), whilst admitting that the actions of men emanate only from the will and the omnipotence of God (see Part III<sup></sup> of this work, chap. XVII, 3<sup>rd</sup> opinion), sought nevertheless to attribute unto man, in the causality of actions, a small part whereby he acquireth merit or demerit; this concurrence of man in the action created by God, an ungraspable hypothesis and void of sense, is designated by the word <i>acquisition</i> <span dir="rtl">اكتساب كسب</span>. See Pococke, <i>Specimen hist. Arab.</i>, pp. 239, 240, 248 et seq.), and mine article <i>Arabes in</i> the <i>Dictionnaire des sciences philosophiques</i>, vol. I, p. 176. Cf. hereafter, chap. LXXIII, towards the end of the 6<sup>th</sup> proposition, and Aaron ben-Elijah, <span dir="rtl">עץ חיים</span>, chaps. IV and LXXXVI (pages 17 and 115 of the Leipzig edition), where <i>acquisition</i> is designated, in Hebrew, by the word <span dir="rtl">ריוח</span>.

**French (Source):** Plusieurs docteurs de la secte des [[t:0]]Ascharites[[t:1]] (qui professe un fatalisme absolu), tout en admettant que les actions des hommes n’émanent que de la volonté et de la toute-puissance de Dieu (voir la III[[t:2]]e[[t:3]] partie de cet ouvrage, chap. XVII, 3[[t:4]]e[[t:5]] opinion), cherchaient néanmoins à attribuer à l’homme, dans la causalité des actions, une petite part par laquelle il acquiert un mérite ou un démérite; ce concours de l’homme dans l’action créée par Dieu, hypothèse insaisissable et vide de sens, est désigné par le mot [[t:6]]acquisition[[t:7]] [[t:8]]اكتساب كسب[[t:9]] . Voy. Pococke, [[t:10]]Specimen hist. Arab.[[t:11]], pag. 239, 240, 248 et suiv.), et mon article [[t:12]]Arabes dans[[t:13]] le [[t:14]]Dictionnaire des sciences philosophiques[[t:15]], tome I, pag. 176. Cf. ci-après, chap. LXXIII, vers la fin de la 6[[t:16]]e[[t:17]] proposition, et Ahron ben-Elie, [[t:18]]עץ חיים[[t:19]], chap. IV et LXXXVI (pages 17 et 115 de l’édition de Leipzic), où l’[[t:20]]acquisition[[t:21]] est désignée, en hébreu, par le mot [[t:22]]ריוח[[t:23]].

### fn.467 (Footnote)
**English:** The author alludeth to the verbose style and the pompous images which the <i>Motécallemîn</i> employed in their books, as well as to the vehemence they displayed in discussion, supplying the place of arguments with invectives and sophisms. Cf. hereafter, at the beginning of chap. LXXIV. The version of Ibn Tibbon doth not entirely accord with the text; the words <span dir="rtl">במאמרים שמשתדל אומרם לשמרם ברוב דברים</span> must be erased therefrom, as they seem to be but an explanatory gloss which the copyists have introduced into the text. The words <span dir="rtl">ובהוצאת דִּבּוות והרחקות</span> are a double translation of the Arabic word <span dir="rtl">ואלתשניעאת</span>, and we believe that the word <span dir="rtl">והרחקות</span> should be suppressed; the Arabic words <span dir="rtl">באלציאח ואלתشניעאת</span> signify literally: <i>clamoribus et infamationibus</i>. The version of Al-Harizi is here preferable to that of Ibn Tibbon; it runneth: <span dir="rtl">כי יעשו להם חיוק ברוב הטלות והדמיונים המזויפים ויחזקו אותם בקולות ובגערות</span>.
**Correction:** The author alludeth to the verbose style and the pompous images which the <i>Motécallemîn</i> employed in their books, as well as to the vehemence they displayed in discussion, supplying the place of arguments with invectives and sophisms. Cf. hereafter, at the beginning of chap. LXXIV. The version of Ibn Tibbon doth not entirely accord with the text; the words <span dir="rtl">במאמרים שמשתדל אומרם לשמרם ברוב דברים</span> must be erased therefrom, as they seem to be but an explanatory gloss which the copyists have introduced into the text. The words <span dir="rtl">ובהוצאת דִּבּוות והרחקות</span> are a double translation of the Arabic word <span dir="rtl">ואלתשניעאת</span>, and we believe that the word <span dir="rtl">והרחקות</span> should be suppressed; the Arabic words <span dir="rtl">באלציאח ואלתشניעאת</span> signify literally: <i>clamoribus et infamationibus</i>. The version of Al-Harizi is here preferable to that of Ibn Tibbon; it runneth: <span dir="rtl">כי יעשו להם חיוק ברוב הטלות והדמיונים המזויפים ויחזקו אותם בקולות ובגערות</span>.

**French (Source):** L’auteur fait allusion au style verbeux et aux images pompeuses que les [[t:0]]Motécallemîn[[t:1]] employaient dans leurs livres, ainsi qu’à la véhémence qu’ils mettaient dans la discussion en suppléant aux arguments par des invectives et des sophismes. Cf. ci-après, au commencement du chap. LXXIV. La version d’Ibn-Tibbon ne s’accorde pas entièrement avec le texte; il faut y effacer les mots [[t:2]]במאמרים שמשתדל אומרם לשמרם ברוב דברים[[t:3]], qui ne paraissent être qu’une glose explicative que les copistes ont fait entrer dans le texte. Les mots [[t:4]]ובהוצאת דִּבּות והרחקות[[t:5]] sont une double traduction du mot arabe [[t:6]]ואלתשניעאת[[t:7]], et nous croyons qu’il faut supprimer le mot [[t:8]]והרחקות[[t:9]]; les mots arabes [[t:10]]באלציאח ואלתשניעאת[[t:11]] signifient littéralement: [[t:12]]clamoribus et infamationibus[[t:13]]. La version d’Al-’Harizi est ici préférable à celle d’Ibn-Tibbon; elle porte: [[t:14]]כי יעשו להם חיוק ברוב הטלות והדמיונים המזוייפים ויחזקו אותם בקולות ובגערות[[t:15]].

**Correction:** Vide the II<sup>nd</sup> part of this work, introduction (Props. XIX et seq.) and chap. I.

### fn.537 (Footnote)
**English:** The author alludeth, on the one hand, to the <i>cifâtiyya</i> or partisans of attributes, who, without any evasion, acknowledge in God eternal attributes, and, on the other hand, to a portion of the <i>Mo’tazales</i>, who thought to satisfy the principle of absolute unity by saying that God is <i>powerful</i>, not by power, but by His <i>essence</i>, that He is <i>living</i>, not by life, but by His <i>essence</i>, and so forth. Vide Pococke, <i>l. c.</i>, p. 214, and the text of Abu’l-Faraj, p. 19; Schahrestâni, <i>l. c.</i>, p. 42; Schmölders, <i>l. c.</i>, pp. 196, 197. The discussion touching the attributes was renewed among the Jewish theologians; there were some (notably in the sect of the Karaites) who, following the example of certain <i>Mo’tazales</i>, admitted essential attributes (<span dir="rtl">ספורים עצמיים</span>, vide Ahron ben-Elie, <i>Tree of Life</i>, at the end of chap. LXVII). Among the Rabbanite doctors, Maimonides himself nameth the Spaniard R. Joseph ibn-Zaddik as having followed the opinion of the <span dir="rtl">בעלי התארים</span> or <i>partisans of attributes;</i> vide the collection of the Letters of Maimonides (letter to R. Samuel ibn-Tibbon), Amsterdam edition, fol. 14 <i>b</i>. — As for the expression <span dir="rtl">لذاته</span>, <i>by His essence</i>, the Muslim theologians themselves were not altogether agreed upon its precise meaning (Vide Pococke, <i>ibid.</i>, p. 215 et seq.); for which reason Maimonides saith that <i>they do not express themselves in intelligible words</i>. According to Ibn-Falaquera (<i>Moré-ha-Moré</i>, p. 151), the <i>Mutakallimûn</i> understood by this expression that power, knowledge, etc., attributed to God, are somewhat which belongeth <i>to His essence alone</i>, and that He hath therein nothing in common with any being.
**Correction:** The author alludeth, on the one hand, to the <i>cifâtiyya</i> or partisans of attributes, who, without any evasion, acknowledge in God eternal attributes, and, on the other hand, to a portion of the <i>Mo’tazales</i>, who thought to satisfy the principle of absolute unity by saying that God is <i>powerful</i>, not by power, but by His <i>essence</i>, that He is <i>living</i>, not by life, but by His <i>essence</i>, and so forth. Vide Pococke, <i>l. c.</i>, p. 214, and the text of Abu’l-Faraj, p. 19; Schahrestâni, <i>l. c.</i>, p. 42; Schmölders, <i>l. c.</i>, pp. 196, 197. The discussion touching the attributes was renewed among the Jewish theologians; there were some (notably in the sect of the Karaites) who, following the example of certain <i>Mo’tazales</i>, admitted essential attributes (<span dir="rtl">ספורים עצמיים</span>, vide Ahron ben-Elie, <i>Tree of Life</i>, at the end of chap. LXVII). Among the Rabbanite doctors, Maimonides himself nameth the Spaniard R. Joseph ibn-Zaddik as having followed the opinion of the <span dir="rtl">בעלי התארים</span> or <i>partisans of attributes;</i> vide the collection of the Letters of Maimonides (letter to R. Samuel ibn-Tibbon), Amsterdam edition, fol. 14 <i>b</i>. — As for the expression <span dir="rtl">لذاته</span>, <i>by His essence</i>, the Muslim theologians themselves were not altogether agreed upon its precise meaning (Vide Pococke, <i>ibid.</i>, p. 215 et seq.); for which reason Maimonides saith that <i>they do not express themselves in intelligible words</i>. According to Ibn-Falaquera (<i>Moré-ha-Moré</i>, p. 151), the <i>Mutakallimûn</i> understood by this expression that power, knowledge, etc., attributed to God, are somewhat which belongeth <i>to His essence alone</i>, and that He hath therein nothing in common with any being.

**French (Source):** L’auteur fait allusion, d’une part, aux [[t:0]]cifâtiyya[[t:1]] ou partisans des attributs, qui, sans aucun détour, reconnaissent à Dieu des attributs éternels, et, d’autre part, à une partie des [[t:2]]mo’tazales[[t:3]], qui croyaient satisfaire au principe de l’unité absolue en disant que Dieu est [[t:4]]puissant[[t:5]], non par la puissance, mais par son [[t:6]]essence[[t:7]], qu’il est [[t:8]]vivant[[t:9]], non par la vie, mais par son [[t:10]]essence[[t:11]], et ainsi de suite. Voy. Pococke, [[t:12]]l. c.[[t:13]], pag. 214, et le texte d’Abou’l-Faradj, pag. 19; Schahrestâni, [[t:14]]l. c.[[t:15]], pag. 42; Schmölders, [[t:16]]l. c.[[t:17]], pag. 196, 197. La discussion sur les attributs se reproduisit chez les théologiens juifs; il y en eut (notamment dans la secte des Karaïtes) qui, suivant l’exemple de certains [[t:18]]mo’tazales[[t:19]], admettaient des attributs essentiels ([[t:20]]ספורים עצמיים[[t:21]], voy. Ahron ben-Elie, [[t:22]]Arbre de la vie[[t:23]], à la fin du chap. LXVII). Parmi les docteurs rabbanites, Maïmonide nomme lui-même l’espagnol R. Joseph ibn-Çaddîk comme ayant suivi l’opinion des [[t:24]]בעלי התארים[[t:25]] ou [[t:26]]partisans des attributs;[[t:27]] voy. le recueil des Lettres de Maïmonide (lettre à R. Samuel ibn-Tibbon), édit. d’Amsterdam, fol. 14 [[t:28]]b[[t:29]]. — Quant à l’expression [[t:30]]لذاته[[t:31]] , [[t:32]]par son essence[[t:33]], les théologiens musulmans eux mêmes n’étaient pas tout à fait d’accord sur son sens précis (Voy. Pococke, [[t:34]]ibid.[[t:35]], pag. 215 et suiv.); c’est pourquoi Maïmonide dit qu’[[t:36]]ils ne s’expriment pas par des paroles intelligibles[[t:37]]. Selon Ibn-Falaquera ([[t:38]]Moré-ha-Moré[[t:39]], pag. 151), les [[t:40]]motécallemîn[[t:41]] entendaient par cette expression que la puissance, la science, etc., attribuées à Dieu, sont quelque chose qui appartient [[t:42]]à son essence seule[[t:43]], et qu’il n’a en cela rien de commun avec aucun être.

### fn.597.sub_0 (Footnote)
**English:** As the commentator Shem-Tob observeth, that which the author saith here is nowhere to be found in the <i>Metaphysics</i> of Aristotle, but rather the contrary. Indeed, the <i>one</i> (<i>τὸ ὄν</i>) <i>per se</i>, unto which Aristotle assigneth four principal significations, which are: the <i>continuous</i>, the <i>whole</i>, the <i>individual</i>, and the <i>universal</i> (according as one considereth therein the indivisibility of motion or that of the idea), is inseparable from the very nature and quiddity of the thing which is <i>one</i>, and cannot be considered as an <i>accident</i> of the quiddity. The <i>one</i> is not in itself an essence, and it can exist only in a subject. Its proper character is to be indivisible and the first measure in every genus, and notably the measure of quantity; but, even as <i>being</i> (<i>τὸ ὄν</i>), with which it is confounded and identified, it applyeth to all the <i>categories</i>, and is not found particularly in one alone. The <i>manifold</i> is opposed to the <i>one</i>, as the divisible to the indivisible, and not as non-being to being; for the <i>manifold</i> is in <i>being</i>, as well as the <i>one</i>, and is attributed, like the latter, to all the categories. The <i>one</i> and the <i>manifold</i> in number, designating quantity alone, are abstractions and entirely distinct from <i>being.</i> See the details in the <i>Metaphysics</i> of Aristotle, book V, chap. 6, and book X, chap. 1 et seq. — Maimonides, who seeth in the <i>one</i> and the <i>manifold</i> naught but <i>accidents</i> distinct from <i>being</i>, hath further followed the opinion of Ibn-Sīnā, who, as Ibn-Roschd observeth, hath confounded the <i>one</i> of number with the absolute <i>one</i>: “A difficulty which may be found here,” saith Ibn-Roschd (loc. cit., book III), “is this: how, whilst admitting that the <i>one</i> of number belongeth to the category of <i>quantity</i>, can we subsequently admit that it is found in all the categories, and moreover that it is in the categories themselves, and not something which is accessory thereto? Wherefore Ibn-Sīnā hath thought that its substratum must be an <i>accident</i> which is found in all the categories.”
**Correction:** As the commentator Shem-Tob observeth, that which the author saith here is nowhere to be found in the <i>Metaphysics</i> of Aristotle, but rather the contrary. Indeed, the <i>one</i> (<i>τὸ ὄν</i>) <i>per se</i>, unto which Aristotle assigneth four principal significations, which are: the <i>continuous</i>, the <i>whole</i>, the <i>individual</i>, and the <i>universal</i> (according as one considereth therein the indivisibility of motion or that of the idea), is inseparable from the very nature and quiddity of the thing which is <i>one</i>, and cannot be considered as an <i>accident</i> of the quiddity. The <i>one</i> is not in itself an essence, and it can exist only in a subject. Its proper character is to be indivisible and the first measure in every genus, and notably the measure of quantity; but, even as <i>being</i> (<i>τὸ ὄν</i>), with which it is confounded and identified, it applyeth to all the <i>categories</i>, and is not found particularly in one alone. The <i>manifold</i> is opposed to the <i>one</i>, as the divisible to the indivisible, and not as non-being to being; for the <i>manifold</i> is in <i>being</i>, as well as the <i>one</i>, and is attributed, like the latter, to all the categories. The <i>one</i> and the <i>manifold</i> in number, designating quantity alone, are abstractions and entirely distinct from <i>being.</i> See the details in the <i>Metaphysics</i> of Aristotle, book V, chap. 6, and book X, chap. 1 et seq. — Maimonides, who seeth in the <i>one</i> and the <i>manifold</i> naught but <i>accidents</i> distinct from <i>being</i>, hath further followed the opinion of Ibn-Sīnā, who, as Ibn-Roschd observeth, hath confounded the <i>one</i> of number with the absolute <i>one</i>: “A difficulty which may be found here,” saith Ibn-Roschd (loc. cit., book III), “is this: how, whilst admitting that the <i>one</i> of number belongeth to the category of <i>quantity</i>, can we subsequently admit that it is found in all the categories, and moreover that it is in the categories themselves, and not something which is accessory thereto? Wherefore Ibn-Sīnā hath thought that its substratum must be an <i>accident</i> which is found in all the categories.”

**French (Source):** Ainsi que le fait observer le commantateur Schem-Tob, ce que l’auteur dit ici ne se trouve nulle part dans la [[t:0]]Métaphysique[[t:1]] d’Aristote, mais bien le contraire. En effet, l’[[t:2]]un[[t:3]] ([[t:4]]τὸ ὄν[[t:5]]) [[t:6]]en soi[[t:7]], auquel Aristote donne quatre significations principales, qui sont: le [[t:8]]continu[[t:9]], le [[t:10]]tout[[t:11]], l’[[t:12]]individu[[t:13]] et l’[[t:14]]universel[[t:15]] (selon qu’on y considère l’idivisibilité du mouvement ou celle de l’idée), est inséparable de la nature même et de la quiddité de la chose qui est [[t:16]]une[[t:17]], et ne saurait être considéré comme un [[t:18]]accident[[t:19]] de la quiddité. L’[[t:20]]un[[t:21]] n’est pas en lui-même une essence, et il ne peut être que dans un sujet. Son caractère propre est d’être indivisible et la première mesure dans chaque genre, et notamment la mesure de la quantité; mais, de même que l’[[t:22]]être[[t:23]] ([[t:24]]τὸ ὄν[[t:25]]), avec lequel il se confond et s’identifie, il s’applique à toutes les [[t:26]]catégories[[t:27]], et ne se trouve pas particulièrement dans une seule. Le [[t:28]]multiple[[t:29]] est opposé à l’[[t:30]]un[[t:31]], comme le divisible à l’indivisible, et non pas comme le non-être à l’être; car le [[t:32]]multiple[[t:33]] est dans l’[[t:34]]être[[t:35]], aussi bien que l’[[t:36]]un[[t:37]], et s’attribue, comme ce dernier, à toutes les catégories. L’[[t:38]]un[[t:39]] et le [[t:40]]multiple[[t:41]] dans le nombre, désignant la quantité seule, sont des abstractions et entièrement distincts de l’[[t:42]]être.[[t:43]] Voir les détails dans la [[t:44]]Métaphysique[[t:45]] d’Aristote, liv. V, chap. 6, et liv. X, chap. 1 et suiv. — Maïmonide, qui ne voit dans l’[[t:46]]un[[t:47]] et le [[t:48]]multiple[[t:49]] que des [[t:50]]accidents[[t:51]] distincts de l’[[t:52]]être[[t:53]], a encore suivi l’opinion d’Ibn-Sînâ, qui, comme le fait observer Ibn-Roschd, a confondu l’[[t:54]]un[[t:55]] du nombre avec l’[[t:56]]un[[t:57]] absolu: «Une difficulté qu’on peut trouver ici, dit Ibn-Roschd (l. c., liv. III), est cellc-ci: comment, en admettant que l’[[t:58]]un[[t:59]] du nombre est de la catégorie de la [[t:60]]quantité[[t:61]], pouvons-nous admettre ensuite qu’il se trouve dans toutes les catégories, et encore qu’il est dans les catégories mêmes, et non pas quelque chose qui y soit accessoire? C’est pourquoi Ibn-Sînâ a pensé que son substratum doit être un [[t:62]]accident[[t:63]] qui se trouve dans toutes les catégories.

### fn.607.sub_1 (Footnote)
**English:** Here follow the terms of the Arabic original of the treatise on the <i>Duties of the Hearts</i>: <span dir="rtl">ולהדֹא קאל ארמטוטאלים אלמואלב מן צפאת אלכֹאלק תעאלי אצדק מן אלמוגֹכאת לאן כל מא יוגֹב לה מן אלצפאת לא תכֹלו פן צפאת אלגֹוהר או אלערץֹ וכֹאלק אלגֹוהר ואלערץֹ לא ילחקה פי דֹאתה וצף מן אוצאפהמא וכל מא ינפי ענה מן אלצפאת פהי צאדקה̈ לא מהאלה̈</span> “Wherefore Aristotle saith: The negative attributes of the Creator are more true than the affirmative; for all the attributes that one might assign Him affirmatively could only be attributes of substance or of accident, and the Creator of substance and of accident cannot have in His essence any of their attributes. But the attributes which are removed from Him (that is to say, the attributes expressed negatively) are indubitably true.” — This passage, which is cited also by Ibn-Falaquera (<i>Moreh ha-Moreh</i>, p. 29), is evidently apocryphal. It is true that Aristotle, in speaking of the <i>first mover</i>, very often expresseth himself by negative terms, such as: <i>being immutable, separate from all that is sensible</i>, which <i>can have no extension</i>, which is <i>indivisible, etc.</i> (Metaph., XII, 7 and <i>passim</i>); but he in no wise excludeth affirmative terms, and that which the doctrine of the Arabian philosophers possesseth of the absolute in this regard appeareth to be drawn from the Neoplatonic commentators and to be connected with the doctrines of Plotinus (cf. Ritter, <i>Geschichte der Philosophie</i>, vol. IV, p. 573 et seq.). St. Thomas in his turn, in saying that we cannot express ourselves concerning God save by the <i>negative way</i>, followed the Arabian philosophers, from whom he doubtless borrowed the term <i>via remotionis</i> <span dir="rtl">(طريق النفي)</span> . Vide <i>Summa C. Gent</i>., bk. I, chap. 14.
**Correction:** Here follow the terms of the Arabic original of the treatise on the <i>Duties of the Hearts</i>: <span dir="rtl">ולהדֹא קאל ארמטוטאלים אלמואלב מן צפאת אלכֹאלק תעאלי אצדק מן אלמוגֹכאת לאן כל מא יוגֹב לה מן אלצפאת לא תכֹלו פן צפאת אלגֹוהר או אלערץֹ וכֹאלק אלגֹוהר ואלערץֹ לא ילחקה פי דֹאתה וצף מן אוצאפהמא וכל מא ינפי ענה מן אלצפאת פהי צאדקה̈ לא מהאלה̈</span> “Wherefore Aristotle saith: The negative attributes of the Creator are more true than the affirmative; for all the attributes that one might assign Him affirmatively could only be attributes of substance or of accident, and the Creator of substance and of accident cannot have in His essence any of their attributes. But the attributes which are removed from Him (that is to say, the attributes expressed negatively) are indubitably true.” — This passage, which is cited also by Ibn-Falaquera (<i>Moreh ha-Moreh</i>, p. 29), is evidently apocryphal. It is true that Aristotle, in speaking of the <i>first mover</i>, very often expresseth himself by negative terms, such as: <i>being immutable, separate from all that is sensible</i>, which <i>can have no extension</i>, which is <i>indivisible, etc.</i> (Metaph., XII, 7 and <i>passim</i>); but he in no wise excludeth affirmative terms, and that which the doctrine of the Arabian philosophers possesseth of the absolute in this regard appeareth to be drawn from the Neoplatonic commentators and to be connected with the doctrines of Plotinus (cf. Ritter, <i>Geschichte der Philosophie</i>, vol. IV, p. 573 et seq.). St. Thomas in his turn, in saying that we cannot express ourselves concerning God save by the <i>negative way</i>, followed the Arabian philosophers, from whom he doubtless borrowed the term <i>via remotionis</i> <span dir="rtl">(طريق النفي)</span> . Vide <i>Summa C. Gent</i>., bk. I, chap. 14.

**French (Source):** Voici les termes de l’original arabe du traité des [[t:44]]Devoirs des cœurs[[t:45]]: [[t:46]]ולהדׄא קאל ארםטוטאלים אלםואלב מן צפאת אלכׄאלק תעאלי אצדק מן אלמוגׄכאת לאן כל מא יוגׄב לה מן אלצפאת לא תכׄלו פן צפאת אלגׄוהר או אלערץׄ וכׄאלק אלגׄוהר ואלערץׄ לא ילחקה פי דׄאתה וצף מן אוצאפהמא וכל מא ינפי ענה מן אלצפאת פהי צאדקה̈ לא מהאלה̈[[t:47]] «c’est peurquoi Aristote dit: Les attributs négatifs du Créateur sont plus vrais que les affirmatifs; car tous les attributs qu’on lui donnerait affirmativement ne pourraient être que des attributs de la substance ou de l’accident, et le Créateur de la substance et de l’accident ne peut avoir dans son essence aucun de leurs attributs. Mais les attributs qu’on écarte de lui (c’est-à-dire, les attributs exprimés négativement) sont indubitablement vrais.» — Ce passage, qui est cité aussi par Ibn-Falaquera ([[t:48]]Moré ha-Moré[[t:49]], pag. 29), est évidemment apocryphe. Il est vrai qu’Aristote, en parlant du [[t:50]]premier moteur[[t:51]], s’exprime très souvent par des termes négatifs, tels que: [[t:52]]être immuable, séparé de tout ce qui est sensible[[t:53]], qui [[t:54]]ne peut avoir d’étendue[[t:55]], qui est [[t:56]]indivisible, etc.[[t:57]] (Métaph., XII, 7 et [[t:58]]passim[[t:59]]); mais il n’exclut nullement les termes affirmatifs, et ce que la doctrine des philosophes arabes a d’absolu à cet égard paraît être puisé dans les commentateurs néoplatoniciens et se rattacher aux doctrines de Plotin (cf. Ritter, [[t:60]]Geschichte der Philosophie[[t:61]], t. IV, pag. 573 et suiv.). St. Thomas à son tour, en disant que nous ne pouvons nous exprimer sur Dieu que par la [[t:62]]voie négative[[t:63]], a suivi les philosophes arabes, auxquels il a sans doute emprunté le terme de [[t:64]]via remotionis[[t:65]] [[t:66]](طريق النفي)[[t:67]] . Voy. [[t:68]]Summa C. Gent[[t:69]]., liv. I, chap. 14.

### fn.611 (Footnote)
**English:** Literally: <i>we apprehend but His</i> <small>THAT</small> <i>only, not His</i> <small>WHAT</small> (for God, being incapable of definition, we cannot say <i>what He is;</i> vide supra, p. 190). The word <span dir="rtl">אני̈</span> <span dir="rtl">(أنيّة)</span> is derived, doubtless, from the conjunction <span dir="rtl">أن</span> or <span dir="rtl">أنَّ</span>, <i>that</i> (<i>quod</i>), and, to render this Arabic term exactly, one should have to form the word <i>quoddity</i> (analogous to <i>quiddity</i>). It is the <i>ὅτ’</i>, which Aristotle placeth at the head of those objects which the intelligence hath in view in every science, and which designateth the pure <i>existence</i> of the thing (vid. <i>Posterior Analytics</i>, bk. II, chap. 1). It is only after having recognised <i>that</i> the thing <i>is</i>, that one enquireth <i>what it is</i> (<i>μετὰ ταῦτα γνόντες, ὅ τ ι ἐστί τι, τ ί οὖν τοῦτ’ ἐστί, ζ<span dir="rtl">ד</span> τοῦμεν. Ibid.</i>, chap. 2). In the <i>Kitâb al-Ta’rifât</i>, we read the following definition: <span dir="rtl">الانية تحقق الوجود العينى</span> <span dir="rtl">من حيث رتبة الذاتية</span> “The <i>anniyya</i> establisheth the existence in itself, considered at the degree of pure essence.” The sense of this definition is thus: the <i>ὅτι</i> (the <i>that</i> or the <i>quoddity</i>) is limited to establishing pure and abstract existence, from the point of view of <i>being</i>, or of <i>essence</i> (to render <span dir="rtl">الذاتية</span> exactly, one would need to form the word <i>essentiality</i>); it is <i>being</i> considered in itself and whilst making abstraction of all that might serve to determine it, such as <i>quiddity</i> (<i>τί ἐστιι</i>), <i>quality</i> (<i>πσ̃ς ἐστι</i>) and <i>cause</i> (<i>τὸ διότι</i>). Cf. my Extracts from the <i>Source of Life</i> of Ibn Gabirol, or Avicebron, bk. V, § 30. M. Silv. de Sacy hath given of the said definition a translation entirely different from mine own; yet he himself confesseth that he doth not warrant the accuracy of his translation, and he offereth upon the etymology of the word <span dir="rtl">الإنية</span> a conjecture of little plausibility. Vid. Notices et extraits des manuscrits, t. X, p. 85.
**Correction:** Literally: <i>we apprehend but His</i> <small>THAT</small> <i>only, not His</i> <small>WHAT</small> (for God, being incapable of definition, we cannot say <i>what He is;</i> vide supra, p. 190). The word <span dir="rtl">אני̈</span> <span dir="rtl">(أنيّة)</span> is derived, doubtless, from the conjunction <span dir="rtl">أن</span> or <span dir="rtl">أنَّ</span>, <i>that</i> (<i>quod</i>), and, to render this Arabic term exactly, one should have to form the word <i>quoddity</i> (analogous to <i>quiddity</i>). It is the <i>ὅτ’</i>, which Aristotle placeth at the head of those objects which the intelligence hath in view in every science, and which designateth the pure <i>existence</i> of the thing (vid. <i>Posterior Analytics</i>, bk. II, chap. 1). It is only after having recognised <i>that</i> the thing <i>is</i>, that one enquireth <i>what it is</i> (<i>μετὰ ταῦτα γνόντες, ὅ τ ι ἐστί τι, τ ί οὖν τοῦτ’ ἐστί, ζ<span dir="rtl">ד</span> τοῦμεν. Ibid.</i>, chap. 2). In the <i>Kitâb al-Ta’rifât</i>, we read the following definition: <span dir="rtl">الانية تحقق الوجود العينى</span> <span dir="rtl">من حيث رتبة الذاتية</span> “The <i>anniyya</i> establisheth the existence in itself, considered at the degree of pure essence.” The sense of this definition is thus: the <i>ὅτι</i> (the <i>that</i> or the <i>quoddity</i>) is limited to establishing pure and abstract existence, from the point of view of <i>being</i>, or of <i>essence</i> (to render <span dir="rtl">الذاتية</span> exactly, one would need to form the word <i>essentiality</i>); it is <i>being</i> considered in itself and whilst making abstraction of all that might serve to determine it, such as <i>quiddity</i> (<i>τί ἐστιι</i>), <i>quality</i> (<i>πσ̃ς ἐστι</i>) and <i>cause</i> (<i>τὸ διότι</i>). Cf. my Extracts from the <i>Source of Life</i> of Ibn Gabirol, or Avicebron, bk. V, § 30. M. Silv. de Sacy hath given of the said definition a translation entirely different from mine own; yet he himself confesseth that he doth not warrant the accuracy of his translation, and he offereth upon the etymology of the word <span dir="rtl">الإنية</span> a conjecture of little plausibility. Vid. Notices et extraits des manuscrits, t. X, p. 85.

**French (Source):** Littéralement: [[t:0]]nous ne saisissons que son[[t:1]] [[t:2]]QUE[[t:3]] [[t:4]]seulement, non son[[t:5]] [[t:6]]QUOI[[t:7]] (car Dieu, ne pouvant être défini, nous ne pouvons pas dire [[t:8]]ce qu’il est;[[t:9]] voy. ci-dessus, pag. 190). Le mot [[t:10]]אניח̈[[t:11]] [[t:12]](أنيّة)[[t:13]]  est dérivé, sans doute, de la conjonction [[t:14]]أن[[t:15]]  ou [[t:16]]أَنَّ[[t:17]] , [[t:18]]que[[t:19]] ([[t:20]]quod[[t:21]]), et, pour rendre exactement ce terme arabe, il faudrait former le mot [[t:22]]quoddité[[t:23]] (analogue à [[t:24]]quiddité[[t:25]]). C’est le [[t:26]]ὅτ׳[[t:27]], qu’Aristote met à la tête des objets que l’intelligence a en vue dans toute science, et qui désigne la pure [[t:28]]existence[[t:29]] de la chose (voy. [[t:30]]Derniers Analitiques[[t:31]], liv. II, chap. 1). Ce n’est qu’après avoir reconnu [[t:32]]que[[t:33]] la chose [[t:34]]est[[t:35]], qu’on s’enquiert de [[t:36]]ce qu’elle est[[t:37]] ([[t:38]]μετὰ ταῦτα γνόντες, ὅ τ ι ἐστί τι, τ ί οὖν τοῦτ’ ἐστι, ζ[[t:39]]ד[[t:40]] τοῦμεν. Ibid.[[t:41]], chap. 2). Dans le [[t:42]]Kitâb al-Ta’rifât[[t:43]], on lit la définition suivante: [[t:44]]الانية تحقق الوجود العينى[[t:45]]  [[t:46]]من حيث رتبة الذاتية[[t:47]]  «L’[[t:48]]anniyya[[t:49]] constate l’existence en elle-même, considérée au degré de la pure essence.» Le sens de cette définition est celui-ci: le [[t:50]]ὅτι[[t:51]] (le [[t:52]]que[[t:53]] ou la [[t:54]]quoddité[[t:55]]) se borne à constater l’existence pure et abstraite, au point de vue de l’[[t:56]]être[[t:57]], ou de l’[[t:58]]essence[[t:59]] (pour rendre exactement [[t:60]]الذاتية[[t:61]] , il faudrait former le mot [[t:62]]essentialité[[t:63]]); c’est l’[[t:64]]être[[t:65]] considéré en lui-même et en faisant abstraction de tout ce qui peut servir à le déterminer, comme la [[t:66]]quiddité[[t:67]] ([[t:68]]τί ἐστιι[[t:69]]), la [[t:70]]qualité[[t:71]] ([[t:72]]πσ̃ς ἐστι[[t:73]]) et la [[t:74]]cause[[t:75]] ([[t:76]]τὸ διότι[[t:77]]). Cf. mes Extraits de la [[t:78]]Source de la vie[[t:79]] d’Ibn-Gebirol, ou Avicebron, liv. V, § 30. M. Silv. de Sacy a donné de ladite définition une traduction toute différente de la mienne; mais il avoue lui-même qu’il ne garantit pas l’exactitude de sa traduction, et il donne sur l’étymologie du mot [[t:80]]الإنية[[t:81]]  une conjecture fort peu plausible. Voy. Notices et extraits des manuscrits, t. X, pag. 85.

### fn.677 (Footnote)
**English:** The words <span dir="rtl">ענקא מגרב</span> <span dir="rtl">(عَنْقَاءُ مُغْرِبِ)</span> designate, among the Arabs, a fabulous bird, like unto our <i>griffon</i>. The word <span dir="rtl">مغرب</span> is an epithet concerning the meaning of which the Arabs themselves are not in accord; but it is most generally believed that this epithet was bestowed upon the bird <i>’ankâ</i> because, it is said, it <i>beareth far away</i> all the prey which it seizeth. Vid. <i>Les oiseaux et les fleurs</i>, by M. Garcin de Tassy, p. 218 et seq., and the <i>Séances de Hariri</i>, edit. of MM. Reinaud and Derenbourg, p. 678, and the French notes, <i>ibid.</i>, p. 202. — The two Hebrew translators have put <span dir="rtl">העזניה הנפלאה</span>, taking the word <span dir="rtl">مغرب</span> in the sense of <i>marvellous</i>, and seeing in the bird ’<i>ankâ</i> one of the species of eagle, that which, in the Pentateuch (Levit., XI, 13, and Deut., XIV, 12), is called <span dir="rtl">עזניה</span>. Verily, Saadia, in his Arabic version of the Pentateuch, rendereth this word by <span dir="rtl">عنقاء</span>; but he hath been censured by Ibn Ezra, who rightly observeth that the bird <i>’ankâ</i>, being a fabulous creature, could not appear among the animals whose flesh was forbidden unto the Hebrews. It seemeth nonetheless to result from the whole of our passage that Maimonides also believed that the word <span dir="rtl">عنقاء</span> designated originally a real being; indeed, according to Damiri, cited by Bochart, this word would denote a species of eagle, and would be synonymous with <span dir="rtl">عُقَاب</span>. Vid. Bochart, <i>Hierozoïcon</i>, t. II, p. 812 (edit. of Rosenmüller, t. III, p. 803).
**Correction:** The words <span dir="rtl">ענקא מגרב</span> <span dir="rtl">(عَنْقَاءُ مُغْرِبِ)</span> designate, among the Arabs, a fabulous bird, like unto our <i>griffon</i>. The word <span dir="rtl">مغرب</span> is an epithet concerning the meaning of which the Arabs themselves are not in accord; but it is most generally believed that this epithet was bestowed upon the bird <i>’ankâ</i> because, it is said, it <i>beareth far away</i> all the prey which it seizeth. Vid. <i>Les oiseaux et les fleurs</i>, by M. Garcin de Tassy, p. 218 et seq., and the <i>Séances de Hariri</i>, edit. of MM. Reinaud and Derenbourg, p. 678, and the French notes, <i>ibid.</i>, p. 202. — The two Hebrew translators have put <span dir="rtl">העזניה הנפלאה</span>, taking the word <span dir="rtl">مغرب</span> in the sense of <i>marvellous</i>, and seeing in the bird ’<i>ankâ</i> one of the species of eagle, that which, in the Pentateuch (Levit., XI, 13, and Deut., XIV, 12), is called <span dir="rtl">עזניה</span>. Verily, Saadia, in his Arabic version of the Pentateuch, rendereth this word by <span dir="rtl">عنقاء</span>; but he hath been censured by Ibn Ezra, who rightly observeth that the bird <i>’ankâ</i>, being a fabulous creature, could not appear among the animals whose flesh was forbidden unto the Hebrews. It seemeth nonetheless to result from the whole of our passage that Maimonides also believed that the word <span dir="rtl">عنقاء</span> designated originally a real being; indeed, according to Damiri, cited by Bochart, this word would denote a species of eagle, and would be synonymous with <span dir="rtl">عُقَاب</span>. Vid. Bochart, <i>Hierozoïcon</i>, t. II, p. 812 (edit. of Rosenmüller, t. III, p. 803).

**French (Source):** Les mots [[t:0]]ענקא מגרב[[t:1]] [[t:2]](عَنْقَاءُ مُغْرِبِ)[[t:3]]  désignent, chez les Arabes, un oiseau fabuleux, comme notre [[t:4]]griffon[[t:5]]. Le mot [[t:6]]مغرب[[t:7]]  est une épithète sur le sens de laquelle les Arabes eux-même ne sont pas d’accord; mais on croit le plus généralement que cette épithète a été donnée à l’oiseau [[t:8]]’ankâ[[t:9]] parce que, dit-on, il [[t:10]]emporte bien loin[[t:11]] toutes les proies qu’il saisit. Voy. [[t:12]]Les oiseaux et les fleurs[[t:13]], par M. Garcin de Tassy, pag. 218 et suiv., etles [[t:14]]Séances de Hariri[[t:15]], édit. de MM. Reinaud et Derenbourg, pag. 678, et les notes françaises, [[t:16]]ibid.[[t:17]], pag. 202. — Les deux traducteurs hébreux ont mis [[t:18]]העזניה הנפלאה[[t:19]], en prenant le mot [[t:20]]مغرب[[t:21]]  dans le sens de [[t:22]]merveilleux[[t:23]], et en voyant dans l’oiseau ’[[t:24]]ankâ[[t:25]] une des espèces de l’aigle, celle qui, dans le Pentateuque (Lévit., 11, 13, et Deut., XIV, 12), est appelée [[t:26]]עזניה[[t:27]]. En effet, Saadia, dans sa version arabe du Pentateuque, rend ce mot par [[t:28]]عنقاء[[t:29]] ; mais il a été critiqué par Ibn-Ezra, qui fait observer avec raison que l’oiseau [[t:30]]’ankâ[[t:31]], étant un être fabuleux, ne saurait figurer parmi les animaux dont la chair était défendue aux Hébreux. Il paraît néanmoins résulter de l’ensemble de notre passage que Maïmonide aussi croyait que le mot [[t:32]]عنقاء[[t:33]]  désignait primitivement un être réel; en effet, selon Damiri, cité par Bochart, ce mot désignerait une espèce d’aigle, et serait synonyme de [[t:34]]عُقَاب[[t:35]] . Voy. Bochart, [[t:36]]Hierozoïcon[[t:37]], t. II, pag. 812 (édit de Rosenmüller, t. III, pag. 803).

### fn.865 (Footnote)
**English:** Literally: <i>as it was said, according to what was said</i>, that is, according to the traditional explanation given by the rabbis for the vision of Ezekiel, which mentions the <i>four animals</i> and the celestial throne. See Ezekiel, ch. I, verses 5 et seq., and cf. the III<sup>rd</sup> part of this work, ch. II.
**Correction:** Literally: <i>as it was said, according to what was said</i>, that is, according to the traditional explanation given by the rabbis for the vision of Ezekiel, which mentions the <i>four animals</i> and the celestial throne. See Ezekiel, ch. I, verses 5 et seq., and cf. the III<sup>rd</sup> part of this work, ch. II.

Voy. Ézéchiel, chap. I, versets 5 et suiv., et cf. la III[[t:4]]e[[t:5]] partie de cet ouvrage, chap. II.

### fn.880.sub_0 (Footnote)
**English:** The word <i>kalam</i> <span dir="rtl">(كلام)</span>, which means <i>word</i>, <i>discourse</i>, also designates a science that formed among the Muslims as early as the 2nd century of the Hegira, and whose goal was to combat the doctrines of heterodox sects and later those of the philosophers, by using against its adversaries the weapons of dialectic and reasoning borrowed from philosophical schools. This science can be called <i>dogmatics</i>, or rational theology, and it has much analogy with Christian <i>scholasticism</i>. <i>Kalam</i> developed especially since the introduction of Peripatetic philosophy among the Arabs, and it will be seen further on (ch. LXXIII et seq.) what doctrines were established by this science and how they proceeded to demonstrate the truth of the principal religious dogmas, particularly those establishing the unity and immateriality of God and Creation. As for the name <i>kalam</i>, the Arabs themselves do not agree on its origin; according to some, this science was so named because it primarily discussed what was meant by the <i>divine word</i> or by the <i>word</i> (<i>kalam</i>) attributed to God (cf. below, p. 343, note 3); according to others, the word <i>kalam</i> is merely an imitation of the word <i>mantiq</i> <span dir="rtl">(منطق)</span>, which also means <i>speech</i>, or <i>discourse</i>, and by which philosophers designated one of the principal branches of their doctrine, namely, <i>logic</i>. See Shahrastani, <i>History of Religious and Philosophical Sects</i>, Arabic text, p. 18 (German trans., vol. I, p. 26); cf. Pococke, <i>Specimen hist. ar.</i>, p. 195. From the word <i>kalam</i> comes the verb <i>takallam</i> <span dir="rtl">(تكلم)</span>, having the sense of <i>professing kalam</i>, and whose participle <i>mutakallim</i>, in the plural <i>mutakallimun</i>, designates the partisans of <i>kalam</i>.
**Correction:** The word <i>kalam</i> <span dir="rtl">(كلام)</span>, which means <i>word</i>, <i>discourse</i>, also designates a science that formed among the Muslims as early as the 2nd century of the Hegira, and whose goal was to combat the doctrines of heterodox sects and later those of the philosophers, by using against its adversaries the weapons of dialectic and reasoning borrowed from philosophical schools. This science can be called <i>dogmatics</i>, or rational theology, and it has much analogy with Christian <i>scholasticism</i>. <i>Kalam</i> developed especially since the introduction of Peripatetic philosophy among the Arabs, and it will be seen further on (ch. LXXIII et seq.) what doctrines were established by this science and how they proceeded to demonstrate the truth of the principal religious dogmas, particularly those establishing the unity and immateriality of God and Creation. As for the name <i>kalam</i>, the Arabs themselves do not agree on its origin; according to some, this science was so named because it primarily discussed what was meant by the <i>divine word</i> or by the <i>word</i> (<i>kalam</i>) attributed to God (cf. below, p. 343, note 3); according to others, the word <i>kalam</i> is merely an imitation of the word <i>mantiq</i> <span dir="rtl">(منطق)</span>, which also means <i>speech</i>, or <i>discourse</i>, and by which philosophers designated one of the principal branches of their doctrine, namely, <i>logic</i>. See Shahrastani, <i>History of Religious and Philosophical Sects</i>, Arabic text, p. 18 (German trans., vol. I, p. 26); cf. Pococke, <i>Specimen hist. ar.</i>, p. 195. From the word <i>kalam</i> comes the verb <i>takallam</i> <span dir="rtl">(تكلم)</span>, having the sense of <i>professing kalam</i>, and whose participle <i>mutakallim</i>, in the plural <i>mutakallimun</i>, designates the partisans of <i>kalam</i>.

**French (Source):** Le mot [[t:0]]calâm[[t:1]] [[t:2]](كلام)[[t:3]] , qui signifie [[t:4]]parole[[t:5]], [[t:6]]discours[[t:7]], désigne aussi une science qui se forma chez les musulmans dès le II[[t:8]]e[[t:9]] siècle de l’hégire, et qui avait pour but de combattre les doctrines des sectes hétérodoxes et plus tard celle des philosophes, en employant contre ses adversaires les armes de la dialectique et les raisonnements empruntés aux écoles philosophiques. On peut donner à cette science le nom de [[t:10]]dogmatique[[t:11]], ou de théologie rationnelle, et elle a beaucoup d’analogie avec la [[t:12]]scolastique[[t:13]] chrétienne. Le [[t:14]]calâm[[t:15]] se développa surtout depuis l’introduction, parmi les Arabes, de la philosophie péripatéticienne, et on verra plus loin (chap. LXXIII et suiv.) quelles furent les doctrines établies par cette science et comment elles s’y prenaient pour démontrer la vérité des principaux dogmes religieux, notamment de ceux qui établissent l’unité et l’immatérialité de Dieu et la Création. Quant au nom de [[t:16]]calâm[[t:17]], les Arabes eux-mêmes ne sont pas d’accord sur son origine; selon les uns, on appelait ainsi ladite science parce qu’on y avait d’abord discuté principalement sur ce qu’il fallait entendre par [[t:18]]parole divine[[t:19]] ou par la [[t:20]]parole[[t:21]] ([[t:22]]calâm[[t:23]]) attribuée à Dieu (cf. ci-après, pag. 343, note 3); selon les autres, le mot [[t:24]]calâm[[t:25]] ne serait qu’une imitation du mot [[t:26]]mantik[[t:27]] [[t:28]](منطق)[[t:29]] , qui signifie également [[t:30]]parole[[t:31]], ou [[t:32]]discours[[t:33]], et par lequel les philosophes désignaient une des principales branches de leur doctrine, savoir, la [[t:34]]logique[[t:35]]. Voy. Schahrestâni, [[t:36]]Histoire des sectes religieuses et philosophiques[[t:37]], texte ar., pag. 18 (trad. all., t. I, pag. 26); cf. Pococke, [[t:38]]Specimen hist. ar[[t:39]]., pag. 195. Du mot [[t:40]]calâm[[t:41]] vient le verbe [[t:42]]técallam[[t:43]] [[t:44]](تكلم)[[t:45]] , ayant le sens de [[t:46]]professer le calâm[[t:47]], et dont le participe [[t:48]]motécallem[[t:49]], au pluriel [[t:50]]motécallémîn[[t:51]], désigne les partisans du [[t:52]]calâm[[t:53]].

### fn.880.sub_1 (Footnote)
**English:** Having regard for the meaning of <i>word</i> that the word <i>kalam</i> possesses, the Hebrew translators called this science <span dir="rtl">חכמת הדבר</span> and designated the Mutakallimun under the name <i>Medabberim</i> (<span dir="rtl">מדברים</span>), which the Latin translators, in turn, rendered as <i>loquentes</i>. One may also see what I have said on <i>kalam</i> in the <i>Dictionnaire des sciences philosophiques</i>, vol. I, art. <i>Arabes</i> (pp. 169 and 174), and in my <i>Notice on R. Saadia Gaon</i>, p. 16 et seq. (Bible by M. Cahen, vol. IX, p. 88 et seq.). — The two Hebrew translators did not fully grasp the meaning that the word <span dir="rtl">אלכלאם</span> has in our passage, and they took it in its primitive sense of <i>word</i> or <i>discourse;</i> Ibn Tibbon translates: <span dir="rtl">מדברי ענין היחוד</span>, and Al-Harizi: <span dir="rtl">מדבריהם בענין היחוד</span>. Following the words we have just cited, one should read in Ibn Tibbon's version: <span dir="rtl">ומה שנתלה בזה הענין</span>, as found in the manuscripts and the <i>editio princeps;</i> in the other editions, it was erroneously put as: <span dir="rtl">ומה שנתגלה מזה הענין</span>.
**Correction:** Having regard for the meaning of <i>word</i> that the word <i>kalam</i> possesses, the Hebrew translators called this science <span dir="rtl">חכמת הדבר</span> and designated the Mutakallimun under the name <i>Medabberim</i> (<span dir="rtl">מדברים</span>), which the Latin translators, in turn, rendered as <i>loquentes</i>. One may also see what I have said on <i>kalam</i> in the <i>Dictionnaire des sciences philosophiques</i>, vol. I, art. <i>Arabes</i> (pp. 169 and 174), and in my <i>Notice on R. Saadia Gaon</i>, p. 16 et seq. (Bible by M. Cahen, vol. IX, p. 88 et seq.). — The two Hebrew translators did not fully grasp the meaning that the word <span dir="rtl">אלכלאם</span> has in our passage, and they took it in its primitive sense of <i>word</i> or <i>discourse;</i> Ibn Tibbon translates: <span dir="rtl">מדברי ענין היחוד</span>, and Al-Harizi: <span dir="rtl">מדבריהם בענין היחוד</span>. Following the words we have just cited, one should read in Ibn Tibbon's version: <span dir="rtl">ומה שנתלה בזה הענין</span>, as found in the manuscripts and the <i>editio princeps;</i> in the other editions, it was erroneously put as: <span dir="rtl">ומה שנתגלה מזה הענין</span>.

**French (Source):** Ayant égard au sens de [[t:54]]parole[[t:55]] qu’a le mot [[t:56]]calâm[[t:57]], les traducteurs hébreux ont appelé cette science [[t:58]]חכמת הדבר[[t:59]] et ont désigné les Motécallémîn sous le nom de [[t:60]]Medabberim[[t:61]] ([[t:62]]מדברים[[t:63]]), que les traducteurs latins, à leur tour, ont rendu par [[t:64]]loquentes[[t:65]]. On peut voir aussi ce que j’ai dit sur le [[t:66]]calâm[[t:67]] dans le [[t:68]]Dictionnaire des sciences philosophiques[[t:69]], t. I, art. [[t:70]]Arabes[[t:71]] (pag. 169 et 174), et dans ma [[t:72]]Notice sur R. Saadia Gaôn[[t:73]], pag. 16 et suiv. (Bible de M. Cahen, t. IX, pag. 88 et suiv.). — Les deux traducteurs hébreux ne se sont pas bien rendu compte du sens que le mot [[t:74]]אלכלאם[[t:75]] a dans notre passage, et ils l’ont pris dans son sens primitif de [[t:76]]parole[[t:77]] ou [[t:78]]discours;[[t:79]] Ibn-Tibbon traduit: [[t:80]]מדברי ענין היחוד[[t:81]], et Al-’Harizi: [[t:82]]מדבריהם בענין היחוד[[t:83]]. A la suite des mots que nous venons de citer, il faut lire, dans la version d’Ibn-Tibbon: [[t:84]]ומה שנתלה בזה הענין[[t:85]], comme l’ont les manuscrits et l’édition [[t:86]]princeps;[[t:87]] dans les autres éditions, on a mis par erreur: [[t:88]]ומה שנתגלה מזה הענין[[t:89]].

### fn.881 (Footnote)
**English:** It is known that the title of <i>Gaon</i> <span dir="rtl">גאון</span> (pl. <i>Geonim</i>) is that held by the heads of the Jewish academies of Babylon under Arabic rule, from the end of the 7th until around the middle of the 11th century. The period of the <i>Geonim</i> coincides with that of the development of <i>kalam</i> among the Arabs; the example set by Muslim theologians was followed by certain Jewish doctors of the East who, like the <i>Mutakallimun</i>, sought to support religious dogmas through philosophical speculation and laid the foundations of a systematic and rational theology. It was the doctors of the Karaite sect who first entered this path and themselves adopted the name <i>Mutakallimun</i> (see the <i>Khozari</i>, book V, § 15), without, however, admitting all the hypotheses of Muslim <i>kalam</i>, which will be set forth further on (ch. LXXIII). Some Rabbanite doctors among the <i>Geonim</i> imitated the example of the Karaites (cf. above, p. 286, note 3, and p. 290, note 2). We have remaining, in the <i>Book of Beliefs and Opinions</i> of Rabbi Saadia Gaon, an important monument of what can be called Jewish <i>kalam</i>, and it is to this celebrated doctor that Maimonides here appears to allude, as Moses of Narbonne observes in his commentary on our passage. In vol. III of the <i>Dictionnaire des sciences philosophiques</i>, art. <i>Juifs</i> (pp. 355-357), I have given some other details on this subject. I will further add that Arabic authors of the 10th century expressly speak of Jewish <i>Mutakallimun</i>. Al-Masudi cites several whom he had personally known (see Silv. de Sacy, <i>Notices et Extraits des manuscrits</i>, vol. VIII, p. 167). Isa ibn Zara‘a, a Christian Arabic author of Baghdad, speaking in his theological treatises of a certain Abu al-Khayr Dawud ibn Mushaj, says that he was one of the principal Jewish <i>Mutakallimun</i> and a profound thinker <span dir="rtl">(...من متكلّمي اليهود وكان متقدّماً فيهم نظّارا)</span>. See Arab. ms. of the Biblioth. imp., ancient collection, no. 98, fol. 40 <i>b</i> and 42 <i>a</i>.
**Correction:** It is known that the title of <i>Gaon</i> <span dir="rtl">גאון</span> (pl. <i>Geonim</i>) is that held by the heads of the Jewish academies of Babylon under Arabic rule, from the end of the 7th until around the middle of the 11th century. The period of the <i>Geonim</i> coincides with that of the development of <i>kalam</i> among the Arabs; the example set by Muslim theologians was followed by certain Jewish doctors of the East who, like the <i>Mutakallimun</i>, sought to support religious dogmas through philosophical speculation and laid the foundations of a systematic and rational theology. It was the doctors of the Karaite sect who first entered this path and themselves adopted the name <i>Mutakallimun</i> (see the <i>Khozari</i>, book V, § 15), without, however, admitting all the hypotheses of Muslim <i>kalam</i>, which will be set forth further on (ch. LXXIII). Some Rabbanite doctors among the <i>Geonim</i> imitated the example of the Karaites (cf. above, p. 286, note 3, and p. 290, note 2). We have remaining, in the <i>Book of Beliefs and Opinions</i> of Rabbi Saadia Gaon, an important monument of what can be called Jewish <i>kalam</i>, and it is to this celebrated doctor that Maimonides here appears to allude, as Moses of Narbonne observes in his commentary on our passage. In vol. III of the <i>Dictionnaire des sciences philosophiques</i>, art. <i>Juifs</i> (pp. 355-357), I have given some other details on this subject. I will further add that Arabic authors of the 10th century expressly speak of Jewish <i>Mutakallimun</i>. Al-Masudi cites several whom he had personally known (see Silv. de Sacy, <i>Notices et Extraits des manuscrits</i>, vol. VIII, p. 167). Isa ibn Zara‘a, a Christian Arabic author of Baghdad, speaking in his theological treatises of a certain Abu al-Khayr Dawud ibn Mushaj, says that he was one of the principal Jewish <i>Mutakallimun</i> and a profound thinker <span dir="rtl">(...من متكلّمي اليهود وكان متقدّماً فيهم نظّارا)</span>. See Arab. ms. of the Biblioth. imp., ancient collection, no. 98, fol. 40 <i>b</i> and 42 <i>a</i>.

**French (Source):** On sait que le titre de [[t:0]]Gaôn[[t:1]] [[t:2]]גאון[[t:3]] (au plur. [[t:4]]Gueônîm[[t:5]]) est celui que portaient les chefs des académies juives de Babylone, sous la domination arabe, depuis la fin du VII[[t:6]]e[[t:7]] jusque vers le milieu du XI[[t:8]]e[[t:9]] siècle. La période des [[t:10]]Gueônîm[[t:11]] coïncide avec celle du développement du [[t:12]]calâm[[t:13]] chez les Arabes; l’exemple donné par les théologiens musulmans fut suivi par certains docteurs juifs d’Orient, qui, comme les [[t:14]]Motécallemîn[[t:15]], cherchèrent à soutenir les dogmes religieux par la spéculation philosophique, et jetèrent les bases d’une théologie systématique et rationnelle. Ce furent les docteurs de la secte des Karaïtes qui les premiers entrèrent dans cette voie et adoptèrent eux-mêmes le nom de [[t:16]]Motécallemîn[[t:17]] (voy. le [[t:18]]Khozari[[t:19]], liv. V, § 15), sans toutefois admettre toutes les hypothèses du [[t:20]]calâm[[t:21]] musulman, qui seront exposées plus loin (ch. LXXIII). Quelques docteurs rabbanites parmi les [[t:22]]Gueônîm[[t:23]] imitèrent l’exemple des Karaïtes (cf. ci-dessus, pag. 286, note 3, et pag. 290, note 2). Il nous reste, dans le [[t:24]]Livre des Croyances et des Opinions[[t:25]] de Rabbi Saadia Gaôn, un monument important de ce qu’on peut appeler le [[t:26]]calâm[[t:27]] juif, et c’est à ce célèbre docteur que Maïmonide paraît ici faire allusion, comme le fait observer Moïse de Narbonne dans son commentaire à notre passage. Dans le t. III du [[t:28]]Dictionnaire des sciences philosophiques[[t:29]], art. [[t:30]]Juifs[[t:31]] (pag. 355-357), j’ai donné quelques autres détails sur ce sujet. J’ajouterai encore que des auteurs arabes du X[[t:32]]e[[t:33]] siècle parlent expressément des [[t:34]]Motécallemîn[[t:35]] juifs. Al-Mas’oudi en cite plusieurs qu’il avait personnellement connus (voy. Silv. de Sacy, [[t:36]]Notices et Extraits des manuscrits[[t:37]], t. VIII, pag. 167). ’Isa ibn-Zara’a, auteur arabe chrétien de Bagdad, en parlant, dans ses opuscules théologiques, d’un certain Abou’l-Khéir Daoud ibn-Mouschadj, dit que c’était un des principaux [[t:38]]Motécallemîn[[t:39]] juifs et un profond penseur [[t:40]](...من متكلّمي اليهود وكان متقدّما فيهم نظّارا)[[t:41]] . Voy. ms. ar. de la Biblioth. imp., ancien fonds, n° 98, fol. 40 [[t:42]]b[[t:43]] et 42 [[t:44]]a[[t:45]].

### fn.882 (Footnote)
**English:** The sect of the <i>Mutazilites</i> <span dir="rtl">(المعتزلة)</span>, whose name means <i>separated</i> or <i>dissidents</i>, was founded by Wasil ibn Ata (born in the year 80 of the Hegira, or 699-700 CE, and died in 131, or 748-749 CE). Wasil, a disciple of Al-Hasan al-Basri (of Basra), having <i>separated</i> <span dir="rtl">(اعتزل)</span> from the school over some religious dogma, became a school leader himself and established a doctrine whose elements were borrowed from various previous sects (cf. Shahrastani, p. 18 ff.; Ger. trans., vol. I, p. 25 ff.; Pococke, <i>Specimen hist. ar</i>., p. 211 ff.). The Mutazilites, subdivided into several branches, all agree on the following two points: 1. Man has perfect freedom in his actions; he does good and evil of his own accord, and thus has merits or demerits. 2. God, being absolutely <i>one</i>, has no attributes distinct from His essence (cf. above, p. 209, note 1). It is because of these two principal points of their doctrine, aimed at establishing the absolute justice and unity of God, that the Mutazilites refer to themselves by the name <span dir="rtl">أصحاب العدل والتوحيد</span> (partisans of <i>justice</i> and <i>unity</i>). These are precisely the same expressions that the Arabic historian Al-Masudi uses to designate the doctrine of the disciples of Anan (see de Sacy, <i>Notices et Extraits</i>, loc. cit.; <i>Chrest. ar</i>., vol. I, pp. 349-351), which proves that the Karaite theologians took as their primary model the Muslim doctors of the <i>Mutazilite</i> sect. In agreement with Maimonides, the Karaite author Aaron ben Elijah expressly says that the Karaite scholars and a portion of the Rabbanites followed the doctrines of the <i>Mutazilites</i> (see <span dir="rtl">עץ חיים</span> or <i>Tree of Life</i>, Leipzig edition, p. 4).
**Correction:** The sect of the <i>Mutazilites</i> <span dir="rtl">(المعتزلة)</span>, whose name means <i>separated</i> or <i>dissidents</i>, was founded by Wasil ibn Ata (born in the year 80 of the Hegira, or 699-700 CE, and died in 131, or 748-749 CE). Wasil, a disciple of Al-Hasan al-Basri (of Basra), having <i>separated</i> <span dir="rtl">(اعتزل)</span> from the school over some religious dogma, became a school leader himself and established a doctrine whose elements were borrowed from various previous sects (cf. Shahrastani, p. 18 ff.; Ger. trans., vol. I, p. 25 ff.; Pococke, <i>Specimen hist. ar</i>., p. 211 ff.). The Mutazilites, subdivided into several branches, all agree on the following two points: 1. Man has perfect freedom in his actions; he does good and evil of his own accord, and thus has merits or demerits. 2. God, being absolutely <i>one</i>, has no attributes distinct from His essence (cf. above, p. 209, note 1). It is because of these two principal points of their doctrine, aimed at establishing the absolute justice and unity of God, that the Mutazilites refer to themselves by the name <span dir="rtl">أصحاب العدل والتوحيد</span> (partisans of <i>justice</i> and <i>unity</i>). These are precisely the same expressions that the Arabic historian Al-Masudi uses to designate the doctrine of the disciples of Anan (see de Sacy, <i>Notices et Extraits</i>, loc. cit.; <i>Chrest. ar</i>., vol. I, pp. 349-351), which proves that the Karaite theologians took as their primary model the Muslim doctors of the <i>Mutazilite</i> sect. In agreement with Maimonides, the Karaite author Aaron ben Elijah expressly says that the Karaite scholars and a portion of the Rabbanites followed the doctrines of the <i>Mutazilites</i> (see <span dir="rtl">עץ חיים</span> or <i>Tree of Life</i>, Leipzig edition, p. 4).

**French (Source):** La secte des [[t:0]]Mo’tazales[[t:1]] [[t:2]](المعتزلة)[[t:3]] , dont le nom signifie [[t:4]]séparés[[t:5]] ou [[t:6]]dissidents[[t:7]], eut pour fondateur Wâcil ibn-’Atâ (né l’an 80 de l’hégire, ou 699-700 de l’ère chrét., et mort l’an 131, ou 748-749 de l’ère chrét.). Wâcil, disciple d’Al-’Hasan al-Baçri (de Bassora), s’étant [[t:8]]séparé[[t:9]] [[t:10]](اعتزل)[[t:11]]  de l’école au sujet de quelque dogme religieux, se fit lui-même chef d’école, et établit une doctrine dont les éléments étaient empruntés à différentes sectes précédentes (cf. Schahrestâni, pag. 18 et suiv.; trad. all., t. I, pag. 25 et suiv.; Pococke, [[t:12]]Specimen hist. ar[[t:13]]., pag. 211 et suiv.). Les Mo’tazales, subdivisés en plusieurs branches, s’accordent tous sur les deux points suivants: 1° L’homme a une liberté parfaite dans ses actions; il fait de son propre mouvement le bien et le mal, et il a ainsi des mérites ou des démérites. 2° Dieu, absolument [[t:14]]un[[t:15]], n’a point d’attributs distincts de son essence (cf. ci-dessus, pag. 209, note 1). C’est à cause de ces deux points principaux de leur doctrine, ayant pour but d’établir la justice et l’unité absolues de Dieu, que les Mo’tazales se désignent eux-mêmes par la dénomination de [[t:16]]أصحاب العدل والتوحيد[[t:17]]  (partisans de la [[t:18]]justice[[t:19]] et de l’[[t:20]]unité[[t:21]]). Ce sont précisément ces mêmes expressions que l’historien arabe Al-Mas’oudi emploie pour désigner la doctrine des disciples de ’Anân (voir de Sacy, [[t:22]]Notices et Extraits[[t:23]], I. c.; [[t:24]]Chrest. ar[[t:25]]., t. I, pag. 349-351), ce qui prouve que les théologiens karaïtes prirent surtout pour modèle les docteurs musulmans de la secte des [[t:26]]Mo’tazales[[t:27]]. D’accord avec Maïmonide, l’auteur karaïte Ahron ben-Élie dit expressément que les savants karaïtes et une partie des rabbanites suivaient les doctrines des [[t:28]]Mo’tazales[[t:29]] (voy. [[t:30]]עץ חיים[[t:31]] ou [[t:32]]Arbre de la iev[[t:33]], édit. de Leipzig, pag. 4).

### fn.886 (Footnote)
**English:** The author alludes to the ecclesiastical writers, Greek and Syrian, who, from the 6th<sup></sup> century onwards, had to defend Christian dogmas against the increasingly dominant Peripatetic philosophy and against the numerous heresies resulting from that same philosophy. The defenders of religion used the weapons of dialectics that the study of Aristotle's philosophy had placed in their hands. Cf. Brucker, <i>Hist. crit. philos</i>., vol. III, p. 534; Ritter, <i>Geschichte der Philosophie</i>, vol. VI, p. 458 ff. (<i>Philos. chrét</i>., book VII, ch. 1). The same method, as the author will explain, was followed by the Muslim <i>Mutakallimun</i>, who were acquainted with the writings of several writers of the Greek Church, such as John Philoponus, John of Damascus, and others, and who were even better positioned to draw from the writings of the Christian theologians of Syria and Mesopotamia. Christian scholars, both Nestorian and Jacobite, lived in great numbers among the Muslims, who owed them their knowledge of Greek philosophy and science. Cf. Wenrich, <i>De auctorum Graecorum versionibus et commentariis syriacis arabicis etc</i>., pp. 7-22; E. Renan, <i>De Philosophia peripatetica apud Syros</i>, Paris 1852.
**Correction:** The author alludes to the ecclesiastical writers, Greek and Syrian, who, from the 6th<sup></sup> century onwards, had to defend Christian dogmas against the increasingly dominant Peripatetic philosophy and against the numerous heresies resulting from that same philosophy. The defenders of religion used the weapons of dialectics that the study of Aristotle's philosophy had placed in their hands. Cf. Brucker, <i>Hist. crit. philos</i>., vol. III, p. 534; Ritter, <i>Geschichte der Philosophie</i>, vol. VI, p. 458 ff. (<i>Philos. chrét</i>., book VII, ch. 1). The same method, as the author will explain, was followed by the Muslim <i>Mutakallimun</i>, who were acquainted with the writings of several writers of the Greek Church, such as John Philoponus, John of Damascus, and others, and who were even better positioned to draw from the writings of the Christian theologians of Syria and Mesopotamia. Christian scholars, both Nestorian and Jacobite, lived in great numbers among the Muslims, who owed them their knowledge of Greek philosophy and science. Cf. Wenrich, <i>De auctorum Graecorum versionibus et commentariis syriacis arabicis etc</i>., pp. 7-22; E. Renan, <i>De Philosophia peripatetica apud Syros</i>, Paris 1852.

**French (Source):** L’auteur fait allusion aux écrivains ecclésiastiques, grecs et syriens, qui, à partir du VI[[t:0]]e[[t:1]] siècle, durent défendre les dogmes chrétiens contre la philosophie péripatéticienne de plus en plus dominante et contre les nombreuses hérésies issues de cette même philosophie. Les défenseurs de la religion se servirent des armes de la dialectique que l’étude de la philosophie d’Aristote leur avait mises entre les mains. Cf. Brucker, [[t:2]]Hist. crit. philos[[t:3]]., t. III, pag. 534; Ritter, [[t:4]]Geschichte der Philosophie[[t:5]], t. VI, pag. 458 et suiv. ([[t:6]]Philos. chrét[[t:7]]., liv. VII, chap. 1). La même méthode, comme l’auteur va l’expliquer, fut suivie par les [[t:8]]Motécallemîn[[t:9]] musulmans, qui connaissaient les écrits de plusieurs écrivains de l’Église grecque, tels que Jean Philopone, Jean Damascène et autres, et qui étaient encore plus à même de puiser dans les écrits des théologiens chrétiens de Syrie et de Mésopotamie. Des savants chrétiens, tant nestoriens que jacobites, vivaient en grand nombre au milieu des musulmans, qui leur devaient la connaissance de la philosophie et des sciences des Grecs. Cf. Wenrich, [[t:10]]De auctorum Grœcorum versionibus et commentariis syriacis arabicis etc[[t:11]]., pag. 7-22; E. Renan, [[t:12]]De Philosophia peripatetica apud Syros[[t:13]], Paris 1852.

### fn.909 (Footnote)
**English:** Literally: <i>and we shall say thereupon everything by which it is possible to argue</i>, that is to say, we will produce all the arguments that can be alleged in favor of the novelty of the world, or at least against its eternity. See Part II, ch. XV et seq.
**Correction:** Literally: <i>and we shall say thereupon everything by which it is possible to argue</i>, that is to say, we will produce all the arguments that can be alleged in favor of the novelty of the world, or at least against its eternity. See Part II, ch. XV et seq.

**French (Source):** Littéralement: [[t:0]]et nous dirons là-dessus tout ce par quoi il est possible d’argumenter[[t:1]], c’est-à-dire, nous produirons tous les arguments qu’on peut alléguer en faveur de la nouveauté du monde, ou du moins contre son éternité. Voy. la II[[t:2]]e[[t:3]] partie, chap. XV et suiv.

### fn.924.sub_1 (Footnote)
**English:** The vapor of the blood existing in the liver and in the veins that originate from it is called the <i>natural spirit</i> or <i>physical</i> (<i>φυσικὸν πνεῦμα</i>); the vapor of the blood existing in the heart and in the arteries is called the <i>vital spirit</i> (<i>ζωτικὸν πν</i>.); finally, the vapor found in the cavities of the brain and that which spreads from it into the channels of the nerves is called <i>animal spirit</i> (<i>ψυχικὸν πν</i>.). The origin of all (these <i>spirits</i>) and their principal matter come (as we have said) from the air drawn in by respiration; if, therefore, this air is corrupted, or foul-smelling, or murky, all these <i>spirits</i> are altered and are no longer what they ought to be... The more subtle the spirit is, the more it is altered by the alteration of the air. Now, as the <i>natural</i> spirit is thicker than the <i>vital</i> spirit, and the latter thicker than the <i>animal</i> spirit, it follows that at the slightest alteration of the air, the <i>animal</i> spirit will undergo a perceptible alteration. This is why you will find many men in whom a weakening of the <i>animal</i> or <i>psychic</i> functions (that is, the functions depending on the <i>animal</i> spirits) is observed when the air is corrupted; I mean that it happens to them to have dull intelligence, difficult understanding, and a weakened memory, although no alteration is noticed in their <i>vital</i> and <i>natural</i> functions.' Cf. Galen, <i>De Hippocr. et Plat. placitis</i>, book VII, chapter 3, and above, p. 111, note 2. — The passage we have just cited is found at the beginning of book IV of the treatise titled <span dir="rtl">في تدبير الصحة</span> (On the Regimen of Health), which Maimonides composed at the request of Al-Malik al-Afdhal, son of Saladin, and whose Arabic original, copied in Hebrew characters, is in the Imperial Library (see Hebrew mss. of the old collection no. 412, fol. 107 <i>a.b</i>, and no. 411, fol. 57 <i>b</i> and 46 <i>a)</i>. The Hebrew version of this treatise, by R. Moses Ibn Tibbon, was published in the collection titled <span dir="rtl">כרם חמד</span>, vol. III (Prague, 1838, in-8vo), p. 9 et seq. There also exists a Latin version of it which has been printed several times.
**Correction:** The vapor of the blood existing in the liver and in the veins that originate from it is called the <i>natural spirit</i> or <i>physical</i> (<i>φυσικὸν πνεῦμα</i>); the vapor of the blood existing in the heart and in the arteries is called the <i>vital spirit</i> (<i>ζωτικὸν πν</i>.); finally, the vapor found in the cavities of the brain and that which spreads from it into the channels of the nerves is called <i>animal spirit</i> (<i>ψυχικὸν πν</i>.). The origin of all (these <i>spirits</i>) and their principal matter come (as we have said) from the air drawn in by respiration; if, therefore, this air is corrupted, or foul-smelling, or murky, all these <i>spirits</i> are altered and are no longer what they ought to be... The more subtle the spirit is, the more it is altered by the alteration of the air. Now, as the <i>natural</i> spirit is thicker than the <i>vital</i> spirit, and the latter thicker than the <i>animal</i> spirit, it follows that at the slightest alteration of the air, the <i>animal</i> spirit will undergo a perceptible alteration. This is why you will find many men in whom a weakening of the <i>animal</i> or <i>psychic</i> functions (that is, the functions depending on the <i>animal</i> spirits) is observed when the air is corrupted; I mean that it happens to them to have dull intelligence, difficult understanding, and a weakened memory, although no alteration is noticed in their <i>vital</i> and <i>natural</i> functions.' Cf. Galen, <i>De Hippocr. et Plat. placitis</i>, book VII, chapter 3, and above, p. 111, note 2. — The passage we have just cited is found at the beginning of book IV of the treatise titled <span dir="rtl">في تدبير الصحة</span> (On the Regimen of Health), which Maimonides composed at the request of Al-Malik al-Afdhal, son of Saladin, and whose Arabic original, copied in Hebrew characters, is in the Imperial Library (see Hebrew mss. of the old collection no. 412, fol. 107 <i>a.b</i>, and no. 411, fol. 57 <i>b</i> and 46 <i>a)</i>. The Hebrew version of this treatise, by R. Moses Ibn Tibbon, was published in the collection titled <span dir="rtl">כרם חמד</span>, vol. III (Prague, 1838, in-8vo), p. 9 et seq. There also exists a Latin version of it which has been printed several times.

**French (Source):** La vapeur du sang existant dans le foie et dans les veines qui partent de celui-ci s’appelle l’[[t:42]]esprit naturel[[t:43]] ou [[t:44]]physique[[t:45]] ([[t:46]]φυσιϰὸν πνεῦμα[[t:47]]); la vapeur du sang existant dans le cœur et dans les artères s’appelle l’[[t:48]]esprit vital[[t:49]] ([[t:50]]ζωτιϰὸν πν[[t:51]].); enfin, la vapeur qui se trouve dans les cavités du cerveau et ce qui s’en répand dans les canaux des nerfs s’appelle [[t:52]]esprit animal[[t:53]] ([[t:54]]ψυχιϰὸν πν[[t:55]].). L’origine de tous (ces [[t:56]]esprits[[t:57]]) et leur principale matière viennent (comme nous l’avons dit) de l’air attiré par la respiration; si donc cet air est corrompu, ou puant, ou trouble, tous ces [[t:58]]esprits[[t:59]] s’altèrent et ne sont plus ce qu’ils doivent être….. Plus l’esprit est subtil, et plus il est altéré par l’altération de l’air. Or, comme l’esprit [[t:60]]naturel[[t:61]] est plus épais que l’esprit [[t:62]]vital[[t:63]] et celui-ci plus épais que l’esprit [[t:64]]animal[[t:65]], il s’ensuit qu’à la moindre altération de l’air l’esprit [[t:66]]animal[[t:67]] subira une altération sensible. C’est pourquoi tu trouveras beaucoup d’hommes dans lesquels on remarque un affaiblissement des fonctions [[t:68]]animales[[t:69]] ou [[t:70]]psychiques[[t:71]] (c’est-à-dire des fonctions dépendant des esprits [[t:72]]animaux[[t:73]]), quand l’air est corrompu; je veux dire, qu’il leur arrive d’avoir l’intelligence obtuse, la compréhension difficile et la mémoire affaiblie, bien qu’on ne remarque pas d’altération dans leurs fonctions [[t:74]]vitales[[t:75]] et [[t:76]]naturelles[[t:77]].» Cf. Galien, [[t:78]]De Hippocr. et Plat. placitis[[t:79]], liv. VII, chap. 3, et ci-dessus, pag. 111, note 2. — Le passage que nous venons de citer se trouve au commencement du liv. IV du traité intitulé [[t:80]]في تدبير الصحة[[t:81]]  (du Régime de la santé), que Maïmonide composa sur la demande d’Al-Mâlic al-Afdhal, fils de Saladin, et dont l’original arabe, copié en caractères hébraïques, existe à la Bibliothèque impériale (voy. mss. hébr. de l’ancien fonds n° 412, fol. 107 [[t:82]]a.b[[t:83]], et n° 411, fol. 57 [[t:84]]b[[t:85]] et 46 [[t:86]]a)[[t:87]]. La version hébraïque de ce traité, due à R. Moïse Ibn-Tibbon, a été publiée dans le recueil intitulé [[t:88]]כרם חמד[[t:89]], t. III (Prague, 1838, in-8°), pag. 9 et suiv. Il en existe aussi une version latine qui a été imprimée plusieurs fois.

### fn.961 (Footnote)
**English:** Ancient physicians count four principal <i>humors</i> in the human body, which are blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile. These humors do not have, like the limbs, an individual existence; for they are mixed, and it is sometimes one, sometimes the other, that predominates. The nature of the mixture constitutes the <i>temperament</i>, which is either sanguine, phlegmatic, bilious, or melancholic. See Galen, <i>Definitiones medicae</i>, LXV-LXX (<i>ed</i>. by Kühn, vol. XIX, p. 363 et seq.). Cf. Sprengel, <i>Versuch einer pragmatischen Geschichte der Arzeneykunde</i>, vol. II, p. 164.
**Correction:** Ancient physicians count four principal <i>humors</i> in the human body, which are blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile. These humors do not have, like the limbs, an individual existence; for they are mixed, and it is sometimes one, sometimes the other, that predominates. The nature of the mixture constitutes the <i>temperament</i>, which is either sanguine, phlegmatic, bilious, or melancholic. See Galen, <i>Definitiones medicae</i>, LXV-LXX (<i>ed</i>. by Kühn, vol. XIX, p. 363 et seq.). Cf. Sprengel, <i>Versuch einer pragmatischen Geschichte der Arzeneykunde</i>, vol. II, p. 164.

**French (Source):** Les médecins anciens comptent dans le corps humain quatre [[t:0]]humeurs[[t:1]] principales, qui sont le sang, le flegme, la bile jaune et la bile noire. Ces humeurs n’ont point, comme les membres, une existence individuelle; car elles sont mêlées, et c’est tantôt l’une, tantôt l’autre, qui prédomine. La nature du mélange constitue le [[t:2]]tempérament[[t:3]], qui est ou sanguin, ou flegmatique, ou bilieux, ou mélancolique. Voy. Galien, [[t:4]]Definitiones medicœ[[t:5]], LXV-LXX ([[t:6]]édit[[t:7]]. de Kühn, t. XIX, pag. 363 et suiv.). Cf. Sprengel, [[t:8]]Versuch einer pragmatischen Geschichte der Arzeneykunde[[t:9]], t. II, pag. 164.

**English:** That is to say, the <i>privation</i> of the atom, or the <i>void</i>, and how they generally understand <i>privation</i> or <i>non-being</i>. See propositions II and VII.
**Correction:** That is to say, the <i>privation</i> of the atom, or the <i>void</i>, and how they generally understand <i>privation</i> or <i>non-being</i>. See propositions II and VII.

**French (Source):** C’est-à-dire, la [[t:0]]privation[[t:1]] de l’atome, ou le [[t:2]]vide[[t:3]], et comment ils entendent en général la [[t:4]]privation[[t:5]] ou le [[t:6]]non-être[[t:7]]. Voir les propositions II et VII.
