### fn.1003 (Footnote)
**English:** That is to say, in the <i>Physics;</i> the Arabs designate Aristotle's Physics under the title <span dir="rtl">السماع</span> or <span dir="rtl">السماع الطبيعي</span>, corresponding to the Greek title: <i>φυσικὴ ἀκρὸασις</i> (<i>physica auscultatio</i>). — The author refers to the VI<sup>th</sup> book of the Physics (chap. 1 et seq.), where Aristotle explains that what is <i>continuous</i> cannot be composed of indivisible parts, and where, after having established the continuity and divisibility of <i>magnitude</i> or <i>extension</i> (<i>μέγεθος</i> or <i>μῆκος</i>), of <i>time</i> and of <i>motion</i>, he demonstrates that these three continuous things are linked together by a mutual connection, and that what is said of one of them is necessarily also said of the others. Maimonides has particularly in mind the following passage from chapter II: <i>ἔτι δὲ καὶ ἐκ τῶν εἰωθότων λόγων φανερὸν ὡς εἴπερ ὁ χρόνος ἐστὶ συνεχής, ὅτι καὶ τὸ μέγεθος, εἴπερ ἐν τῷ ἡμίσει χρόνῳ ἥμισυ διέρχεται καὶ ἁπλῶς ἐν τῷ ἐλάττονι ἔλαττον · αἱ γὰρ αὐταὶ διαιρέσεις ἔσονται τοῦ χρόνου καὶ τοῦ μεγέθους. καὶ εἰ ὁποτερονοῦν ἄπειρον, καὶ θάτερον, καὶ ὡς θάτερον, καὶ θάτερον, κ. τ. λ</i>.
**Correction:** That is to say, in the <i>Physics;</i> the Arabs designate Aristotle's Physics under the title <span dir="rtl">السماع</span> or <span dir="rtl">السماع الطبيعي</span>, corresponding to the Greek title: <i>φυσικὴ ἀκρὸασις</i> (<i>physica auscultatio</i>). — The author refers to the VI<sup>th</sup> book of the Physics (chap. 1 et seq.), where Aristotle explains that what is <i>continuous</i> cannot be composed of indivisible parts, and where, after having established the continuity and divisibility of <i>magnitude</i> or <i>extension</i> (<i>μέγεθος</i> or <i>μῆκος</i>), of <i>time</i> and of <i>motion</i>, he demonstrates that these three continuous things are linked together by a mutual connection, and that what is said of one of them is necessarily also said of the others. Maimonides has particularly in mind the following passage from chapter II: <i>ἔτι δὲ καὶ ἐκ τῶν εἰωθότων λόγων φανερὸν ὡς εἴπερ ὁ χρόνος ἐστὶ συνεχής, ὅτι καὶ τὸ μέγεθος, εἴπερ ἐν τῷ ἡμίσει χρόνῳ ἥμισυ διέρχεται καὶ ἁπλῶς ἐν τῷ ἐλάττονι ἔλαττον · αἱ γὰρ αὐταὶ διαιρέσεις ἔσονται τοῦ χρόνου καὶ τοῦ μεγέθους. καὶ εἰ ὁποτερονοῦν ἄπειρον, καὶ θάτερον, καὶ ὡς θάτερον, καὶ θάτερον, κ. τ. λ</i>.

**French (Source):** C’est-à-dire, dans la [[t:0]]Physique;[[t:1]] les Arabes désignent la Physique d’Aristote sous le titre de [[t:2]]السماع[[t:3]]  ou [[t:4]]السماع الطبيعي[[t:5]] , correspondant au titre grec: [[t:6]]φυσιϰὴ ἀϰρὸασις[[t:7]] ([[t:8]]physica auscultatio[[t:9]]). — L’auteur veut parler du VI[[t:10]]e[[t:11]] livre de la Physique (chap. 1 et suiv.), où Aristote expose que ce qui est [[t:12]]continu[[t:13]] ne saurait être composé de parties indivisibles, et où, après avoir établi la continuité et la divisibilité de la [[t:14]]grandeur[[t:15]] ou de l’[[t:16]]étendue[[t:17]] ([[t:18]]μέγεθος[[t:19]] ou [[t:20]]μῆϰος[[t:21]]), du [[t:22]]temps[[t:23]] et du [[t:24]]mouvement[[t:25]], il démontre que ces trois choses continues sont liées ensemble par un enchaînement mutuel, et que ce qui se dit de l’une d’elles se dit nécessairement aussi des autres. Maïmonide a surtout en vue le passage suivant du chapitre II: [[t:26]]ἔτι δὲ ϰαὶ ἐϰ τῶν εἰωθότων λóγων λέγεοθαι φανερὸν ὡς εἴπερ ὁ χρόνος ἐστὶ συνεχής, ὅτι ϰαὶ τὸ μέγεθος, εἴπερ ἐν τῷ ἡμίσει χρόνῳ ἥμισυ διέρχεται ϰαὶ ἁπλῶς ἐν τῷ ἐλάττονι ἔλαττον · αἱ γὰρ αὐταὶ διαιρέσεις ἔσονται τοῦ χρόνου ϰαὶ τοῦ μεγέθους. ϰαὶ εὶ ὁποτερονοῦν ἄπειρον, ϰαὶ θάτερον, ϰαὶ ὡς θάτερον, ϰαὶ θάτερον, ϰ. τ. λ[[t:27]].

### fn.1004 (Footnote)
**English:** That is to say: according to the hypotheses of the <i>Mutakallimun</i>, time would be something positive and real, composed of perceptible elements succeeding one another in a certain order (Cf. Schmölders, <i>Essai, etc.</i>, p. 163 et seq.); but in reality, time has only two parts, one of which no longer exists and the other of which is not yet, for the present moment (<i>τὸ νῦν</i>) is only an ideal limit between the past and the future and cannot be considered as a part of time capable of serving to measure it. See Aristotle, <i>Physics</i>, book IV, chap. 10, and cf. above, p. 199, note 1.
**Correction:** That is to say: according to the hypotheses of the <i>Mutakallimun</i>, time would be something positive and real, composed of perceptible elements succeeding one another in a certain order (Cf. Schmölders, <i>Essai, etc.</i>, p. 163 et seq.); but in reality, time has only two parts, one of which no longer exists and the other of which is not yet, for the present moment (<i>τὸ νῦν</i>) is only an ideal limit between the past and the future and cannot be considered as a part of time capable of serving to measure it. See Aristotle, <i>Physics</i>, book IV, chap. 10, and cf. above, p. 199, note 1.

**French (Source):** C’est-à-dire: selon les hypothèses des [[t:0]]Motécallemîn[[t:1]], le temps serait quelque chose de positif et de réel, composé d’éléments saisissables qui se succéderaient dans un certain ordre (Cf. Schmœlders, [[t:2]]Essai, etc.[[t:3]], pag. 163 et suiv.); mais en réalité, le temps n’a que deux parties dont l’une n’est plus et dont l’autre n’est pas encore, car le moment présent ([[t:4]]τὸ νῦν[[t:5]]) n’est qu’une limite idéale entre le passé et l’avenir et ne saurait être considéré comme une partie du temps et pouvant servir à le mesurer. Voy. Aristote, [[t:6]]Physique[[t:7]], liv. IV, chap. 10, et cf. ci-dessus, pag. 199, note 1.

### fn.1028 (Footnote)
**English:** That is to say, according to this opinion: that there exists no <i>nature of things</i>, nor any causality, and that everything we see happen is the immediate act of the divine will. In this system, therefore, if one granted the accident a certain duration after which it ceased to be, since this disappearance of the accident would not be the effect of a natural cause, it would have to be God who <i>produced</i> non-being, which nevertheless is not <i>produced</i>. — Some commentators have found that this reasoning is in contradiction with the VII<sup>th</sup> proposition of the <i>Mutakallimun</i>, according to which <i>privations</i> are likewise real accidents that require an <i>efficient cause;</i> but this proposition speaks of the privation of a certain determined quality, or, so to speak, of <i>capacities</i> (<i>ἕς́ειϛ</i>) that are <i>negative</i>, and these, according to the <i>Mutakallimun</i>, must not be confused with absolute non-being. It is therefore not necessary to suppose, with Ibn Caspi, that the author reasons here in the sense of certain <i>Mutakallimun</i> who do not admit the VII<sup>th</sup> proposition. See the commentaries of Efodi and Shem-Tob.
**Correction:** That is to say, according to this opinion: that there exists no <i>nature of things</i>, nor any causality, and that everything we see happen is the immediate act of the divine will. In this system, therefore, if one granted the accident a certain duration after which it ceased to be, since this disappearance of the accident would not be the effect of a natural cause, it would have to be God who <i>produced</i> non-being, which nevertheless is not <i>produced</i>. — Some commentators have found that this reasoning is in contradiction with the VII<sup>th</sup> proposition of the <i>Mutakallimun</i>, according to which <i>privations</i> are likewise real accidents that require an <i>efficient cause;</i> but this proposition speaks of the privation of a certain determined quality, or, so to speak, of <i>capacities</i> (<i>ἕς́ειϛ</i>) that are <i>negative</i>, and these, according to the <i>Mutakallimun</i>, must not be confused with absolute non-being. It is therefore not necessary to suppose, with Ibn Caspi, that the author reasons here in the sense of certain <i>Mutakallimun</i> who do not admit the VII<sup>th</sup> proposition. See the commentaries of Efodi and Shem-Tob.

**French (Source):** C’est-à-dire, selon cette opinion: qu’il n’existe aucune [[t:0]]nature des choses[[t:1]], ni aucune causalité, et que tout ce que nous voyons arriver est le fait immédiat de la volonté divine. Dans ce système donc, si l’on accordait à l’accident une certaine durée, après laquelle il cessât d’être, cette disparition de l’accident n’étant point l’effet d’une cause naturelle, il faudrait que ce fût Dieu qui [[t:2]]fît[[t:3]] le non-être, lequel pourtant ne se [[t:4]]fait[[t:5]] pas. — Quelques commentateurs ont trouvé que ce raisonnement est en contradiction avec la VII[[t:6]]e[[t:7]] proposition des [[t:8]]Motécallemîn[[t:9]], d’après laquelle les [[t:10]]privations[[t:11]] sont également des accidents réels qui ont besoin d’un [[t:12]]efficient;[[t:13]] mais cette proposition parle de la privation de certaine qualité déterminée, ou, pour ainsi dire, des [[t:14]]capacités[[t:15]] ([[t:16]]ἕς́ειϛ[[t:17]]) [[t:18]]négatives[[t:19]], et cellesci, selon les [[t:20]]Motécallemîn[[t:21]], ne doivent point se confondre avec le non-être absolu. Il n’est donc pas nécessaire de supposer, avec Ibn-Caspi, que l’auteur raisonne ici dans le sens de certains [[t:22]]Motécallemîn[[t:23]] qui n’admettent pas la VII[[t:24]]e[[t:25]] proposition. Voir les commentaires d’Éphodi et de Schem-Tob.

---

### fn.1029 (Footnote)
**English:** The author likely means that in principle it is true to say that non-being does not require an efficient cause, but that one must not, as the <i>Mutakallimun</i> do here, confuse absolute non-being with the non-existence of that which has existed, for the latter requires an efficient cause.
**Correction:** The author likely means that in principle it is true to say that non-being does not require an efficient cause, but that one must not, as the <i>Mutakallimun</i> do here, confuse absolute non-being with the non-existence of that which has existed, for the latter requires an efficient cause.

**French (Source):** L’auteur veut dire probablement qu’en principe il est vrai de dire que le non-être n’a pas besoin d’efficient, mais qu’il ne faut pas, comme le font ici les [[t:0]]Motécallemîn[[t:1]], confondre le non-être absolu avec la non-existence de ce qui a existé, car celle-ci a besoin d’un efficient.

### fn.1031 (Footnote)
**English:** According to this latter opinion, which is that of the <i>Mu'tazilites</i>, it would not be enough for the destruction of the world for God to <i>refrain</i> from creating accidents in the atoms, but it would be necessary for God to create specifically the accident of destruction, without however this accident being in a subject or a substance; for if it were in a subject, it would be the combination of being and non-being, which is impossible. — Ibn Rushd has highlighted everything that is absurd in this hypothesis, which, he says, does not deserve serious refutation. First, <i>destruction</i> and <i>non-being</i> are synonyms, and if God does not create non-being, he does not create destruction either. Furthermore, an accident without a <i>substratum</i> is in itself an absurd thing; and besides, how can one imagine that non-being produces non-being? See <i>Destruction of the Destruction</i>, II<sup>nd</sup> question, where one finds other details relating to this VI<sup>th</sup> proposition of the <i>Mutakallimun</i>.
**Correction:** According to this latter opinion, which is that of the <i>Mu'tazilites</i>, it would not be enough for the destruction of the world for God to <i>refrain</i> from creating accidents in the atoms, but it would be necessary for God to create specifically the accident of destruction, without however this accident being in a subject or a substance; for if it were in a subject, it would be the combination of being and non-being, which is impossible. — Ibn Rushd has highlighted everything that is absurd in this hypothesis, which, he says, does not deserve serious refutation. First, <i>destruction</i> and <i>non-being</i> are synonyms, and if God does not create non-being, he does not create destruction either. Furthermore, an accident without a <i>substratum</i> is in itself an absurd thing; and besides, how can one imagine that non-being produces non-being? See <i>Destruction of the Destruction</i>, II<sup>nd</sup> question, where one finds other details relating to this VI<sup>th</sup> proposition of the <i>Mutakallimun</i>.

**French (Source):** Selon cette dernière opinion, qui est celle des [[t:0]]Mo’tazales[[t:1]], il ne suffirait pas pour la destruction du monde que Dieu [[t:2]]s’abstînt[[t:3]] de créer des accidents dans les atomes, mais il faudrait que Dieu créât tout exprès l’accident de la destruction, sans pourtant que cet accident fût dans un sujet ou dans une substance; car, s’il était dans un sujet, ce serait la réunion de l’être et du non-être, ce qui est impossible. — Ibn-Roschd a fait ressortir tout ce qu’il y a d’absurde dans cette hypothèse, qui, dit-il, ne mérite pas de réfutation sérieuse. D’abord, [[t:4]]destruction[[t:5]] et [[t:6]]non-être[[t:7]] sont synonymes, et si Dieu ne crée pas le non-être, il ne crée pas non plus la destruction. Ensuite un accident sans [[t:8]]substratum[[t:9]] est en lui-même une chose absurde; et d’ailleurs comment peut-on se figurer que le non-être fasse le non-être? Voy. [[t:10]]Destr. de la Destruction[[t:11]], II[[t:12]]e[[t:13]] question, où l’on trouve d’autres détails relatifs à cette VI[[t:14]]e[[t:15]] proposition des [[t:16]]Motécallemîn[[t:17]].

### fn.1091 (Footnote)
**English:** It is evident that the two lines in question here, which, as the author is about to state, are one curved and the other straight, are none other than the <i>hyperbolic curve</i> and the <i>asymptote</i>. The treatise on <i>Conic Sections</i> cited by the author is that of Apollonius, entitled Κ<i>ωνιϰὰ στοιχεῖα</i>, and in Arabic <span dir="rtl">كتاب المخروطات</span>; the theorem in question is the XIII<sup>th</sup> of Book II. On the Arabic versions of Apollonius's treatise, see Wenrich, <i>De auctorum graecorum versionibus, etc.</i>, p. 198 et seq.
**Correction:** It is evident that the two lines in question here, which, as the author is about to state, are one curved and the other straight, are none other than the <i>hyperbolic curve</i> and the <i>asymptote</i>. The treatise on <i>Conic Sections</i> cited by the author is that of Apollonius, entitled Κ<i>ωνιϰὰ στοιχεῖα</i>, and in Arabic <span dir="rtl">كتاب المخروطات</span>; the theorem in question is the XIII<sup>th</sup> of Book II. On the Arabic versions of Apollonius's treatise, see Wenrich, <i>De auctorum graecorum versionibus, etc.</i>, p. 198 et seq.

**French (Source):** Il est évident que les deux lignes dont il s’agit ici, et qui, comme l’auteur va le dire, sont, l’une courbe, l’autre droite, ne sont autres que la [[t:0]]courbe hyperbolique[[t:1]] et l’[[t:2]]asymptote[[t:3]]. Le traité des [[t:4]]Sections coniques[[t:5]] cité par l’auteur est celui d’Apollonius, intitulé Κ[[t:6]]ωνιϰὰ στοιχεῖα[[t:7]], et en arabe [[t:8]]كتاب المخروطات[[t:9]] ; le théorème dont il s’agit est le XIII[[t:10]]e[[t:11]] du livre II. Sur les versions arabes du traité d’Apollonius, voy. Wenerich, [[t:12]]De auctorum grœcorum versionibus, etc[[t:13]]., pag. 198 et suiv.

### fn.1099 (Footnote)
**English:** The author deems it necessary to make a reservation regarding this X<sup>th</sup> proposition; for he himself recognizes this principle of <i>admissibility</i> for a part of the universe, and he believes that, for everything above the lunar sphere, it is not given to us to recognize an immutable natural law, and that many a thing could be otherwise than it actually is. The author bases his proofs of Creation on a reasoning analogous to that which the <i>Motécallemîn</i> derived from this X<sup>th</sup> proposition. Cf. the following chapter (V<sup>th</sup> method), and Part II<sup>nd</sup> of this work, chaps. XIX et seq.—The words <span dir="rtl">פתאום ברגע קטן</span>, which are read here in the editions of Ibn-Tibbon's version, contain a double translation of the Arabic word <span dir="rtl">באלהוינא</span> <span dir="rtl">(بِالْهُوَيْنَا)</span>; the word <span dir="rtl">פתאום</span> must be deleted, as it is not found in the manuscripts of the said version. Ibn-Falaquéra renders the word <span dir="rtl">באלהוינא</span> by <span dir="rtl">בדבר קל</span>, <i>with little effort</i>, that is to say, <i>inconsiderately</i> or <i>lightly;</i> see the Appendix to the <i>Moré ha-Moré</i>, p. 153.
**Correction:** The author deems it necessary to make a reservation regarding this X<sup>th</sup> proposition; for he himself recognizes this principle of <i>admissibility</i> for a part of the universe, and he believes that, for everything above the lunar sphere, it is not given to us to recognize an immutable natural law, and that many a thing could be otherwise than it actually is. The author bases his proofs of Creation on a reasoning analogous to that which the <i>Motécallemîn</i> derived from this X<sup>th</sup> proposition. Cf. the following chapter (V<sup>th</sup> method), and Part II<sup>nd</sup> of this work, chaps. XIX et seq.—The words <span dir="rtl">פתאום ברגע קטן</span>, which are read here in the editions of Ibn-Tibbon's version, contain a double translation of the Arabic word <span dir="rtl">באלהוינא</span> <span dir="rtl">(بِالْهُوَيْنَا)</span>; the word <span dir="rtl">פתאום</span> must be deleted, as it is not found in the manuscripts of the said version. Ibn-Falaquéra renders the word <span dir="rtl">באלהוינא</span> by <span dir="rtl">בדבר קל</span>, <i>with little effort</i>, that is to say, <i>inconsiderately</i> or <i>lightly;</i> see the Appendix to the <i>Moré ha-Moré</i>, p. 153.

**French (Source):** L’auteur croit devoir faire une réserve à l’égard de cette X[[t:0]]e[[t:1]] proposition; car il reconnaît lui-même ce principe d[[t:2]]’admissibilité[[t:3]] pour une partie de l’univers, et il croit que, pour tout ce qui est au dessus de la sphère de la Lune, il ne nous est pas donné de reconnaître une loi naturelle immuable, et que mainte chose pourrait être autrement qu’elle n’est réellement. L’auteur base ses preuves de la Création sur un raisonnement analogue à celui que les [[t:4]]Motécallemîn[[t:5]] ont puisé dans cette X[[t:6]]e[[t:7]] proposition. Cf. le chapitre suivant (V[[t:8]]e[[t:9]] méthode), et la II[[t:10]]e[[t:11]] partie de cet ouvrage, chap. XIX et suiv.—Les mots [[t:12]]פתאום ברגע קטן[[t:13]], qu’on lit ici dans les éditions de la version d’Ibn-Tibbon, renferment une double traduction du mot arabe [[t:14]]באלהוינא[[t:15]] [[t:16]](بِالْهُوَيْنَا)[[t:17]] ; il faut effacer le mot [[t:18]]פתאום[[t:19]], qui ne se trouve point dans les manuscrits de ladite version. Ibn-Falaquéra rend le mot [[t:20]]באלהוינא[[t:21]] par [[t:22]]בדבר קל[[t:23]], [[t:24]]avec peu de chose[[t:25]], c’est-à-dire, [[t:26]]inconsidérément[[t:27]] ou [[t:28]]légèrement;[[t:29]] voy. l’Appendice du [[t:30]]Moré ha-Moré[[t:31]], pag 153.

### fn.1149 (Footnote)
**English:** That is to say, in the elements; for everything is born and perishes, is composed and decomposes, through the mixing of the elements, which is effected by the movement of the celestial sphere, as seen in Ch. LXXII, p. 358 et seq.
**Correction:** That is to say, in the elements; for everything is born and perishes, is composed and decomposes, through the mixing of the elements, which is effected by the movement of the celestial sphere, as seen in Ch. LXXII, p. 358 et seq.

### fn.1162 (Footnote)
**English:** Cf. Schmœlders, <i>Essai</i>, pp. 155 and 156: "Possible beings can neither exist nor not exist without a distinct cause. This quality of being possible is accepted by everyone; its value alone is debated, that is, one wonders whether it is an immediate notion, or whether this assertion still requires proof. Now, it is evident that we know this immediately; for, since reality has as much hold on a possible being before its appearance as non-reality, it is absolutely necessary to suppose a <i>motive</i> that determines the existence or non-existence of this being."
**Correction:** Cf. Schmœlders, <i>Essai</i>, pp. 155 and 156: "Possible beings can neither exist nor not exist without a distinct cause. This quality of being possible is accepted by everyone; its value alone is debated, that is, one wonders whether it is an immediate notion, or whether this assertion still requires proof. Now, it is evident that we know this immediately; for, since reality has as much hold on a possible being before its appearance as non-reality, it is absolutely necessary to suppose a <i>motive</i> that determines the existence or non-existence of this being."

**French (Source):** Cf. Schmœlders, [[t:0]]Essai[[t:1]], pag. 155 et 156: «Les êtres possibles ne peuvent ni exister ni ne pas exister sans une cause distincte. Cette qualité de l’être possible est admise par tout le monde; on discute seulement sur sa valeur, c’est-à-dire, on se demande si c’est une notion immédiate, ou si cette assertion a encore besoin d’une preuve. Or, il est évident que nous savons cela immédiatement; car, la réalité ayant autant de prise sur un être possible avant son apparition que la non-réalité, il faut absolument supposer un [[t:2]]motif[[t:3]] qui détermine l’existence ou la non-existence de cet être.»

---

### fn.1163 (Footnote)
**English:** That is to say: in the preceding method, one argues from the conditions or ways of being of the universe and its parts—conditions <i>determined</i> by the Creator—whereas here one argues from the very existence of the world, which the Creator <i>preferred</i> over its non-existence.
**Correction:** That is to say: in the preceding method, one argues from the conditions or ways of being of the universe and its parts—conditions <i>determined</i> by the Creator—whereas here one argues from the very existence of the world, which the Creator <i>preferred</i> over its non-existence.

**French (Source):** C’est-à-dire: dans la méthode précédente, on argumente par les conditions ou les manières d’être de l’univers et de ses parties, conditions [[t:0]]déterminées[[t:1]] par le Créateur, tandis qu’ici on argumente par l’existence même du monde, que le Créateur a [[t:2]]préférée[[t:3]] à sa non-existence.

---

### fn.1164 (Footnote)
**English:** The author means that the <i>Mutakallim</i> takes the word <i>possible</i> in an absolute sense, meaning thereby that which in reality can be or not be, while the philosopher understands by <i>possible</i> that which is <i>in potency</i> and which a necessary cause brings from <i>potency</i> to <i>act</i>. Consequently, what the philosopher calls <i>possible</i> is also necessary because it has an efficient cause that is necessary. See the introduction to Part II<sup></sup> of the <i>Guide</i>, prop. XIX et seq.
**Correction:** The author means that the <i>Mutakallim</i> takes the word <i>possible</i> in an absolute sense, meaning thereby that which in reality can be or not be, while the philosopher understands by <i>possible</i> that which is <i>in potency</i> and which a necessary cause brings from <i>potency</i> to <i>act</i>. Consequently, what the philosopher calls <i>possible</i> is also necessary because it has an efficient cause that is necessary. See the introduction to Part II<sup></sup> of the <i>Guide</i>, prop. XIX et seq.

**French (Source):** L’auteur veut dire que le [[t:0]]Motécallem[[t:1]] prend le mot [[t:2]]possible[[t:3]] dans un sens absolu, entendant par là ce qui en réalité peut être ou ne pas être, tandis que le philosophe entend par [[t:4]]possible[[t:5]] ce qui est [[t:6]]en puissance[[t:7]] et ce qu’une cause nécessaire fait passer de la [[t:8]]puissance[[t:9]] à l’[[t:10]]acte[[t:11]]. Par conséquent, ce que le philosophe appelle [[t:12]]possible[[t:13]] est nécessaire aussi parce qu’il a une cause efficiente qui est nécessaire. Voy. l’introduction de la II[[t:14]]e[[t:15]] partie du [[t:16]]Guide[[t:17]], propos. XIX et suiv.

---

### fn.1184 (Footnote)
**English:** That is to say: the totality of all souls forms only one <i>unity</i>. This doctrine of the <i>unity of souls</i> was professed by Abu-Becr ibn-al-Çâyeg, or Ibn Bajja, in his <i>Letter of Farewell</i> <span dir="rtl">(رسالة الوداع)</span> and in other writings. See the commentary of Moses of Narbonne on our passage, and Ibn Falaquera, <i>Moré ha-Moré</i>, p. 65. — Averroes proclaims the same doctrine: 'Zeid and 'Amr,' he says (<i>loc. cit.</i>), 'are numerically two, but they are <i>one</i> by their form, which is the <i>soul;</i> now, if the souls of Zeid and 'Amr were numerically distinct, as their persons are, their souls would be <i>two</i> in number, but a <i>unity</i> by form, so that the soul would have a soul. Since, therefore, the soul of Zeid and 'Amr is necessarily <i>one</i> by form, and since that which is <i>one</i> by form receives numerical multiplicity or division only through matter, it follows that if souls do not die with the bodies, they necessarily form, after having separated from the bodies, a numerical <i>unity</i>.' Developed by Averroes in several of his writings, this doctrine caused a great sensation among the Christian theologians of the 13<sup>th</sup> century; Albert the Great refuted it in a specific work entitled: <i>Libellus contra eos qui dicunt quod post separationem ex omnibus animabus non remanet nisi inlellectus unus et anima una</i> (see <i>Alberti Opera</i>, vol. V, p. 218 et seq., Jammy edition).
**Correction:** That is to say: the totality of all souls forms only one <i>unity</i>. This doctrine of the <i>unity of souls</i> was professed by Abu-Becr ibn-al-Çâyeg, or Ibn Bajja, in his <i>Letter of Farewell</i> <span dir="rtl">(رسالة الوداع)</span> and in other writings. See the commentary of Moses of Narbonne on our passage, and Ibn Falaquera, <i>Moré ha-Moré</i>, p. 65. — Averroes proclaims the same doctrine: 'Zeid and 'Amr,' he says (<i>loc. cit.</i>), 'are numerically two, but they are <i>one</i> by their form, which is the <i>soul;</i> now, if the souls of Zeid and 'Amr were numerically distinct, as their persons are, their souls would be <i>two</i> in number, but a <i>unity</i> by form, so that the soul would have a soul. Since, therefore, the soul of Zeid and 'Amr is necessarily <i>one</i> by form, and since that which is <i>one</i> by form receives numerical multiplicity or division only through matter, it follows that if souls do not die with the bodies, they necessarily form, after having separated from the bodies, a numerical <i>unity</i>.' Developed by Averroes in several of his writings, this doctrine caused a great sensation among the Christian theologians of the 13<sup>th</sup> century; Albert the Great refuted it in a specific work entitled: <i>Libellus contra eos qui dicunt quod post separationem ex omnibus animabus non remanet nisi inlellectus unus et anima una</i> (see <i>Alberti Opera</i>, vol. V, p. 218 et seq., Jammy edition).

**French (Source):** C’est-à-dire: l’ensemble de toutes les âmes ne forme qu’une [[t:0]]unité[[t:1]]. Cette doctrine de l’[[t:2]]unité des âmes[[t:3]] a été professée par Abou-Becr ibn-al-Çâyeg, ou Ibn-Bâdja, dans sa [[t:4]]Lettre d’Adieux[[t:5]] [[t:6]](رسالة الوداع)[[t:7]]  et dans d’autres écrits. Voy. le commentaire de Moïse de Narbonne à notre passage, et Ibn-Falaquéra, [[t:8]]Moré ha-Moré[[t:9]], pag. 65. — Ibn-Roschd proclame la même doctrine: «Zéid et ’Amr, dit-il ([[t:10]]l. c.[[t:11]]), sont numériquement deux, mais ils sont [[t:12]]un[[t:13]] par leur forme, qui est l’[[t:14]]âme;[[t:15]] or, si les âmes de Zéid et de ’Amr étaient numériquement distinctes, comme le sont leurs personnes, leurs âmes seraient [[t:16]]deux[[t:17]] en nombre, mais une [[t:18]]unité[[t:19]] par la forme, de sorte que l’âme aurait une âme. Puis donc que l’âme de Zéid et de ’Amr est nécessairement [[t:20]]une[[t:21]] par la forme, et que ce qui est [[t:22]]un[[t:23]] par la forme ne reçoit la multiplicité numérique ou la division que par la matière, il s’ensuit que, si les âmes ne meurent pas avec les corps, elles forment nécessairement, après s’être séparées des corps, une [[t:24]]unité[[t:25]] numérique.» Développée par Ibn-Roschd dans plusieurs de ses écrits, cette doctrine fit une grande sensation parmi les théologiens chrétiens du XIII[[t:26]]e[[t:27]] siècle; Albert le Grand la réfuta dans un écrit particulier intitulé: [[t:28]]Libellus contra eos qui dicunt quod post separationem ex omnibus animabus non remanet nisi inlellectus unus et anima una[[t:29]] (voy. [[t:30]]Alberti Opera[[t:31]], t. V, pag. 218 et suiv., édit. de Jammy).

### fn.1196 (Footnote)
**English:** One sees that the proofs alleged by the <i>Mutakallimūn</i> to establish the existence of God are of the kind that have been called <i>physical proofs</i>. It might appear that the methods enumerated in this chapter, and notably the I<sup>st</sup> and II<sup>nd</sup>, are based on the <i>principle of causality</i>, and yet it has been seen that the <i>Mutakallimūn</i> reject this principle in an absolute manner. But the contradiction is only apparent. For the <i>Mutakallimūn</i>, indeed, the proof of the existence of the Creator results immediately from each <i>newly produced</i> or <i>created</i> thing, which, according to them, requires the direct action of the divinity to pass from non-being to being; and if, to reach the Creator, they have us ascend through a series of creations that seem to produce one another, it is only to show that the series of transformations would be infinite if one did not stop at a first creation, and that even those who admit a series of causes and effects in nature are forced to stop at a first <i>created thing</i> which has no other cause than the will of the Creator. For them, nothing is <i>caused</i>, but everything in nature is <i>made</i> or <i>created;</i> thus they carefully avoid using the word <i>cause</i> in their arguments and refuse to adopt the designation <i>first cause</i> used by the philosophers to designate God. See above, at the beginning of Chapter LXIX. — Several of the methods enumerated in this chapter were adopted in substance by Jewish theologians of the Middle Ages; cf. Saadia, <i>Beliefs and Opinions</i>, Book I, chap. 1; Bahya, <i>Duties of the Hearts</i>, Book I, chap. 5. The Karaites especially imitated, on this point as on many others, the arguments of the Arabic <i>kalam</i>. See Judah ha-Levi, <i>Kuzari</i>, Book V, § 18.
**Correction:** One sees that the proofs alleged by the <i>Mutakallimūn</i> to establish the existence of God are of the kind that have been called <i>physical proofs</i>. It might appear that the methods enumerated in this chapter, and notably the I<sup>st</sup> and II<sup>nd</sup>, are based on the <i>principle of causality</i>, and yet it has been seen that the <i>Mutakallimūn</i> reject this principle in an absolute manner. But the contradiction is only apparent. For the <i>Mutakallimūn</i>, indeed, the proof of the existence of the Creator results immediately from each <i>newly produced</i> or <i>created</i> thing, which, according to them, requires the direct action of the divinity to pass from non-being to being; and if, to reach the Creator, they have us ascend through a series of creations that seem to produce one another, it is only to show that the series of transformations would be infinite if one did not stop at a first creation, and that even those who admit a series of causes and effects in nature are forced to stop at a first <i>created thing</i> which has no other cause than the will of the Creator. For them, nothing is <i>caused</i>, but everything in nature is <i>made</i> or <i>created;</i> thus they carefully avoid using the word <i>cause</i> in their arguments and refuse to adopt the designation <i>first cause</i> used by the philosophers to designate God. See above, at the beginning of Chapter LXIX. — Several of the methods enumerated in this chapter were adopted in substance by Jewish theologians of the Middle Ages; cf. Saadia, <i>Beliefs and Opinions</i>, Book I, chap. 1; Bahya, <i>Duties of the Hearts</i>, Book I, chap. 5. The Karaites especially imitated, on this point as on many others, the arguments of the Arabic <i>kalam</i>. See Judah ha-Levi, <i>Kuzari</i>, Book V, § 18.

**French (Source):** On voit que les preuves alléguées par les [[t:0]]Motécallemîn[[t:1]] pour établir l’existence de Dieu sont du genre de celles qu’on a appelées [[t:2]]preuves physiques[[t:3]]. Il pourrait paraître que les méthodes énumérées dans ce chapitre, et notamment la I[[t:4]]re[[t:5]] et la II[[t:6]]e[[t:7]], ont pour base le [[t:8]]principe de causalité[[t:9]], et cependant on a vu que les [[t:10]]Motécallemîn[[t:11]] rejettent ce principe d’une manière absolue. Mais la contradiction n’est qu’apparente. Pour les [[t:12]]Motécallemîn[[t:13]], en effet, la preuve de l’existence du Créateur résulterait immédiatement de chaque chose [[t:14]]nouvellement produite[[t:15]] ou [[t:16]]créée[[t:17]], qui, selon eux, a besoin de l’action directe de la divinité pour passer du non-être à l’être; et si, pour arriver jusqu’au Créateur, ils nous font remonter par une série de créations qui paraissent se produire les unes les autres, ce n’est que pour montrer que la série des transformations serait infinie si l’on ne s’arrêtait pas à une première création, et que ceux-là même qui admettent dans la nature une série de causes et d’effets sont forcés de s’arrêter à une première [[t:18]]chose créée[[t:19]] qui n’a d’autre cause que la volonté du Créateur. Pour eux rien n’est [[t:20]]causé[[t:21]], mais tout dans la nature est [[t:22]]fait[[t:23]] ou [[t:24]]créé;[[t:25]] aussi évitent-ils avec soin, dans leurs argumentations, de se servir du mot [[t:26]]cause[[t:27]] et refusent-ils d’adopter la dénomination de [[t:28]]cause première[[t:29]] employée par les philosophes pour désigner Dieu. Voy. ci-dessus, au commencement du chapitre LXIX. — Plusieurs des méthodes énumérées dans ce chapitre ont été adoptées en substance par les théologiens juifs du moyen âge; cf. Saadia, [[t:30]]Croyances et Opinions[[t:31]], liv. I, chap. 1; Ba’hya, [[t:32]]Devoirs des cœurs[[t:33]], liv. I, chap. 5. Les Karaïtes surtout imitèrent sur ce point, comme sur beaucoup d’autres, les argumentations du [[t:34]]calâm[[t:35]] arabe. Voy. Juda ha-Lévi, [[t:36]]Khozari[[t:37]], liv. V, § 18.

---

### fn.1197 (Footnote)
**English:** It will be seen from what follows that these two methods (to which three others are added) are <i>indirect;</i> for they demonstrate the unity of God by showing that the supposition of the contrary, or of dualism, leads to the impossible or the absurd. It is difficult to render exactly the two Arabic terms used by the <i>Mutakallimūn</i> to designate these two methods; both words are <i>verbal nouns</i> of the VI<sup>th</sup> form indicating reciprocity. The verb <span dir="rtl">تمانע</span> means <i>to hinder one another, to be an obstacle to one another;</i> Ibn Tibbon doubtless attached the same meaning to the word <span dir="rtl">המנעות</span> which he uses in his version, while Al-Harizi commits a misinterpretation by translating: <span dir="rtl">דרך הנמנע</span>, <i>the method <span dir="rtl">דה</span> of the impossible</i>. Bahya, who provides a demonstration of the unity of God combining the 1<sup>st</sup> and 5<sup>th</sup> methods of the <i>Mutakallimūn</i>, likewise employs the word <span dir="rtl">تهازع</span> in the sense we have just indicated; see <i>Duties of the Hearts</i>, Book I, ch. 7 (7<sup>th</sup> demonstration), where one reads, in the original Arabic: <span dir="rtl">ולו גׄאז אן יכון אכתׄר מן ואחד לאמכן אן יכון בינהם תמאנע ואכׄתלאף פי כׄלקה̈ אלמכׄלוקין</span> (the Hebrew version reads: <span dir="rtl">ואלו היה אפשר להיות יותר מאחד היה אפשר שתהיה ביניהם מחלוקת בבריאת הברואים</span>). — The verb <span dir="rtl">تغاير</span> means <i>to differ reciprocally</i>, and expresses the <i>non-identity</i> of two things. In the editions of Ibn Tibbon’s version, one reads: <span dir="rtl">דרך ההמנעות ודרך ההשתנות והחלוק</span>; the word <span dir="rtl">והחלוק</span> is found neither in the manuscripts nor in the <i>editio princeps</i>. Some manuscripts read: <span dir="rtl">ודרך ההשתנות ודרך החלוף והחלוק</span>; it is evident that Ibn Tibbon’s version rendered the Arabic word <span dir="rtl">אלתגאיר</span> in two different ways and that copyists brought the two translations together, one of which was probably written in the margin.
**Correction:** It will be seen from what follows that these two methods (to which three others are added) are <i>indirect;</i> for they demonstrate the unity of God by showing that the supposition of the contrary, or of dualism, leads to the impossible or the absurd. It is difficult to render exactly the two Arabic terms used by the <i>Mutakallimūn</i> to designate these two methods; both words are <i>verbal nouns</i> of the VI<sup>th</sup> form indicating reciprocity. The verb <span dir="rtl">تمانע</span> means <i>to hinder one another, to be an obstacle to one another;</i> Ibn Tibbon doubtless attached the same meaning to the word <span dir="rtl">המנעות</span> which he uses in his version, while Al-Harizi commits a misinterpretation by translating: <span dir="rtl">דרך הנמנע</span>, <i>the method <span dir="rtl">דה</span> of the impossible</i>. Bahya, who provides a demonstration of the unity of God combining the 1<sup>st</sup> and 5<sup>th</sup> methods of the <i>Mutakallimūn</i>, likewise employs the word <span dir="rtl">تهازع</span> in the sense we have just indicated; see <i>Duties of the Hearts</i>, Book I, ch. 7 (7<sup>th</sup> demonstration), where one reads, in the original Arabic: <span dir="rtl">ולו גׄאז אן יכון אכתׄר מן ואחד לאמכן אן יכון בינהם תמאנע ואכׄתלאף פי כׄלקה̈ אלמכׄלוקין</span> (the Hebrew version reads: <span dir="rtl">ואלו היה אפשר להיות יותר מאחד היה אפשר שתהיה ביניהם מחלוקת בבריאת הברואים</span>). — The verb <span dir="rtl">تغاير</span> means <i>to differ reciprocally</i>, and expresses the <i>non-identity</i> of two things. In the editions of Ibn Tibbon’s version, one reads: <span dir="rtl">דרך ההמנעות ודרך ההשתנות והחלוק</span>; the word <span dir="rtl">והחלוק</span> is found neither in the manuscripts nor in the <i>editio princeps</i>. Some manuscripts read: <span dir="rtl">ודרך ההשתנות ודרך החלוף והחלוק</span>; it is evident that Ibn Tibbon’s version rendered the Arabic word <span dir="rtl">אלתגאיר</span> in two different ways and that copyists brought the two translations together, one of which was probably written in the margin.

**French (Source):** On verra par ce qui suit que ces deux méthodes (auxquelles se joignent encore trois autres) sont [[t:0]]indirectes;[[t:1]] car elles démontrent l’unité de Dieu, en montrant que la supposition du contraire, ou du dualisme, conduit à l’impossible ou à l’absurde. Il est difficile de rendre exactement les deux termes arabes employés par les [[t:2]]Motécallemîn[[t:3]] pour désigner ces deux méthodes; les deux mots sont des [[t:4]]noms d’action[[t:5]] de la VI[[t:6]]e[[t:7]] forme indiquant la réciprocité. Le verbe [[t:8]]تمانع[[t:9]]  signifie [[t:10]]s’empêcher l’un l’autre, être un obstacle l’un pour l’autre;[[t:11]] Ibn-Tibbon attachait sans doute le même sens au mot [[t:12]]המנעות[[t:13]] qu’il emploie dans sa version, tandis qu’Al-’Harizi fait un contre-sens en traduisant: [[t:14]]דרך הנמנע[[t:15]], [[t:16]]la méthode [[t:17]]ده[[t:18]]  l’impossible[[t:19]]. Ba’hya, qui donne sur l’unité de Dieu une démonstration combinée de la I[[t:20]]re[[t:21]] et de la V[[t:22]]e[[t:23]] méthode des [[t:24]]Motécallemîn[[t:25]], y emploie également le mot [[t:26]]تهازع[[t:27]]  dans le sens que nous venons d’indiquer; voy. [[t:28]]Devoirs des cœurs[[t:29]], liv. I, ch. 7 (7[[t:30]]e[[t:31]] démonstration), où on lit, dans l’original arabe: [[t:32]]ולו גׄאז אן יכון אכתׄר מן ואחד לאמכן אן יכון בינהם תמאנע ואכׄתלאף פי כׄלקה̈ אלמכׄלוקין[[t:33]] (la version hébr. porte: [[t:34]]ואלו היה אפשר להיות יותר מאחד היה אפשר שתהיה ביניהם מחלוקת בבריאת הברואים[[t:35]]). — Le verbe [[t:36]]تغاير[[t:37]]  signifie [[t:38]]différer réciproquement[[t:39]], et exprime la [[t:40]]non-identité[[t:41]] de deux choses. Dans les éditions de la version d’Ibn-Tibbon on lit: [[t:42]]דרך ההמנעות ודרך ההשתנות והחלוק[[t:43]]; le mot [[t:44]]והחלוק[[t:45]] ne se trouve ni dans les manuscrits, ni dans l’édition [[t:46]]princeps[[t:47]]. Quelques manuscrits portent: [[t:48]]ודרך ההשתנות ודרך החלוף והחלוק[[t:49]]; il est évident que la version d’Ibn-Tibbon rendait le mot arabe [[t:50]]אלתגאיר[[t:51]] de deux manières différentes et que les copistes ont réuni ensemble les deux traductions, dont l’une était probablement écrite en marge.

### fn.1213 (Footnote)
**English:** Cf. above, chap. LIII, p. 208 et seq. — The reading we have adopted agrees with Ibn Tibbon's version; in most manuscripts we read: <span dir="rtl">ומעני אלעלם גיר מעני אלקדרה̈ ענדה גיר מעני אלאראדה̈</span>, and in one of the Leiden manuscripts: <span dir="rtl">וגיר מעני אלאראדה̈</span>; similarly Al-Harizi: <span dir="rtl">ועניין המדע זולת עניין היכולת אצלו וזולת עניין החפץ</span>.
**Correction:** Cf. above, chap. LIII, p. 208 et seq. — The reading we have adopted agrees with Ibn Tibbon's version; in most manuscripts we read: <span dir="rtl">ומעני אלעלם גיר מעני אלקדרה̈ ענדה גיר מעני אלאראדה̈</span>, and in one of the Leiden manuscripts: <span dir="rtl">וגיר מעני אלאראדה̈</span>; similarly Al-Harizi: <span dir="rtl">ועניין המדע זולת עניין היכולת אצלו וזולת עניין החפץ</span>.

### fn.1224 (Footnote)
**English:** Literally: <i>And perhaps, just as the Christians believe that He is</i> <small>THREE</small>, <i>without it being so, we likewise believe that He is</i> <small>ONE</small>, <i>without such being the case;</i> that is to say: the argument in question being insufficient to establish the <i>non-plurality</i> of God, it might be that our belief is no more true than that of the Christians, and that God is neither <i>one</i> nor <i>three</i>, but some sort of plurality. — In Ibn-Tibbon's version, one should read: <span dir="rtl">שהם שלשה ואינו בן</span>, as found in the manuscripts and the <i>editio princeps;</i> the words <span dir="rtl">ואינו בן</span> are missing from our editions.
**Correction:** Literally: <i>And perhaps, just as the Christians believe that He is</i> <small>THREE</small>, <i>without it being so, we likewise believe that He is</i> <small>ONE</small>, <i>without such being the case;</i> that is to say: the argument in question being insufficient to establish the <i>non-plurality</i> of God, it might be that our belief is no more true than that of the Christians, and that God is neither <i>one</i> nor <i>three</i>, but some sort of plurality. — In Ibn-Tibbon's version, one should read: <span dir="rtl">שהם שלשה ואינו בן</span>, as found in the manuscripts and the <i>editio princeps;</i> the words <span dir="rtl">ואינו בן</span> are missing from our editions.

**French (Source):** Littéralement: [[t:0]]Et peut-être, de même que les chrétiens croient qu’il est[[t:1]] [[t:2]]TROIS[[t:3]], [[t:4]]sans qu’il en soit ainsi, croyons nous de même qu’il est[[t:5]] [[t:6]]UN[[t:7]], [[t:8]]sans que la chose soit ainsi;[[t:9]] c’est-à-dire: l’argumentation en question étant insuffisante pour établir la [[t:10]]non-pluralité[[t:11]] de Dieu, il se pourrait que notre croyance ne fût pas plus vraie que celle des chrétiens, et que Dieu ne fût ni [[t:12]]un[[t:13]] ni [[t:14]]trois[[t:15]], mais une pluralité quelconque. — Dans la version d’Ibn-Tibbon, il faut lire: [[t:16]]שהם שלשה ואינו בן[[t:17]], comme l’ont les manuscrits et l’édition [[t:18]]princeps;[[t:19]] les mots [[t:20]]ואינו בן[[t:21]] manquent dans nos éditions.

### fn.1249 (Footnote)
**English:** That is to say, to the sublunar bodies, composed of the four elements, and whose matter (<i>ὕλη</i>) is different from that of the celestial spheres. See above, p. 356 et seq., and cf. p. 442, note 2.
**Correction:** That is to say, to the sublunar bodies, composed of the four elements, and whose matter (<i>ὕλη</i>) is different from that of the celestial spheres. See above, p. 356 et seq., and cf. p. 442, note 2.

**French (Source):** C’est-à-dire, aux corps sublunaires, composés des quatre éléments, et dont la matière ([[t:0]]ὕλη[[t:1]]) est différente de celle des sphères célestes. Voy. ci-dessus, pag. 356 et suiv., et cf. pag. 442, note 2.

### fn.1281.sub_1 (Footnote)
**English:** Now, he observes, this motion must be inherent to the nature of this substance; for it would be astonishing and entirely unreasonable if this motion, which alone is continuous and eternal, could be <i>contrary to nature</i>, since generally what is contrary to nature is quickly destroyed: E<i>ἰ δὲ παρὰ φύσιν φέρεται τά φερόμενα ϰύϰλῳ τὴν περὶξ φοράν, θαυμαστὸν ϰαὶ παντελῶς ἀλογον τὸ μόνέν εἶναι συνεχῆ τάυτην τὴν ϰὶνησιν ϰαι ἀίδιον, οὖσαν παρὰ, φύσιν · φαίνεται γὰρ ἔν γε τοῖς ἀλλοις τάχιστα φθειρόμενα τὰ παρὰ φύσιν</i>. The Arabic version, which rendered the words <i>contrary to nature</i> (<i>παρὰ φύσιν</i>) as <i>accidental</i>, better explains the terms of Maimonides' proposition. Here is how the passage we have just cited was paraphrased in Ibn Rushd's Middle Commentary on <i>De Caelo et Mundo,</i> Book I, <i>Summa</i> IV, <i>Demonstration</i> 5 (Hebrew version): <span dir="rtl">והוא שזאת התנועה הסבובית שהיא סביב האמצע לא המלט משתהיה לזה הגשם טבעית או מקרית ושקר הוא שתהיה מקרית בי התנועה המקרית אי אפשר שתמצא תמידית אין תכלית לה ושהצעת זה יוצאה מכל הקש כי אԃנחנו רואים הדברים המקריים כלים אובדים</span>.
**Correction:** Now, he observes, this motion must be inherent to the nature of this substance; for it would be astonishing and entirely unreasonable if this motion, which alone is continuous and eternal, could be <i>contrary to nature</i>, since generally what is contrary to nature is quickly destroyed: E<i>ἰ δὲ παρὰ φύσιν φέρεται τά φερόμενα ϰύϰλῳ τὴν περὶξ φοράν, θαυμαστὸν ϰαὶ παντελῶς ἀλογον τὸ μόνέν εἶναι συνεχῆ τάυτην τὴν ϰὶνησιν ϰαι ἀίδιον, οὖσαν παρὰ, φύσιν · φαίνεται γὰρ ἔν γε τοῖς ἀλλοις τάχιστα φθειρόμενα τὰ παρὰ φύσιν</i>. The Arabic version, which rendered the words <i>contrary to nature</i> (<i>παρὰ φύσιν</i>) as <i>accidental</i>, better explains the terms of Maimonides' proposition. Here is how the passage we have just cited was paraphrased in Ibn Rushd's Middle Commentary on <i>De Caelo et Mundo,</i> Book I, <i>Summa</i> IV, <i>Demonstration</i> 5 (Hebrew version): <span dir="rtl">והוא שזאת התנועה הסבובית שהיא סביב האמצע לא המלט משתהיה לזה הגשם טבעית או מקרית ושקר הוא שתהיה מקרית בי התנועה המקרית אי אפשר שתמצא תמידית אין תכלית לה ושהצעת זה יוצאה מכל הקש כי אԃנחנו רואים הדברים המקריים כלים אובדים</span>.

**French (Source):** Or, fait-il observer, ce mouvement doit être inhérent à la nature de cette substance; car il serait étonnant et tout à fait irraisonnable que ce mouvement, qui seul est continuel et éternel, pût être [[t:14]]contre nature[[t:15]], puisqu’en général ce qui est contre nature est promptement détruit: E[[t:16]]ἰ δὲ παρὰ φύσιν φέρεται τά φερόμενα ϰύϰλῳ τὴν περὶξ φοράν, θαυμαστὸν ϰαὶ παντελῶς ἀλογον τὸ μόνην εἶναι συνεχῆ τάυτην τὴν ϰὶνησιν ϰαι ἀίδιον, οὖσαν παρὰ, φύσιν · φαίνεται γὰρ ἔν γε τοῖς ἀλλοις τάχιστα φθειρόμενα τὰ παρὰ φύσιν[[t:17]]. La version arabe qui rendait les mots [[t:18]]contre nature[[t:19]] ([[t:20]]παρὰ φύσιν[[t:21]]) par [[t:22]]accidentel[[t:23]], explique mieux les termes de la proposition de Maïmonide. Voici comment le passage que nous venons de citer a été paraphrasé dans le commentaire moyen d’Ibn-Roschd, [[t:24]]de Cœlo et Mundo,[[t:25]] liv. I, [[t:26]]summa[[t:27]] IV, [[t:28]]demonstrat.[[t:29]] 5 (vers, hébr.): [[t:30]]והוא שזאת התנועה הסבובית שהיא סביב האמצע לא המלט משתהיה לזה הגשם טבעית או מקרית ושקר הוא שתהיה מקרית בי התנועה המקרית אי אפשר שתמצא תמידית אין תכלית לה ושהצעת זה יוצאה מכל הקש כי אנחנו רואים הדברים המקריים כלים אובדים[[t:31]].

### fn.1293.sub_1 (Footnote)
**English:** One might initially find it surprising that Maimonides places transformation before generation and corruption; however, it appears that our author considers transformation from a more general perspective—that is, not only in relation to the category of quality, as in the IV<sup>th</sup> proposition, but also in relation to <i>generation</i>, which is itself in some sense a transformation of matter by form, a transformation effected by an agent more or less remote, which needs to draw closer to the matter to be transformed. It is also in this sense that Averroes explains the passage in the <i>Physics</i>. See the Works of Aristotle with the Commentaries of Averroes, folio ed., vol. IV, fol. 180 <i>c</i>: «Deinde dicit: <i>Et manifestum est quod dispositio motoris tunc non currit eodem modo</i>, <i>sed forte quandoque erit propinquior alterato et quandoque remotior</i>, etc. Id est, et quia primum alterans, quod non alteratur, non alterat semper, sed quandoque, necesse est ut non habeat se cum alterato in eadem dispositione, sed quandoque appropinquetur ei, et alteret, et quandoque removeatur, et non alteret: et propinquitas, et distantia non est, nisi per translationem: ergo translatio præcedit naturaliter alterationem, scilicet quod, cum utraque fuerit in actu: deinde alterans alteravit, postquam non alterabat: necesse est ut alterum moveatur in loco aut alterans, aut alteratum, aut utrumque. Si autem alterum fuerit <i>generatum</i>, aut utrumque, et posuerimus hoc esse causam ejus, quod quandoque alterat, et quandoque non, <i>manifestabitur quod translatio debet prœcedere eodem modo, cum alteratio etiam prœcedat generationem; generatio enim aut est alteratio aut sequitur alterationem</i>.»
**Correction:** One might initially find it surprising that Maimonides places transformation before generation and corruption; however, it appears that our author considers transformation from a more general perspective—that is, not only in relation to the category of quality, as in the IV<sup>th</sup> proposition, but also in relation to <i>generation</i>, which is itself in some sense a transformation of matter by form, a transformation effected by an agent more or less remote, which needs to draw closer to the matter to be transformed. It is also in this sense that Averroes explains the passage in the <i>Physics</i>. See the Works of Aristotle with the Commentaries of Averroes, folio ed., vol. IV, fol. 180 <i>c</i>: «Deinde dicit: <i>Et manifestum est quod dispositio motoris tunc non currit eodem modo</i>, <i>sed forte quandoque erit propinquior alterato et quandoque remotior</i>, etc. Id est, et quia primum alterans, quod non alteratur, non alterat semper, sed quandoque, necesse est ut non habeat se cum alterato in eadem dispositione, sed quandoque appropinquetur ei, et alteret, et quandoque removeatur, et non alteret: et propinquitas, et distantia non est, nisi per translationem: ergo translatio præcedit naturaliter alterationem, scilicet quod, cum utraque fuerit in actu: deinde alterans alteravit, postquam non alterabat: necesse est ut alterum moveatur in loco aut alterans, aut alteratum, aut utrumque. Si autem alterum fuerit <i>generatum</i>, aut utrumque, et posuerimus hoc esse causam ejus, quod quandoque alterat, et quandoque non, <i>manifestabitur quod translatio debet prœcedere eodem modo, cum alteratio etiam prœcedat generationem; generatio enim aut est alteratio aut sequitur alterationem</i>.»

**French (Source):** On pourrait s’étonner d’abord que Maïmonide place la transformation avant la naissance et la corruption; mais il paraît que notre auteur considère la transformation à un point de vue plus général, c’est-à-dire non-seulement par rapport à la catégorie de la qualité, comme dans la IV[[t:20]]e[[t:21]] proposition, mais aussi par rapport à la [[t:22]]naissance[[t:23]], qui est elle-même en quelque sorte une transformation de la matière par la forme, transformation qui s’opère par un agent plus ou moins éloigné, qui a besoin de se rapprocher de la matière à transformer. C’est aussi dans ce sens qu’Ibn-Roschd explique le passage de la [[t:24]]Physique[[t:25]]. Voy. les OEuvres d’Aristote avec les Commentaires d’Averroës, édit. in-fol., t. IV, fol. 180 [[t:26]]c[[t:27]]: «Deinde dicit: [[t:28]]Et manifestum est quod dispositio motoris tunc non currit eodem modo[[t:29]], [[t:30]]sed forte quandoque erit propinquior alterato et quandoque remotior[[t:31]], etc. Id est, et quia primum alterans, quod non alteratur, non alterat semper, sed quandoque, necesse est ut non habeat se cum alterato in eadem dispositione, sed quandoque appropinquetur ei, et alteret, et quandoque removeatur, et non alteret: et propinquitas, et distantia non est, nisi per translationem: ergo translatio præcedit naturaliter alterationem, scilicet quod, cum utraque fuerit in actu: deinde alterans alteravit, postquam non alterabat: necesse est ut alterum moveatur in loco aut alterans, aut alteratum, aut utrumque. Si autem alterum fuerit [[t:32]]generatum[[t:33]], aut utrumque, et posuerimus hoc esse causam ejus, quod quandoque alterat, et quandoque non, [[t:34]]manifestabitur quod translatio debet prœcedere eodem modo, cum alteratio etiam prœcedat generationem; generatio enim aut est alteratio aut sequitur alterationem[[t:35]].»

### fn.1309 (Footnote)
**English:** This proposition and the following two are borrowed from Ibn Sina, who was the first to make, within the concept <i>of necessary being</i> (as opposed to the absolute <i>possible</i>, which comes into being and perishes), this distinction between that which is <i>necessary in itself</i> or the absolute necessary, and that which is <i>necessary through another</i>, being by its own essence in the category of the possible. To the second species belong, according to Ibn Sina, the celestial spheres, in which one distinguishes between potency and act, matter and form, and which derive the quality of <i>necessary beings</i> only from their relationship with the first cause, or God. Ibn Sina departs, in this respect, from Aristotle, who expressly extends the concept of <i>necessary being</i> to that which is eternally moved, or to the celestial spheres, which, he says, are not at all in potency and have no matter properly so called—that is, matter subject to generation and destruction. See <i>Metaph</i>., Book IX, chap. 8: O<i>ὐθὲν ἄρα τῶν ἀφθάρτων ἁπλῶς δυνάμει ἐστὶν ὂν ἁπλῶς οὐδὲ τῶν ἐξ ἀνάγκης ὄντων, καὶ τοι ταῦτα πρῶτα.... διὸ ἀεὶ ἐνεργεῖ ἥλιος καὶ ἄστρα καὶ ὅλος ὁ οὐρανός, κ. τ. λ</i>. Cf. Treatise <i>On the Heavens</i>, I, 2. Ibn Rushd contested Ibn Sina's theory in several places in his works. Cf. my <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, pp. 358-359.
**Correction:** This proposition and the following two are borrowed from Ibn Sina, who was the first to make, within the concept <i>of necessary being</i> (as opposed to the absolute <i>possible</i>, which comes into being and perishes), this distinction between that which is <i>necessary in itself</i> or the absolute necessary, and that which is <i>necessary through another</i>, being by its own essence in the category of the possible. To the second species belong, according to Ibn Sina, the celestial spheres, in which one distinguishes between potency and act, matter and form, and which derive the quality of <i>necessary beings</i> only from their relationship with the first cause, or God. Ibn Sina departs, in this respect, from Aristotle, who expressly extends the concept of <i>necessary being</i> to that which is eternally moved, or to the celestial spheres, which, he says, are not at all in potency and have no matter properly so called—that is, matter subject to generation and destruction. See <i>Metaph</i>., Book IX, chap. 8: O<i>ὐθὲν ἄρα τῶν ἀφθάρτων ἁπλῶς δυνάμει ἐστὶν ὂν ἁπλῶς οὐδὲ τῶν ἐξ ἀνάγκης ὄντων, καὶ τοι ταῦτα πρῶτα.... διὸ ἀεὶ ἐνεργεῖ ἥλιος καὶ ἄστρα καὶ ὅλος ὁ οὐρανός, κ. τ. λ</i>. Cf. Treatise <i>On the Heavens</i>, I, 2. Ibn Rushd contested Ibn Sina's theory in several places in his works. Cf. my <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, pp. 358-359.

**French (Source):** Cette proposition et les deux suivantes sont empruntées à Ibn-Sinâ, qui le premier a fait, dans l’idée [[t:0]]d’étre nécessaire[[t:1]] (opposé au [[t:2]]possible[[t:3]] absolu, qui naît et périt), cette distinction entre ce qui est [[t:4]]nécessaire en lui-même[[t:5]] ou le nécessaire absolu, et ce qui est [[t:6]]nécessaire par autre chose[[t:7]], étant par sa propre essence dans la catégorie du possible. De la deuxième espèce sont, selon Ibn-Sinâ, les sphères célestes, dans lesquelles on distingue la puissance et l’acte, la matière et la forme, et qui ne tiennent la qualité d’[[t:8]]êtres nécessaires[[t:9]] que de leur rapport avec la cause première, ou Dieu. Ibn-Sinâ s’écarte, sous ce rapport, d’Aristote, qui étend expressément l’idée d’[[t:10]]être nécessaire[[t:11]] à ce qui est mû éternellement, ou aux sphères célestes, lesquelles, dit-il, ne sont point en puissance et n’ont pas de matière proprement dite, c’est-à-dire de matière sujette à la naissance et à la destruction. Voy. [[t:12]]Métaph[[t:13]]., l. IX, chap. 8: O[[t:14]]ὐθέν ἂρα τῶν ἀφθάρτων ἁπλῶς δυνάμει ἐστὶν ὄν ἁπλῶς οὐδὲ τῶν ἐξ ἀνὰγϰης ὄντων, ϰαί τοι ταῦτα πρῶτα.... διὸ ἀεὶ ἐνεργεῖ ἥλιος ϰαὶ ἄστρα ϰαὶ ὅλος ὁ οὐρανὸς, ϰ. τ. λ[[t:15]]. Cf. Traité [[t:16]]du Ciel[[t:17]], I, 2. Ibn-Roschd a combattu la théorie d’Ibn-Sinâ dans plusieurs endroits de ses ouvrages. Cf. mes [[t:18]]Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe[[t:19]], p. 358-359.

### fn.1314 (Footnote)
**English:** The concepts of potency and act, of matter and form, are so familiar to the Peripatetics that the proposition in question here requires no explanation. It should only be noted that the author understands by <i>body</i> here not only that which is subject to generation and corruption, but also the celestial bodies; these, according to Aristotle, while having no matter susceptible to generation, do have matter that serves as a substratum for local motion. See Arist., <i>Metaph</i>., IX, 8, at the end, and XII, 2, and cf. <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, p. 4, note 1, and p. 18, note 1. Maimonides adopted the opinion of Ibn Sina, who claimed to provide a general demonstration of the existence of matter and form in bodies, applying to <i>all bodies</i>, including the celestial bodies. Cf. Shahrastani, p. 366 (German trans., II, pp. 239-240). This opinion, by which one might be led to attribute also to celestial bodies a <i>being in potency</i> (which would be contrary to Aristotle's theories), was contested by Ibn Rushd; the latter considers the celestial bodies to be simple bodies that find their form or their entelechy in the <i>separate intelligences</i>. Cf. Ibn Falaquera, <i>Moreh ha-Moreh</i>, on this proposition (pp. 71-72).
**Correction:** The concepts of potency and act, of matter and form, are so familiar to the Peripatetics that the proposition in question here requires no explanation. It should only be noted that the author understands by <i>body</i> here not only that which is subject to generation and corruption, but also the celestial bodies; these, according to Aristotle, while having no matter susceptible to generation, do have matter that serves as a substratum for local motion. See Arist., <i>Metaph</i>., IX, 8, at the end, and XII, 2, and cf. <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, p. 4, note 1, and p. 18, note 1. Maimonides adopted the opinion of Ibn Sina, who claimed to provide a general demonstration of the existence of matter and form in bodies, applying to <i>all bodies</i>, including the celestial bodies. Cf. Shahrastani, p. 366 (German trans., II, pp. 239-240). This opinion, by which one might be led to attribute also to celestial bodies a <i>being in potency</i> (which would be contrary to Aristotle's theories), was contested by Ibn Rushd; the latter considers the celestial bodies to be simple bodies that find their form or their entelechy in the <i>separate intelligences</i>. Cf. Ibn Falaquera, <i>Moreh ha-Moreh</i>, on this proposition (pp. 71-72).

**French (Source):** Les idées de puissance et d’acte, de matière et de forme, sont si familières aux péripatéticiens, que la proposition dont il s’agit ici n’a pas besoin d’explication. Il faut faire remarquer seulement que l’auteur entend ici par [[t:0]]corps[[t:1]], non-seulement ce qui est soumis à la naissance et à la corruption, mais aussi les corps célestes; ceux-ci, selon Aristote, tout en n’ayant pas de matière susceptible de génération, en ont une qui sert de substratum au mouvement de translation. Voy. Arist., [[t:2]]Métaph[[t:3]]., IX, 8, à la fin et XII, 2, et Cf. [[t:4]]Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe[[t:5]], pag. 4, note 1, et p. 18, note 1. Maïmonide a adopté l’opinion d’Ibn-Sinâ, qui a prétendu donner, de l’existence de la matière et de la forme dans les corps, une démonstration générale, s’appliquant à [[t:6]]tous les corps[[t:7]], y compris les corps célestes. Cf. Schahrestàni, pag. 366 (tr. all., II, p. 239-240). Cette opinion, par laquelle on pourrait être amené à attribuer aussi aux corps célestes un [[t:8]]être en puissance[[t:9]] (ce qui serait contraire aux théories d’Aristote), a été combattue par Ibn-Roschd; celui-ci considère les corps célestes comme des corps simples qui trouvent leur forme ou leur entéléchie dans les [[t:10]]intelligences séparées[[t:11]]. Cf. Ibn-Falaquéra, [[t:12]]Moré ha-Moré[[t:13]], sur cette proposition (pag. 71-72).

### fn.1334 (Footnote)
**English:** See <i>Phys</i>., Book VIII, chap. 2. After discussing this objection, drawn from the movement of animals, which appears to be spontaneous and not to proceed from any movement coming from the outside, Aristotle observes that this is only a false appearance, and that we always notice, in what composes the animal organism, certain movements whose cause should not be sought in the animal itself, but in what surrounds it from the outside, such that there are external movements that act upon the intellectual and appetitive faculties: ὁ<i>ρῶμεν γὰρ ἀεί τι ϰινούμενον ἐν τῷ ζῴῳ τῶν συμφύτων.... οὐδὲν οὖν ϰωλύει, μᾶλλον δ’ ἴσως ἀναγϰαῖον τῷ σώματι πολλὰς ἐγγίγνεσθαι ϰινήσεις ὑπὸ τοῦ περιέχοντος, τούτων δ’ ἐνίας τὴν διάνοιαν ἢ τὴν ὄρεξιν ϰινεῖν, ϰ. τ. λ.</i> Cf. the treatise <i>On the Motion of Animals</i>, chap. 6: ὁ<i>ρῶμεν δὲ τὰ ϰινοῦντα τὸ ζῷον διάνοιαν, ϰαὶ φαντασίαν ϰαὶ προαίρεσιν, ϰαὶ βούλησιν, ϰαὶ ἐπιθυμίαν · ταῦτα δε πάντα ἀνάγεται εἰς νοῦν ϰαὶ ὄρεξιν</i>. — To better understand the terms that Maimonides reports here in Aristotle's name, we will further cite the passage from the <i>Physics</i> according to the Arabo-Latin version with the explanation of Ibn Rushd (Works of Aristotle with the commentaries of Averroes, vol. IV, f. 161, col. 3): "Semper enim invenimus aliquid moveri in animali, quod est naturale in eo; et causa istius motus animalis non est anima ejus, sed aer qui continet animal in eo, quod reputo. Et cum dicimus ipsum moveri a se, non intendimus omni motu, sed motu locali. Et nihil prohibet, immo dignum est ut sit necessarium, ut in corpore fiant plures motus a continente, quorum quidam movent voluntatem et appetitum, et tunc ista movebunt animal secundum totum." Here is how Ibn Rushd explains the end of this passage: "Et dixit <i>et nihil prohibet</i>, <i>etc</i>., id est et necesse est ut principium motuum animalis sit ex continente: immo hoc est necessarium. Et quia posuit quod in corpore animalis fiunt plures motus ex continente, narravit quomodo accidit ex istis motibus ut animal moveatur in loco, et dixit <i>quo rumquædam moventur</i>, <i>etc</i>., id est, et hoc est ita, quod in animali fiet ab eo, quod accidit sibi, opinio, voluntas, et appetitus ad motum, aut ad expellendum nocumentum contingens ex continente, aut ad inducendum juvamentum. Et intendit hic per <i>opinionem</i> aliquid commune virtuti imaginativæ, et rationali. Animali enim non accidit appetitus, nisi ex imaginatione; e.g., that when it suffers and when it fears, it flees; and when appetites are removed, it rests; or, when fatigue occurs to it, and it desires rest." On <i>imagination</i>, see also below, at the beginning of chap. IV.
**Correction:** See <i>Phys</i>., Book VIII, chap. 2. After discussing this objection, drawn from the movement of animals, which appears to be spontaneous and not to proceed from any movement coming from the outside, Aristotle observes that this is only a false appearance, and that we always notice, in what composes the animal organism, certain movements whose cause should not be sought in the animal itself, but in what surrounds it from the outside, such that there are external movements that act upon the intellectual and appetitive faculties: ὁ<i>ρῶμεν γὰρ ἀεί τι ϰινούμενον ἐν τῷ ζῴῳ τῶν συμφύτων.... οὐδὲν οὖν ϰωλύει, μᾶλλον δ’ ἴσως ἀναγϰαῖον τῷ σώματι πολλὰς ἐγγίγνεσθαι ϰινήσεις ὑπὸ τοῦ περιέχοντος, τούτων δ’ ἐνίας τὴν διάνοιαν ἢ τὴν ὄρεξιν ϰινεῖν, ϰ. τ. λ.</i> Cf. the treatise <i>On the Motion of Animals</i>, chap. 6: ὁ<i>ρῶμεν δὲ τὰ ϰινοῦντα τὸ ζῷον διάνοιαν, ϰαὶ φαντασίαν ϰαὶ προαίρεσιν, ϰαὶ βούλησιν, ϰαὶ ἐπιθυμίαν · ταῦτα δε πάντα ἀνάγεται εἰς νοῦν ϰαὶ ὄρεξιν</i>. — To better understand the terms that Maimonides reports here in Aristotle's name, we will further cite the passage from the <i>Physics</i> according to the Arabo-Latin version with the explanation of Ibn Rushd (Works of Aristotle with the commentaries of Averroes, vol. IV, f. 161, col. 3): "Semper enim invenimus aliquid moveri in animali, quod est naturale in eo; et causa istius motus animalis non est anima ejus, sed aer qui continet animal in eo, quod reputo. Et cum dicimus ipsum moveri a se, non intendimus omni motu, sed motu locali. Et nihil prohibet, immo dignum est ut sit necessarium, ut in corpore fiant plures motus a continente, quorum quidam movent voluntatem et appetitum, et tunc ista movebunt animal secundum totum." Here is how Ibn Rushd explains the end of this passage: "Et dixit <i>et nihil prohibet</i>, <i>etc</i>., id est et necesse est ut principium motuum animalis sit ex continente: immo hoc est necessarium. Et quia posuit quod in corpore animalis fiunt plures motus ex continente, narravit quomodo accidit ex istis motibus ut animal moveatur in loco, et dixit <i>quo rumquædam moventur</i>, <i>etc</i>., id est, et hoc est ita, quod in animali fiet ab eo, quod accidit sibi, opinio, voluntas, et appetitus ad motum, aut ad expellendum nocumentum contingens ex continente, aut ad inducendum juvamentum. Et intendit hic per <i>opinionem</i> aliquid commune virtuti imaginativæ, et rationali. Animali enim non accidit appetitus, nisi ex imaginatione; e.g., that when it suffers and when it fears, it flees; and when appetites are removed, it rests; or, when fatigue occurs to it, and it desires rest." On <i>imagination</i>, see also below, at the beginning of chap. IV.

**French (Source):** Voyez [[t:0]]Phys[[t:1]]., liv. VIII, chap. 2. Après avoir parlé de cette objection, tirée du mouvement des animaux, qui paraît être spontané et ne procéder d’aucun mouvement venu du dehors, Aristote fait observer que ce n’est là qu’une fausse apparence, et que nous remarquons toujours, dans ce qui compose l’organisme animal, certains mouvements dont la cause ne doit pas être cherchée dans l’animal même, mais dans ce qui l’environne au dehors, de sorte qu’il y a des mouvements extérieurs qui agissent sur les facultés intellectuelles et appétitives: Ὁ[[t:2]]ρῶμεν γὰρ ἀεί τι ϰινούμενον ἐν τῷ ζῴῳ τῶν συμφύτων.... οὐδὲν οὖν ϰωλύει, μᾶλλον δ’ ἴσως ἀναγϰαῖον τῷ σώματι πολλὰς ἐγγίγνεσθαι ϰινήσεις ὑπὸ τοῦ περιέχοντος, τούτων δ’ ὲνίας τὴν διὰνοιαν ἢ τὴν ὄρεξιν ϰινεῖν, ϰ. τ. λ.[[t:3]] Cf. le traité [[t:4]]du Mouvement des animaux[[t:5]], chap. 6: Ὁ[[t:6]]ρῶμεν δὲ τὰ ϰινοῦντα τὸ ζῷον διάνοιαν, ϰαὶ φαντασίαν ϰαὶ προαίρεσιν, ϰαὶ βούλησιν, ϰαὶ ἐπιθυμίαν · ταῦτα δε πάντα ἀνάγεται εἰς νοῦν ϰαὶ ὄρεξιν[[t:7]]. — Pour qu’on puisse mieux comprendre les termes que Maïmonide rapporte ici au nom d’Aristote, nous citerons encore le passage de la [[t:8]]Physique[[t:9]] d’après la version arabe-latine avec l’explication d’Ibn-Roschd (OEuvres d’Aristote avec les commentaires d’Averroès, t. IV, f. 161, col. 3): «Semper enim invenimus aliquid moveri in animali, quod est naturale in eo; et causa istius motus animalis non est anima ejus, sed aer qui continet animal in eo, quod reputo. Et cum dicimus ipsum moveri a se, non intendimus omni motu, sed motu locali. Et nihil prohibet, immo dignum est ut sit necessarium, ut in corpore fiant plures motus a continente, quorum quidam movent voluntatem et appetitum, et tunc ista movebunt animal secundum totum.» Voici comment Ibn-Roschd explique la fin de ce passage: «Et dixit [[t:10]]et nihil prohibet[[t:11]], [[t:12]]etc[[t:13]]., id est et necesse est ut principium motuum animalis sit ex continente: immo hoc est necessarium. Et quia posuit quod in corpore animalis fiunt plures motus ex continente, narravit quomodo accidit ex istis motibus ut animal moveatur in loco, et dixit [[t:14]]quo rumquœdam moventur[[t:15]], [[t:16]]etc[[t:17]]., id est, et hoc est ita, quod in animali fiet ab eo, quod accidit sibi, opinio, voluntas, et appetitus ad motum, aut ad expellendum nocumentum contingens ex continente, aut ad inducendum juvamentum. Et intendit hic per [[t:18]]opinionem[[t:19]] aliquid commune virtuti imaginativæ, et rationali. Animali enim non accidit appetitus, nisi ex imaginatione; v. g. quod, cum patitur et cum timet, fugit: et cum auferuntur appetitus, quiescit: aut, cum accidit ei fatigatio, et appetit quietem.» Sur l’[[t:20]]imagination[[t:21]], voir aussi plus loin, au commencement du chap. IV.

### fn.1335 (Footnote)
**English:** See <i>Phys</i>., VIII, 1 (p. 251 <i>a</i>). Aristotle, after recalling the definition of motion given above, adds that, even without this definition of motion, everyone will grant that regarding every motion, it is necessary that what moves be capable of moving, such as being capable of transforming what is transformed, and capable of changing the place of what is transported, <i>so that a thing must be combustible before burning, and capable of igniting before it ignites</i>. (<i>ὥστε δεῖ πρότερον ϰαυστὸν εἶναι πρὶν ϰάεσθαι, ϰαὶ ϰαυστιϰὸν πρὶν ϰάειν</i>). According to Ibn Rushd (loc. cit., f. 155, c. 3), this passage means nothing other than that motion (which, according to Aristotle, is <i>the entelechy of a mobile thing insofar as it is mobile</i>) must exist in potentiality in every mobile thing. But the Arabic commentator informs us that Al-Farabi understood this passage in the sense that every power must <i>temporally</i> precede the act, not only in motion, but generally in everything that occurs: "Dico secundum hanc expositionem intellexit Alpharabius et alii hoc capitulum, scilicet quod induxit definitionem motus ad declarandum potentiam esse ante actum; et hoc non est proprium motui, secundum quod est motus, sed est proprium novo facto secundum quod est novum factum, scilicet ut potentia et posse novi præcedat ipsum <i>secundum tempus</i>." It is evident that Maimonides borrowed the terms of Al-Farabi's explanation, which Ibn Rushd declares erroneous: "Et hoc quod dixit <i>et sine hac definitione</i>, etc., hoc decepit homines in hoc: et existimaverunt ipsum declarare potentiam esse ante actum in tempore, et ipse intendebat dicere quod non dicitur moveri nisi illud in cujus natura est motus, scilicet corpus mobile: et quod non invenitur in immobili."
**Correction:** See <i>Phys</i>., VIII, 1 (p. 251 <i>a</i>). Aristotle, after recalling the definition of motion given above, adds that, even without this definition of motion, everyone will grant that regarding every motion, it is necessary that what moves be capable of moving, such as being capable of transforming what is transformed, and capable of changing the place of what is transported, <i>so that a thing must be combustible before burning, and capable of igniting before it ignites</i>. (<i>ὥστε δεῖ πρότερον ϰαυστὸν εἶναι πρὶν ϰάεσθαι, ϰαὶ ϰαυστιϰὸν πρὶν ϰάειν</i>). According to Ibn Rushd (loc. cit., f. 155, c. 3), this passage means nothing other than that motion (which, according to Aristotle, is <i>the entelechy of a mobile thing insofar as it is mobile</i>) must exist in potentiality in every mobile thing. But the Arabic commentator informs us that Al-Farabi understood this passage in the sense that every power must <i>temporally</i> precede the act, not only in motion, but generally in everything that occurs: "Dico secundum hanc expositionem intellexit Alpharabius et alii hoc capitulum, scilicet quod induxit definitionem motus ad declarandum potentiam esse ante actum; et hoc non est proprium motui, secundum quod est motus, sed est proprium novo facto secundum quod est novum factum, scilicet ut potentia et posse novi præcedat ipsum <i>secundum tempus</i>." It is evident that Maimonides borrowed the terms of Al-Farabi's explanation, which Ibn Rushd declares erroneous: "Et hoc quod dixit <i>et sine hac definitione</i>, etc., hoc decepit homines in hoc: et existimaverunt ipsum declarare potentiam esse ante actum in tempore, et ipse intendebat dicere quod non dicitur moveri nisi illud in cujus natura est motus, scilicet corpus mobile: et quod non invenitur in immobili."

**French (Source):** Voy. [[t:0]]Phys[[t:1]]., VIII, 1 (p. 251 [[t:2]]a[[t:3]]). Aristote, après avoir rappelé la définition du mouvement donnée plus haut, ajoute que, même sans cette définition du mouvement, chacun accordera qu’à l’égard de chaque mouvement, il faut que ce qui se meut soit capable de se mouvoir, comme par exemple capable de transformer ce qui se transforme, et capable de changer de place ce qui se transporte, [[t:4]]de sorte qu’il faut qu’une chose soit combustible avant de brûler, et capable d’enflammer avant qu’elle enflamme[[t:5]]. ([[t:6]]ὤστε δεῖ πρότερον ϰαυστὸν εἶναι πρὶν ϰάεσθαι, ϰαὶ ϰαυστιϰὸν πρὶν ϰάειν[[t:7]]). Selon Ibn-Roschd (l. c., f. 155, c. 3) ce passage ne veut dire autre chose, si ce n’est que le mouvement (qui, selon Aristote, est [[t:8]]l’entéléchie d’une chose mobile en tant que mobile[[t:9]]) doit exister en puissance dans toute chose mobile. Mais le commentateur arabe nous apprend qu’Al-Farabi entendait ce passage dans ce sens que toute puissance doit [[t:10]]temporellement[[t:11]] précéder l’acte, non-seulement dans le mouvement, mais en général dans tout ce qui survient: «Dico secundum hanc expositionem intellexit Alpharabius et alii hoc capitulum, scilicet quod induxit definitionem motus ad declarandum potentiam esse ante actum; et hoc non est proprium motui, secundum quod est motus, sed est proprium novo facto secundum quod est novum factum, scilicet ut potentia et posse novi præcedat ipsum [[t:12]]secundum tempus[[t:13]].» Il est évident que Maïmonide a emprunté les termes de l’explication d’Al-Farabi, qu’Ibn-Roschd déclare erronés: «Et hoc quod dixit [[t:14]]et sine hac definitione[[t:15]], etc., hoc decepit homines in hoc: et existimaverunt ipsum declarare potentiam esse ante actum in tempore, et ipse intendebat dicere quod non dicitur moveri nisi illud in cujus natura est motus, scilicet corpus mobile: et quod non invenitur in immobili.»
