### fn.1342 (Footnote)
**English:** In this chapter, the author provides various demonstrations of the existence of a unique and immaterial God. His demonstrations are of the type referred to as <i>physical</i> or <i>cosmological</i>, and which lead us from the contingent existence of the world to the conception of a necessary being. His proofs are primarily based on <i>motion;</i> it is demonstrated that, since inert matter cannot move itself, and the causes of motion cannot regress to infinity, it is necessary to acknowledge a <i>first mover</i> that is itself immobile. The argumentation is, in substance, borrowed from Aristotle (<i>Phys.</i>, Book VIII, ch. 5 et seq.; <i>Metaph.</i>, Book XII, ch. 6 and 7); but it has been, on various points, supplemented and modified by Arabic philosophers, and one will recognize, notably in the 3<sup>rd</sup> <i>Speculation</i>, theories specific to Ibn-Sīnā.
**Correction:** In this chapter, the author provides various demonstrations of the existence of a unique and immaterial God. His demonstrations are of the type referred to as <i>physical</i> or <i>cosmological</i>, and which lead us from the contingent existence of the world to the conception of a necessary being. His proofs are primarily based on <i>motion;</i> it is demonstrated that, since inert matter cannot move itself, and the causes of motion cannot regress to infinity, it is necessary to acknowledge a <i>first mover</i> that is itself immobile. The argumentation is, in substance, borrowed from Aristotle (<i>Phys.</i>, Book VIII, ch. 5 et seq.; <i>Metaph.</i>, Book XII, ch. 6 and 7); but it has been, on various points, supplemented and modified by Arabic philosophers, and one will recognize, notably in the 3<sup>rd</sup> <i>Speculation</i>, theories specific to Ibn-Sīnā.

**French (Source):** L’auteur donne, dans ce chapitre, différentes démonstrations de l’existence d’un Dieu unique et immatériel. Ses démonstrations sont de celles qu’on a appelées [[t:0]]physiques[[t:1]] ou [[t:2]]cosmologiques[[t:3]], et qui nous conduisent de l’existence contingente du monde à la conception d’un être nécessaire. Ses preuves sont principalement fondées sur le [[t:4]]mouvement;[[t:5]] on démontre que, la matière inerte ne pouvant se mouvoir elle-même, et les causes du mouvement ne pouvant pas remonter à l’infini, il est nécessaire de reconnaître un [[t:6]]premier moteur[[t:7]] qui soit lui-même immobile. L’argumentation est, en substance, empruntée à Aristote ([[t:8]]Phys.[[t:9]], l. VIII, chap. 5 et suiv.; [[t:10]]Métaph.[[t:11]], l. XII, ch. 6 et 7); mais elle a été, sur divers points, complétée et modifiée par les philosophes arabes, et on reconnaîtra, notamment dans la 3[[t:12]]e[[t:13]] [[t:14]]Spéculation[[t:15]], des théories particulières à Ibn-Sinâ.

---

### fn.1351 (Footnote)
**English:** The author means that the expression <i>outside</i> implies the idea of <i>place</i> and corporeality, and that when speaking of an incorporeal thing—a pure intelligence—one should not say that it is <i>outside</i> the body, but that it is <i>separate</i> from it. The word <span dir="rtl">مفارق</span>, <i>separate</i>, is used by Arabic philosophers to designate purely spiritual substances, separate from any kind of matter, to which the idea of <i>being in potentiality</i>, or any category other than that of <i>substance</i>, does not apply in any respect. They understood in this sense what Aristotle (<i>On the Soul</i>, Book III, Chap. 7) calls <i>τὰ κεχωρισμένα, separate things</i> (<i>from extension</i>)<italic/>, and it is here that one must seek the origin of the Arabic term. See my <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, p. 449, and cf. Vol. I, p. 434.
**Correction:** The author means that the expression <i>outside</i> implies the idea of <i>place</i> and corporeality, and that when speaking of an incorporeal thing—a pure intelligence—one should not say that it is <i>outside</i> the body, but that it is <i>separate</i> from it. The word <span dir="rtl">مفارق</span>, <i>separate</i>, is used by Arabic philosophers to designate purely spiritual substances, separate from any kind of matter, to which the idea of <i>being in potentiality</i>, or any category other than that of <i>substance</i>, does not apply in any respect. They understood in this sense what Aristotle (<i>On the Soul</i>, Book III, Chap. 7) calls <i>τὰ κεχωρισμένα, separate things</i> (<i>from extension</i>)<italic/>, and it is here that one must seek the origin of the Arabic term. See my <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, p. 449, and cf. Vol. I, p. 434.

**French (Source):** L’auteur veut dire que l’expression [[t:0]]en dehors[[t:1]] implique l’idée de [[t:2]]lieu[[t:3]] et de corporéité, et qu’en parlant d’une chose incorporelle, d’une pure intelligence, on ne doit pas dire qu’elle est [[t:4]]en dehors[[t:5]] du corps, mais qu’elle en est [[t:6]]séparée[[t:7]]. Le mot [[t:8]]مفارق[[t:9]] , [[t:10]]séparé[[t:11]], est employé par les philosophes arabes pour désigner les substances purement spirituelles, séparées de toute espèce de matière, et auxquelles ne s’applique, sous aucun rapport, l’idée d’[[t:12]]être en puissance[[t:13]], ni aucune autre catégorie que celle de la [[t:14]]substance[[t:15]]. Ils ont entendu dans ce sens ce qu’Aristote (traité [[t:16]]de l’Ame[[t:17]], liv. III, chap. 7) appelle [[t:18]]τὰ ϰεχωρισμένα, les choses séparées[[t:19]] ([[t:20]]de l’étendue[[t:21]])[[t:22]], et c’est là qu’il faut chercher l’origine du terme arabe. Voy. mes [[t:23]]Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe[[t:24]], pag. 449, et cf. t. I, pag. 434.

### fn.1374.sub_1 (Footnote)
**English:** "This mover, therefore, is immune from potentiality, and exists in no matter." As for the proposition attributed by Maimonides to Aristotle, some commentators have objected that, in what is composed of <i>substance</i> and <i>accident</i>, one cannot imagine the existence of the accident alone, although evidently the substance can exist alone. This objection fails due to the express condition set by Alexander: <i>nisi compositio sit substantiæ et accidentis</i>. Maimonides, in copying Alexander, perhaps omitted this condition through inadvertence.
**Correction:** "This mover, therefore, is immune from potentiality, and exists in no matter." As for the proposition attributed by Maimonides to Aristotle, some commentators have objected that, in what is composed of <i>substance</i> and <i>accident</i>, one cannot imagine the existence of the accident alone, although evidently the substance can exist alone. This objection fails due to the express condition set by Alexander: <i>nisi compositio sit substantiæ et accidentis</i>. Maimonides, in copying Alexander, perhaps omitted this condition through inadvertence.

**French (Source):** Hoc igitur movens immune est a potentia, et in nulla materia existit.» Quant à la proposition attribuée par Maïmonide à Aristote, quelques commentateurs ont objecté que, dans ce qui est composé de [[t:16]]substance[[t:17]] et d’[[t:18]]accident[[t:19]], on ne saurait se figurer l’existence de l’accident seul, quoiqu’évidemment la substance puisse exister seule. Cette objection tombe par la condition expresse posée par Alexandre: [[t:20]]nisi compositio sit substantiœ et accidentis[[t:21]]. Maïmonide, en copiant Alexandre, a peut-être omis cette condition par inadvertance.

### fn.1375 (Footnote)
**English:** The author means that this demonstration does not, strictly speaking, belong to Aristotle, but that it is he who provided its main elements. It is when he wishes to demonstrate the eternity of the world as a whole that Aristotle enters into details on the idea of the perishable and the imperishable, and on that which, in itself, is or is not subject to birth and corruption; see especially the treatise <i>On the Heavens</i>, Book I, chap. X et seq. It will be seen that this third demonstration is based on the theories of Ibn-Sīnā.
**Correction:** The author means that this demonstration does not, strictly speaking, belong to Aristotle, but that it is he who provided its main elements. It is when he wishes to demonstrate the eternity of the world as a whole that Aristotle enters into details on the idea of the perishable and the imperishable, and on that which, in itself, is or is not subject to birth and corruption; see especially the treatise <i>On the Heavens</i>, Book I, chap. X et seq. It will be seen that this third demonstration is based on the theories of Ibn-Sīnā.

**French (Source):** L’auteur veut dire que cette démonstration n’appartient pas, à vrai dire, à Aristote, mais que c’est lui qui en a fourni les principaux éléments. C’est lorsqu’il veut démontrer l’éternité du monde dans son ensemble, qu’Aristote entre dans des détails sur l’idée du périssable et de l’impérissable, et sur ce qui, en lui-même, est ou n’est pas sujet à la naissance et à la corruption; voy. surtout le traité [[t:0]]du Ciel[[t:1]], liv. I, chap. X et suiv. On va voir que cette troisième démonstration est basée sur les théories d’Ibn-Sinâ.

### fn.1398 (Footnote)
**English:** As the commentator Ephodi observes here, there can be no rigorous demonstration for the existence of God based on well-defined premises, since, as the author has stated elsewhere, God has no <i>prior causes</i> and cannot be defined. The proofs alleged for the existence of God are thus among those based on imperfect definitions, where the prior is defined by the posterior. See Vol. I of this work, p. 190, and <i>ibid.</i>, notes 3 and 4. Cf. <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, pp. 192 and 193.
**Correction:** As the commentator Ephodi observes here, there can be no rigorous demonstration for the existence of God based on well-defined premises, since, as the author has stated elsewhere, God has no <i>prior causes</i> and cannot be defined. The proofs alleged for the existence of God are thus among those based on imperfect definitions, where the prior is defined by the posterior. See Vol. I of this work, p. 190, and <i>ibid.</i>, notes 3 and 4. Cf. <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, pp. 192 and 193.

**French (Source):** Ainsi que le fait observer ici le commentateur Ephodi, il ne peut y avoir, pour l’existence de Dieu, de démonstration rigoureuse, basée sur des prémisses bien définies, puisque, comme l’auteur l’a dit ailleurs, Dieu n’a pas de [[t:0]]causes antérieures[[t:1]] et ne saurait être défini. Les preuves qu’on allègue pour l’existence de Dieu sont donc de celles qui sont basées sur des définitions imparfaites, où l’antérieur est défini par le postérieur. Voy. le t. I de cet ouvrage, pag. 190, et [[t:2]]ibid.[[t:3]], notes 3 et 4. Cf. [[t:4]]Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe[[t:5]], pag. 192 et 193.

### fn.1415 (Footnote)
**English:** Cf. the treatise <i>On the Heavens</i>, Book II, chap. 2, where Aristotle calls the heaven a living being, having in itself a principle of movement (<i>ὁ δ’οὐρανὸς ἔμψυχος ϰαί ἔχει ϰινήσεως ἀρχήν</i>). Elsewhere, however, Aristotle seems to attribute the circular motion of the heaven, like the rectilinear motion of the elements, to a nature inherent in the celestial body (<i>Ibid.</i>, Book 1, ch. 2), and at other times, he attributes it to a desire awakened in it by the supreme intelligence, toward which it is attracted (<i>Metaph</i>., XII, 7). Here, as in the theory of the different intellects (see vol. I, p. 304 ff.), the obscurity and vagueness prevailing in Aristotle's theories have given rise to various interpretations. The Arabic philosophers, and notably Ibn Sina, combining the opinions of the Neoplatonic commentators with astronomical theories, formed, regarding the movement of celestial bodies and the order of the spheres and Intelligences, the doctrine developed by Maimonides in this and the following chapters, which has been contested on various points by Ibn Rushd. See <i>Avicennæ opera</i> (Venice, 1495, folio), <i>Metaph</i>., Book IX, chap. <small>II</small>-<small>IV</small>; Scbahrestâni, p. 380 ff. (German trans., vol. II, p. 261 ff.); <i>Al-Nadjâh</i>, or <i>Abridgment of the Philosophy of Ibn Sina</i> (following the <i>Canon</i>), p. 71 ff.; Averroes, <i>Epitome in libros Metaphys. Aristotelis</i>, tract. IV. Cf. Albert the Great, <i>De Causis et processu universitatis</i>, lib. I, tract. IV, cap. 7 and 8, and lib. II, tract. II, cap. 1 (<i>opp. omn</i>., vol. V, p. 559 ff., p. 586 ff.); Saint Thomas Aquinas, <i>De Substantiis separatis</i>, cap. II (opp. vol. XVII, fol. 16 <i>verso</i> ff.)
**Correction:** Cf. the treatise <i>On the Heavens</i>, Book II, chap. 2, where Aristotle calls the heaven a living being, having in itself a principle of movement (<i>ὁ δ’οὐρανὸς ἔμψυχος ϰαί ἔχει ϰινήσεως ἀρχήν</i>). Elsewhere, however, Aristotle seems to attribute the circular motion of the heaven, like the rectilinear motion of the elements, to a nature inherent in the celestial body (<i>Ibid.</i>, Book 1, ch. 2), and at other times, he attributes it to a desire awakened in it by the supreme intelligence, toward which it is attracted (<i>Metaph</i>., XII, 7). Here, as in the theory of the different intellects (see vol. I, p. 304 ff.), the obscurity and vagueness prevailing in Aristotle's theories have given rise to various interpretations. The Arabic philosophers, and notably Ibn Sina, combining the opinions of the Neoplatonic commentators with astronomical theories, formed, regarding the movement of celestial bodies and the order of the spheres and Intelligences, the doctrine developed by Maimonides in this and the following chapters, which has been contested on various points by Ibn Rushd. See <i>Avicennæ opera</i> (Venice, 1495, folio), <i>Metaph</i>., Book IX, chap. <small>II</small>-<small>IV</small>; Scbahrestâni, p. 380 ff. (German trans., vol. II, p. 261 ff.); <i>Al-Nadjâh</i>, or <i>Abridgment of the Philosophy of Ibn Sina</i> (following the <i>Canon</i>), p. 71 ff.; Averroes, <i>Epitome in libros Metaphys. Aristotelis</i>, tract. IV. Cf. Albert the Great, <i>De Causis et processu universitatis</i>, lib. I, tract. IV, cap. 7 and 8, and lib. II, tract. II, cap. 1 (<i>opp. omn</i>., vol. V, p. 559 ff., p. 586 ff.); Saint Thomas Aquinas, <i>De Substantiis separatis</i>, cap. II (opp. vol. XVII, fol. 16 <i>verso</i> ff.)

**French (Source):** Cf. le traité [[t:0]]Du Ciel[[t:1]], l. II, chap. 2, où Aristote appelle le ciel un être animé, ayant en lui-même un principe de mouvement ([[t:2]]ὁ δ’οὑρανὸς ἒμψυχος ϰαί ἒχει ϰινἠσεως ἀρχήν[[t:3]]). Ailleurs, cependant, Aristote paraît attribuer le mouvement circulaire du ciel, comme le mouvement droit des éléments, à une nature inhérente au corps céleste ([[t:4]]Ibid.[[t:5]], liv. 1, ch. 2), et d’autres fois, il l’attribue à un désir que fait naître en lui l’intelligence suprême, vers laquelle il est attiré ([[t:6]]Métaph[[t:7]]., XII, 7). Ici, comme dans la théorie des différents intellects (voir t. I, p. 304 et suiv.), l’obscurité et le vague qui régnent dans les théories d’Aristote ont donné lieu à des interprétations diverses. Les philosophes arabes, et notamment Ibn-Sinâ, combinant ensemble les opinions des commentateurs néoplatoniciens et les théories astronomiques, ont formé, sur le mouvement des corps célestes et sur l’ordre des sphères et des Intelligences, la doctrine développée par Maïmonide dans ce chapitre et dans les suivants, et qui, sur divers points, a été combattue par Ibn-Roschd. Voy. [[t:8]]Avicennœ opera[[t:9]] (Venise, 1495, in-fol.), [[t:10]]Métaph[[t:11]]., liv. IX, chap. [[t:12]]II[[t:13]]-[[t:14]]IV[[t:15]]; Scbahrestâni, p. 380 et suiv. (trad. all., t. II, p. 261 et suiv.); [[t:16]]Al-Nadjâh[[t:17]], ou [[t:18]]Abrégé de la Philosophie d’Ibn-Sinâ[[t:19]] (à la suite du [[t:20]]Canon[[t:21]]), pag. 71 et suiv.; Averroès, [[t:22]]Epitome in libros Metaphys. Aristotelis[[t:23]], tract. IV. Cf. Albert le Grand, [[t:24]]De Causis et processu universitatis[[t:25]], lib. I, tract. IV, cap. 7 et 8, et lib. II, tract. II, cap. 1 ([[t:26]]opp. omn[[t:27]]., t. V, p. 559 et suiv., p. 586 et suiv.); Saint Thomas d’Aquin, [[t:28]]De Substantiis separatis[[t:29]], cap. II (opp. t. XVII, fol. 16 [[t:30]]verso[[t:31]] et suiv.)

### fn.1419 (Footnote)
**English:** The verb <span dir="rtl">تصورُ</span> is derived from <span dir="rtl">صورة</span>, <i>form</i>, and signifies, according to the book <i>Ta’rîfât</i>, <span dir="rtl">حصول صورة الشى فى العقل</span>, <i>to receive in the intellect the form of a thing</i>. The infinitive, which we translate here as <i>conception</i>, could also be rendered as <i>thought</i> or <i>idea</i> (cf. vol. I, p. 116, note 3); in the Arabic versions of Aristotle, the words <i>τὸ νοεῖν, νόησις</i> and <i>νόημα</i> are often rendered by <span dir="rtl">تصوّر بالعقل</span>, or simply by <span dir="rtl">تصوُّر</span>. See, for example, the treatise <i>On the Soul</i>, Book I, chap. 1 (§ 9): <i>Μάλιστα δ’ ἔοιϰεν ἰδίῳ τὸ νοεῖν</i>; in Arabic: <span dir="rtl">والذى يشبه ان يكون يخصها هو التصور بالعقل</span> (Hebrew MS of the Imperial Library, old collection, no. 317, fol. 104 <i>recto</i>, col. <i>b</i>). <i>Ibid.</i>, chap. 4 (§ 14): <i>ϰαί τὸ νοεῖν δή</i>; in Arabic: <span dir="rtl">والتصور بالعقل</span> (same MS, fol. 113, col. d). <i>Metaph</i>., Book XII, chap. 7: <i>ἀρχὴ δὲ ἡ νόησις</i>; Hebrew version: <span dir="rtl">וההתחלה הוא הציור בשבל</span>; Latin-Arabic version (folio, fol. 150, col. a): «Principium autem est <i>imaginatio per intellectum</i>.» The word <span dir="rtl">تصور</span> also indicates the simple <i>notion</i> (<i>νόημα</i>), in which there is neither truth nor falsehood, and in this sense it is opposed to <span dir="rtl">تصديق</span> (affirmation), indicating the combination of thoughts (<i>σύνθεσις νοημάτων</i>), in which there is error and truth. See Arist., <i>Categories</i>, chap. 2 beginning, and chap. 4 (II) end; treatise <i>On Interpretation</i>, chap. <small>I</small>; treatise <i>On the Soul</i>, Book III, chap. 6 (§ 1); and <i>ibid.</i> the commentary of Averroes (folio edition, fol. 171, col. <i>c</i>).
**Correction:** The verb <span dir="rtl">تصورُ</span> is derived from <span dir="rtl">صورة</span>, <i>form</i>, and signifies, according to the book <i>Ta’rîfât</i>, <span dir="rtl">حصول صورة الشى فى العقل</span>, <i>to receive in the intellect the form of a thing</i>. The infinitive, which we translate here as <i>conception</i>, could also be rendered as <i>thought</i> or <i>idea</i> (cf. vol. I, p. 116, note 3); in the Arabic versions of Aristotle, the words <i>τὸ νοεῖν, νόησις</i> and <i>νόημα</i> are often rendered by <span dir="rtl">تصوّر بالعقل</span>, or simply by <span dir="rtl">تصوُّر</span>. See, for example, the treatise <i>On the Soul</i>, Book I, chap. 1 (§ 9): <i>Μάλιστα δ’ ἔοιϰεν ἰδίῳ τὸ νοεῖν</i>; in Arabic: <span dir="rtl">والذى يشبه ان يكون يخصها هو التصور بالعقل</span> (Hebrew MS of the Imperial Library, old collection, no. 317, fol. 104 <i>recto</i>, col. <i>b</i>). <i>Ibid.</i>, chap. 4 (§ 14): <i>ϰαί τὸ νοεῖν δή</i>; in Arabic: <span dir="rtl">والتصور بالعقل</span> (same MS, fol. 113, col. d). <i>Metaph</i>., Book XII, chap. 7: <i>ἀρχὴ δὲ ἡ νόησις</i>; Hebrew version: <span dir="rtl">וההתחלה הוא הציור בשבל</span>; Latin-Arabic version (folio, fol. 150, col. a): «Principium autem est <i>imaginatio per intellectum</i>.» The word <span dir="rtl">تصور</span> also indicates the simple <i>notion</i> (<i>νόημα</i>), in which there is neither truth nor falsehood, and in this sense it is opposed to <span dir="rtl">تصديق</span> (affirmation), indicating the combination of thoughts (<i>σύνθεσις νοημάτων</i>), in which there is error and truth. **French (Source):** Le verbe [[t:0]]تصورُ[[t:1]]  est dérivé de [[t:2]]صورة[[t:3]] , [[t:4]]forme[[t:5]], et signifie, selon le livre [[t:6]]Ta’rîfât[[t:7]], [[t:8]]حصول صورة الشى فى العقل[[t:9]] , [[t:10]]recevoir dans l’intellect la forme d’une chose[[t:11]]. ### fn.1426 (Footnote)
**English:** This is not entirely accurate, and the author merely intended to give a round number. Aristotle says that, according to one system, fifty-five spheres must be admitted, and according to another, forty-seven. See <i>Metaph</i>., XII, 8. Ibn Sina, whom our author probably followed, expresses himself more precisely by saying that, according to Aristotle, if the Intelligences are in proportion to the spheres, there will be <i>about</i> fifty: "et sequetur secundum sententiam magistri primi quod sunt <i>circiter quin-quaginta et amplius</i>." See <i>Avicennæ opera</i>, <i>Metaphysica</i>, bk. IX, ch. 3 (fol. 104 <i>recto</i>, col. <i>b</i>.)
**Correction:** This is not entirely accurate, and the author merely intended to give a round number. Aristotle says that, according to one system, fifty-five spheres must be admitted, and according to another, forty-seven. See <i>Metaph</i>., XII, 8. Ibn Sina, whom our author probably followed, expresses himself more precisely by saying that, according to Aristotle, if the Intelligences are in proportion to the spheres, there will be <i>about</i> fifty: "et sequetur secundum sententiam magistri primi quod sunt <i>circiter quin-quaginta et amplius</i>." See <i>Avicennæ opera</i>, <i>Metaphysica</i>, bk. IX, ch. 3 (fol. 104 <i>recto</i>, col. <i>b</i>.)

**French (Source):** Ceci n’est pas entièrement exact, et l’auteur a seulement voulu donner un nombre rond. Aristote dit que, selon un système, on doit admettre cinquante-cinq sphères, et selon un autre, quarante-sept. Voy. [[t:0]]Métaph[[t:1]]., XII, 8. Ibn-Sinâ, que notre auteur a probablement suivi, s’exprime plus exactement en disant que, selon Aristote, si les Intelligences sont en raison des sphères, il y en aura [[t:2]]environ[[t:3]] cinquante: «et sequetur secundum sententiam magistri primi quod sunt [[t:4]]circiter quin-quaginta et amplius[[t:5]].» Voy. [[t:6]]Avicennœ opera[[t:7]], [[t:8]]Metaphysica[[t:9]], liv. IX, chap. 3 (fol. 104 [[t:10]]recto[[t:11]], col. [[t:12]]b[[t:13]].)

### fn.1429.sub_0 (Footnote)
**English:** As we have said, the author, in this enumeration of the Intelligences, followed Ibn Sina, who, besides the supreme Intelligence, or God, admits ten Intelligences, the first of which, emanating directly from God (cf. below, ch. <small>XXII</small>), is that of the sphere of diurnal motion, which surrounds the entire universe, and the last of which, emanating from the Intelligence of the lunar sphere, is the <i>active intellect</i>. See <i>Avicennæ opera</i>, <i>Metaph</i>., IX, <i>loc. cit.</i>: «Si autem circuli planetarum fuerint sic quod principium motus circulorum uniuscujus-que planetarum sit virtus fluens a planeta, tunc non erit longe quin <i>separata</i> sint secundum numerum planetarum, non secundum numerum circulorum; et tune eorum numerus est decem post primum (that is to say, God). Primum autem eorum est Intelligentia quæ non movetur, cujus est movere sphæram corporis ultimi (that is to say, the <i>diurnal</i> sphere). Deinde id quod sequitur est quod movet sphæram fixarum. Deinde sequitur quod movet sphæram Saturni. Simililer est quousque pervenitur ad Intelligentiam a qua fluit super nostras animas; et hæc est Intelligentia mundi terreni, et vocamus eam <i>Intelligentiam agentem</i>.» Ibn Rushd, identifying the sphere of diurnal motion with that of the fixed stars, admits only eight spheres. See <i>Epitome in libros metaph</i>., tract. <small>IV</small> (fol. 182, col. <i>a</i>): «Tandem apud me est remotum quod inveniatur orbis nonus sine stellis; nam orbis est stellarum gratia, quæ sunt nobilior pars ejus…. Orbis autem qui movet motum magnum est nobilior cæteris or-bibus; quapropter non videtur nobis quod sit sine stellis, immo apud me est impossibile.» According to this, there would be in total only <i>nine separate Intelligences</i>, the last of which, as in Ibn Sina's theory, is the <i>active intellect</i>. This, therefore, according to the theory of the Arabs, is in a close relationship with the Intelligence of the lunar sphere, from which it emanates directly, but with which it must not be identified. — [In my <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, at the beginning of page 332 (cf. also pp. 165 and 448, and Vol. I of this work, p.

Simililer est quousque pervenitur ad Intelligentiam a qua fluit super nostras animas; et hæc est Intelligentia mundi terreni, et vocamus eam <i>Intelligentiam agentem</i>.» Ibn Rushd, identifying the sphere of diurnal motion with that of the fixed stars, admits only eight spheres. See <i>Epitome in libros metaph</i>., tract. <small>IV</small> (fol. 182, col. <i>a</i>): «Tandem apud me est remotum quod inveniatur orbis nonus sine stellis; nam orbis est stellarum gratia, quæ sunt nobilior pars ejus…. **French (Source):** Ainsi que nous l’avons dit, l’auteur, dans cette énumération des Intelligences, a suivi Ibn-Sinâ, qui, outre l’Intelligence suprême, ou Dieu, admet dix Intelligences, dont la première, qui émàne directement de Dieu (Cf. ci-après, chap. [[t:0]]XXII[[t:1]]), est celle de la sphère du mouvement diurne, qui environne tout l’univers, et dont la dernière, émanée de l’Intelligence de la sphère lunaire, est l’[[t:2]]intellect actif[[t:3]]. [[t:4]]Avicennœ opera[[t:5]], [[t:6]]Metaph[[t:7]]., IX, [[t:8]]l: c.[[t:9]]: «Si autem circuli planetarum fuerint sic quod principium motus circulorum uniuscujus-que planetarum sit virtus fluens a planeta, tunc non erit longe quin [[t:10]]separata[[t:11]] sint secundum numerum planetarum, non secundum numerum circulorum; et tune eorum numerus est decem post primum (c’est-à-dire Dieu). Primum autem eorum est Intelligentia quæ non movetur, cujus est movere sphæram corporis ultimi (c’est-à-dire la sphère [[t:12]]diurne[[t:13]]). Deinde id quod sequitur est quod movet sphæram fixarum. Deinde sequitur quod movet sphæram Saturni. Similiter est quousque pervenitur ad Intelligentiam a qua fluit super nostras animas; et hæc est Intelligentia mundi terreni, et vocamus eam [[t:14]]Intelligentiam agentem[[t:15]].» Ibn-Roschd, identifiant la sphère du mouvement diurne avec celle des étoiles fixes, n’admet que huit sphères. Voy. [[t:16]]Epitome in libros metaph[[t:17]]., tract. [[t:18]]IV[[t:19]] (fol. 182, col. [[t:20]]a[[t:21]]): «Tandem apud me est remotum quod inveniatur orbis nonus sine stellis; nam orbis est stellarum gratia, quæ sunt nobilior pars ejus…. Orbis autem qui movet motum magnum est nobilior cæteris or-bibus; quapropter non videtur nobis quod sit sine stellis, immo apud me est impossibile.» D’après cela, il n’y aurait en tout que [[t:22]]neuf Intelligences séparées[[t:23]], dont la dernière, comme dans la théorie d’Ibn-Sinâ, est l’[[t:24]]intellect actif[[t:25]]. Celui-ci donc, selon la théorie des Arabes, est dans un rapport intime avec l’Intelligence de la sphère lunaire, dont il émane directement, mais avec laquelle il ne faut pas l’identifier. — [Dans mes [[t:26]]Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe[[t:27]], au commencement de la page 332 (Cf. aussi pag. 165 et 448, et le t. ### fn.1429.sub_1 (Footnote)
**English:** 277, and note 3), I expressed myself on this matter in an inaccurate manner, and I should have said: «From the last of these <i>separate Intelligences</i>, which presides over the movement of the sphere closest to us (that of the moon), the active intellect <i>emanates</i>, etc.»] — The passage from Ibn Rushd (<i>loc. cit</i>., fol. 184, col. <i>d</i>), which has often been cited to show that, according to this author, the active intellect is itself the mover of the lunar sphere (see Ritter, <i>Geschichte der philosophie</i>, Vol. VIII, p. 148), is thus phrased in the Hebrew version: <span dir="rtl">והשכל הפועל הוא יםודר מםוף אלו המניעים במדרגה ונניחו מניע גלגל הירח</span>. The word <span dir="rtl">מםוף</span> seems to me to be an inaccurate translation of the Arabic words <span dir="rtl">من آخر</span> , and it should be translated as follows: «The active intellect emanates from the last of these movers in rank (i.e., from that one of these movers which by its rank is the last), which we assume to be the mover of the sphere of the moon.» To leave no doubt in this regard, I shall cite here a passage from Moses of Narbonne's commentary on Al-Ghazali's <i>Maqāṣid</i>, towards the end of the II<sup>nd</sup> part, or of the <i>Metaph</i>., where the theory of the spheres and their intelligences is set forth (according to Ibn Sina) (see Hebrew MS. of the Oratory collection, no. 93, fol. 179 <i>a</i>): <span dir="rtl">ואמנם בן רשד ובל הפילוםופים מודים שהשבל הפועל בלתי מניע לגלגל רק שהוא עשירי לשבלים הנפרדים לפי בן רשד והשבל הראשון בבלל והוא עשירי לשבלים העלולים מבלתי מנוה במחוייב המציאות לפי אבוחמד</span>. «Ibn Rushd and all the philosophers agree that the <i>active intellect</i> is not the mover of any celestial sphere. But, according to Ibn Rushd, it is the tenth of the separate Intelligences, if the first Intelligence (or God) is also included; whereas, according to Abu Hamid (al-Ghazali), it is the tenth of the <i>caused</i> Intelligences, without counting the <i>Necessary Being</i> (i.e., the first absolute cause, or God).»—Cf. Albert the Great, <i>De Causis et processu universitatis</i>, bk. I, tract <small>IV</small>, ch. 8 (Opp. Vol. V, p.

But, according to Ibn Rushd, it is the tenth of the separate Intelligences, if the first Intelligence (or God) is also included; whereas, according to Abu Hamid (al-Ghazali), it is the tenth of the <i>caused</i> Intelligences, without counting the <i>Necessary Being</i> (i.e., the first absolute cause, or God).»—Cf. Albert the Great, <i>De Causis et processu universitatis</i>, bk. I, tract <small>IV</small>, ch. 8 (Opp. Vol. V, p.

**French (Source):** 277, et note 3), je me suis exprimé à cet égard d’une manière inexacte, et j’aurais dû dire: «De la dernière de ces [[t:28]]Intelligences séparées[[t:29]], qui préside au mouvement de la sphère la plus rapprochée de nous (celle de la lune), [[t:30]]émane[[t:31]] l’intellect actif, etc.»] — Le passage d’Ibn-Roschd ([[t:32]]l. c[[t:33]]., fol. 184, col. [[t:34]]d[[t:35]]), qu’on a souvent cité pour montrer que, selon cet auteur, l’intellect actif est lui-même le moteur de la sphère lunaire (voy. Ritter, [[t:36]]Geschichte der philosophie[[t:37]], t. VIII, p. 148), est ainsi conçu dans la version hébraïque: [[t:38]]והשכל הפועל הוא יםודר מםוף אלו המניעים במדרגה ונניחו מניע גלגל הירח[[t:39]]. Le mot [[t:40]]מםוף[[t:41]] me paraît être une traduction inexacte des mots arabes [[t:42]]من آخر[[t:43]] , et il faut traduire ainsi: «L’intellect actif émane du dernier de ces moteurs en rang (c.-à-d. de celui de ces moteurs qui par son rang est le dernier), que nous supposons être le moteur de la sphère de la lune.» Pour ne laisser aucun doute à cet égard, je citerai ici un passage du commentaire de Moïse de Narbonne sur le [[t:44]]Makâcid[[t:45]] d’Al-Gazâli, vers la fin de la II[[t:46]]e[[t:47]] partie, ou de la [[t:48]]Métaph[[t:49]]., où l’on expose (d’après Ibn-Sîna) la théorie des sphères et de leurs intelligences (voy. ms. hébr. du fonds de l’Oratoire, n° 93, fol. 179 [[t:50]]a[[t:51]]): [[t:52]]ואמנם בן רשד ובל הפילוםופים מודים שהשבל הפועל בלתי מניע לגלגל רק שהוא עשירי לשבלים הנפרדים לפי בן רשד והשבל הראשון בבלל והוא עשירי לשבלים העלולים מבלתי מנוה במחוייב המציאות לפי אבוחמד[[t:53]]. «Ibn-Roschd et tous les philosophes conviennent que l’[[t:54]]intellect actif[[t:55]] n’est le moteur d’aucune sphère céleste. Mais, selon Ibn-Roschd, il est la dixième des Intelligences séparées, si l’on y comprend aussi l’Intelligence première (ou Dieu); tandis que, selon Abou-’Hamed (al-Gazâli), il est la dixième des Intelligences [[t:56]]causées[[t:57]], sans compter l’[[t:58]]Être nécessaire[[t:59]] (c.-à-d. la première cause absolue, ou Dieu).»—Cf. Albert le Grand, [[t:60]]De Causis et processu universitatis[[t:61]], liv. I, tract, [[t:62]]IV[[t:63]], cap. 8 (opp. t. V, p.

### fn.1460 (Footnote)
**English:** Albert the Great, who opposes this identification of angels with the separate Intelligences, says that this is a theory which belongs particularly to Isaac Israeli, Maimonides, and other Jewish philosophers: «Ordines autem intelligentiarum quos non determinavimus quidam dicunt esse ordines angelorum, et intelligentias vocant angelos; et hoc quidem dicunt Isaac et Rabbi Moyses et cæteri philosophi Judæorum. Sed nos hoc verum esse non credimus. Ordines enim angelorum distinguuntur secundum differentias illuminationum et theophaniarum, quæ revelatione accipiuntur et fide creduntur, et ad perfectionem regni cœlestis ordinantur in gratia et beatitudine. De quibus philosophia nihil potest per rationem philosophicam determinare.» See <i>De causis et processu universitatis</i>, Bk. I, tract. <small>IV</small>, ch. 8 (opp. Vol. V, p. 563 <i>a</i>). It has been seen, however, that the Arabic philosophers hold the same opinion as Maimonides regarding angels. Cf. above, p. 60, note 3.
**Correction:** Albert the Great, who opposes this identification of angels with the separate Intelligences, says that this is a theory which belongs particularly to Isaac Israeli, Maimonides, and other Jewish philosophers: «Ordines autem intelligentiarum quos non determinavimus quidam dicunt esse ordines angelorum, et intelligentias vocant angelos; et hoc quidem dicunt Isaac et Rabbi Moyses et cæteri philosophi Judæorum. Sed nos hoc verum esse non credimus. Ordines enim angelorum distinguuntur secundum differentias illuminationum et theophaniarum, quæ revelatione accipiuntur et fide creduntur, et ad perfectionem regni cœlestis ordinantur in gratia et beatitudine. De quibus philosophia nihil potest per rationem philosophicam determinare.» See <i>De causis et processu universitatis</i>, Bk. I, tract. <small>IV</small>, ch. 8 (opp. Vol. V, p. 563 <i>a</i>). It has been seen, however, that the Arabic philosophers hold the same opinion as Maimonides regarding angels. Cf. above, p. 60, note 3.

**French (Source):** Albert le grand, qui combat cette identification des anges avec les Intelligences séparées, dit que c’est là une théorie qui appartient particulièrement à Isaac Israeli, à Maïmonide et à d’autres philosophes juifs: «Ordines autem intelligentiarum quos non determinavimus quidam dicunt esse ordines angelorum, et intelligentias vocant angelos; et hoc quidem dicunt Isaac et Rabbi Moyses et cæteri philosophi Judæorum. Sed nos hoc verum esse non credimus. Ordines enim angelorum distinguuntur secundum differentias illuminationum et theophaniarum, quæ revelatione accipiuntur et fide creduntur, et ad perfectionem regni cœlestis ordinantur in gratia et beatitudine. De quibus philosophia nihil potest per rationem philosophicam determinare.» Voy. [[t:0]]De causis et processu universitatis[[t:1]], liv. I, tract. [[t:2]]IV[[t:3]], cap. 8 (opp. t. V, pag. 563 [[t:4]]a[[t:5]]). On a vu cependant que les philosophes arabes professent sur les anges la même opinion que Maïmonide. Cf. ci-dessus, pag. 60, note 3.

### fn.1513 (Footnote)
**English:** The author refers to the <span dir="rtl">كتاب الهيئة</span> or <i>book of astronomy</i> by Abu Muhammad Jabir ibn Aflah, an author who flourished in Spain at the beginning of the 12th <sup>th</sup> century, and who is often cited by the scholastics under the name of <i>Geber</i>. Ibn Aflah's work is an abridgment of the Almagest; but the author, on several important points, departs from Ptolemy, and notably contests the latter's opinion regarding the position occupied by the planets Venus and Mercury. Cf. my <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, pp. 519-520. The Arabic original of Ibn Aflah's work exists in the library of the Escorial, and the Hebrew version in the Imperial Library of Paris. See my <i>Notice sur Joseph-ben-Iehouda</i> in the <i>Journal Asiatique</i>, July 1842, p. 15, note 3. A Latin version by Gerard of Cremona has been published: <i>Gebri filii Afla hispalensis de Astronomia libri IX</i>, etc. <i>Norimbergae</i>, 1533. I have provided a French extract concerning one of the inequalities of the moon in the Comptes rendus des séances de l’Académie des sciences, vol. XVII, pp. 76 et seq. See also, on this work, Delambre, <i>Histoire de l’Astronomie du moyen âge</i>, p. 179 et seq., and on the question of the planets Venus and Mercury, <i>ibid.</i>, p. 184. Delambre states in conclusion (p. 185) that it is not possible to decide during what period Geber lived; but the epoch can be specified by our very passage; for we know that Maimonides, who says he was associated in his youth with the son of Ibn Aflah or Geber, was born in 1135. Ibn Rushd or Averroes, born in 520 of the Hegira (1126), when speaking in his <i>Abrégé de l’Almageste</i> of this same question regarding the planets Venus and Mercury, expressly says that Ibn Aflah lived in the same century. The Hebrew version reads: <span dir="rtl">והאיש שהיה בדורנו והוא בן אפלח אלאשבילי וכו׳</span> (Cf. <i>Moré ha-Moré</i>, p. 89).
**Correction:** The author refers to the <span dir="rtl">كتاب الهيئة</span> or <i>book of astronomy</i> by Abu Muhammad Jabir ibn Aflah, an author who flourished in Spain at the beginning of the 12th <sup>th</sup> century, and who is often cited by the scholastics under the name of <i>Geber</i>. Ibn Aflah's work is an abridgment of the Almagest; but the author, on several important points, departs from Ptolemy, and notably contests the latter's opinion regarding the position occupied by the planets Venus and Mercury. Cf. my <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, pp. 519-520. The Arabic original of Ibn Aflah's work exists in the library of the Escorial, and the Hebrew version in the Imperial Library of Paris. See my <i>Notice sur Joseph-ben-Iehouda</i> in the <i>Journal Asiatique</i>, July 1842, p. 15, note 3. A Latin version by Gerard of Cremona has been published: <i>Gebri filii Afla hispalensis de Astronomia libri IX</i>, etc. <i>Norimbergae</i>, 1533. I have provided a French extract concerning one of the inequalities of the moon in the Comptes rendus des séances de l’Académie des sciences, vol. XVII, pp. 76 et seq. See also, on this work, Delambre, <i>Histoire de l’Astronomie du moyen âge</i>, p. 179 et seq., and on the question of the planets Venus and Mercury, <i>ibid.</i>, p. 184. Delambre states in conclusion (p. 185) that it is not possible to decide during what period Geber lived; but the epoch can be specified by our very passage; for we know that Maimonides, who says he was associated in his youth with the son of Ibn Aflah or Geber, was born in 1135. Ibn Rushd or Averroes, born in 520 of the Hegira (1126), when speaking in his <i>Abrégé de l’Almageste</i> of this same question regarding the planets Venus and Mercury, expressly says that Ibn Aflah lived in the same century. The Hebrew version reads: <span dir="rtl">והאיש שהיה בדורנו והוא בן אפלח אלאשבילי וכו׳</span> (Cf. <i>Moré ha-Moré</i>, p. 89).

**French (Source):** L’auteur veut parler du [[t:0]]كتاب الهيئة[[t:1]]  ou [[t:2]]livre d’astronomie[[t:3]], d’Abou-Mo’hammed Djâber ibn-Afla’h, auteur qui florissait en Espagne au commencement du XII[[t:4]]e[[t:5]] siècle, et qui est souvent cité par les scolastiques sous le nom de [[t:6]]Géber[[t:7]]. L’ouvrage d’Ibn-Afla’h est un abrégé de l’Almageste; mais l’auteur, sur plusieurs points importants, s’écarte de Ptolémée, et il combat notamment l’opinion de ce dernier à l’égard de la place qu’occupent les planètes de Vénus et de Mercure. Cf. mes [[t:8]]Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe[[t:9]], pag. 519-520. L’original arabe de l’ouvrage d’Ibn-Afla’h existe dans la bibliothèque de l’Escurial, et la version hébraïque dans la bibliothèque impériale de Paris. Voy. ma [[t:10]]Notice sur Joseph-ben-Iehouda[[t:11]], dans le [[t:12]]Journal Asiatique[[t:13]], juillet 1842, pag. 15, note 3. Il en a été publié une version latine due à Gerard de Crémone: [[t:14]]Gebri filii Afla hispalensis de Astronomia libri IX[[t:15]], etc. [[t:16]]Norimbergæ[[t:17]], 1533. J’en ai donné en français un extrait relatif à l’une des inégalités de la lune, dans les Comptes rendus des séances de l’Académie des sciences, t. XVII, pag. 76 et suiv. Voy. aussi, sur cet ouvrage, Delambre, [[t:18]]Histoire de l’Astronomie du moyen âge[[t:19]], p. 179 et suiv., et sur la question des planètes de Vénus et de Mercure, [[t:20]]ibid.[[t:21]], p. 184. Delambre dit en terminant (p. 185) qu’il n’est pas possible de décider en quel temps Géber a vécu; mais l’époque peut se préciser par notre passage même; car nous savons que Maïmonide, qui dit avoir été lié dans sa jeunesse avec le fils d’Ibn-Afla’h ou Géber, était né en 1135. Ibn-Roschd ou Averroès, né en 520 de l’hégire (1126), en parlant, dans son [[t:22]]Abrégé de l’Almageste[[t:23]], de cette même question relative aux planètes de Vénus et de Mercure, dit expressèment qu’Ibn-Afla’h avait vécu au même siècle. La version hébraïque porte: [[t:24]]והאיש שהיה בדורנו והוא בן אפלח אלאשבילי וכו׳[[t:25]] (Cf. [[t:26]]Moré ha-Moré[[t:27]], pag. 89).

### fn.1592 (Footnote)
**English:** Literally: <i>that any being whatever having matter and form should be born from the absolute non-being of this matter, nor that it should perish into the absolute non-being of this matter.</i> All manuscripts have on both occasions <span dir="rtl">תלך אלמאדה̈</span>, <i>this matter;</i> likewise, the versions of Ibn Tibbon and Al-Harizi: <span dir="rtl">החמר ההוא</span>. In Ibn Falaquéra's <i>Moré ha-Moré</i>, both in the printed edition (p. 95) and in the manuscripts, one reads <span dir="rtl">אותה הצורה</span>, <i>this form;</i> but this reading is inadmissible.
**Correction:** Literally: <i>that any being whatever having matter and form should be born from the absolute non-being of this matter, nor that it should perish into the absolute non-being of this matter.</i> All manuscripts have on both occasions <span dir="rtl">תלך אלמאדה̈</span>, <i>this matter;</i> likewise, the versions of Ibn Tibbon and Al-Harizi: <span dir="rtl">החמר ההוא</span>. In Ibn Falaquéra's <i>Moré ha-Moré</i>, both in the printed edition (p. 95) and in the manuscripts, one reads <span dir="rtl">אותה הצורה</span>, <i>this form;</i> but this reading is inadmissible.

**French (Source):** Littéralement: [[t:0]]qu’il naisse un être quelconque ayant matière et forme du non-être absolu de cette matière, ni qu’il périsse en le non-être absolu de cette matière.[[t:1]] Tous les mss. ont les deux fois [[t:2]]תלך אלמאדה̈[[t:3]], [[t:4]]cette matière;[[t:5]] de même, les versions d’Ibn-Tibbon et d’Al-’Harîzi: [[t:6]]החמר ההוא[[t:7]]. Dans le [[t:8]]Moré ha-Moré[[t:9]] d’Ibn-Falaquéra, tant dans l’édition imprimée (pag. 95) que dans les mss., on lit [[t:10]]אותה הצורה[[t:11]], [[t:12]]cette forme;[[t:13]] mais cette leçon est inadmissible.

### fn.1598.sub_0 (Footnote)
**English:** It can be seen that, according to Maimonides, the difference between Plato and Aristotle is this: that the latter admits not only the eternity of prime matter, but also that of motion and time; whereas Plato, while admitting the eternity of matter and chaos, nevertheless believes that the world, as it is, had a beginning, that the heavens were, like sublunary things, produced from chaos, and that consequently motion and time had a beginning. It is in this sense that Plato's opinion was generally interpreted by the Arabs and the Scholastics, and it is in this sense also that one of the most learned adherents of the new Platonic school of Italy expresses himself. This is how Leone Ebreo speaks on this question (<i>Dialoghi di amore</i>, III, Venice edition, 1572, fol. 145 et seq.): «Concedendo tutti gli huomini che’l sommo Dio genitore et opifice del mondo sia eterno, senza alcun principio temporale, son divisi nella produttion del mondo, se è ab eterno, o da qualche tempo in quà.
**Correction:** It can be seen that, according to Maimonides, the difference between Plato and Aristotle is this: that the latter admits not only the eternity of prime matter, but also that of motion and time; whereas Plato, while admitting the eternity of matter and chaos, nevertheless believes that the world, as it is, had a beginning, that the heavens were, like sublunary things, produced from chaos, and that consequently motion and time had a beginning. It is in this sense that Plato's opinion was generally interpreted by the Arabs and the Scholastics, and it is in this sense also that one of the most learned adherents of the new Platonic school of Italy expresses himself. This is how Leone Ebreo speaks on this question (<i>Dialoghi di amore</i>, III, Venice edition, 1572, fol. 145 et seq.): «Concedendo tutti gli huomini che’l sommo Dio genitore et opifice del mondo sia eterno, senza alcun principio temporale, son divisi nella produttion del mondo, se è ab eterno, o da qualche tempo in quà.

**French (Source):** On voit que, selon Maïmonide, la différence entre Platon et Aristote est celle-ci: que ce dernier admet, non-seulement l’éternité de la matière première, mais aussi celle du mouvement et du temps, tandis que Platon, tout en admettant l’éternité de la matière et du chaos, croit pourtant que le monde, tel qu’il est, a eu un commencement, que le ciel a été, comme les choses sublunaires, produit du chaos, et que par conséquent le mouvement et le temps ont eu un commencement. C’est dans ce sens que l’opinion de Platon a été généralement interprétée par les Arabes et par les scolastiques, et c’est dans ce sens encore que se prononce l’un des plus savants adeptes de la nouvelle école platonique d’Italie. Voici comment Léon Hébreu s’exprime sur cette question ([[t:0]]Dialoghi di amore[[t:1]], III, édit. de Venise, 1572, fol. 145 et suiv.): «Concedendo tutti gli huomini che’l sommo Dio genitore et opifice del mondo sia eterno, senza alcun principio temporale, son divisi nella produttion del mondo, se è ab eterno, o da qualche tempo in quà.

### fn.1598.sub_1 (Footnote)
**English:** Molti dei filosofi tengono essere prodotto ab eterno da Dio, e non havere mai havuto principio temporale, così come esso Dio non l’ha mai havuto, et di questa opinione è il grande Aristolile, et tutti i peripatetici… Ma gli fideli, et tutti quelli che credono la sacra legge di Moise, tengono che’l mondo fusse non ab eterno produtto, anzi di nulla creato in principio temporale, et ancora alcuni dei filosofi par que sentino questo; per quali è il divino Platone, che nel Timeo pone il mondo essere fatto et genito da Dio, produtto del chaos, che è la materia confusa, del quale le cose sono generate… È ben vero che lui fa il chaos, di che le cose sono fatte, eterno, cioè eternalmente produtto da Dio, laquai cosa non tengono gli fideli; perche loro tengono che fino all’ hora della creatione solo Dio fusse in essere senza mondo, et senza chaos, et che l’omnipotentia di Dio di nulla tutte le cose in principio di tempo habbia produtto, che in effetto non par già chiaramente in Moise, che’l ponga materia coeterna a Dio.— Sono adunque tre opinioni nella produttione del mondo da Dio: la prima d’Aristotile, che tutto il mondo fue produtto ab eterno; la seconda di Platone, che solamente la materia, o chaos, fue produtto ab eterno, ma il mondo in principio di tempo; et la terza delli fideli, che tutto sia produtto di nulla in principio di tempo.» — But one recognizes from our passage that in the time of Maimonides, as in all eras, opinions were divided on the true sense of Plato's doctrine; and, indeed, Plato's poetic language and the mythical veil under which he often presents his doctrines only too well justify this divergence of opinions. While several of the oldest Platonists, and later the Neoplatonists, claimed that Plato had admitted, like Aristotle, the eternity of the world, others on the contrary (notably some Christians, such as Clement of Alexandria) went so far as to maintain that Plato had professed the doctrine of creation <i>ex nihilo</i>, considering God to be the author not only of the order of the world, but of matter itself. The same divergence is still observed among the scholars of our day. On this subject, see Zeller, <i>Die Philosophie der Griechen</i>, vol. II, p. 508 et seq. (2<sup>nd</sup> ed. Tübingen, 1859), and Henri Martin, <i>Études sur le Timée de Platon</i>, vol. II, p. 179 et seq.
**Correction:** Molti dei filosofi tengono essere prodotto ab eterno da Dio, e non havere mai havuto principio temporale, così come esso Dio non l’ha mai havuto, et di questa opinione è il grande Aristolile, et tutti i peripatetici… Ma gli fideli, et tutti quelli che credono la sacra legge di Moise, tengono che’l mondo fusse non ab eterno produtto, anzi di nulla creato in principio temporale, et ancora alcuni dei filosofi par que sentino questo; per quali è il divino Platone, che nel Timeo pone il mondo essere fatto et genito da Dio, produtto del chaos, che è la materia confusa, del quale le cose sono generate… È ben vero che lui fa il chaos, di che le cose sono fatte, eterno, cioè eternalmente produtto da Dio, laquai cosa non tengono gli fideli; perche loro tengono che fino all’ hora della creatione solo Dio fusse in essere senza mondo, et senza chaos, et che l’omnipotentia di Dio di nulla tutte le cose in principio di tempo habbia produtto, che in effetto non par già chiaramente in Moise, che’l ponga materia coeterna a Dio.— Sono adunque tre opinioni nella produttione del mondo da Dio: la prima d’Aristotile, che tutto il mondo fue produtto ab eterno; la seconda di Platone, che solamente la materia, o chaos, fue produtto ab eterno, ma il mondo in principio di tempo; et la terza delli fideli, che tutto sia produtto di nulla in principio di tempo.» — But one recognizes from our passage that in the time of Maimonides, as in all eras, opinions were divided on the true sense of Plato's doctrine; and, indeed, Plato's poetic language and the mythical veil under which he often presents his doctrines only too well justify this divergence of opinions. While several of the oldest Platonists, and later the Neoplatonists, claimed that Plato had admitted, like Aristotle, the eternity of the world, others on the contrary (notably some Christians, such as Clement of Alexandria) went so far as to maintain that Plato had professed the doctrine of creation <i>ex nihilo</i>, considering God to be the author not only of the order of the world, but of matter itself. The same divergence is still observed among the scholars of our day. On this subject, see Zeller, <i>Die Philosophie der Griechen</i>, vol. II, p. 508 et seq. (2<sup>nd</sup> ed. Tübingen, 1859), and Henri Martin, <i>Études sur le Timée de Platon</i>, vol. II, p. 179 et seq.

**French (Source):** Molti dei filosofi tengono essere prodotto ab eterno da Dio, e non havere mai havuto principio temporale, così come esso Dio non l’ha mai havuto, et di questa opinione è il grande Aristolile, et tutti i peripatetici… Ma gli fideli, et tutti quelli che credono la sacra legge di Moise, tengono che’l mondo fusse non ab eterno produtto, anzi di nulla creato in principio temporale, et ancora alcuni dei filosofi par che sentino questo; per quali è il divino Platone, che nel Timeo pone il mondo essere fatto et genito da Dio, produtto del chaos, che è la materia confusa, del quale le cose sono generate… È ben vero che lui fa il chaos, di che le cose sono fatte, eterno, cioè eternalmente produtto da Dio, laquai cosa non tengono gli fideli; perche loro tengono che fino all’ hora della creatione solo Dio fusse in essere senza mondo, et senza chaos, et che l’omnipotentia di Dio di nulla tutte le cose in principio di tempo habbia produtto, che in effetto non par già chiaramente in Moise, che’l ponga materia coeterna a Dio.— Sono adunque tre opinioni nella produttione del mondo da Dio: la prima d’Aristotile, che tutto il mondo fu produtto ab eterno; la seconda di Platone, che solamente la materia, o chaos, fu produtto ab eterno, ma il mondo in principio di tempo; et la terza delli fideli, che tutto sia produtto di nulla in principio di tempo.» — Mais on reconnaît par notre passage qu’à l’époque de Maïmonide, comme à toutes les époques, les opinions étaient divisées sur le vrai sens de la doctrine de Platon; et, en effet, le langage poétique de Platon et l’enveloppe mythique sous laquelle il présente souvent ses doctrines ne justifient que trop cette divergence des opinions. Tandis que plusieu s des plus anciens Platoniciens, et plus tard les Néoplatoniciens, prétendaient que Platon avait admis, comme Aristote, l’éternité du monde, d’autres au contraire (notamment quelques chrétiens, comme par exemple Clément d’Alexandrie) allaient jusqu’à soutenir que Platon avait professé la doctrine de la création [[t:2]]ex nihilo[[t:3]], en considérant Dieu comme l’auteur non-seulement de l’ordre du monde, mais de la matière même. La même divergence se fait remarquer encore parmi les savants de nos jours. On peut voir, à cet égard, Zeller, [[t:4]]Die Philosophie der Griechen[[t:5]], t. II, pag. 508 et suiv. (2[[t:6]]e[[t:7]] édit. Tubingue, 1859), et Henri Martin, [[t:8]]Études sur le Timée de Platon[[t:9]], t. II, pag. 179 et suiv.

---

### fn.1598.sub_2 (Footnote)
**English:** This latter scholar, after a thorough examination of the texts, arrives at conclusions that agree with the opinion of Maimonides and Leone Ebreo, namely that, according to the <i>Timaeus</i>, 1. God did not create the prime matter of bodies, that is, the indeterminate substance; 2. he did not even create secondary matter, that is, eternal chaos; 3. he produced the order of the world, but not from all eternity. — The distinction between incorporeal prime matter and corporeal chaos is of no interest to us here, any more than the question of how Plato understood prime matter and in what way his doctrine, in this regard, differs from that of Aristotle. Suffice it to say that Plato admits an eternal principle opposed to the <i>idea,</i> as blind necessity is to reason, non-being to being, the variable and the multiple to the permanent and the absolute <i>one</i>. But this is not the <i>ὕλη</i> of Aristotle, that <i>being in potentiality</i> which necessarily calls for form to become <i>being in actuality;</i> for, according to Plato, it is with consciousness and freedom, and not by necessity, that the idea realizes itself in the indeterminate substratum, which it causes to pass from confusion to order. The term <i>ὕλη</i> is itself unknown to Plato, although Aristotle uses this term when speaking of his master's doctrine. This confused and indeterminate substance that the idea <i>orders</i> and in which it manifests itself is called by Plato: <i>that which receives the imprint</i> (<i>τὸ ἐϰμαγεῖον</i>), <i>that in which things are born, the place</i>, <i>space,</i> etc.; it is also presented as <i>the mother</i> or <i>the receptacle and the nurse of all generation;</i> and Plato himself says (<i>Timaeus</i>, p. 49 <i>a</i>) that it is a species quite obscure and very difficult to understand. Cf. Ritter, <i>Geschichte der Philosophie</i>, vol. II, p. 347 et seq.; Brandis, <i>Handbuch der Geschichte der griechisch-römischen Philosophie</i>, vol. II, A, p. 297 et seq.; Zeller, <i>l. c.</i>, p. 457 et seq.; Henri Martin, <i>l. c</i>., vol. I, p. 16 et seq.
**Correction:** This latter scholar, after a thorough examination of the texts, arrives at conclusions that agree with the opinion of Maimonides and Leone Ebreo, namely that, according to the <i>Timaeus</i>, 1. God did not create the prime matter of bodies, that is, the indeterminate substance; 2. he did not even create secondary matter, that is, eternal chaos; 3. he produced the order of the world, but not from all eternity. — The distinction between incorporeal prime matter and corporeal chaos is of no interest to us here, any more than the question of how Plato understood prime matter and in what way his doctrine, in this regard, differs from that of Aristotle. Suffice it to say that Plato admits an eternal principle opposed to the <i>idea,</i> as blind necessity is to reason, non-being to being, the variable and the multiple to the permanent and the absolute <i>one</i>. But this is not the <i>ὕλη</i> of Aristotle, that <i>being in potentiality</i> which necessarily calls for form to become <i>being in actuality;</i> for, according to Plato, it is with consciousness and freedom, and not by necessity, that the idea realizes itself in the indeterminate substratum, which it causes to pass from confusion to order. The term <i>ὕλη</i> is itself unknown to Plato, although Aristotle uses this term when speaking of his master's doctrine. This confused and indeterminate substance that the idea <i>orders</i> and in which it manifests itself is called by Plato: <i>that which receives the imprint</i> (<i>τὸ ἐϰμαγεῖον</i>), <i>that in which things are born, the place</i>, <i>space,</i> etc.; it is also presented as <i>the mother</i> or <i>the receptacle and the nurse of all generation;</i> and Plato himself says (<i>Timaeus</i>, p. 49 <i>a</i>) that it is a species quite obscure and very difficult to understand. Cf. Ritter, <i>Geschichte der Philosophie</i>, vol. II, p. 347 et seq.; Brandis, <i>Handbuch der Geschichte der griechisch-römischen Philosophie</i>, vol. II, A, p. 297 et seq.; Zeller, <i>l. c.</i>, p. 457 et seq.; Henri Martin, <i>l. c</i>., vol. I, p. 16 et seq.

**French (Source):** Ce dernier savant, après un examen approfondi des textes, arrive à des conclusions qui s’accordent avec l’opinion de Maïmonide et de Léon Hébreu, à savoir que, d’après le [[t:10]]Timée[[t:11]], 1°Dieu n’a pas créé la matière première des corps, c’est-à-dire la substance indéterminée; 2° il n’a pas même créé la matière seconde, c’est-à-dire le chaos éternel; 3° il a produit l’ordre du monde, mais non de toute éternité. — La distinction entre la matière première incorporelle et le chaos corporel ne nous intéresse point ici, pas plus que la question de savoir comment Platon entendait la matière première et en quoi sa doctrine, à cet égard, diffère de celle d’Aristote. Qu’il nous suffise de dire que Platon admet un principe éternel opposé à l’[[t:12]]idée,[[t:13]] comme l’aveugle nécessité l’est à la raison, le nonêtre à l’être, le variable et le multiple au permanent et à l’[[t:14]]un[[t:15]] absolu. Mais ce n’est pas là la [[t:16]]ὕλη[[t:17]] d’Aristote, cet [[t:18]]être en puissance[[t:19]] qui appelle nécessairement la forme pour devenir [[t:20]]être en acte;[[t:21]] car, selon Platon, c’est avec conscience et liberté, et non par nécessité, que l’idée se réalise dans le substratum indéterminé, qu’elle fait passer de la confusion à l’ordre. Le terme de [[t:22]]ὕλη[[t:23]] est lui-même inconnu à Platon, quoique Aristote emploie ce terme en parlant de la doctrine de son maître. Cette substance confuse et indéterminée que l’idée [[t:24]]ordonne[[t:25]] et dans laquelle elle se manifeste est appelée par Platon: [[t:26]]ce qui reçoit l’empreinte[[t:27]] ([[t:28]]τὸ ἐϰμαγεῖον[[t:29]]), [[t:30]]ce dans quoi les choses naissent, le lieu[[t:31]], [[t:32]]l’espace,[[t:33]] etc.; elle est aussi présentée comme [[t:34]]la mère[[t:35]] ou [[t:36]]le réceptacle et la nourrice de toute génération;[[t:37]] et Platon dit lui-même ([[t:38]]Timée[[t:39]], pag. 49 [[t:40]]a[[t:41]]) que c’est une espèce bien obscure et bien difficile à comprendre. Cf. Ritter, [[t:42]]Geschichte der Philosophie[[t:43]], t. II, pag. 347 et suiv.; Brandis, [[t:44]]Handbuch der Geschichte der griechisch-römischen Philosophie[[t:45]], t. II, A, pag. 297 et suiv.; Zeller, [[t:46]]l. c.[[t:47]], pag. 457 et suiv.; Henri Martin, [[t:48]]l. c[[t:49]]., t. I, pag. 16 et suiv.

### fn.1617 (Footnote)
**English:** Albertus Magnus (<i>loc. cit.</i>, pp. 58-59), without reproducing the entire demonstration, presents its conclusion as follows: "Tertia via est sumpta de natura cœli, cujus materia elongata est a generatione et corruptione; propter quod omnes antiqui convenerunt, quod cœlum esset locus Dei. Manente autem cœlo nunquam defuit motus ejus; manente motu, nunquam cessavit mundus producere animalia et plantas. Substantia orbis et motus sine initio manserunt, et manent, et manebunt sine fine; ergo mundus sine initio mansit, manet, et manebit sine fine."
**Correction:** Albertus Magnus (<i>loc. cit.</i>, pp. 58-59), without reproducing the entire demonstration, presents its conclusion as follows: "Tertia via est sumpta de natura cœli, cujus materia elongata est a generatione et corruptione; propter quod omnes antiqui convenerunt, quod cœlum esset locus Dei. Manente autem cœlo nunquam defuit motus ejus; manente motu, nunquam cessavit mundus producere animalia et plantas. Substantia orbis et motus sine initio manserunt, et manent, et manebunt sine fine; ergo mundus sine initio mansit, manet, et manebit sine fine."

**French (Source):** Albert le Grand ([[t:0]]l. c.[[t:1]], pag. 58-59), sans reproduire toute la démonstration, en expose ainsi la conclusion: «Tertia via est sumpta de natura cœli, cujus materia elongata est a generatione et corruptione; propter quod omnes antiqui convenerunt, quod cœlum esset locus Dei. Manente autem cœlo nunquam defuit motus ejus; manente motu, nunquam cessavit mundus producere animalia et plantas. Substantia orbis et motus sine initio manserunt, et manent, et manebunt sine fine; ergo mundus sine initio mansit, manet, et manebit sine fine.»

### fn.1628 (Footnote)
**English:** That is to say, the perpetuity of the Heaven. — See the treatise <i>On the Heavens</i>, book I, chap. 3: <i>ἔοιϰε δ’ὅ τε λόγος φαινομένοις μαρτυρεῖν ϰαὶ τὰ φαινόμενα τῷ λόγῳ πάντες γὰρ ἄνθρωποι περὶ θεῶν ἔχουσιν ὑπόληψιν ϰαὶ πάντες τὸν ἀνοτάτω τῷ θείῳ τόπον ἀποδιδίασι, ϰαὶ βάρβαροι ϰαὶ Ἕλληνες, ὅσοι περ εἶναι νομίζουσι θεούς, δῆλον ὅτι ὡς τῷ ἀθανάτῳ τὸ ἀθάνατον συνηρτημένον.</i> Averroes, in his large commentary (folio ed., vol. ### fn.1636 (Footnote)
**English:** Literally: <i>by conceding to him what he concedes to himself regarding the accuracy of the arguments.</i> — One would hardly recognize, in the citation just read, the Greek text to which it corresponds (<i>loc. cit.</i>: <i>ἅμα δὲ καὶ μᾶλλον ἂν εἴη πιστὰ κ. τ. λ.</i>), of which the following is a literal translation: "What is about to be said will appear more credible to those who have first heard the justifications of the opposing reasons; for it would hardly befit us to appear to judge in absentia. Indeed, those who wish to pronounce a sufficiently true judgment must be arbiters and not adversaries." However, the accuracy of Maimonides' citation, according to the Arabic version, is guaranteed for us by the Arab-Latin version, which, in turn, has poorly paraphrased the Arabic terms. See the Works of Aristotle with the commentaries of Averroes, folio edition, vol. V, f. 32, col. <i>d</i>: "Et nos cum hoc fecerimus, tune sermo noster erit dignior ut recipiatur apud eos qui sont bonæ discretionis (vel considerationis in discretione ipsorum, vel intellectu); et maxime, cum audierint rationes contradicentium primo. Et jam scimus quod cum nos dixerimus in aliquo, necesse est ut sic sit, aut sic visum est nobis, et cum hac opinione et necessitate non dixerimus rationes contradicentium in eis, tunc minus erunt recipiendæ apud audientes ex distinguentibus propositiones signorum, et contemplationes intellectuum; et oportet qui voluerit judicare vere, ut non sit contradicens. Et odiens eum qui contradicit: sed oportet esse diligentem ipsum et pacificum ei. Et ex pacificatione est ut concedat ei, sicut concederet sibi de sermonibus recte et de scientia apud terminos demonstrationum". — The Latin version of the treatise <i>On the Heavens</i>, attributed in the printed edition to <i>Paul the Israelite</i>, is, for the most part, identical to that of Michael Scot, which is found in several manuscripts of the Imperial Library.
**Correction:** Literally: <i>by conceding to him what he concedes to himself regarding the accuracy of the arguments.</i> — One would hardly recognize, in the citation just read, the Greek text to which it corresponds (<i>loc. cit.</i>: <i>ἅμα δὲ καὶ μᾶλλον ἂν εἴη πιστὰ κ. τ. λ.</i>), of which the following is a literal translation: "What is about to be said will appear more credible to those who have first heard the justifications of the opposing reasons; for it would hardly befit us to appear to judge in absentia. Indeed, those who wish to pronounce a sufficiently true judgment must be arbiters and not adversaries." However, the accuracy of Maimonides' citation, according to the Arabic version, is guaranteed for us by the Arab-Latin version, which, in turn, has poorly paraphrased the Arabic terms. See the Works of Aristotle with the commentaries of Averroes, folio edition, vol. V, f. 32, col. <i>d</i>: "Et nos cum hoc fecerimus, tune sermo noster erit dignior ut recipiatur apud eos qui sont bonæ discretionis (vel considerationis in discretione ipsorum, vel intellectu); et maxime, cum audierint rationes contradicentium primo. Et jam scimus quod cum nos dixerimus in aliquo, necesse est ut sic sit, aut sic visum est nobis, et cum hac opinione et necessitate non dixerimus rationes contradicentium in eis, tunc minus erunt recipiendæ apud audientes ex distinguentibus propositiones signorum, et contemplationes intellectuum; et oportet qui voluerit judicare vere, ut non sit contradicens. Et odiens eum qui contradicit: sed oportet esse diligentem ipsum et pacificum ei. Et ex pacificatione est ut concedat ei, sicut concederet sibi de sermonibus recte et de scientia apud terminos demonstrationum". — The Latin version of the treatise <i>On the Heavens</i>, attributed in the printed edition to <i>Paul the Israelite</i>, is, for the most part, identical to that of Michael Scot, which is found in several manuscripts of the Imperial Library.

**French (Source):** Littéralement: [[t:0]]en lui concédant ce qu’il se concède à lui-même en fait de la justesse des argumentations.[[t:1]] — On reconnaîtrait à peine, dans la citation qu’on vient de lire, le texte grec auquel elle correspond ([[t:2]]l. c.[[t:3]]: [[t:4]]Ἅμα δὲ ϰαὶ μᾶλλον ἂν εἴη πιστἀ ϰ. τ. λ.[[t:5]]), et dont voici la traduction littérale: «Ce qui va être dit paraîtra plus croyable à ceux qui auront entendu d’abord les justifications des raisons adverses; car il nous conviendrait fort peu de paraître juger par contumace. En effet, ceux qui veulent prononcer un jugement suffisamment vrai doivent être des arbitres et non pas des adversaires.» Mais l’exactitude de la citation de Maïmonide, d’après la version arabe, nous est garantie par la version arabelatine, qui, à son tour, a mal paraphrasé les termes arabes. Voyez les Œuvres d’Aristote avec les commentaires d’Averroès, édit. in-fol., t. V, f. 32, col. [[t:6]]d[[t:7]]: «Et nos cum hoc fecerimus, tune sermo noster erit dignior ut recipiatur apud eos qui sunt bonæ discretionis (vel considerationis in discretione ipsorum, vel intellectu); et maxime, cum audierint rationes contradicentium primo. Et jam scimus quod cum nos dixerimus in aliquo, necesse est ut sic sit, aut sic visum est nobis, et cum hac opinione et necessitate non dixerimus rationes contradicentium in eis, tunc minus erunt recipiendæ apud audientes ex distinguentibus propositiones signorum, et contemplationes intellectuum; et oportet qui voluerit judicare vere, ut non sit contradicens. Et odiens eum qui contradicit: sed oportet esse diligentem ipsum et pacificum ei. Et ex pacificatione est ut concedat ei, sicut concederet sibi de sermonibus recte et de scientia apud terminos demonstrationum». — La version latine du traité [[t:8]]du Ciel[[t:9]], attribuée dans l’édition imprimée à [[t:10]]Paul Israélite[[t:11]], est, à très peu de chose près, identique avec celle de Michel Scott, qui se trouve dans plusieurs mss. de la Bibliothèque impériale.

### fn.1670 (Footnote)
**English:** Literally: <i>for we maintain that</i>, <i>after movement has existed according to that nature upon which it was fixed</i>, <i>it is unimaginable that it could have been born and perish by a total birth and a total corruption</i>, <i>as partial movements which are born are born</i>, <i>and as partial movements perish.</i> The author has expressed himself in a convoluted and unclear manner. The sense is: We admit with Aristotle that the universal movement of the world is of such a nature that it could not have been born from a prior movement that caused it to pass from potentiality to actuality, as occurs in partial movements, for example in that of animals. But we do not conclude from this that universal movement is eternal; for, while it is true that it could not have had a prior movement as its cause, it could nevertheless have had a beginning and have been created by God. It is in this sense that Albert the Great refutes the <i>first method</i> of Aristotle, and he summarizes his refutation in these terms: 'Et de hac via constat, per antedicta, quod non probat motum non incepisse per creationem, sed quod non incepit per mutationem et motum. <i>'</i> See <i>Summa theologiae</i>, part II, tract. I, quest. IV, partic. 3 (opp. vol. XVIII, p. 58, col. <i>b</i>).
**Correction:** Literally: <i>for we maintain that</i>, <i>after movement has existed according to that nature upon which it was fixed</i>, <i>it is unimaginable that it could have been born and perish by a total birth and a total corruption</i>, <i>as partial movements which are born are born</i>, <i>and as partial movements perish.</i> The author has expressed himself in a convoluted and unclear manner. The sense is: We admit with Aristotle that the universal movement of the world is of such a nature that it could not have been born from a prior movement that caused it to pass from potentiality to actuality, as occurs in partial movements, for example in that of animals. But we do not conclude from this that universal movement is eternal; for, while it is true that it could not have had a prior movement as its cause, it could nevertheless have had a beginning and have been created by God. It is in this sense that Albert the Great refutes the <i>first method</i> of Aristotle, and he summarizes his refutation in these terms: 'Et de hac via constat, per antedicta, quod non probat motum non incepisse per creationem, sed quod non incepit per mutationem et motum. <i>'</i> See <i>Summa theologiae</i>, part II, tract. I, quest. IV, partic. 3 (opp. vol. XVIII, p. 58, col. <i>b</i>).

**French (Source):** Littéralement: [[t:0]]car nous soutenons que[[t:1]], [[t:2]]après que le mouvement a existé selon cette nature sur laquelle il a été fixé[[t:3]], [[t:4]]il est inimaginable qu’il ait pu naître et périr par une naissance totale et par une corruption totale[[t:5]], [[t:6]]comme naissent les mouvements partiels qui naissent[[t:7]], [[t:8]]et comme périssent les mouvements partiels.[[t:9]] L’auteur s’est exprimé d’une manière embarrassée et peu claire. Le sens est: Nous admettons avec Aristote que le mouvement universel du monde est de nature telle qu’il n’a pu naître d’un mouvement antérieur qui l’ait fait passer de la puissance à l’acte, comme cela a lieu dans les mouvements partiels, par exemple dans celui des animaux. Mais nous ne concluons pas de là que le mouvement universel soit éternel; car, s’il est vrai qu’il n’a pu avoir pour cause un mouvement antérieur, il a pu cependant avoir un commencement et avoir été créé par Dieu. C’est dans ce sens qu’Albert le Grand réfute la [[t:10]]première méthode[[t:11]] d’Aristote, et il résume sa réfutation en ces termes: «Et de hac via constat, per antedicta, quod non probat motum non incepisse per creationem, sed quod non incepit per mutationem et motum. [[t:12]]»[[t:13]] Voy. [[t:14]]Summa theologiœ[[t:15]], pars. II, tract. I, quæst. IV, partic. 3 (opp. t. XVIII, pag. 58, col. [[t:16]]b[[t:17]].)

### fn.1729 (Footnote)
**English:** The author refers to the superior sphere which, as has been seen, moves from east to west. He is evidently alluding to a passage in the treatise <i>On the Heavens</i>, Book II, Ch. 5, where Aristotle seeks to indicate the reason why the heaven moves from left to right; just as, he says, in rectilinear movements (of the elements) the one directed upwards is the most noble, so in the circular movements of the celestial spheres, it is the one directed forward or toward the right. It would therefore appear that Aristotle is speaking here of the movement of the planets rather than that of the superior heaven; for <i>forward</i> (<i>εἰς τὸ πρόσθεν</i>), or <i>toward the right</i>, means <i>toward the east</i>. Cf. <i>ibid.</i>, Ch. 2: <i>δεξιὸν γὰρ ἑκάστου λέגוμεν, ὅθεν ἡ ἀρχὴ τῆς κατὰ τόπον κινήσεως · τοῦ δ’ οὐρανοῦ ἀρχὴν τῆς περιφορᾶς, ὅθεν αἱ ἀνατολαὶ τῶν ἄστρων, ὥστε τοῦט’ ἂν εἴה δεξιόν, οὗ δ’ αἱ δύσεις, ἀριστερόν</i>. But Maimonides' assertion is based on the Arabic version, which, as is recognizable from the Arabo-Latin version, had significantly altered the Greek text. The final sentence of Ch. V (<i>βέλτιστον γὰρ κινεῖσθαι ἁπλῆν τε κίνησιν καὶ ἄπαυστον, καὶ ταύτην ἐπὶ τὸ τιμιώτερον</i>) is paraphrased as follows in the Arabo-Latin version (fol. 55, col. <i>b</i>): 'Melius enim et nobilius est ut cœlum moveatur semper sine cessatione, et quod motus ejus sit <i>ex nobilissimo locorum</i>, quod est dextrum. <i>Manifestum est igitur quare cœlum movetur ex oriente ad occidentem, et non e converso</i>.'
**Correction:** The author refers to the superior sphere which, as has been seen, moves from east to west. He is evidently alluding to a passage in the treatise <i>On the Heavens</i>, Book II, Ch. 5, where Aristotle seeks to indicate the reason why the heaven moves from left to right; just as, he says, in rectilinear movements (of the elements) the one directed upwards is the most noble, so in the circular movements of the celestial spheres, it is the one directed forward or toward the right. It would therefore appear that Aristotle is speaking here of the movement of the planets rather than that of the superior heaven; for <i>forward</i> (<i>εἰς τὸ πρόσθεν</i>), or <i>toward the right</i>, means <i>toward the east</i>. Cf. <i>ibid.</i>, Ch. 2: <i>δεξιὸν γὰρ ἑκάστου λέגוμεν, ὅθεν ἡ ἀρχὴ τῆς κατὰ τόπον κινήσεως · τοῦ δ’ οὐρανοῦ ἀρχὴν τῆς περιφορᾶς, ὅθεν αἱ ἀνατολαὶ τῶν ἄστρων, ὥστε τοῦט’ ἂν εἴה δεξιόν, οὗ δ’ αἱ δύσεις, ἀριστερόν</i>. But Maimonides' assertion is based on the Arabic version, which, as is recognizable from the Arabo-Latin version, had significantly altered the Greek text. The final sentence of Ch. V (<i>βέλτιστον γὰρ κινεῖσθαι ἁπλῆν τε κίνησιν καὶ ἄπαυστον, καὶ ταύτην ἐπὶ τὸ τιμιώτερον</i>) is paraphrased as follows in the Arabo-Latin version (fol. 55, col. <i>b</i>): 'Melius enim et nobilius est ut cœlum moveatur semper sine cessatione, et quod motus ejus sit <i>ex nobilissimo locorum</i>, quod est dextrum. <i>Manifestum est igitur quare cœlum movetur ex oriente ad occidentem, et non e converso</i>.'

**French (Source):** L’auteur veut parler de la sphère supérieure, qui, comme on l’a vu, se meut d’orient en occident. Il fait évidemment allusion à un passage du traité [[t:0]]du Ciel[[t:1]], liv. II, chap. 5, où Aristote cherche à indiquer la raison pourquoi le ciel se meut de gauche à droite; de même, dit-il, que dans les mouvements droits (des éléments) celui qui se dirige vers le haut est le plus noble, de même, dans les mouvements circulaires des sphères célestes, c’est celui qui se dirige en avant ou vers la droite. Il paraîtrait donc qu’Aristote parle ici plutôt du mouvement des planètes que de celui du ciel supérieur; car, [[t:2]]en avant[[t:3]] ([[t:4]]εἰς τὸ πρόσθεν[[t:5]]), ou [[t:6]]vers la droite[[t:7]], signifie [[t:8]]vers l’orient[[t:9]]. Cf. [[t:10]]ibid.[[t:11]], chap. 2: [[t:12]]δεξιὸν γὰρ ἑϰάστου λέγομεν, ὅθεν ἡ ἀρχὴτῆς ϰατὰ τόπον ϰινήσεως · τοῦ δ’ οὐρανοῦ αρχὴν τῆς περιφορᾶς, ὅθεν αἱ ἀνατολαὶ τῶν ἄστρων, ὥστε τοῦτ’ ἂν εἴη δεξιόν, οὗ δ’αἱ δύσεις, ἀριστερόν[[t:13]]. Mais l’assertion de Maïmonide est fondée sur la version arabe, qui, comme on le reconnaît par la version arabe-latine, avait sensiblement altéré le texte grec. La dernière phrase du chap. V ([[t:14]]βέλτιστον γὰρ ϰινεῖσθαι ἁπλῆν τε ϰίνησιν ϰαὶ ἄπαυστον, ϰαὶ ταύτην ἐπὶ τὸ τιμιώτερον[[t:15]]) est ainsi paraphrasée dans la version arabe-latine (fol. 55, col. [[t:16]]b[[t:17]]): «Melius enim et nobilius est ut cœlum moveatur semper sine cessatione, et quod motus ejus sit [[t:18]]ex nobilissimo locorum[[t:19]], quod est dextrum. [[t:20]]Manifestum est igitur quare cœlum movetur ex oriente ad occidentem, et non e converso[[t:21]].»
