### fn.1737 (Footnote)
**English:** This passage is taken from the treatise <i>On the Heavens</i>, book II, chap. 12, where Aristotle examines the following two questions: 1. Why the respective motions of each planet do not increase in proportion to their distance from the superior sphere, which has only a single motion? For we see, on the contrary, that the sun and the moon have fewer motions than the planets situated above them, although the latter are farther from the center and closer to the superior sphere. 2. Why the superior sphere has a large number of stars, while each of the inferior spheres has only one? — The Arabic version is but a very free paraphrase of the Greek text, of which we content ourselves with citing the beginning: <i>δυοῖν δ’ ἀπορίαιν οὔσαιν, περὶ ὧν εἰκότως ἂν ὁστισοῦν ἀπορήσειε, πειρατέον λέγειν τὸ φαινόμενον</i>. This means: “Since there are two difficulties that could rightly perplex anyone, one must try to say <i>what seems to us to be the case</i>.” We see that the words <i>τὸ φ ανόμενον</i>, <i>id quod videtur</i>, were paraphrased in Arabic by <i>what our intellect</i>, <i>our science, and our opinion allow</i>. Maimonides thus took unnecessary pains by explaining further on, in a very subtle manner, what Aristotle meant by the three words <i>intellect</i>, <i>science</i>, and <i>opinion;</i> for not a single one of these words is found in the Greek text. — The two Arabic-Latin versions of the treatise <i>On the Heavens</i> do not have the word <i>intellect</i>. That of Michael Scot (published under the name of <i>Paul the Israelite</i>) reads: “Et volumus modo perscrutari de duabus quæstionibus, de quibus oporlet perscrutatorem perscrutari; et dicemus in eis <i>secundum nostram scientiam et nostram opinionem</i>.” The other version, anonymous and unpublished (Latin MS of the Imperial Library, Saint-Victor collection, no. 872, fol. 147), has the following terms: “Volo autem nunc inquirere de duabus quæstionibus inquisitione sufficiente; convenit autem ut inquirat de his inquisitor. Dicam ergo in utrisque <i>secundum summam scientiæ nostræ et nostræ sententiæ</i>.”
**Correction:** This passage is taken from the treatise <i>On the Heavens</i>, book II, chap. 12, where Aristotle examines the following two questions: 1. Why the respective motions of each planet do not increase in proportion to their distance from the superior sphere, which has only a single motion? For we see, on the contrary, that the sun and the moon have fewer motions than the planets situated above them, although the latter are farther from the center and closer to the superior sphere. 2. Why the superior sphere has a large number of stars, while each of the inferior spheres has only one? — The Arabic version is but a very free paraphrase of the Greek text, of which we content ourselves with citing the beginning: <i>δυοῖν δ’ ἀπορίαιν οὔσαιν, περὶ ὧν εἰκότως ἂν ὁστισοῦν ἀπορήσειε, πειρατέον λέγειν τὸ φαινόμενον</i>. This means: “Since there are two difficulties that could rightly perplex anyone, one must try to say <i>what seems to us to be the case</i>.” We see that the words <i>τὸ φ ανόμενον</i>, <i>id quod videtur</i>, were paraphrased in Arabic by <i>what our intellect</i>, <i>our science, and our opinion allow</i>. Maimonides thus took unnecessary pains by explaining further on, in a very subtle manner, what Aristotle meant by the three words <i>intellect</i>, <i>science</i>, and <i>opinion;</i> for not a single one of these words is found in the Greek text. — The two Arabic-Latin versions of the treatise <i>On the Heavens</i> do not have the word <i>intellect</i>. That of Michael Scot (published under the name of <i>Paul the Israelite</i>) reads: “Et volumus modo perscrutari de duabus quæstionibus, de quibus oporlet perscrutatorem perscrutari; et dicemus in eis <i>secundum nostram scientiam et nostram opinionem</i>.” The other version, anonymous and unpublished (Latin MS of the Imperial Library, Saint-Victor collection, no. 872, fol. 147), has the following terms: “Volo autem nunc inquirere de duabus quæstionibus inquisitione sufficiente; convenit autem ut inquirat de his inquisitor. Dicam ergo in utrisque <i>secundum summam scientiæ nostræ et nostræ sententiæ</i>.”

**French (Source):** Ce passage est tiré du traité [[t:0]]du Ciel[[t:1]], liv. II, chap. 12, où Aristote examine les deux questions suivantes: 1° Pourquoi les mouvements respectifs de chaque planète n’augmentent pas en raison de leur distance de la sphère supérieure, qui n’a qu’un seul mouvement? car nous voyons, au contraire, que le soleil et la lune ont moins de mouvements que les planètes situées au-dessus, quoique celles-ci soient plus éloignées du centre et plus rapprochées de la sphère supérieure. 2° Pourquoi la sphère supérieure a un grand nombre d’étoiles, tandis que chacune des sphères inférieures n’en a qu’une seule? — La version arabe n’est qu’une paraphrase très libre du texte grec, dont nous nous contentons de citer le commencement: [[t:2]]δυοῖν δ’ἀπορίαιν οὔσαιν, περὶ ὧν εἰϰότως ἄν ὁστισοῦν ἀπορήσειε, πειρατέον λἑγειν τὸ φαινόμενον[[t:3]]. Ce qui veut dire: «Comme il existe deux difficultés qui pourraient à bon droit embarrasser chacun, il faut essayer de dire [[t:4]]ce qu’il nous en semble[[t:5]].» On voit que les mots [[t:6]]τὸ φ ανόμενον[[t:7]], [[t:8]]id quod videtur[[t:9]], ont été paraphrasés, en arabe, par [[t:10]]ce que comportent notre intelligence[[t:11]], [[t:12]]notre science et notre opinion[[t:13]]. Maïmonide s’est donc donné une peine inutile, en expliquant plus loin, d’une manière très subtile, ce qu’Aristote a voulu dire par les trois mots [[t:14]]intelligence[[t:15]], [[t:16]]science[[t:17]] et [[t:18]]opinion;[[t:19]] car pas un seul de ces mots ne se trouve dans le texte grec. — Les deux versions arabes-latines du traité [[t:20]]du Ciel[[t:21]] n’ont pas le mot [[t:22]]intelligence[[t:23]]. Celle de Michel Scott (publiée sous le nom de [[t:24]]Paul Israélite[[t:25]]) porte: «Et volumus modo perscrutari de duabus quæstionibus, de quibus oporlet perscrutatorem perscrutari; et dicemus in eis [[t:26]]secundum nostram scientiam et nostram opinionem[[t:27]].» L’autre version, anonyme et inédite (ms. lat. de la Biblioth. imp., fonds de Saint-Victor, n° 872, fol. 147), a les termes suivants: «Volo autem nunc inquirere de duabus quæstionibus inquisitione sufficiente; convenit autem ut inquirat de his inquisitor. Dicam ergo in utrisque [[t:28]]secundum summam scientiœ nostrœ et nostrœ sententiœ[[t:29]].»

### fn.1784 (Footnote)
**English:** This proposition, which Maimonides borrowed from Avicenna, was not stated by Aristotle; however, in the latter's writings, one finds several passages that could have given rise to it. Thus, for example, Aristotle says in the <i>Physics</i> (Book VIII, chap. VI end) that what is not moved (the first mover), remaining simple and always the same, will only be able to produce a single simple motion (<i>ὸ ὃ’ἀϰίνητον, ὥσπερ εἴρηται, ἅτε ἁπλῶς ϰαὶ ὡσαύτως ϰαὶ ἐν τῷ αὐτῷ διαμένον, μίαν ϰαὶ ἁπλῆν ϰινήσει ϰίνησιν</i>). In the <i>Metaphysics</i> (Book XII, chap. VIII towards the end), it is said that the first unmoved mover being <i>one</i> in idea and in number, it follows that what is moved always and continuously is also <i>one;</i> and that, consequently, there is only one heaven (<i>ἓν ἄρα ϰαὶ λόγῳ ϰαὶ ἀριθμῷ τὸ πρῶτον ϰινοῦν ἀϰίνητον ὄν · ϰαὶ τὸ ϰινούμενον ἀεὶ ϰαὶ συνεχῶς ἓν μόνον · εἷς ἄρα οὐρανὸς μόνος</i>). Here, as elsewhere, Maimonides attributes to Aristotle a theory of Avicenna, which says that the first cause, being simple and absolute unity, could only cause a single simple intelligence to emanate directly from itself; Averroes observes that it is by error that this proposition has been attributed to Aristotle, by misunderstanding the meaning that this philosopher attaches to the idea of <i>unity</i>, when he presents the universe as a unity, or an organic whole, emanated from a first and unique cause. See, on this subject, my <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, p. 360 ff. — The author will show in this chapter that the entire scaffolding of the successive emanation of the Intelligences and of the spheres, according to the theory of Aristotle (or rather of Avicenna), does not suffice to explain the multiplicity and diversity that reign in the world, but that all difficulties disappear as soon as one admits a creator God, having created the world by his free will.
**Correction:** This proposition, which Maimonides borrowed from Avicenna, was not stated by Aristotle; however, in the latter's writings, one finds several passages that could have given rise to it. Thus, for example, Aristotle says in the <i>Physics</i> (Book VIII, chap. VI end) that what is not moved (the first mover), remaining simple and always the same, will only be able to produce a single simple motion (<i>ὸ ὃ’ἀϰίνητον, ὥσπερ εἴρηται, ἅτε ἁπλῶς ϰαὶ ὡσαύτως ϰαὶ ἐν τῷ αὐτῷ διαμένον, μίαν ϰαὶ ἁπλῆν ϰινήσει ϰίνησιν</i>). In the <i>Metaphysics</i> (Book XII, chap. VIII towards the end), it is said that the first unmoved mover being <i>one</i> in idea and in number, it follows that what is moved always and continuously is also <i>one;</i> and that, consequently, there is only one heaven (<i>ἓν ἄρα ϰαὶ λόγῳ ϰαὶ ἀριθμῷ τὸ πρῶτον ϰινοῦν ἀϰίνητον ὄν · ϰαὶ τὸ ϰινούμενον ἀεὶ ϰαὶ συνεχῶς ἓν μόνον · εἷς ἄρα οὐρανὸς μόνος</i>). Here, as elsewhere, Maimonides attributes to Aristotle a theory of Avicenna, which says that the first cause, being simple and absolute unity, could only cause a single simple intelligence to emanate directly from itself; Averroes observes that it is by error that this proposition has been attributed to Aristotle, by misunderstanding the meaning that this philosopher attaches to the idea of <i>unity</i>, when he presents the universe as a unity, or an organic whole, emanated from a first and unique cause. See, on this subject, my <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, p. 360 ff. — The author will show in this chapter that the entire scaffolding of the successive emanation of the Intelligences and of the spheres, according to the theory of Aristotle (or rather of Avicenna), does not suffice to explain the multiplicity and diversity that reign in the world, but that all difficulties disappear as soon as one admits a creator God, having created the world by his free will.

**French (Source):** Cette proposition, que Maïmonide a empruntée à Ibn-Sinâ, n’a point été énoncée par Aristote; mais on trouve dans les écrits de ce dernier plusieurs passages qui ont pu y donner lieu. Ainsi, par exemple, Aristote dit dans la [[t:0]]Physique[[t:1]] (liv. VIII, chap. VI fin) que ce qui n’est pas mû (le moteur premier), restant simple et toujours le même, ne pourra produire qu’un seul mouvement simple ([[t:2]]τὸ ὃ’ἀϰίνητον, ὥσπερ εἴρηται, ἅτε ἁπλῶς ϰαὶ ὡσαύτως ϰαὶ ἐν τῷ αὐτῷ διαμένον, μίαν ϰαὶ ἁπλῆν ϰινήσει ϰίνησιν[[t:3]]). Dans la [[t:4]]Métaphysique[[t:5]] (liv. XII, chap. VIII vers la fin), il est dit que le moteur premier non mû étant [[t:6]]un[[t:7]] par l’idée et par le nombre, il s’ensuit que ce qui est mû toujours et d’une manière continue est également [[t:8]]un;[[t:9]] et que, par conséquent, il n’y a qu’un seul ciel ([[t:10]]ἓν ἄρα ϰαὶ λόγῳ ϰαὶ ἀριθμῷ τὸ πρῶτον ϰινοῦν ἀϰίνητον ὄν · ϰαὶ τὸ ϰινούμενον ἄρα ἀεὶ ϰαὶ συνεχῶς ἓν μόνον · εἷς ἄρα οὐρανὸς μόνος[[t:11]]). Ici, comme ailleurs, Maïmonide attribue à Aristote une théorie d’Ibn-Sinâ, qui dit que la cause première, étant l’unité simple et absolue, n’a pu faire directement émaner d’elle qu’une seule intelligence simple; Ibn-Roschd fait observer que c’est par erreur qu’on a attribué cette proposition à Aristote, en se méprenant sur le sens que ce philosophe attache à l’idée d’[[t:12]]unité[[t:13]], lorsqu’il présente l’univers comme une unité, ou un tout organique, émané d’une cause première et unique. Voy., sur ce sujet, mes [[t:14]]Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe[[t:15]], p. 360 et suiv. — L’auteur va montrer dans ce chapitre que tout l’échafaudage de l’émanation successive des Intelligences et des sphères, selon la théorie d’Aristote (ou mieux d’Ibn-Sinâ), ne suffit pas pour expliquer la multiplicité et la diversité qui régnent dans le monde, mais que toutes les difficultés disparaissent, dès qu’on admet un Dieu créateur, ayant créé le monde par sa libre volonté.

### fn.1814 (Footnote)
**English:** Literally: <i>for this opinion contains the subversion of the foundation of the Law, and heresy toward God.</i> By the words <i>this opinion</i>, the author refers to the opinion of the eternity of the world, although he has not explicitly spoken of it in the preceding text. On the word <span dir="rtl">אפתאית</span>, which we translate here as <i>heresy</i>, see my <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe,</i> p. 269, n. 3, and Cf. Part III<sup></sup> of the <i>Guide</i>, at the beginning of chap. XVI.
**Correction:** Literally: <i>for this opinion contains the subversion of the foundation of the Law, and heresy toward God.</i> By the words <i>this opinion</i>, the author refers to the opinion of the eternity of the world, although he has not explicitly spoken of it in the preceding text. On the word <span dir="rtl">אפתאית</span>, which we translate here as <i>heresy</i>, see my <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe,</i> p. 269, n. 3, and Cf. Part III<sup></sup> of the <i>Guide</i>, at the beginning of chap. XVI.

**French (Source):** Littéralement: [[t:0]]car cette opinion renferme le renversement de la base de la loi, et une hérésie à l’égard de Dieu.[[t:1]] Par les mots [[t:2]]cette opinion[[t:3]], l’auteur désigne l’opinion de l’éternité du monde, quoiqu’il n’en ait pas expressément parlé dans ce qui précède. Sur le mot [[t:4]]אפתיאת[[t:5]], que nous traduisons ici par [[t:6]]hérésie[[t:7]], voy. mes [[t:8]]Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe,[[t:9]] p. 269, n. 3, et Cf. la III[[t:10]]e[[t:11]] partie du [[t:12]]Guide[[t:13]], au commencement du chap. XVI.

### fn.1823 (Footnote)
**English:** Ancient astronomers, in order to explain the apparent inequalities of planetary movements, made them move, sometimes in eccentrics, that is, in spheres whose center deviates from that of the zodiac or the earth, and sometimes in epicycles, that is, in small secondary spheres carried by large concentric or eccentric spheres, and whose center is supposed to move on the surface of the large sphere along the circumference of a great circle called the <i>deferent</i> (See <i>Almagest</i>, book VI, ch. 3). These purely geometric hypotheses, very ingenious and at the same time very complicated, remained one of the bases of astronomical science throughout the Middle Ages. They became obsolete with the discovery of the elliptical orbits of the planets and the laws of attraction. From the beginning of the 12th century, Arab philosophers were troubled by how improbable Ptolemy's hypotheses appeared and their lack of conformity with the physical principles and theories of motion developed by Aristotle. Attempts were made to substitute other hypotheses for them; but no system was successfully developed that could compete with that of Ptolemy (See my <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, pp. 412, 430, 520 et seq.). These attempts had no success among astronomers, and Maimonides himself, who here attacks Ptolemy's hypotheses from a philosophical point of view, does not hesitate, in his treatise <i>Kiddousch ha-’hodesch</i>, or <i>on the fixing of the new moons</i>, to admit them in their full extent and to take them as the basis for his astronomical calculations.
**Correction:** Ancient astronomers, in order to explain the apparent inequalities of planetary movements, made them move, sometimes in eccentrics, that is, in spheres whose center deviates from that of the zodiac or the earth, and sometimes in epicycles, that is, in small secondary spheres carried by large concentric or eccentric spheres, and whose center is supposed to move on the surface of the large sphere along the circumference of a great circle called the <i>deferent</i> (See <i>Almagest</i>, book VI, ch. 3). These purely geometric hypotheses, very ingenious and at the same time very complicated, remained one of the bases of astronomical science throughout the Middle Ages. They became obsolete with the discovery of the elliptical orbits of the planets and the laws of attraction. From the beginning of the 12th century, Arab philosophers were troubled by how improbable Ptolemy's hypotheses appeared and their lack of conformity with the physical principles and theories of motion developed by Aristotle. Attempts were made to substitute other hypotheses for them; but no system was successfully developed that could compete with that of Ptolemy (See my <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, pp. 412, 430, 520 et seq.). These attempts had no success among astronomers, and Maimonides himself, who here attacks Ptolemy's hypotheses from a philosophical point of view, does not hesitate, in his treatise <i>Kiddousch ha-’hodesch</i>, or <i>on the fixing of the new moons</i>, to admit them in their full extent and to take them as the basis for his astronomical calculations.

**French (Source):** Les astronomes anciens, pour expliquer les inégalités apparentes des mouvements des planètes, les font mouvoir, tantôt dans des excentriques, c’est-à-dire dans des sphères dont le centre s’écarte de celui du zodiaque ou de la terre, tantôt dans des épicycles, c’est-à-dire dans de petites sphères secondaires, portées par les grandes sphères concentriques ou excentriques, et dont le centre est supposé se mouvoir à la surface de la grande sphère sur la circonférence d’un grand cercle, appelé le [[t:0]]déférent[[t:1]] (Voy. [[t:2]]Almageste[[t:3]], liv. VI, chap. 3). Ces hypothèses, purement géométriques, très ingénieuses et en même temps très compliquées, restèrent, pendant tout le moyen âge, une des bases de la science astronomique. Elles sont devenues inutiles par la découverte des orbes elliptiques des planètes et par celle des lois de l’attraction. Dès le commencement du XII[[t:4]]e[[t:5]] siècle, les philosophes arabes s’émurent de ce que les hypothèses de Ptolémée offraient d’invraisemblable et de peu conforme aux principes physiques et aux théories du mouvement développées par Aristote. On essaya de leur substituer d’autres hypothèses; mais on ne parvint point à élaborer un système qui pût lutter avec celui de Ptolémée (Voy. mes [[t:6]]Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe[[t:7]], p. 412, 430, 520 et suiv.). Ces tentatives n’eurent aucun succès parmi les astronomes, et Maïmonide lui-même, qui attaque ici les hypothèses de Ptolémée au point de vue philosophique, n’hésite pas, dans son traité [[t:8]]Kiddousch ha-’hodesch[[t:9]], ou [[t:10]]de la fixation des néoménies[[t:11]], à les admettre dans toute leur étendue et à les prendre pour bases de ses calculs astronomiques.

### fn.1847 (Footnote)
**English:** Cf. above, p. 187, n. 1, and the III<sup>rd</sup> part of this work, chap. XIV. We find no information anywhere concerning the astronomer Al-Kabīcī, nor his treatise <i>On Distances</i>, and we do not know how he demonstrated the distances between the centers of the eccentrics and the center of the zodiac, taking the semi-diameter or radius of the earth as a unit. The astronomer in question is undoubtedly the same one whom the Scholastics frequently cite under the patronymic name <i>Alkabitius</i>, and who, according to Albertus Magnus, was called <i>Abdilazil</i>, or more correctly <i>‘Abd al-‘Azīz</i>. See <i>Speculum astronomiae</i>, chap. V and XI (Opp. vol. V, pp. 659 and 663). The Biblioth. Imp. [Imperial Library] possesses several MSS containing an <i>Introduction to Astrology</i> by Alkabitius, which begins with these words: <i>Postulata a Domino prolixitate vitae Ceyfaddaula, id est gladii regni</i> (MS from the Sorbonne collection, no. 976). From this it can be concluded that our astronomer lived at the court of Sayf al-Dawla, ‘Alī ibn Hamdān, in Aleppo, and, consequently, that he flourished in the first half of the X<sup>th</sup> century of the Christian era. Cf. the <i>Bibliographical Dictionary</i> of Hajji Khalifa, ed. Flügel, vol. V, p. 473. The two works indicated by the Arabic bibliographer under nos<sup>.</sup> 11,681 and 11,682 appear to me to be identical. The first, which was dedicated to Sayf al-Dawla, is anonymous; the second is attributed to ‘Abd al-‘Azīz ibn ‘Uthmān al-Kabīcī. Both bear the title <i>Introduction to Astrology</i>. — As for the name <span dir="rtl">القبصي</span>, it may be pronounced <i>Al Kabīcī</i> or <i>Al-Kobéicī</i>; Ibn Tibbon’s version might justify this latter pronunciation, for it reads <span dir="rtl">הקבאייצי</span>.
**Correction:** Cf. above, p. 187, n. 1, and the III<sup>rd</sup> part of this work, chap. XIV. We find no information anywhere concerning the astronomer Al-Kabīcī, nor his treatise <i>On Distances</i>, and we do not know how he demonstrated the distances between the centers of the eccentrics and the center of the zodiac, taking the semi-diameter or radius of the earth as a unit. The astronomer in question is undoubtedly the same one whom the Scholastics frequently cite under the patronymic name <i>Alkabitius</i>, and who, according to Albertus Magnus, was called <i>Abdilazil</i>, or more correctly <i>‘Abd al-‘Azīz</i>. See <i>Speculum astronomiae</i>, chap. V and XI (Opp. vol. V, pp. 659 and 663). The Biblioth. Imp. [Imperial Library] possesses several MSS containing an <i>Introduction to Astrology</i> by Alkabitius, which begins with these words: <i>Postulata a Domino prolixitate vitae Ceyfaddaula, id est gladii regni</i> (MS from the Sorbonne collection, no. 976). From this it can be concluded that our astronomer lived at the court of Sayf al-Dawla, ‘Alī ibn Hamdān, in Aleppo, and, consequently, that he flourished in the first half of the X<sup>th</sup> century of the Christian era. Cf. the <i>Bibliographical Dictionary</i> of Hajji Khalifa, ed. Flügel, vol. V, p. 473. The two works indicated by the Arabic bibliographer under nos<sup>.</sup> 11,681 and 11,682 appear to me to be identical. The first, which was dedicated to Sayf al-Dawla, is anonymous; the second is attributed to ‘Abd al-‘Azīz ibn ‘Uthmān al-Kabīcī. Both bear the title <i>Introduction to Astrology</i>. — As for the name <span dir="rtl">القبصي</span>, it may be pronounced <i>Al Kabīcī</i> or <i>Al-Kobéicī</i>; Ibn Tibbon’s version might justify this latter pronunciation, for it reads <span dir="rtl">הקבאייצי</span>.

**French (Source):** Cf. ci-dessus, p. 187, n. 1, et la III[[t:0]]e[[t:1]] partie de cet ouvrage, chap. XIV. Nous ne trouvons nulle part des renseignements sur l’astronome Al-Kabîci, ni sur son traité [[t:2]]des Distances[[t:3]], et nous ne savons pas de quelle manière il a démontré les distances entre les centres des excentriques et le centre du zodiaque, en prenant pour unité le demi-diamètre ou le rayon de la terre. L’astronome dont il s’agit est sans doute le même que les scolastiques citent souvent sous le nom patronymique d’[[t:4]]Alkabitius[[t:5]], et qui, selon Albert le Grand, s’appelait [[t:6]]Abdilazil[[t:7]], ou mieux [[t:8]]’Abd-al-’Azîz[[t:9]]. Voy. [[t:10]]Speculum astronomiœ[[t:11]], chap. V et XI (Opp. t. V, p. 659 et 663). La Biblioth. Imp. possède plusieurs mss. renfermant une [[t:12]]Introduction à l’astrologie[[t:13]], par Alkabitius, qui commence par ces mots: [[t:14]]Postulata a Domino prolixitate vitœ Ceyfaddaula, id est gladii regni[[t:15]] (ms. du fonds de la Sorbonne, n. 976). On peut conclure de là que notre astronome vivait à la cour de Seif-ed-Daula, ’Ali-ben-’Hamdân, à Alep, et, par conséquent, qu’il florissait dans la première moitié du X[[t:16]]e[[t:17]] siècle de l’ère chrétienne. Cf. le [[t:18]]Dictionnaire bibliographique[[t:19]] de ’Hadji-Khalfa, édit. de M. Flügel, t. V, p. 473. Les deux ouvrages indiqués par le bibliographe arabe sous les n[[t:20]]os[[t:21]] 11,681 et 11,682 me paraissent être identiques. Le premier, qui était dédié à Seif-ed-Daula, est anonyme; le second est attribué à ’Abd-al-’Azîz ben-’Othmân al-Kabîci. L’un et l’autre portent le titre d’[[t:22]]Introduction à l’Astrologie[[t:23]]. — Quant au nom de [[t:24]]القبيصي[[t:25]] , on peut le prononcer [[t:26]]Al Kabîci[[t:27]] ou [[t:28]]Al-Kobéici[[t:29]]; la version d’Ibn-Tibbon pourrait justifier cette dernière prononciation, car elle porte [[t:30]]הקבאיצי[[t:31]].

### fn.1864 (Footnote)
**English:** By the word <span dir="rtl">باطן</span>, <i>internal</i> (in Hebrew <span dir="rtl">תוך</span>), the Arabs designate the allegorical sense of the words of the Qur'an, as opposed to <span dir="rtl">ظاهر</span>, <i>external</i>, which designates the literal sense. From this comes the name <i>Batenis</i> (<span dir="rtl">الباطنية</span> or <span dir="rtl">اهל הבאטן</span>), that is, partisans of the allegorical sense, a mystical Muslim sect that formed under the influence of Neo-Platonic doctrines and which has much analogy with the Jewish <i>Kabbalists</i>. See on this sect, its names, and its various branches, Shahrastani, <i>History of Religious and Philosophical Sects</i>, Arabic text published by Mr. Cureton, p. 147 et seq. (German trans. by Mr. Haarbrücker, Vol. I, p. 221 et seq.).
**Correction:** By the word <span dir="rtl">باطן</span>, <i>internal</i> (in Hebrew <span dir="rtl">תוך</span>), the Arabs designate the allegorical sense of the words of the Qur'an, as opposed to <span dir="rtl">ظاهر</span>, <i>external</i>, which designates the literal sense. From this comes the name <i>Batenis</i> (<span dir="rtl">الباطنية</span> or <span dir="rtl">اهל הבאטן</span>), that is, partisans of the allegorical sense, a mystical Muslim sect that formed under the influence of Neo-Platonic doctrines and which has much analogy with the Jewish <i>Kabbalists</i>. See on this sect, its names, and its various branches, Shahrastani, <i>History of Religious and Philosophical Sects</i>, Arabic text published by Mr. Cureton, p. 147 et seq. (German trans. by Mr. Haarbrücker, Vol. I, p. 221 et seq.).

**French (Source):** Par le mot [[t:0]]باطن[[t:1]] , [[t:2]]intérieur[[t:3]] (en hébreu [[t:4]]תוך[[t:5]]), les Arabes désignent le sens allégorique des paroles du Corân, opposé à [[t:6]]ظاهر[[t:7]] , [[t:8]]extérieur[[t:9]], qui désigne le sens littéral. De là vient le nom de [[t:10]]Bâtenis[[t:11]] ([[t:12]]الباطنية[[t:13]]  ou [[t:14]]اهل الباطن[[t:15]] ), c’est-à-dire, partisans du sens allégorique, secte musulmane mystique qui se forma sous l’influence des doctrines néo-platoniciennes et qui a beaucoup d’analogie avec les [[t:16]]kabbalistes[[t:17]] juifs. Voy. sur cette secte, sur ses noms et sur ses différentes branches, Schahrestâni, [[t:18]]Histoire des sectes religieuses et philosophiques[[t:19]], texte arabe publié par M. Cureton, p. 147 et suiv. (trad. all. de M. Haarbrucker. t. I, p. 221 et suiv.).

### fn.1981 (Footnote)
**English:** This is how Ibn Sina expresses himself when speaking of the sphere of elementary fire: <span dir="rtl">واما هي النار فانها طبقة واحدة ولا ضوء لها بل هي</span> <span dir="rtl">كالهواء المشفّ الذي لا لون له</span> “Fire forms only a single layer; it has no light, but is like diaphanous air, which has no color.” See Shahrastani, <i>Histoire des sectes religieuses et philosophiques</i>, p. 410 (German trans., Vol. II, p. 305). —This refers here to the <i>potential diaphanous</i>, which can even be darkness, while the <i>actual diaphanous</i> is inseparable from light. Cf. Aristotle, treatise <i>On the Soul</i>, Book II, chap. 7: <i>Φῶς δέ ἐστιν ἡ τούτου ἐνέργεια τοῦ διαφανοῦς ᾗ διαφανές · δυνάμει δὲ ἐν ᾧ τοῦτ’ ἐστί, καὶ τὸ σκότος</i>.
**Correction:** This is how Ibn Sina expresses himself when speaking of the sphere of elementary fire: <span dir="rtl">واما هي النار فانها طبقة واحدة ولا ضوء لها بل هي</span> <span dir="rtl">كالهواء المشفّ الذي لا لون له</span> “Fire forms only a single layer; it has no light, but is like diaphanous air, which has no color.” See Shahrastani, <i>Histoire des sectes religieuses et philosophiques</i>, p. 410 (German trans., Vol. II, p. 305). —This refers here to the <i>potential diaphanous</i>, which can even be darkness, while the <i>actual diaphanous</i> is inseparable from light. Cf. Aristotle, treatise <i>On the Soul</i>, Book II, chap. 7: <i>Φῶς δέ ἐστιν ἡ τούτου ἐνέργεια τοῦ διαφανοῦς ᾗ διαφανές · δυνάμει δὲ ἐν ᾧ τοῦτ’ ἐστί, καὶ τὸ σκότος</i>.

**French (Source):** Voici comment s’exprime Ibn-Sinâ, en parlant de la sphère du feu élémentaire: [[t:0]]واما هي النار فانها طبقة واحدة ولا ضوء لها بل هي[[t:1]]  [[t:2]]كالهواء المشفّ الذي لا لون له[[t:3]]  «Le feu ne forme qu’une seule couche; il n’a pas de lumière, mais il est comme l’air diaphane, qui n’a pas de couleur.» Voy. Schahrestâni, [[t:4]]Histoire des sectes religieuses et philosophiques[[t:5]], p. 410 (trad. all., t. II, p. 305). —Il s’agit ici du [[t:6]]diaphane en puissance[[t:7]], qui peut être même l’obscurité, tandis que le [[t:8]]diaphane en acte[[t:9]] est inséparable de la lumière. Cf. Aristote, traité [[t:10]]de l’Ame[[t:11]], liv. II, chap. 7: [[t:12]]Φῶς δέ ἐστιν ἡ τούτου ἐνέργεια τοῦ διαφανοῦς ᾗ διαφανές · δυνάμει δἑ ἐν ᾧ τοῦτ ἐστί, ϰαί τὸ σϰὀτος[[t:13]].

### fn.2069 (Footnote)
**English:** The author refers to the Arabic Peripatetics, who consider the gift of prophecy as the highest degree of development of the rational and moral faculties of the soul, a degree to which man attains less through study than through the purification of the soul, by detaching himself completely from the things of this world and thus preparing himself for the most intimate union with the active intellect, which brings to actuality all the faculties that our soul possesses in potentiality. See Ibn Sina, in the analysis of Shahrastani, pp. 428-429 (German trans., vol. II, pp. 331-332), and cf. my <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, pp. 364-365.
**Correction:** The author refers to the Arabic Peripatetics, who consider the gift of prophecy as the highest degree of development of the rational and moral faculties of the soul, a degree to which man attains less through study than through the purification of the soul, by detaching himself completely from the things of this world and thus preparing himself for the most intimate union with the active intellect, which brings to actuality all the faculties that our soul possesses in potentiality. See Ibn Sina, in the analysis of Shahrastani, pp. 428-429 (German trans., vol. II, pp. 331-332), and cf. my <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, pp. 364-365.

**French (Source):** L’auteur veut parler des péripatéticiens arabes, qui considèrent le don de prophétie comme le plus haut degré de développement des facultés rationnelles et morales de l’âme, degré auquel l’homme parvient moins par l’étude que par la purification de l’âme, en se détachant complétement des choses de ce monde et en se préparant ainsi à l’union la plus intime avec l’intellect actif, qui fait passer à l’acte toutes les facultés que notre âme possède en puissance. Voy. Ibn-Sînâ, dans l’analyse de Schahrestâni, p. 428-429 (trad. all., t. II, p. 331-332), et cf. mes [[t:0]]Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe[[t:1]], p. 364-365.

---

### fn.2074 (Footnote)
**English:** The opinion that the author is about to present, and which he considers the orthodox opinion, is far from being that of the principal Jewish theologians, who generally believe that the high faculties possessed by prophets come to them from the will of God, and not from a certain innate nature, <span dir="rtl">מצד הבורא ולא מצד עצמם</span>, as Saadia says (<i>Book of Beliefs and Opinions</i>, book III, chap. 4, end). Thus, the opinion of Maimonides, which attributes too large a share in prophecy to the intellectual faculties of man, and which makes the divine will intervene only as an obstacle, has not failed to find numerous detractors. See Joseph Albo, <i>’Ikkarim</i>, book III, chap. 8; Isaac Arama, <i>’Akedah</i>, chap. 35 (Pressburg edition, 8vo, vol. II, fol. 10 <i>b</i> et seq.), and Abravanel's postscript to his commentary on this chapter, <i>loc. cit.</i>, fol. 22 <i>a</i>.
**Correction:** The opinion that the author is about to present, and which he considers the orthodox opinion, is far from being that of the principal Jewish theologians, who generally believe that the high faculties possessed by prophets come to them from the will of God, and not from a certain innate nature, <span dir="rtl">מצד הבורא ולא מצד עצמם</span>, as Saadia says (<i>Book of Beliefs and Opinions</i>, book III, chap. 4, end). Thus, the opinion of Maimonides, which attributes too large a share in prophecy to the intellectual faculties of man, and which makes the divine will intervene only as an obstacle, has not failed to find numerous detractors. See Joseph Albo, <i>’Ikkarim</i>, book III, chap. 8; Isaac Arama, <i>’Akedah</i>, chap. 35 (Pressburg edition, 8vo, vol. II, fol. 10 <i>b</i> et seq.), and Abravanel's postscript to his commentary on this chapter, <i>loc. cit.</i>, fol. 22 <i>a</i>.

**French (Source):** L’opinion que l’auteur va exposer, et qu’il considère comme l’opinion orthodoxe, est loin d’être celle des principaux théologiens juifs, qui croient en général que les hautes facultés que possèdent les prophètes leur viennent de la volonté de Dieu, et non pas d’une certaine nature innée, [[t:0]]מצד הבורא ולא מצד עצמם[[t:1]], comme dit Saadia ([[t:2]]Livre des croyances et des opinions[[t:3]], liv. III, chap. 4, fin). Aussi l’opinion de Maïmonide, qui attribue aux facultés intellectuelles de l’homme une trop grande part dans la prophétie, et qui ne fait intervenir la volonté divine que comme un obstacle, n’a-t-elle pas manqué de trouver de nombreux contradicteurs. Voy. Joseph Albo, [[t:4]]’Ikkarîm[[t:5]], liv. III, chap. 8; Isaac Arama, [[t:6]]’Akedâ[[t:7]], chap. 35 (édition de Presbourg, in-8°, t. II, fol. 10 [[t:8]]b[[t:9]] et suiv.), et le post-scriptum d’Abravanel à son commentaire sur ce chapitre, [[t:10]]l. c.[[t:11]], fol. 22 [[t:12]]a[[t:13]].

### fn.2090 (Footnote)
**English:** It appears evident to us that the word <span dir="rtl">רויה̈</span> <span dir="rtl">(رؤية)</span> must be taken here in the sense of <span dir="rtl">رؤيا</span>, <i>dream</i>, and Ibn Tibbon wrongly rendered this word by <span dir="rtl">מחשבה</span>, <i>thought.</i> The <i>true dream</i> is what Aristotle calls <i>εὐθυονειρία</i>. See my <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, p. 95, note 1.

### fn.2160 (Footnote)
**English:** It seems to me that by <i>solitary</i> the author here means the sage, who isolates himself from human society in order to remain out of reach of their vices and not to be disturbed in his meditations; this is the sage mentioned by Ibn Bājja in his treatise <i>The Regime of the Solitary</i>. See my <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, p. 388 ff.
**Correction:** It seems to me that by <i>solitary</i> the author here means the sage, who isolates himself from human society in order to remain out of reach of their vices and not to be disturbed in his meditations; this is the sage mentioned by Ibn Bājja in his treatise <i>The Regime of the Solitary</i>. See my <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, p. 388 ff.

**French (Source):** Il me semble que par [[t:0]]solitaire[[t:1]] l’auteur entend ici le sage, qui s’isole de la société des hommes, afin de se trouver hors des atteintes de leurs vices et de ne pas être troublé dans ses méditations; c’est le sage dont parle Ibn-Bâdja dans son traité [[t:2]]du Régime du solitaire[[t:3]]. Voy. mes [[t:4]]Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe[[t:5]], p. 388 et suiv.

### fn.2325 (Footnote)
**English:** The author wishes to justify his citation of the passage from Ezekiel in support of this 5<sup>th</sup> category, where the prophet hears a voice without seeing any figure. He says, therefore, that it must not be believed that what was speaking to Ezekiel was that sublime apparition of which he speaks in ch. I, v. 26, and whose surroundings he describes with so much detail; it was, on the contrary, an invisible being speaking to him to give him the mission set forth in ch. II, v. 3 et seq.
**Correction:** The author wishes to justify his citation of the passage from Ezekiel in support of this 5<sup>th</sup> category, where the prophet hears a voice without seeing any figure. He says, therefore, that it must not be believed that what was speaking to Ezekiel was that sublime apparition of which he speaks in ch. I, v. 26, and whose surroundings he describes with so much detail; it was, on the contrary, an invisible being speaking to him to give him the mission set forth in ch. II, v. 3 et seq.

**French (Source):** L’auteur veut justifier la citation qu’il fait du passage d’Ézéchiel à l’appui de cette 5[[t:0]]e[[t:1]] catégorie, où le prophète entend une voix sans voir aucune figure. Il dit donc qu’il ne faut pas croire que ce qui parlait à Ézéchiel, c’était cette sublime apparition dont il parle au ch. I, v. 26, et dont il décrit l’entourage avec tant de détails; ce fut, au contraire, un être invisible qui lui parlait pour lui donner la mission exposée au ch. II, v. 3 et suiv.

### fn.2403 (Footnote)
**English:** For, as the author stated above (ch. XLI, p. 314): "Whenever a passage (of Scripture) says of someone that an angel spoke to him, or that the word of God was addressed to him, this could not have taken place except in a dream or in a prophetic vision." The author here alludes to several passages where one finds the formula <span dir="rtl">ויאמר יי׳ אלי</span>, <i>and the Eternal said to me,</i> and where either actions, movements, or conversations with God are attributed to the prophet. See, e.g.: Jeremiah, ch. 1, vv. 7, 9, 12 and 14; ch. XIII, vv. 6 et seq.; Ezekiel, ch. XLIV. vv. 1 et seq.; Zechariah, ch. 11, vv. 13 et seq.
**Correction:** For, as the author stated above (ch. XLI, p. 314): "Whenever a passage (of Scripture) says of someone that an angel spoke to him, or that the word of God was addressed to him, this could not have taken place except in a dream or in a prophetic vision." The author here alludes to several passages where one finds the formula <span dir="rtl">ויאמר יי׳ אלי</span>, <i>and the Eternal said to me,</i> and where either actions, movements, or conversations with God are attributed to the prophet. See, e.g.: Jeremiah, ch. 1, vv. 7, 9, 12 and 14; ch. XIII, vv. 6 et seq.; Ezekiel, ch. XLIV. vv. 1 et seq.; Zechariah, ch. 11, vv. 13 et seq.

**French (Source):** Car, comme l’auteur l’a dit plus haut (chap. XLI, p. 314): «Toutes les fois qu’un passage (de l’Écriture) dit de quelqu’un qu’un ange lui parla, ou que la parole de Dieu lui fut adressée, cela n’a pu avoir lieu autrement que dans un songe ou dans une vision prophétique.» L’auteur fait ici allusion à plusieurs passages où l’on trouve la formule [[t:0]]ויאמר י״י אלי[[t:1]], [[t:2]]et l’Éternel me dit,[[t:3]] et où l’on attribue au prophète soit des actions, soit des déplacements, soit des entretiens avec Dieu. Voy. p. ex.: Jérémie, chap. 1, v. 7, 9, 12 et 14; chap. XIII, v. 6 et suiv.; Ézéchiel, chap. XLIV. v. 1 et suiv.; Zacharie, chap. 11, v. 13 et suiv.

### fn.2558 (Footnote)
**English:** As seen above, p. 23, note 2, the human figure represents, according to the commentators, the first of the separate Intelligences or that of the ninth sphere; this Intelligence, although emanated from the absolutely simple being, is nonetheless composite in that it perceives at once the first cause, or God, and itself. It would therefore be this double perception that the prophet indicated by the <i>divided</i> human figure. The theory to which, according to the commentators, Maimonides alludes, belongs to Ibn Sina. See my <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, p. 360.
**Correction:** As seen above, p. 23, note 2, the human figure represents, according to the commentators, the first of the separate Intelligences or that of the ninth sphere; this Intelligence, although emanated from the absolutely simple being, is nonetheless composite in that it perceives at once the first cause, or God, and itself. It would therefore be this double perception that the prophet indicated by the <i>divided</i> human figure. The theory to which, according to the commentators, Maimonides alludes, belongs to Ibn Sina. See my <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, p. 360.

**French (Source):** Ainsi qu’on l’a vu plus haut, p. 23, note 2, la figure humaine représente, selon les commentateurs, la première des Intelligences séparées ou celle de la neuvième sphère; cette Intelligence, quoique émanée de l’être absolument simple, est pourtant composée en ce qu’elle perçoit à la fois la cause première, ou Dieu, et soi-même. Ce serait donc cette double perception que le prophète aurait indiquée par la figure humaine [[t:0]]partagée.[[t:1]] La théorie à laquelle, selon les commentateurs, Maïmonide ferait allusion, appartient à Ibn-Siná. Voy. mes [[t:2]]Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe[[t:3]], p. 360.

---

### fn.2560 (Footnote)
**English:** According to the commentators, Maimonides indicated by these last words that the perception of the <i>’hayyôth</i>, or the study relative to the celestial spheres and the stars, being within the domain of the mathematical sciences, must in the order of studies precede the perception of the <i>ophannîm</i> (elements), or the study of physics. See Vol. I, chap. <small>XXXIV</small>, p. 123: “It is therefore necessary that he who wishes to attain human perception should be instructed in logic, then gradually in mathematics, then in the physical sciences, and after that in metaphysics.” Cf. <i>ibidem</i>, p. 13, note 2, and <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, p. 357. — Abravanel correctly observes that by accepting the explanation Maimonides gives for the whole of Ezekiel’s vision, it would have been much more rational to begin with the <i>ophannîm</i> or elements, and to ascend from there to the <i>’hayyôth</i> or celestial spheres in order then to reach the Intelligences of the spheres, or else to follow the reverse order. See Abravanel's second preface to the Book of Ezekiel, second method, fourth objection.
**Correction:** According to the commentators, Maimonides indicated by these last words that the perception of the <i>’hayyôth</i>, or the study relative to the celestial spheres and the stars, being within the domain of the mathematical sciences, must in the order of studies precede the perception of the <i>ophannîm</i> (elements), or the study of physics. See Vol. I, chap. <small>XXXIV</small>, p. 123: “It is therefore necessary that he who wishes to attain human perception should be instructed in logic, then gradually in mathematics, then in the physical sciences, and after that in metaphysics.” Cf. <i>ibidem</i>, p. 13, note 2, and <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, p. 357. — Abravanel correctly observes that by accepting the explanation Maimonides gives for the whole of Ezekiel’s vision, it would have been much more rational to begin with the <i>ophannîm</i> or elements, and to ascend from there to the <i>’hayyôth</i> or celestial spheres in order then to reach the Intelligences of the spheres, or else to follow the reverse order. See Abravanel's second preface to the Book of Ezekiel, second method, fourth objection.

**French (Source):** Selon les commentateurs, Maïmonide aurait indiqué par ces derniers mots que la perception des [[t:0]]’hayyôth[[t:1]], ou l’étude relative aux sphères célestes et aux astres, étant du domaine des sciences mathématiques, doit dans l’ordre des études précéder la perception des [[t:2]]ophannîm[[t:3]] (éléments), ou l’étude de la physique. Voy. t. I, chap. [[t:4]]XXXIV[[t:5]], p. 123: «Il faut donc nécessairement que celui qui veut obtenir la perception humaine s’instruise dans la logique, ensuite graduellement dans les mathématiques, ensuite dans les sciences physiques, et après cela dans la métaphysique.» Cf. [[t:6]]ibidem[[t:7]], p. 13, note 2, et [[t:8]]Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe[[t:9]], p. 357. — Abravanel fait observer avec raison qu’en admettant l’explication que Maïmonide donne de l’ensemble de la vision d’Ézéchiel, il eût été bien plus rationnel de commencer par les [[t:10]]ophannîm[[t:11]] ou éléments, et de remonter de là aux [[t:12]]’hayyôth[[t:13]] ou sphères célestes pour arriver ensuite aux Intelligences des sphères, ou bien de suivre l’ordre inverse. Voy. la seconde préface d’Abravanel sur le livre d’Ézéchiel, deuxième méthode, quatrième objection.

### fn.2583 (Footnote)
**English:** This obscure passage was explained by Moses of Narbonne, and after him by Ephodi and Shem-Tov, in a manner far too elaborate, and I do not believe these commentators have grasped Maimonides' thought. According to them, the author intended to say that Ezekiel here compares the disposition of the prophet's soul to receive inspiration emanating from the <i>glory of the Eternal</i>, or from the separate Intelligence, to the disposition of a cloud laden with rain to receive the impression of the sun's rays, which produce the rainbow. This is what the author supposedly intended to indicate by speaking of an extraordinary or highly remarkable allegory and adding the words: <span dir="rtl">והוא בלא ספק בכח נבואה</span>, as the version of Ibn-Tibbon expresses it. But evidently, the author means by these words that the comparison in question could only have come from the imagination of a prophet, strengthened by inspiration. Furthermore, as Abravanel observes (Commentary on Ezekiel, I, 28), Ezekiel’s purpose is only to recount the vision he had, and this allegory concerning prophetic inspiration would be an entirely misplaced digression here. Moreover, the <i>glory of the Eternal</i> here indicates something objective that the prophet believed he saw, and could not be an image to designate prophetic inspiration. Finally, one would not see, according to this explanation, what the author intended to say by these words: <i>the matter of the rainbow, its reality, and its quiddity are known;</i> these words, certainly, must have their significance. —We do not claim to have grasped the true sense of this passage; but we propose the following explanation, which seems at least simpler than that of the commentators. According to Maimonides, the splendor surrounding the <i>human figure</i> (see above, p. 23, note 2) appeared to the prophet like the rainbow, in which we see only the <i>reverberation</i> of light, refracted and disturbed by humidity. We know the matter or substratum of the rainbow, which is the rain cloud. We also know its quiddity or form, which is light. But we do not understand the rainbow as it appears to us, that is, the colors of which it is composed; for, according to Ibn-Sinâ, we do not know its causes (see my <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, p. 317, and Shahrastânî, p. 413). Similarly, we can only perceive a pale reflection of the splendor surrounding the first of the Intelligences, represented by the <i>human figure</i>, and it is this reflection that the prophet represents as the <i>likeness</i> of the glory of the Eternal (that is, according to the explanation the author gave earlier for the word <i>likeness</i>, as something vague and doubtful). This comparison seems to Maimonides so extraordinary that he can only see in it the product of prophetic inspiration.
**Correction:** This obscure passage was explained by Moses of Narbonne, and after him by Ephodi and Shem-Tov, in a manner far too elaborate, and I do not believe these commentators have grasped Maimonides' thought. According to them, the author intended to say that Ezekiel here compares the disposition of the prophet's soul to receive inspiration emanating from the <i>glory of the Eternal</i>, or from the separate Intelligence, to the disposition of a cloud laden with rain to receive the impression of the sun's rays, which produce the rainbow. This is what the author supposedly intended to indicate by speaking of an extraordinary or highly remarkable allegory and adding the words: <span dir="rtl">והוא בלא ספק בכח נבואה</span>, as the version of Ibn-Tibbon expresses it. But evidently, the author means by these words that the comparison in question could only have come from the imagination of a prophet, strengthened by inspiration. Furthermore, as Abravanel observes (Commentary on Ezekiel, I, 28), Ezekiel’s purpose is only to recount the vision he had, and this allegory concerning prophetic inspiration would be an entirely misplaced digression here. Moreover, the <i>glory of the Eternal</i> here indicates something objective that the prophet believed he saw, and could not be an image to designate prophetic inspiration. Finally, one would not see, according to this explanation, what the author intended to say by these words: <i>the matter of the rainbow, its reality, and its quiddity are known;</i> these words, certainly, must have their significance. —We do not claim to have grasped the true sense of this passage; but we propose the following explanation, which seems at least simpler than that of the commentators. According to Maimonides, the splendor surrounding the <i>human figure</i> (see above, p. 23, note 2) appeared to the prophet like the rainbow, in which we see only the <i>reverberation</i> of light, refracted and disturbed by humidity. We know the matter or substratum of the rainbow, which is the rain cloud. We also know its quiddity or form, which is light. But we do not understand the rainbow as it appears to us, that is, the colors of which it is composed; for, according to Ibn-Sinâ, we do not know its causes (see my <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, p. 317, and Shahrastânî, p. 413). Similarly, we can only perceive a pale reflection of the splendor surrounding the first of the Intelligences, represented by the <i>human figure</i>, and it is this reflection that the prophet represents as the <i>likeness</i> of the glory of the Eternal (that is, according to the explanation the author gave earlier for the word <i>likeness</i>, as something vague and doubtful). This comparison seems to Maimonides so extraordinary that he can only see in it the product of prophetic inspiration.

**French (Source):** Ce passage obscur a été expliqué par Moïse de Narbonne, et, d’après lui, par Éphôdi et Schem-Tob, d’une manière beaucoup trop recherchée, et je ne crois pas que ces commentateurs aient deviné la pensée de Maïmonide. Selon eux, l’auteur aurait voulu dire qu’Ézéchiel compare ici la disposition de l’âme du prophète pour recevoir l’inspiration émanant de la [[t:0]]gloire de l’Éternel[[t:1]], ou de l’Intelligence séparée, à la disposition d’un nuage gros de pluie pour recevoir l’impression des rayons du soleil, qui produisent l’arc-en-ciel. C’est là ce que l’auteur aurait voulu indiquer en parlant d’une allégorie extraordinaire ou très-remarquable et en ajoutant les mots: [[t:2]]והוא בלא ספק בכח נבואה[[t:3]], comme s’exprime la version d’Ibn-Tibbon. Mais évidemment l’auteur veut dire par ces mots que la comparaison dont il s’agit n’a pu sortir que de l’imagination d’un prophète, fortifiée par l’inspiration. En outre, comme le fait observer Abravanel (Commentaire sur Ézéchiel, I, 28), Ézéchiel n’a pour but que de retracer la vision qu’il a eue, et cette allégorie sur l’inspiration prophétique serait ici un hors-d’œuvre entièrement déplacé. D’ailleurs, la [[t:4]]gloire de l’Éternel[[t:5]] indique ici quelque chose d’objectif que le prophète a cru voir, et ne saurait être une image pour désigner l’inspiration prophétique. Enfin on ne verrait pas, d’après cette explication, ce que l’auteur a voulu dire par ces mots: [[t:6]]on connaît la matière de l’arc-en-ciel, sa réalité et sa quiddité;[[t:7]] ces mots, certes, doivent avoir leur portée. —Nous ne prétendons pas avoir deviné le véritable sens de ce passage; mais nous proposerons l’explication suivante, qui nous paraît du moins plus simple que celle des commentateurs. Selon Maïmonide, la splendeur qui entourait la [[t:8]]figure humaine[[t:9]] (voy. ci-dessus, p. 23, note 2) parut au prophète comme l’arc-en-ciel, dans lequel nous ne voyons que la [[t:10]]réverbération[[t:11]] de la lumière, brisée et troublée par l’humidité. Nous connaissons la matière ou le substratum de l’arc-en-ciel, c’est le nuage de pluie. Nous en connaissons également la quiddité ou la forme, c’est la lumière. Mais nous ne nous rendons pas compte de l’arc-en-ciel tel qu’il nous apparaît, c’est-à-dire des couleurs dont il se compose; car, selon Ibn-Sinâ, nous n’en connaissons pas les causes (voy. mes [[t:12]]Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe[[t:13]], p. 317, et Schahrestâni, p. 413). De même, nous ne pouvons apercevoir qu’un pâle reflet de la splendeur qui environne la première des Intelligences, représentée par la [[t:14]]figure humaine[[t:15]], et c’est ce reflet que le prophète représente comme la [[t:16]]ressemblance[[t:17]] de la gloire de l’Éternel (c’est-à-dire, suivant l’explication que l’auteur a donnée plus haut du mot [[t:18]]ressemblance[[t:19]], comme quelque chose de vague et de douteux). Cette comparaison paraît à Maïmonide tellement extraordinaire qu’il ne peut y voir que le produit de l’inspiration prophétique.

---

**English:** The word <span dir="rtl">שפכה</span>, which is found in Deuteronomy, Chap. XXIII, <i>v.</i> 2, comes from the verb <span dir="rtl">שפך</span>, <i>to pour</i>, and signifies: "Membrum per quod urina aut semen effunditur."
**Correction:** The word <span dir="rtl">שפכה</span>, which is found in Deuteronomy, Chap. XXIII, <i>v.</i> 2, comes from the verb <span dir="rtl">שפך</span>, <i>to pour</i>, and signifies: "Membrum per quod urina aut semen effunditur."

**French (Source):** Le mot [[t:0]]שפכה[[t:1]], qui se trouve dans le Deutéronome, chap. XXIII, [[t:2]]v.[[t:3]] 2, vient du verbe [[t:4]]שפך[[t:5]], [[t:6]]verser[[t:7]], et signifie: «Membrum per quod urina aut semen effunditur.»

### fn.2644 (Footnote)
**English:** That is, the negation or absence of positive qualities. See Volume I, ch. <small>LXXIII</small>, seventh proposition of the Mutakallimūn (p. 395 et seq.). Regarding the meaning of the word <span dir="rtl">מלכה̈</span>, <i>capacity</i>, see <i>ibid.</i>, p. 195, notes 1 and 2.
**Correction:** That is, the negation or absence of positive qualities. See Volume I, ch. <small>LXXIII</small>, seventh proposition of the Mutakallimūn (p. 395 et seq.). Regarding the meaning of the word <span dir="rtl">מלכה̈</span>, <i>capacity</i>, see <i>ibid.</i>, p. 195, notes 1 and 2.

**French (Source):** C’est-à-dire, la négation ou l’absence des qualités positives.Voy. le tome I, chap. [[t:0]]LXXIII[[t:1]], septième proposition des Motécallemîn (p. 395 et suiv.). Sur le sens du mot [[t:2]]מלכה̈[[t:3]], [[t:4]]capacité[[t:5]], voy. [[t:6]]ibidem[[t:7]], p. 195, notes 1 et 2.

### fn.2647 (Footnote)
**English:** That is to say: Since <i>privations</i> are for them positive qualities and they recognize no other non-being than absolute non-being, they could say, in an absolute sense, that non-being does not require an agent; for <i>non-being</i>, as they understand it, is nothingness, or that which has never existed and will never exist, and only the creative act requires an agent.
**Correction:** That is to say: Since <i>privations</i> are for them positive qualities and they recognize no other non-being than absolute non-being, they could say, in an absolute sense, that non-being does not require an agent; for <i>non-being</i>, as they understand it, is nothingness, or that which has never existed and will never exist, and only the creative act requires an agent.

**French (Source):** C’est-à-dire: Comme les [[t:0]]privations[[t:1]] sont pour eux des qualités positives et qu’ils ne reconnaissent d’autre non-être que le non-être absolu, ils ont pu dire, dans un sens absolu, que le non-être n’a pas besoin d’agent; car le [[t:2]]non-être[[t:3]], tel qu’ils l’entendent, c’est le néant, ou ce qui n’a jamais existé et n’existera jamais, et il n’y a que l’acte créateur qui ait besoin d’un agent.

---

### fn.2649 (Footnote)
**English:** That is: their proposition stating that non-being, or privation, does not require an agent, does not prevent them from saying that it is God who makes one blind or deaf; for, according to their principle, blindness and deafness are not <i>privations</i> or negations of sight or hearing, but <i>positive</i> qualities that God creates in man.
**Correction:** That is: their proposition stating that non-being, or privation, does not require an agent, does not prevent them from saying that it is God who makes one blind or deaf; for, according to their principle, blindness and deafness are not <i>privations</i> or negations of sight or hearing, but <i>positive</i> qualities that God creates in man.

**French (Source):** C’est-à-dire: leur proposition qui énonce que le non-être, ou la privation, n’a pas besoin d’agent, ne les empêche pas de dire que c’est Dieu qui rend aveugle ou sourd; car, selon leur principe, la cécité et la surdité ne sont pas les [[t:0]]privations[[t:1]] ou négations de la vue ou de l’ouïe, mais des qualités [[t:2]]positives[[t:3]] que Dieu crée dans l’homme.

---

### fn.2673 (Footnote)
**English:** This refers to the famous physician known in the Middle Ages by the name <i>Rhazes;</i> his Arabic name was: Abu Bakr Muhammad ibn Zakariya <i>al-Razi;</i> he was one of the principal physicians of the Abbasid caliph Al-Muqtadir, made himself known through a prodigious number of writings on medicine, mathematics, and philosophy, and died in 320 of the Hijra (932) or, according to others, in 311 (923). On his life and writings, see d’Herbelot, <i>Bibliothèque orientale</i>, folio edition, p. 713; Casiri, <i>Biblioth. arab. hisp.</i>, Vol. I, p. 262 et seq.; Wüstenfeld, <i>Geschichte der Arabischen Ärzte</i>, p. 40 et seq. His value as a philosopher was not very great, and his philosophical writings are almost never cited as an authority. One sees from our passage that Maimonides had a very low opinion of the work on theology or metaphysics composed by al-Razi. In his letter to Rabbi Samuel Ibn Tibbon, our author likewise speaks with much disdain of this work: <span dir="rtl">וספר חכמה אלהית שחבר אלראזי אין בו תועלת לפי שהאלראזי היה רופא בלבד</span> "The book of metaphysics composed by al-Razi is of no use, for al-Razi was only a physician." See <i>Letters of Maimonides</i>, Amsterdam edition, fol. 14 <i>b</i>. — A Spanish Muslim author, Qadi Sa'id, cited by Ibn Abi Usaibia, passes the same judgment on al-Razi as Maimonides. This is how he expresses himself concerning this physician:
**Correction:** This refers to the famous physician known in the Middle Ages by the name <i>Rhazes;</i> his Arabic name was: Abu Bakr Muhammad ibn Zakariya <i>al-Razi;</i> he was one of the principal physicians of the Abbasid caliph Al-Muqtadir, made himself known through a prodigious number of writings on medicine, mathematics, and philosophy, and died in 320 of the Hijra (932) or, according to others, in 311 (923). On his life and writings, see d’Herbelot, <i>Bibliothèque orientale</i>, folio edition, p. 713; Casiri, <i>Biblioth. arab. hisp.</i>, Vol. I, p. 262 et seq.; Wüstenfeld, <i>Geschichte der Arabischen Ärzte</i>, p. 40 et seq. His value as a philosopher was not very great, and his philosophical writings are almost never cited as an authority. One sees from our passage that Maimonides had a very low opinion of the work on theology or metaphysics composed by al-Razi. In his letter to Rabbi Samuel Ibn Tibbon, our author likewise speaks with much disdain of this work: <span dir="rtl">וספר חכמה אלהית שחבר אלראזי אין בו תועלת לפי שהאלראזי היה רופא בלבד</span> "The book of metaphysics composed by al-Razi is of no use, for al-Razi was only a physician." See <i>Letters of Maimonides</i>, Amsterdam edition, fol. 14 <i>b</i>. — A Spanish Muslim author, Qadi Sa'id, cited by Ibn Abi Usaibia, passes the same judgment on al-Razi as Maimonides. **French (Source):** Il s’agit ici du célèbre médecin connu au moyen âge sous le nom de [[t:0]]Rhasès;[[t:1]] son nom arabe était: Abou-Becr Mo’hammed ben-Zacariyya [[t:2]]al-Râzi;[[t:3]] il fut un des principaux médecins du khalife Abbaside Al-Moktadir, se fit connaître par un nombre prodigieux d’écrits de médecine, de mathématiques et de philosophie, et mourut en 320 de l’hégire (932) ou, selon d’autres, en 311 (923). Sur sa vie et ses écrits, voy. d’Herbelot, [[t:4]]Bibliothèque orientale[[t:5]], édit. in-fol., p. 713; Casiri, [[t:6]]Biblioth. arab. hisp.[[t:7]], t. I, p. 262 et suiv.; Wüstenfeld, [[t:8]]Geschichte der Arabischen Ærzte[[t:9]], p. 40 et suiv. Sa valeur comme philosophe ne fut pas bien grande, et ses écrits philosophiques ne sont presque jamais cités comme autorité. On voit, par notre passage, que Maïmonide faisait bien peu de cas de l’ouvrage de théologie ou de métaphysique composé par Al-Râzi. Dans sa lettre à rabbi Samuel Ibn-Tibbon, notre auteur parle également avec beaucoup de dédain de cet ouvrage: [[t:10]]וספר חכמה אלהית שחבר אלראזי אין בו תועלת לפי שאלראזי היה רופא בלבד[[t:11]] «Le livre de métaphysique composé par Al-Râzi n’a pas d’utilité, car Al-Râzi était seulement médecin.» Voy. [[t:12]]Lettres de Maïmonide[[t:13]], édit. d’Amsterdam, fol. 14 [[t:14]]b[[t:15]]. — Un auteur espagnol musulman, le Kadhi Çâ’id, cité par Ibn-Abi-Océibi’a, porte d’Al-Râzi le même jugement que Maïmonide. Voici comment il s’exprime sur ce médecin:

### fn.2687 (Footnote)
**English:** This rather obscure passage has not, I believe, been fully understood by Ephodi, the only commentator to address it, nor by Buxtorf, who reproduced his explanation. Mr. Scheyer’s translation is also lacking. Here is what seems to me to be the simplest meaning of this passage: He who, while being of flesh and bone—that is to say, a simple mortal—nevertheless wishes to be shielded from external impressions that produce evils, that person, I say, wishes to unite within himself two opposite things; for he wishes to be simultaneously a mortal, subject to impressions, and a non-impressionable being, like the celestial bodies. Obviously, if he were, like the latter, shielded from all external impression, he would not be a being subject to <i>contingence;</i> on the contrary, he would be like those celestial bodies, which are neither <i>born</i> nor perishable, and like them, he would be unique in his species and not simply one of the individuals of the same species.
**Correction:** This rather obscure passage has not, I believe, been fully understood by Ephodi, the only commentator to address it, nor by Buxtorf, who reproduced his explanation. Mr. Scheyer’s translation is also lacking. Here is what seems to me to be the simplest meaning of this passage: He who, while being of flesh and bone—that is to say, a simple mortal—nevertheless wishes to be shielded from external impressions that produce evils, that person, I say, wishes to unite within himself two opposite things; for he wishes to be simultaneously a mortal, subject to impressions, and a non-impressionable being, like the celestial bodies. Obviously, if he were, like the latter, shielded from all external impression, he would not be a being subject to <i>contingence;</i> on the contrary, he would be like those celestial bodies, which are neither <i>born</i> nor perishable, and like them, he would be unique in his species and not simply one of the individuals of the same species.

**French (Source):** Ce passage assez obscur n’a pas été, je crois, entièrement compris par Éphôdi, le seul commentateur qui s’en occupe, ni par Buxtorf, qui a reproduit son explication. La traduction de M. Scheyer laisse également à désirer. Voici quel me paraît être le sens le plus simple de ce passage: Celui qui, tout en étant de chair et d’os, c’est-à-dire un simple mortel, veut néanmoins être à l’abri des impressions du dehors qui produisent les maux, celui-là, dis-je, veut réunir en lui deux choses opposées; car il veut à la fois être un mortel, sujet aux impressions, et un être non impressionnable, comme les corps célestes. Évidemment, s’il était comme ces derniers à l’abri de toute impression du dehors, il ne serait pas un être soumis à la [[t:0]]contingence;[[t:1]] au contraire il serait, comme ces corps célestes, qui ne sont ni [[t:2]]nés[[t:3]] ni périssables, et comme ceux-ci il serait seul de son espèce et ne serait pas simplement un des individus d’une même espèce.

---

### fn.2790 (Footnote)
**English:** The word <span dir="rtl">الاعيان</span>, which means <i>chiefs</i> or <i>principals</i>, is here evidently used by Maimonides to designate the two primary things that constitute the body, namely substance and accidents. However, according to the <i>Kitāb al-Taʿrīfāt</i>, this word specifically designates substances to the exclusion of accidents. The following is read there, according to the translation by Silvestre de Sacy (Notices et Extraits des mss., vol. X, pp. 64-65): "<i>Aʿyān</i>, that is to say, substances. These are things that subsist by themselves. When we say <i>that subsist by themselves</i>, it means that they occupy a space by themselves, without their existence in a space depending on the concomitant existence of another thing. This is the opposite of accidents, whose existence in a space depends on the concomitant existence of the substance that serves as their support, that is, which is the link by which they are sustained." This definition is essentially the same as that given, with further development, in the great Arabic dictionary of technical terms published in Calcutta. See B<small>IBLIOTHECA</small> I<small>NDICA</small>, <i>A Dictionary of the technical terms used in the sciences of the Musulmans</i>, p. 1073.
**Correction:** The word <span dir="rtl">الاعيان</span>, which means <i>chiefs</i> or <i>principals</i>, is here evidently used by Maimonides to designate the two primary things that constitute the body, namely substance and accidents. However, according to the <i>Kitāb al-Taʿrīfāt</i>, this word specifically designates substances to the exclusion of accidents. The following is read there, according to the translation by Silvestre de Sacy (Notices et Extraits des mss., vol. X, pp. 64-65): "<i>Aʿyān</i>, that is to say, substances. These are things that subsist by themselves. When we say <i>that subsist by themselves</i>, it means that they occupy a space by themselves, without their existence in a space depending on the concomitant existence of another thing. This is the opposite of accidents, whose existence in a space depends on the concomitant existence of the substance that serves as their support, that is, which is the link by which they are sustained." This definition is essentially the same as that given, with further development, in the great Arabic dictionary of technical terms published in Calcutta. See B<small>IBLIOTHECA</small> I<small>NDICA</small>, <i>A Dictionary of the technical terms used in the sciences of the Musulmans</i>, p. 1073.

**French (Source):** Le mot [[t:0]]الاعيان[[t:1]] , qui signifie les [[t:2]]chefs[[t:3]] ou les [[t:4]]principaux[[t:5]], est ici évidemment employé par Maïmonide pour désigner les deux choses principales qui constituent le corps, à savoir la substance et les accidents. Cependant, selon le [[t:6]]Kitâb al-Ta’rifât[[t:7]], ce mot désignerait particulièrement les substances à l’exclusion des accidents. Voici ce qu’on y lit, selon la traduction de Silvestre de Sacy (Notices et Extraits des mss., t. X, p. 64-65): «[[t:8]]A’yân[[t:9]], c’est-à-dire les substances. Ce sont les choses qui se soutiennent par elles-mêmes. Quand nous disons [[t:10]]qui se soutiennent par elles-mêmes[[t:11]], cela veut dire qu’elles occupent un espace par elles-mêmes, sans que leur existence dans un espace dépende de l’existence concomitante d’une autre chose. C’est le contraire des accidents, dont l’existence dans un espace dépend de l’existence concomitante de la substance qui leur sert de support, c’est-à-dire qui est le lien par lequel ils sont soutenus.» Cette définition est en substance la même que celle que donne, avec plus de développement, le grand dictionnaire arabe des termes techniques publié à Calcutta. Voy. B[[t:12]]IBLIOTHECA[[t:13]] I[[t:14]]NDICA[[t:15]], [[t:16]]A Dictionary of the technical terms used in the sciences of the Musulmans[[t:17]], p. 1073.
