### fn.2859 (Footnote)
**English:** Among the few <i>Geonim</i> whose writings have reached us, we know of none who professed this strange doctrine; in the writings of Saadia, we find no trace of it. However, it was quite widespread, it appears, among the doctors of the Karaite sect, who followed the doctrines of the Mu'tazilites in all respects. Joseph ha-Roeh, called in Arabic Abu Ya'qub al-Basir, expounded the doctrine of <i>compensation</i> in his dogmatic treatise titled <span dir="rtl">ספר נעימות</span> (cf. on this work, <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, pp. 476–477). Chapter <small>XXIII</small> of this work is titled: <span dir="rtl">בתמור ואשר ידבק בו</span>; the word <span dir="rtl">תמור</span> is the established term by which the Karaite translators rendered the term <span dir="rtl">عوض</span>, <i>compensation</i>. We cite the beginning of this chapter: <span dir="rtl">דע כי בארנו כי הקדמון ית׳ לא יבחר הרע וכי הוא לא ימנע החוב ואם יכאיב החוב והבהמות והטפים והם ԍינם מחוייבים כאשר נבאר בלא ספק מלתת להם תמור שיוציא עמו הכאב מלהיותו חמס</span>. ‘It must be known, as we have explained (chap. 22), that God does not take pleasure in evil and that He does not refuse what is due. If He causes suffering to wild beasts, domestic animals, and children, without their being guilty, as we shall explain, He must undoubtedly grant them a compensation, by which He ensures that the suffering is not an injustice.’ The same subject is treated in chap. <small>XXVII</small> of the <span dir="rtl">מחכימת פתי</span>, an extract from the preceding work. These two works are now found in the Imperial Library, Hebrew supplement ms., no. 127. Aaron ben Elijah (<span dir="rtl">עץ חיים</span>, p. 127) also states that great doctors in Israel adopted this doctrine which reason rejects: <span dir="rtl">ואלו דברים לא ישוערו בשכל וגדולי חכמי ישראל נטו בזה</span>. Here, as elsewhere, some Rabbinite doctors among the <i>Geonim</i> followed the example of the Karaites. Cf. Vol. I, chap. <small>LXXI</small>, pp. 336–337.
**Correction:** Among the few <i>Geonim</i> whose writings have reached us, we know of none who professed this strange doctrine; in the writings of Saadia, we find no trace of it. However, it was quite widespread, it appears, among the doctors of the Karaite sect, who followed the doctrines of the Mu'tazilites in all respects. Joseph ha-Roeh, called in Arabic Abu Ya'qub al-Basir, expounded the doctrine of <i>compensation</i> in his dogmatic treatise titled <span dir="rtl">ספר נעימות</span> (cf. on this work, <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, pp. 476–477). Chapter <small>XXIII</small> of this work is titled: <span dir="rtl">בתמור ואשר ידבק בו</span>; the word <span dir="rtl">תמור</span> is the established term by which the Karaite translators rendered the term <span dir="rtl">عوض</span>, <i>compensation</i>. We cite the beginning of this chapter: <span dir="rtl">דע כי בארנו כי הקדמון ית׳ לא יבחר הרע וכי הוא לא ימנע החוב ואם יכאיב החוב והבהמות והטפים והם ԍינם מחוייבים כאשר נבאר בלא ספק מלתת להם תמור שיוציא עמו הכאב מלהיותו חמס</span>. ‘It must be known, as we have explained (chap. 22), that God does not take pleasure in evil and that He does not refuse what is due. If He causes suffering to wild beasts, domestic animals, and children, without their being guilty, as we shall explain, He must undoubtedly grant them a compensation, by which He ensures that the suffering is not an injustice.’ The same subject is treated in chap. <small>XXVII</small> of the <span dir="rtl">מחכימת פתי</span>, an extract from the preceding work. These two works are now found in the Imperial Library, Hebrew supplement ms., no. 127. Aaron ben Elijah (<span dir="rtl">עץ חיים</span>, p. 127) also states that great doctors in Israel adopted this doctrine which reason rejects: <span dir="rtl">ואלו דברים לא ישוערו בשכל וגדולי חכמי ישראל נטו בזה</span>. Here, as elsewhere, some Rabbinite doctors among the <i>Geonim</i> followed the example of the Karaites. Cf. Vol. I, chap. <small>LXXI</small>, pp. 336–337.

**French (Source):** Parmi le petit nombre de [[t:0]]Guéônim[[t:1]] dont les écrits nous sont parvenus, nous n’en connaissons aucun qui ait professé cette doctrine étrange; dans les écrits de Saadia, nous n’en trouvons aucune trace. Mais elle était assez répandue, à ce qu’il paraît, parmi les docteurs de la secte des Karaïtes, qui suivirent sous tous les rapports les doctrines des Mo’tazales. Joseph ha-Roëh, appelé en arabe Abou-Ya’koub al-Bacîr, a exposé la doctrine de la [[t:2]]compensation[[t:3]] dans son traité de dogmatique intitulé [[t:4]]ספר נעימות[[t:5]] (cf. sur cet ouvrage, [[t:6]]Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe[[t:7]], p. 476-477). Le chapitre [[t:8]]XXIII[[t:9]] de cet ouvrage est intitulé: [[t:10]]בתמור ואשר ידבק בו[[t:11]]; le mot [[t:12]]תמור[[t:13]] est le terme consacré par lequel les traducteurs karaïtes ont rendu le terme [[t:14]]عوض[[t:15]] , [[t:16]]compensation[[t:17]]. Nous citons le commencement de ce chapitre: [[t:18]]דע כי בארנו כי הקדמון ית׳ לא יבחר הרע וכי הוא לא ימנע החוב ואם יכאיב החיות והבהמות והטפים והם אינם מחוייבים כאשר נבאר בלא ספק מלתת להם תמור שיוציא עמו הכאב מלהיותו חמס[[t:19]]. «Il faut savoir, comme nous l’avons exposé (chap. 22), que Dieu ne se complaît pas au mal et qu’il ne refuse point ce qui est dû. S’il fait souffrir les bêtes sauvages; les animaux domestiques et les enfants, sans que ceux-ci soient coupables, comme nous l’exposerons, il doit indubitablement leur accorder une compensation, par laquelle il fait que la souffrance ne soit pas une injustice.» Le même sujet est traité au chap. [[t:20]]XXVII[[t:21]] du [[t:22]]מחכימת פתי[[t:23]], extrait de l’ouvrage précédent. Ces deux ouvrages se trouvent maintenant à la Bibliothèque impériale, ms. du suppl. hébreu, n° 127. Ahron ben Élie ([[t:24]]עץ חיים[[t:25]], p. 127) dit également que de grands docteurs en Israël ont adopté cette doctrine que la raison réprouve: [[t:26]]ואלו דברים לא ישוערו בשכל וגדולי חכמי ישראל נטו בזה[[t:27]]. Ici, comme ailleurs, quelques docteurs rabbanites parmi les [[t:28]]Guéônim[[t:29]] ont suivi l’exemple des Karaïtes. Cf. t. I, chap. [[t:30]]LXXI[[t:31]], p. 336-337.

---

### fn.2862 (Footnote)
**English:** As can be seen, Maimonides recognizes particular Providence only with regard to individuals of the human species, and even then he applies a restriction, saying further on 'that only he to whom something of the <i>overflow</i> (of divine intelligence) adheres shall participate in Providence according to the measure by which he participates in intelligence.' See also further on, chap. <small>LI</small>, and cf. Levi ben Gershom, <i>Guerres du Seigneur</i>, book IV, chap. 7. This doctrine was bound to displease the orthodox rabbis; Christian theologians were also moved by it, and Saint Thomas felt it necessary to refute it in his <i>Summa Theologica</i>, Part I, quest. <small>XXII</small>, art. 2: ‘Quidam vero posuerunt incorruptibilia tantum providentiæ subjacere, corruptibilia vero non secundum individua, sed secundum species; sic enim incorruptibilia sunt.... A corruptibilium autem generalitate excepit <i>Rabbi Moyses</i> homines, propter splendorem intellectus quem participant. In aliis autem individuis corruptibilibus aliorum opinionem est secutus; sed necesse est omnia divinæ providentiæ subjacere, non in universali tantum, sed etiam in singulari. Quod sic patet, etc.’ However, St. Jerome had already expressed an opinion on Providence similar to that of Maimonides. See his commentary on Habakkuk, 1:14 (<i>S. Hieronymi opera</i>, ed. Martianay, Vol. III, col. 1600): ‘Cæterum absurdum est ad hoc Dei deducere majestatem ut sciat per momenta singula quot nascantur culices, quotve moriantur, quæ cimicium et pulicum et muscarum sit in terra multitudo, quanti pisces in aqua natent, et qui de minoribus majorum prædæ cedere debeant. Non simus tam fatui adulatores Dei, ut dum potentiam ejus etiam ad ima detrahimus, in nos ipsos injuriosi simus, eamdem rationabilium quam irrationabilium providentiam esse dicentes.’
**Correction:** As can be seen, Maimonides recognizes particular Providence only with regard to individuals of the human species, and even then he applies a restriction, saying further on 'that only he to whom something of the <i>overflow</i> (of divine intelligence) adheres shall participate in Providence according to the measure by which he participates in intelligence.' See also further on, chap. <small>LI</small>, and cf. Levi ben Gershom, <i>Guerres du Seigneur</i>, book IV, chap. 7. This doctrine was bound to displease the orthodox rabbis; Christian theologians were also moved by it, and Saint Thomas felt it necessary to refute it in his <i>Summa Theologica</i>, Part I, quest. <small>XXII</small>, art. 2: ‘Quidam vero posuerunt incorruptibilia tantum providentiæ subjacere, corruptibilia vero non secundum individua, sed secundum species; sic enim incorruptibilia sunt.... A corruptibilium autem generalitate excepit <i>Rabbi Moyses</i> homines, propter splendorem intellectus quem participant. In aliis autem individuis corruptibilibus aliorum opinionem est secutus; sed necesse est omnia divinæ providentiæ subjacere, non in universali tantum, sed etiam in singulari. Quod sic patet, etc.’ However, St. Jerome had already expressed an opinion on Providence similar to that of Maimonides. See his commentary on Habakkuk, 1:14 (<i>S. Hieronymi opera</i>, ed. Martianay, Vol. III, col. 1600): ‘Cæterum absurdum est ad hoc Dei deducere majestatem ut sciat per momenta singula quot nascantur culices, quotve moriantur, quæ cimicium et pulicum et muscarum sit in terra multitudo, quanti pisces in aqua natent, et qui de minoribus majorum prædæ cedere debeant. Non simus tam fatui adulatores Dei, ut dum potentiam ejus etiam ad ima detrahimus, in nos ipsos injuriosi simus, eamdem rationabilium quam irrationabilium providentiam esse dicentes.’

**French (Source):** Comme on voit, Maïmonide ne reconnaît la Providence particulière qu’à l’égard des individus de l’espèce humaine, et encore y porte-t-il une restriction, en disant plus loin «que celui-là seul auquel il s’attache quelque chose de l’[[t:0]]épanchement[[t:1]] (de l’intelligence divine), participera de la Providence suivant la mesure selon laquelle il participe de l’intelligence.» Voir aussi plus loin, chap. [[t:2]]LI[[t:3]], et cf. Lévi ben-Gerson, [[t:4]]Guerres du Seigneur[[t:5]], liv. IV, chap. 7. Cette doctrine devait nécessairement déplaire aux rabbins orthodoxes; les théologiens chrétiens s’en émurent également, et saint Thomas a cru devoir la réfuter dans sa [[t:6]]Somme de théologie[[t:7]], I[[t:8]]re[[t:9]] partie, quest. [[t:10]]XXII[[t:11]], art. 2: «Quidam vero posuerunt incorruptibilia tantum providentiæ subjacere, corruptibilia vero non secundum individua, sed secundum species; sic enim incorruptibilia sunt..… A corruptibilium autem generalitate excepit [[t:12]]Rabbi Moyses[[t:13]] homines, propter splendorem intellectus quem participant. In aliis autem individuis corruptibilibus aliorum opinionem est secutus; sed necesse est omnia divinæ providentiæ subjacere, non in universali tantum, sed etiam in singulari. Quod sic patet, etc.» Cependant S. Jérôme avait déjà professé sur la Providence une opinion semblable à celle de Maïmonide. Voy. son commentaire sur Habacuc, 1, 14 ([[t:14]]S. Hieronymi opera[[t:15]], éd. Martianay, t. III, col. 1600): «Cæterum absurdum est ad hoc Dei deducere majestatem ut sciat per momenta singula quot nascantur culices, quotve moriantur, quæ cimicium et pulicum et muscarum sit in terra multitudo, quanti pisces in aqua natent, et qui de minoribus majorum prædæ cedere debeant. Non simus tam fatui adulatores Dei, ut dum potentiam ejus etiam ad ima detrahimus, in nos ipsos injuriosi simus, eamdem rationabilium quam irrationabilium providentiam esse dicentes.»

### fn.2901 (Footnote)
**English:** The author here raises the following objection to believers: from what we have just said, two theses rejected by the philosophers would follow: 1. that divine knowledge has non-being as its object, since it knows what does not yet exist; 2. that it encompasses the infinite, since the individuals who do not yet exist, but who will exist in the future, are infinite. — All Arabic manuscripts have <span dir="rtl">אלאעדאם</span>, in the plural, <i>non-beings</i>, non-existent things. Ibn Tibbon has <span dir="rtl">ההעדר</span>, in the singular, which is inaccurate.
**Correction:** The author here raises the following objection to believers: from what we have just said, two theses rejected by the philosophers would follow: 1. that divine knowledge has non-being as its object, since it knows what does not yet exist; 2. that it encompasses the infinite, since the individuals who do not yet exist, but who will exist in the future, are infinite. — All Arabic manuscripts have <span dir="rtl">אלאעדאם</span>, in the plural, <i>non-beings</i>, non-existent things. Ibn Tibbon has <span dir="rtl">ההעדר</span>, in the singular, which is inaccurate.

**French (Source):** L’auteur fait ici aux croyants l’objection suivante: De ce que nous venons de dire, il s’ensuivrait deux thèses repoussées par les philosophes: 1° que la science divine a pour objet le non-être, puisqu’elle connaît ce qui n’existe pas encore; 2° qu’elle embrasse l’infini, puisque les individus qui n’existent pas encore, mais qui existeront dans l’avenir, sont infinis. — Tous les mss. arabes ont [[t:0]]אלאעדאם[[t:1]], au pluriel, [[t:2]]les non-êtres[[t:3]], les choses non-existantes. Ibn-Tibbon a [[t:4]]ההעדר[[t:5]], au singulier, ce qui est inexact.

---

### fn.2902 (Footnote)
**English:** In other words: what divine knowledge cannot have as its object is the absolute non-being that never exists, just as our human knowledge cannot have as its object what does not presently have existence.
**Correction:** In other words: what divine knowledge cannot have as its object is the absolute non-being that never exists, just as our human knowledge cannot have as its object what does not presently have existence.

**French (Source):** En d’autres termes: ce que la science divine ne peut avoir pour objet, c’est le non-être absolu qui n’existe jamais, de même que notre science humaine ne peut avoir pour objet ce qui présentement n’a pas d’existence.

---

### fn.2905 (Footnote)
**English:** That is, he knows only what relates to the upper world, and in the sublunary world, his knowledge embraces genera and species, but not individuals. Cf. <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, p. 319.
**Correction:** That is, he knows only what relates to the upper world, and in the sublunary world, his knowledge embraces genera and species, but not individuals. Cf. <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, p. 319.

**French (Source):** C’est-à-dire, il ne connaît que ce qui est relatif au monde supérieur, et, dans le monde sublunaire, sa science embrasse les genres et les espèces, mais non les individus. Cf. [[t:0]]Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe[[t:1]], p. 319.

---

### fn.2906 (Footnote)
**English:** The author shows how one has arrived successively at summarizing all knowledge of God in the knowledge He has of His essence. In short, the author distinguishes three opinions regarding divine knowledge: 1st, that which admits that God encompasses by a <i>single</i> knowledge diverse things and things that occur every day, so that there is in His knowledge neither multiplicity nor change; but according to this opinion, which is that of the believers, God's knowledge would encompass the infinite and non-being. To avoid this difficulty, it has been maintained 2nd, that divine knowledge extends directly only to stable things, that is to say, to individuals of the upper world and to the genera and species of the sublunary world. But others have rightly objected that this opinion would not exclude the multiplicity of divine knowledge, and they were forced to maintain 3rd, that God knows only His own essence, and that it is in the contemplation of Himself that He contemplates the things of which He is the first cause. Further details on these different opinions can be found in Averroes' <i>Destruction of the Destruction</i>, disputat. XIII.
**Correction:** The author shows how one has arrived successively at summarizing all knowledge of God in the knowledge He has of His essence. In short, the author distinguishes three opinions regarding divine knowledge: 1st, that which admits that God encompasses by a <i>single</i> knowledge diverse things and things that occur every day, so that there is in His knowledge neither multiplicity nor change; but according to this opinion, which is that of the believers, God's knowledge would encompass the infinite and non-being. To avoid this difficulty, it has been maintained 2nd, that divine knowledge extends directly only to stable things, that is to say, to individuals of the upper world and to the genera and species of the sublunary world. But others have rightly objected that this opinion would not exclude the multiplicity of divine knowledge, and they were forced to maintain 3rd, that God knows only His own essence, and that it is in the contemplation of Himself that He contemplates the things of which He is the first cause. Further details on these different opinions can be found in Averroes' <i>Destruction of the Destruction</i>, disputat. XIII.

**French (Source):** L’auteur montre comment l’on est arrivé successivement à résumer toute la science de Dieu dans la science qu’il a de son essence. En somme, l’auteur distingue trois opinions sur la science divine: 1° celle qui admet que Dieu embrasse par une science [[t:0]]unique[[t:1]] les choses variées et les choses qui surviennent chaque jour, de sorte qu’il n’y a dans sa science ni multiplicité, ni changement; mais d’après cette opinion, qui est celle des croyants, la science de Dieu embrasserait l’infini et le nonêtre. Pour éviter cette difficulté, on a soutenu 2° que la science divine ne s’étend directement que sur les choses stables, c’est-à-dire sur les individus du monde supérieur et sur les genres et les espèces du monde sublunaire. Mais d’autres ont objecté avec raison que cette opinion n’exclurait pas la multiplicité de la science divine, et ils sont arrivés forcément à soutenir 3° que Dieu ne connaît que sa propre essence, et que c’est dans la contemplation de lui-même qu’il contemple les choses dont il est la cause première. On trouve de plus amples détails sur ces différentes opinions dans la [[t:2]]Destruction de la Destruction[[t:3]] d’Averrhoès, disputât. XIII.

---

### fn.2925 (Footnote)
**English:** Ibn Rushd expresses himself in the same manner regarding the difference between divine knowledge and human knowledge. See <i>Destruction of the Destruction</i>, at the end of disputation <small>XIII</small>; we cite the Hebrew version: <span dir="rtl">וזה שקר אצל הפילוסופים שיהיה מדעו על הקש מדענו לפי שמדענו עלול מהנמצאות ומדעו עלה להם</span> ···· <span dir="rtl">ובכלל הנה כבר קדם שהענין במדע הראשון מקביל הענין במדע האדם ר״ל שמדעו הוא הפועל לנמצאות לא שתהיינה הנמצאות פועלות מדעו ית׳</span> 'It is false, according to the philosophers, that God's knowledge is analogous to our knowledge, for our knowledge is <i>caused</i> by beings, whereas divine knowledge is their <i>cause</i>…. In short, as we have already said, the knowledge of the first being has a meaning directly opposite to that of the knowledge of man; I mean that it is divine knowledge which is the efficient cause of beings, and that it is not beings which are the efficient causes of divine knowledge.' — However, from the perspective of the philosophers, a difficulty remains in that divine knowledge, being absolute unity, cannot be related to the multiple. Ibn Sina claimed to resolve this difficulty through various hypotheses, which were rejected by Ibn Rushd, who, for his part, refuses to admit that divine knowledge extends to particular and accidental things. (See my <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, pp. 360-362). Maimonides wisely stops before this insoluble problem, and limits himself to establishing that we are incapable of forming an idea of divine knowledge, which is identical with the very essence of God, and is inaccessible to our intellects.
**Correction:** Ibn Rushd expresses himself in the same manner regarding the difference between divine knowledge and human knowledge. See <i>Destruction of the Destruction</i>, at the end of disputation <small>XIII</small>; we cite the Hebrew version: <span dir="rtl">וזה שקר אצל הפילוסופים שיהיה מדעו על הקש מדענו לפי שמדענו עלול מהנמצאות ומדעו עלה להם</span> ···· <span dir="rtl">ובכלל הנה כבר קדם שהענין במדע הראשון מקביל הענין במדע האדם ר״ל שמדעו הוא הפועל לנמצאות לא שתהיינה הנמצאות פועלות מדעו ית׳</span> 'It is false, according to the philosophers, that God's knowledge is analogous to our knowledge, for our knowledge is <i>caused</i> by beings, whereas divine knowledge is their <i>cause</i>…. In short, as we have already said, the knowledge of the first being has a meaning directly opposite to that of the knowledge of man; I mean that it is divine knowledge which is the efficient cause of beings, and that it is not beings which are the efficient causes of divine knowledge.' — However, from the perspective of the philosophers, a difficulty remains in that divine knowledge, being absolute unity, cannot be related to the multiple. Ibn Sina claimed to resolve this difficulty through various hypotheses, which were rejected by Ibn Rushd, who, for his part, refuses to admit that divine knowledge extends to particular and accidental things. (See my <i>Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe</i>, pp. 360-362). Maimonides wisely stops before this insoluble problem, and limits himself to establishing that we are incapable of forming an idea of divine knowledge, which is identical with the very essence of God, and is inaccessible to our intellects.

**French (Source):** C’est de la même manière que s’exprime Ibn-Roschd sur la différence qu’il y a entre la science divine et la science humaine. Voy. [[t:0]]Destruction de la Destruction[[t:1]], à la fin de la disputat. [[t:2]]XIII[[t:3]]; nous citons la version hébraïque: [[t:4]]וזה שקר אצל הפילוסופים שיהיה מדעו על הקש מדענו לפי שמדענו עלול מהנמצאות ומדעו עלה להם[[t:5]] ···· [[t:6]]ובכלל הנה כבר קדם שהענין במדע הראשון מקביל הענין במדע האדם ר״ל שמדעו הוא הפועל לנמצאות לא שתהיינה הנמצאות פועלות מדעו ית׳[[t:7]] «Il est faux, selon les philosophes, que la science de Dieu soit analogue à notre science, car notre science est [[t:8]]causée[[t:9]] par les êtres, tandis que la science divine est leur [[t:10]]cause[[t:11]]…. En somme, comme nous l’avons déjà dit, la science de l’être premier a un sens directement opposé à celui de la science de l’homme; je veux dire que c’est la science divine qui est la cause efficiente des êtres, et que ce ne sont pas les êtres qui sont les causes efficientes de la science divine.» — Mais, au point de vue des philosophes, il reste là une difficulté en ce que la science divine, étant l’unité absolue, ne saurait être mise en rapport avec le multiple. Ibn-Sînâ a prétendu résoudre cette difficulté par diverses hypothèses, rejetées par Ibn-Roschd, qui, de son côté, refuse d’admettre que la science divine s’étende sur les choses particulières et accidentelles. (Voy. mes [[t:12]]Mélanges de philosophie juive et arabe[[t:13]], p. 360-362). Maïmonide s’arrête sagement devant ce problème insoluble, et se borne à établir que nous sommes incapables de nous former une idée de la science divine, identique avec l’essence même de Dieu, qui est inaccessible à nos intelligences.

---

### fn.2987 (Footnote)
**English:** Literally: <i>how He did not neglect the primitive work of formation</i> (<span dir="rtl">كون</span>, <i>γένεσις</i>) <i>of the human individual and his creation, yet neglected to govern him</i>. The words <span dir="rtl">אצל צנע כון</span> must be pronounced <span dir="rtl">أَصْلُ صُنْعِ كَوْنٍ</span>, <i>the principle of the work of formation</i>, that is to say, the primitive work of formation. With these words, the author alludes to Job's description of the formation of the fetus (X, 10 et seq.). The word <span dir="rtl">וכׄלקה</span> was considered by Ibn-Tibbon as a verb <span dir="rtl">(وَخَلَقَهُ)</span>, and he translates: <span dir="rtl">וברא אותו</span>; but in that case it would have been more correct to say <span dir="rtl">فَلَقَةُ</span>. I consider this word to be a verbal noun <span dir="rtl">وَخَلَقَهُ</span>, in the accusative, like <span dir="rtl">أصل</span>. It is in this same sense that Al-Harizi translates this word, although his translation is otherwise very confused: <span dir="rtl">והחל לתמוה איך יתכן להתרשל האומן אשר כיון נוף הארס וברייתו ויתרשל בשמירתו</span>.
**Correction:** Literally: <i>how He did not neglect the primitive work of formation</i> (<span dir="rtl">كون</span>, <i>γένεσις</i>) <i>of the human individual and his creation, yet neglected to govern him</i>. The words <span dir="rtl">אצל צנע כון</span> must be pronounced <span dir="rtl">أَصْلُ صُنْعِ كَوْنٍ</span>, <i>the principle of the work of formation</i>, that is to say, the primitive work of formation. With these words, the author alludes to Job's description of the formation of the fetus (X, 10 et seq.). The word <span dir="rtl">וכׄלקה</span> was considered by Ibn-Tibbon as a verb <span dir="rtl">(وَخَلَقَهُ)</span>, and he translates: <span dir="rtl">וברא אותו</span>; but in that case it would have been more correct to say <span dir="rtl">فَلَقَةُ</span>. I consider this word to be a verbal noun <span dir="rtl">وَخَلَقَهُ</span>, in the accusative, like <span dir="rtl">أصل</span>. It is in this same sense that Al-Harizi translates this word, although his translation is otherwise very confused: <span dir="rtl">והחל לתמוה איך יתכן להתרשל האומן אשר כיון נוף הארס וברייתו ויתרשל בשמירתו</span>.

**French (Source):** Littéralement: [[t:0]]comment il n’a pas négligé l’œuvre primitivede la formation[[t:1]] ([[t:2]]كون[[t:3]] , [[t:4]]γένεσις[[t:5]]) [[t:6]]de l’individu humain et sa création, et a pourtant négligé de le gouverner[[t:7]]. Les mots [[t:8]]אצל צנע כון[[t:9]] doivent être prononcés [[t:10]]أَصْلُ صُنْعِ كَوْنٍ[[t:11]] , [[t:12]]le principe de l’œuvre de la formation[[t:13]], c est-a-dire l’œuvre primitive de la formation. Par ces mots, l’auteur fait allusion à la description que donne Job de la formation du fœtus (X, 10 et suiv.). Le mot [[t:14]]וכׄלקה[[t:15]] a été considéré par Ibn-Tibbon comme un verbe [[t:16]](وَخَلَقَهُ)[[t:17]] , et il traduit: [[t:18]]וברא אותו[[t:19]]; mais dans ce cas il eût été plus correct de dire [[t:20]]فَلَقَةُ[[t:21]] . Je considère ce mot comme un nom d’action [[t:22]]وَخَلَقَهُ[[t:23]] , à l’accusatif, comme [[t:24]]أصل[[t:25]] . C’est dans le même sens qu’Al-’Harîzi traduit ce mot, quoique sa traduction soit d’ailleurs très-confuse: [[t:26]]והחל לתמוה איך יתכן להתרשל האומן אשר כיון נוף הארס וברייתו ויתרשל בשמירתו[[t:27]].

### fn.3123.sub_1 (Footnote)
**English:** We now know that in reality there is only one kind of <i>Sabians</i>, namely the Mandaeans, but that the pagans of Harran, threatened with extermination by Caliph Al-Ma'mun, usurped around the year 830 of the Christian era, on the advice of a Muslim doctor, the name of Sabians, and claimed to be the sect mentioned under this name in the Quran and recommended by the prophet to the protection of Muslims. This fact is reported in the <i>Kitab Al-Fihrist</i> by Muhammad ibn Ishaq al-Nadim (see the extract from this book given by Mr. de Hammer in the Journal Asiatique, September-October 1841, p. 254 et seq.). It had already been published by Hottinger, <i>Historia Orientalis</i>, p. 169, and, following him, by Spencer, <i>De legibus Hebræorum ritualibus</i>, Bk. II, ch. <small>I</small>, sect. 2 (p. 241 of the Cambridge edition, 1685, folio). It is especially in the work published on the Sabians by Mr. Chwolson that the fact in question was brought to light and supported by numerous proofs. The author shows how the name Sabians, applied in the Quran only to the Mandaeans and used in this sense by Arabic authors until the time of Al-Ma'mun, later served to designate the Harranians as well and ended, in the 6<sup>th</sup> century of the Hijra, by being used in the general sense of Pagans. See <i>Die Ssabier und der Ssabismus</i> (2 vols. large 8vo, St. Petersburg, 1856), Vol. I, ch. <small>VI</small>, p. 139 et seq., and the entire ch. <small>VIII</small>. This excellent work contains numerous details on the religion of the Harranians, which can clarify several facts reported by Maimonides in this chapter and in those following, where our author aims to explain a large part of the ceremonial practices prescribed by Moses by means of the superstitious customs of the Sabians or pagans that the Mosaic laws tended to abolish.
**Correction:** We now know that in reality there is only one kind of <i>Sabians</i>, namely the Mandaeans, but that the pagans of Harran, threatened with extermination by Caliph Al-Ma'mun, usurped around the year 830 of the Christian era, on the advice of a Muslim doctor, the name of Sabians, and claimed to be the sect mentioned under this name in the Quran and recommended by the prophet to the protection of Muslims. This fact is reported in the <i>Kitab Al-Fihrist</i> by Muhammad ibn Ishaq al-Nadim (see the extract from this book given by Mr. de Hammer in the Journal Asiatique, September-October 1841, p. 254 et seq.). It had already been published by Hottinger, <i>Historia Orientalis</i>, p. 169, and, following him, by Spencer, <i>De legibus Hebræorum ritualibus</i>, Bk. II, ch. <small>I</small>, sect. 2 (p. 241 of the Cambridge edition, 1685, folio). It is especially in the work published on the Sabians by Mr. Chwolson that the fact in question was brought to light and supported by numerous proofs. The author shows how the name Sabians, applied in the Quran only to the Mandaeans and used in this sense by Arabic authors until the time of Al-Ma'mun, later served to designate the Harranians as well and ended, in the 6<sup>th</sup> century of the Hijra, by being used in the general sense of Pagans. See <i>Die Ssabier und der Ssabismus</i> (2 vols. large 8vo, St. Petersburg, 1856), Vol. I, ch. <small>VI</small>, p. 139 et seq., and the entire ch. <small>VIII</small>. This excellent work contains numerous details on the religion of the Harranians, which can clarify several facts reported by Maimonides in this chapter and in those following, where our author aims to explain a large part of the ceremonial practices prescribed by Moses by means of the superstitious customs of the Sabians or pagans that the Mosaic laws tended to abolish.

**French (Source):** Nous savons maintenant qu’il n’y a en réalité qu’une seule espèce de [[t:40]]Sabiens[[t:41]], à savoir les Mendaïtes, mais que les païens de ’Harran, menacés d’être exterminés par le khalife Al-Mamoun, usurpèrent vers l’an 830 de l’ère chrétienne, sur le conseil d’un docteur musulman, le nom de Sabiens, et prétendirent être la secte mentionnée sous ce nom dans le Coran et recommandée par le prophète à la protection des musulmans. Ce fait est rapporté dans le [[t:42]]Kitab Al-Fihrist[[t:43]] par Mohammed ben Is’hak al-Nedîm (voy. l’extrait de ce livre donné par M. de Hammer dans le Journal Asiatique, septembre-octobre 1841, p. 254 et suiv.). Il avait déjà été publié par Hottinger, [[t:44]]Historia Orientalis[[t:45]], p. 169, et, d’après lui, par Spencer, [[t:46]]De legibus Hebrœorum ritualibus[[t:47]], l. II, cap. [[t:48]]I[[t:49]], sect. 2 (p. 241 de l’édition de Cambridge, 1685, in-fol.). C’est surtout dans l’ouvrage publié sur les Sabiens par M. Chwolson, que le fait en question a été mis en lumière et appuyé de preuves nombreuses. L’auteur montre comment le nom de Sabiens, appliqué dans le Coran aux seuls Mendaïtes et employé dans ce sens par les auteurs arabes, jusqu’à l’époque d’Al-Mamoun, servit ensuite à désigner également les Harraniens et finit, au VI[[t:50]]e[[t:51]] siècle de l’Hégire, par être employé dans le sens général de Païens. Voy. [[t:52]]Die Ssabier und der Ssabismus[[t:53]] (2 vol. gr. in-8°, St-Pétersbourg, 1856), t. I, ch. [[t:54]]VI[[t:55]], p. 139 et suiv., et tout le chap. [[t:56]]VIII[[t:57]]. Cet excellent ouvrage renferme de nombreux détails sur la religion des Harraniens, qui peuvent éclaircir plusieurs faits rapportés par Maïmonide dans ce chapitre et dans les suivants, où notre auteur a pour but d’expliquer en grande partie les pratiques cérémonielles prescrites par Moïse, au moyen des usages superstitieux des Sabiens ou païens que les lois mosaïques tendaient à faire disparaître.

### fn.3131 (Footnote)
**English:** All the Arabic MSS have <span dir="rtl">לטרף אלשאם</span>, <i>towards the region of Syria</i>, while Ibn Tibbon's version reads <span dir="rtl">לקצה המזרח</span>, <i>at the extremity of the East;</i> Al-Harizi translates more accurately <span dir="rtl">ויגרש אותו לקצה ארץ כנען</span>, <i>he expelled him toward the extremity of the land of Canaan.</i> — This narrative shows an echo of the Jewish traditions that attribute Abraham's emigration (Genesis <small>XI</small>:31) to the persecutions he endured in his country. Josephus merely states that, the Chaldeans and other peoples of Mesopotamia having risen against him, he thought it best to emigrate (<i>Antiquities</i>, I, 7, § 1). According to rabbinic traditions, Nimrod had him cast into a furnace, from which he was miraculously saved (see my work <i>Palestine</i>, p. 102 <i>b</i>). This tradition also found a place in some Church Fathers and in the Quran, and Muslim authors have embellished it with many details from their imagination. On these various traditions, see B. Beer, <i>Leben Abrahams</i>, <i>nach Auffassung der jüdischen Sage</i>, chap. II. On Muslim traditions in particular, see d’Herbelot, <i>Bibliothèque orientale</i>, under the entry <i>Abraham;</i> Hyde, <i>De religione veterum Persarum</i>, p. 27 et seq. (2<sup>nd</sup> edition); Reinaud, <i>Monuments arabes</i>, <i>persans et turcs</i>, vol. I, p. 144 et seq.

Selon les traditions rabbiniques, Nemrod le fit jeter dans une fournaise, dont il fut miraculeusement sauvé (voy. mon ouvrage [[t:16]]Palestine[[t:17]], p. 102 [[t:18]]b[[t:19]]). ### fn.3154 (Footnote)
**English:** That is, they assigned to each planet one of the seven metals and the seven climata, attributing to each planet an influence over one of the climata, and, like the medieval alchemists, a participation in the formation of the metals. Oriental writers count seven metals, which are: gold, silver, copper, lead, iron, tin, and a 7th <sup> </sup> called <i>Khar-sini</i> (Chinese iron). See Qazwini, in the <i>Chrestomathie arabe</i> by Mr. Silvestre de Sacy, vol. III, p. 390. According to the Sabians, gold is attributed to the sun, silver to the moon, and so forth. See Dimashqi, <i>loc. cit.</i>, p. 411. As for the seven climata, we have already said that Yaqut indicates their relationship with the planets according to the Persians and the Greeks. According to the Persians, the respective order of the planets presiding over each clima conforms to the natural order of the planets beginning with the last; thus Saturn presides over the 1st <sup> </sup> clima, Jupiter over the 2nd <sup> </sup>, Mars over the 3rd <sup> </sup>, the sun over the 4th <sup> </sup>, Venus over the 5th <sup> </sup>, Mercury over the 6th <sup> </sup>, the moon over the 7th <sup> </sup>. According to the Greeks, it is the following order: Saturn, sun, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, moon, Mars. Dimashqi, in his description of the temples dedicated to the planets (<i>loc. cit.</i>, p. 382 et seq.), follows the order adopted by the Persians; likewise Ibn Ezra, in his astrological work <i>Reshit Hokhmah</i>, ch. <small>IV</small>, speaking of the influence exercised by each planet on sublunary things. If our explanation of the words <span dir="rtl">אקלים אלשמס</span> (p. 224, note 1) is correct, the author of the <i>Nabatean Agriculture</i> would have followed the order adopted by the Greeks.
**Correction:** That is, they assigned to each planet one of the seven metals and the seven climata, attributing to each planet an influence over one of the climata, and, like the medieval alchemists, a participation in the formation of the metals. Oriental writers count seven metals, which are: gold, silver, copper, lead, iron, tin, and a 7th <sup> </sup> called <i>Khar-sini</i> (Chinese iron). See Qazwini, in the <i>Chrestomathie arabe</i> by Mr. Silvestre de Sacy, vol. III, p. 390. According to the Sabians, gold is attributed to the sun, silver to the moon, and so forth. See Dimashqi, <i>loc. cit.</i>, p. 411. As for the seven climata, we have already said that Yaqut indicates their relationship with the planets according to the Persians and the Greeks. According to the Persians, the respective order of the planets presiding over each clima conforms to the natural order of the planets beginning with the last; thus Saturn presides over the 1st <sup> </sup> clima, Jupiter over the 2nd <sup> </sup>, Mars over the 3rd <sup> </sup>, the sun over the 4th <sup> </sup>, Venus over the 5th <sup> </sup>, Mercury over the 6th <sup> </sup>, the moon over the 7th <sup> </sup>. According to the Greeks, it is the following order: Saturn, sun, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, moon, Mars. Dimashqi, in his description of the temples dedicated to the planets (<i>loc. cit.</i>, p. 382 et seq.), follows the order adopted by the Persians; likewise Ibn Ezra, in his astrological work <i>Reshit Hokhmah</i>, ch. <small>IV</small>, speaking of the influence exercised by each planet on sublunary things. If our explanation of the words <span dir="rtl">אקלים אלשמס</span> (p. 224, note 1) is correct, the author of the <i>Nabatean Agriculture</i> would have followed the order adopted by the Greeks.

**French (Source):** C’est-à-dire, ils assignèrent à chaque planète l’un des sept métaux et des sept climats, attribuant à chaque planète une influence sur l’un des climats, et, comme les alchimistes du moyen âge, une participation à la formation des métaux. Les écrivains orientaux comptent sept métaux, qui sont: l’or, l’argent, le cuivre, le plomb, le fer, l’étain et un 7[[t:0]]e[[t:1]] appelé le [[t:2]]Khar-sîni[[t:3]] (fer de Chine). Voy. Kazwini, dans la [[t:4]]Chrestomathie arabe[[t:5]] de M. Silvestre de Sacy, t. III, p. 390. Selon les Sabiens, l’or est attribué au soleil, l’argent à la lune, et ainsi de suite. Voy. Dimeschki, [[t:6]]l. c.[[t:7]], p. 411. Quant aux sept climats, nous avons déjà dit que Yakout en indique les rapports avec les planètes selon les Perses et les Grecs. Selon les Perses, l’ordre respectif des planètes présidant à chaque climat est conforme à l’ordre naturel des planètes en commençant par la dernière; ainsi Saturne préside au 1[[t:8]]er[[t:9]] climat, Jupiter au 2[[t:10]]e[[t:11]], Mars au 3[[t:12]]e[[t:13]], le soleil au 4[[t:14]]e[[t:15]], Vénus au 5[[t:16]]e[[t:17]], Mercure au 6[[t:18]]e[[t:19]], la lune au 7[[t:20]]e[[t:21]]. Selon les Grecs, c’est l’ordre suivant: Saturne, soleil, Mercure, Jupiter, Vénus, lune, Mars. Dimeschki, dans sa description des temples consacrés aux planètes ([[t:22]]l. c.[[t:23]], p. 382 et suiv.), suit l’ordre adopté par les Perses; de même Ibn-Ezra, dans son ouvrage astrologique [[t:24]]Réschith ’Hokhma[[t:25]], ch. [[t:26]]IV[[t:27]], en parlant de l’influence exercée par chaque planète sur les choses sublunaires. Si notre explication des mots [[t:28]]אקלים אלשמס[[t:29]] (p. 224, note 1) est exacte, l’auteur de l’[[t:30]]Agriculture Nabatéenne[[t:31]] aurait suivi l’ordre adopté par les Grecs.

### fn.3157 (Footnote)
**English:** Regarding the word <span dir="rtl">דוחאניה̈</span>, see vol. I, p. 281, note 2. Regarding this paragraph in general, cf. Shahrastani, p. 244 et seq. (German trans., vol. II, p. 66 et seq.), and Pococke, <i>loc. cit.</i>, p. 139 et seq. The latter points out (p. 143) that, according to Shahrastani, the Sabians saw in the spiritual forces of the stars only <i>intermediary</i> beings above whom is the Lord of lords or the supreme God, which is contrary to the idea Maimonides gives of the Sabian religion; however, we believe that Shahrastani was led into error by the writings of some Sabians of Harran, who had mixed the beliefs of the ancient Chaldean pagans with the philosophical doctrines of the Neoplatonists.
**Correction:** Regarding the word <span dir="rtl">דוחאניה̈</span>, see vol. I, p. 281, note 2. Regarding this paragraph in general, cf. Shahrastani, p. 244 et seq. (German trans., vol. II, p. 66 et seq.), and Pococke, <i>loc. cit.</i>, p. 139 et seq. The latter points out (p. 143) that, according to Shahrastani, the Sabians saw in the spiritual forces of the stars only <i>intermediary</i> beings above whom is the Lord of lords or the supreme God, which is contrary to the idea Maimonides gives of the Sabian religion; however, we believe that Shahrastani was led into error by the writings of some Sabians of Harran, who had mixed the beliefs of the ancient Chaldean pagans with the philosophical doctrines of the Neoplatonists.

**French (Source):** Sur le mot [[t:0]]דוחאניה̈[[t:1]], voy. t. I, p. 281, note 2. Sur ce paragraphe en général, cf. Schahrestâni, p. 244 et suiv. (trad. all., t. II, p. 66 et suiv.), et Pococke, [[t:2]]l. c.[[t:3]], p. 139 et suiv. Ce dernier fait ressortir (p. 143) que, selon Schahrestâni, les Sabiens ne voyaient dans les forces spirituelles des astres que des êtres [[t:4]]intermédiaires[[t:5]] au-dessus desquels est le Seigneur des seigneurs ou le Dieu suprême, ce qui est contraire à l’idée que Maïmonide donne de la religion des Sabiens; mais nous croyons que Schahrestâni s’est laissé induire en erreur par les écrits de quelques Sabiens de Harran, qui avaient mêlé ensemble les croyances des anciens païens chaldéens avec les doctrines philosophiques des néoplatoniciens.

### fn.3168 (Footnote)
**English:** Abu Bakr Ahmad ibn Ali ibn Wahshiyya, descended from a Nabataean or Chaldean family that had embraced Islam, published in 291 AH (904 CE) a vast work titled <span dir="rtl">الفلاحة النبطية</span> , <i>Nabataean Agriculture</i>, which he claimed to have translated from Chaldean, and for which he attributed authorship to an ancient Chaldean sage named Quthami. The latter cites many older authors, foremost among whom we note Dewanai, Saghrith, and Yanbushad, the latter being the preceptor of Adami or Adam (cf. the <i>Khozari</i>, book I, § 61). The work in question contains, alongside many rational agronomic theories, a host of absurd fables and alleged historical information regarding the Canaanites, Chaldeans, and Assyrians. This work, which was initially known to European scholars only through Maimonides' citations, has recently been the object of more serious examination. Mr. Étienne Quatremère, who had at his disposal only the 2<sup>nd</sup> and 3<sup>rd</sup> parts of the work (Arabic MS of the Imperial Library, no. 913), dates Quthami back to approximately the beginning of the 6<sup>th</sup> century BCE. See <i>Memoir on the Nabataeans</i>, in the <i>Journal Asiatique</i>, March 1835, p. 231 et seq. Mr. Chwolson, who had before him the Leiden and Saint Petersburg manuscripts containing the entire work divided into nine parts, arrived at a much more astonishing result; according to him, the composition of <i>Nabataean Agriculture</i> by Quthami dates back at least to the 14<sup>th</sup> century BCE. See the memoir cited above, p. 222, note 5. It was not difficult for authors more accustomed than Mr. Chwolson to handling historical criticism to demonstrate the exorbitant nature of his thesis. Already Spencer had thought that this work and Sabian writings in general must belong to that pseudepigraphical literature which developed so extensively in the first centuries of the Christian era:
**Correction:** Abu Bakr Ahmad ibn Ali ibn Wahshiyya, descended from a Nabataean or Chaldean family that had embraced Islam, published in 291 AH (904 CE) a vast work titled <span dir="rtl">الفلاحة النبطية</span> , <i>Nabataean Agriculture</i>, which he claimed to have translated from Chaldean, and for which he attributed authorship to an ancient Chaldean sage named Quthami. The latter cites many older authors, foremost among whom we note Dewanai, Saghrith, and Yanbushad, the latter being the preceptor of Adami or Adam (cf. the <i>Khozari</i>, book I, § 61). The work in question contains, alongside many rational agronomic theories, a host of absurd fables and alleged historical information regarding the Canaanites, Chaldeans, and Assyrians. This work, which was initially known to European scholars only through Maimonides' citations, has recently been the object of more serious examination. Mr. Étienne Quatremère, who had at his disposal only the 2<sup>nd</sup> and 3<sup>rd</sup> parts of the work (Arabic MS of the Imperial Library, no. 913), dates Quthami back to approximately the beginning of the 6<sup>th</sup> century BCE. See <i>Memoir on the Nabataeans</i>, in the <i>Journal Asiatique</i>, March 1835, p. 231 et seq. Mr. Chwolson, who had before him the Leiden and Saint Petersburg manuscripts containing the entire work divided into nine parts, arrived at a much more astonishing result; according to him, the composition of <i>Nabataean Agriculture</i> by Quthami dates back at least to the 14<sup>th</sup> century BCE. See the memoir cited above, p. 222, note 5. It was not difficult for authors more accustomed than Mr. Chwolson to handling historical criticism to demonstrate the exorbitant nature of his thesis. Already Spencer had thought that this work and Sabian writings in general must belong to that pseudepigraphical literature which developed so extensively in the first centuries of the Christian era:

**French (Source):** Abou-Bekr A’hmed ben ’Ali Ibn-Wa’hschiyya, issu d’une famille nabatéenne ou chaldéenne qui avait embrassé l’islamisme, fit paraître, en 291 de l’Hégire (904 de J. C.), un vaste ouvrage intitulé [[t:0]]الفلاحة النبطية[[t:1]] , l’[[t:2]]Agriculture Nabatéenne[[t:3]], qu’il disait avoir traduit du chaldéen, et auquel il donnait pour auteur un ancien sage chaldéen nommé Kothâmi. Celui-ci cite beaucoup d’auteurs plus anciens, en tête desquels nous remarquons Dewanai, Çaghrith et Yanbouschad, ce dernier, précepteur d’Adâmi ou Adam (cf. le [[t:4]]Khozari[[t:5]], l. I, § 61). L’ouvrage en question renferme, à côté de beaucoup de théories agronomiques rationnelles, une foule de fables absurdes et des renseignements prétendus historiques sur les Kananéens, les Chaldéens et les Assyriens. Cet ouvrage, qui n’était d’abord connu des savants d’Europe que par les citations de Maïmonide, a été, dans ces derniers temps, l’objet d’un examen plus sérieux. M. Étienne Quatremère, qui n’avait a sa disposition que la 2[[t:6]]e[[t:7]] et la 3[[t:8]]e[[t:9]] partie de l’ouvrage (ms. ar. de la Biblioth. impér., n° 913), fait remonter Kothâmi jusque vers le commencement du VI[[t:10]]e[[t:11]] siècle avant l’ère chrétienne. Voy. [[t:12]]Mémoire sur les Nabatéens[[t:13]], dans le [[t:14]]Journal Asiatique[[t:15]], mars 1835, p. 231 et suiv. M. Chwolson, qui avait sous les yeux les mss. de Leyde et de Saint-Pétersbourg, renfermant l’ouvrage entier divisé en neuf parties, est arrivé à un résultat bien autrement étonnant; selon lui, la composition de l’[[t:16]]Agriculture Nabatéenne[[t:17]] par Kothâmi remonterait au moins au XIV[[t:18]]e[[t:19]] siècle avant l’ère chrétienne. Voy. le mémoire cité ci-dessus, p. 222, note 5. Il n’a pas été difficile, pour des auteurs plus habitués que M. Chwolson à manier la critique historique, de démontrer tout ce que sa thèse a d’exorbitant. Déjà Spencer avait pensé que cet ouvrage et les écrits sabiens en général devaient appartenir à cette littérature pseudépigraphique qui prit un si grand développement dans les premiers siècles de l’ère chrétienne:

---
